Enumerative invariants in self-dual categories. I. Motivic invariants
Chenjing Bu

Abstract
This is the first paper in a series where we propose a theory of enumerative invariants counting self-dual objects in self-dual categories. Ordinary enumerative invariants in abelian categories can be seen as invariants for the structure group GL ⁡ ( n ) \mathrm{GL}(n) , and our theory is an extension of this to structure groups O ⁡ ( n ) \mathrm{O}(n) and Sp ⁡ ( 2 ​ n ) \mathrm{Sp}(2n) . Examples of our invariants include invariants counting principal orthogonal or symplectic bundles, and invariants counting self-dual quiver representations. In the present paper, we take the motivic approach, and define our invariants as elements in a ring of motives. We also extract numerical invariants by taking Euler characteristics of these elements. We prove wall-crossing formulae relating our invariants for different stability conditions. We also provide an explicit algorithm computing invariants for quiver representations, and we present some numerical results.

MnLargeSymbols’005

MnLargeSymbols’107

Note

This paper is superseded by a new paper,

Orthosymplectic Donaldson–Thomas theory
,
arXiv:
2503.20667
.
The new paper significantly simplifies the theory,
and contains most of the main ideas here.

Section 1Introduction

1.1.1.

A theory of
enumerative invariants

is a certain way to assign numbers, or other types of data,
to certain types of moduli stacks.
One major branch of the study of such invariants is the study of
moduli stacks of objects in an
abelian category
,
such as the category of coherent sheaves on a smooth projective variety,
or the category of representations of a quiver.
Plenty of work has been done in this area,
and different flavours of invariants have been developed.
Notable examples include:

(i)

Thomas’
[
56
]
theory of Donaldson–Thomas invariants
counting coherent sheaves on Calabi–Yau threefolds.

(ii)

Joyce’s series of work

[
26
,
27
,
28
,
30
,
29
]

on motivic invariants in abelian categories.

(iii)

Mochizuki’s
[
43
]
theory of
algebraic Donaldson invariants
counting coherent sheaves on algebraic surfaces.

(iv)

The theory of Pandharipande–Thomas invariants
[
45
]

counting stable pairs of coherent sheaves on a Calabi–Yau
3
-fold.

(v)

The theory of generalized Donaldson–Thomas invariants
in
3
-Calabi–Yau categories, using the motivic approach,
developed independently by Joyce–Song
[
32
]

and Kontsevich–Soibelman
[
35
]
.

(vi)

The theory of cohomological Hall algebras,
initiated by the work of
Kontsevich–Soibelman
[
36
]
,
and the categorification of Donaldson–Thomas invariants,
notable works on which include Efimov
[
16
]
,
Meinhardt–Reineke
[
42
]
,
Davison–Meinhardt
[
12
]
,
and others.

(vii)

The work of Tanaka–Thomas
[
54
,
53
]

and Thomas
[
57
]

on the theory of Vafa–Witten invariants
counting Higgs pairs on a surface.

(viii)

Joyce’s recent work
[
31
]

on homological enumerative invariants,
and the vertex algebra structure on the homology of moduli stacks.

……

1.1.2.

On the other hand, these are all part of a more general problem,
which is that of counting
principal
G
-bundles
on a variety,
or some coherent sheaf analogue of them, for an algebraic group
G
.
One could also consider quiver analogues of this problem,
using a suitable notion of
G
-quivers
,
such as the notion defined in Derksen–Weyman
[
14
]
,
or a modification of it.
We shall refer to such invariants as
G
-invariants
.

Theories of invariants in abelian categories, as discussed above,
address the cases when
G=\mathrm{GL}(n)
, or sometimes
\mathrm{SL}(n)
,
which form the A family of algebraic groups.
One advantage in this case is that the objects in question
often form an
abelian category
,
which provides rich structures one could work with.

The present series of papers will address the cases when

G=\mathrm{O}(n)
or
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
,
which cover the B, C, and D families of algebraic groups.
In this case, the objects in question are often

self-dual objects in a self-dual category
,
which is a useful structure.

In particular, the present paper aims to develop
a theory of
G
-invariants for
G=\mathrm{O}(n)
or
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
,
in the flavour of
Item

(ii)
above.

1.1.3.

Previous work on this topic includes
Young
[
62
,
63
]
,
who studied Donaldson–Thomas theory for self-dual quivers,
which are
G
-quivers for
G=\mathrm{O}(n)
or
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
.
The present work is based on a general setting
that is similar to that of Young’s work,
and a part of our constructions can also be seen as
generalizations of those of Young.

1.1.4.

We summarize our main constructions and results as follows.
We start with the following data:

•

A
self-dual category

\mathcal{A}
,
with moduli stack
\mathcal{M}
.
Here,
self-dual
means that there is an equivalence

(-)^{\vee}\colon\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}
,
which squares to the identity of
\mathcal{A}
.

•

A
groupoid of self-dual objects

\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
,
with moduli stack
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
.
Here, a
self-dual object
is an object
E\in\mathcal{A}

with an isomorphism
\phi\colon E\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}E^{\vee}

such that
\phi=\phi^{\vee}
.

For example, one could consider the following situations:

•

\mathcal{A}
is the category of vector bundles on an algebraic curve
X
,
and
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
is the groupoid of orthogonal or symplectic
principal bundles on
X
.

•

\mathcal{A}
is the category of representations of a self-dual quiver
Q
,
and
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
is the groupoid of
self-dual representations
.

Joyce
[
27
]
defined
motivic Hall algebras

associated to the moduli stack
\mathcal{M}
.
These are algebraic structures
that describe
extensions
in the category
\mathcal{A}
.
Its elements, called
stack functions
,
can be thought of as
motives
relative to
\mathcal{M}
.
The Hall algebra operation is given by parametrizing
all possible middle terms in a short exact sequence
where the first and third terms are given.

Joyce
[
30
]
then defined elements

\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)
and
\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)

in the motivic Hall algebra,
where
\alpha
is a
K
-theory class, and
\tau
is a stability condition on
\mathcal{A}
.
The element
\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)

parametrizes the family of
\tau
-semistable objects of class
\alpha
;
the element
\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)
is similar to
\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
but assigns rational coefficients to strictly semistable objects,
so that these objects can be ‘counted with correct multiplicities’.
The relation between them can be written as

\epsilon(\tau;t)=\log\delta(\tau;t)\ ,

(1.1)

where
t
is a fixed slope.
See
Eq.

5.4

below for the precise meaning of this.

In our case of self-dual categories, we define
motivic Hall modules
,
which are modules for motivic Hall algebras,
describing
self-dual extensions
of a self-dual object by an ordinary object.
We define elements
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
and
\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

in the motivic Hall module,
where
\theta
is a
K
-theory class for self-dual objects.
The element
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

parametrizes the family of
\tau
-semistable self-dual objects of class
\theta
;
the element
\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

assigns rational coefficients to strictly semistable self-dual objects,
which are the correct multiplicities, given by

\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\tau)=\delta(\tau;0)^{-1/2}\diamond\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\tau)\ ,

(1.2)

where
\diamond
is the module operation,
as in
Eq.

5.9
below.
This is one of the key constructions in this paper.

When we change the stability condition
\tau
,
the changes of the elements
\delta,\epsilon,\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}},\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}

are described by
wall-crossing formulae
.
For two stability conditions
\tau,\tilde{\tau}
,
under certain assumptions,
we prove the wall-crossing formula

\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tilde{\tau})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[39.86122pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &n\geqslant 0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A}),\,\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-4.52083pt]
&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\epsilon_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\diamond\cdots\diamond\epsilon_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\diamond\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tau)\ ,

(1.3)

as in
Eq.

5.33
below,
where
U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}({\cdots})\in\mathbb{Q}
are combinatorial coefficients,
and other notations are explained there.
Note that this involves both self-dual and ordinary invariants.
This is the self-dual analogue of
the wall-crossing formula due to Joyce
[
30
]
,
and can also be used as a tool for computing invariants.

We also show that the wall-crossing formulae
can be rewritten in terms of Lie brackets in the motivic Hall algebra,
together with a Lie bracket-like action on the motivic Hall module,
denoted by
\mathbin{\heartsuit}
, as

\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tilde{\tau})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[79.72244pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-9.04166pt]
&n\geqslant 0;\,m_{1},\dotsc,m_{n}>0;\\[-1.50694pt]
&\alpha_{1,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{1,m_{1}};\dotsc;\alpha_{n,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n,m_{n}}\in C(\mathcal{A});\,\rho\in\smash{C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})}\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=(\bar{\alpha}_{1,1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{1,m_{1}})+\cdots+(\bar{\alpha}_{n,1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n,m_{n}})+\rho\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}\tilde{U}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{1,m_{1}};\dotsc;\alpha_{n,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n,m_{n}};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot{}\\[4.30554pt]
\bigl[\bigl[\epsilon_{\alpha_{1,1}}(\tau),\dotsc\bigr],\epsilon_{\alpha_{1,m_{1}}}(\tau)\bigr]\mathbin{\heartsuit}\cdots\mathbin{\heartsuit}\bigl[\bigl[\epsilon_{\alpha_{n,1}}(\tau),\dotsc\bigr],\epsilon_{\alpha_{n,m_{n}}}(\tau)\bigr]\mathbin{\heartsuit}\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tau)\ ,

(1.4)

as in
Theorem

5.4.6
,
where notations are explained there.
This gives hints on a possible
universal wall-crossing theory for type B/C/D invariants,
which can also apply to other situations where
we only have well-behaved Lie algebra and twisted module structures,
but not necessarily associative algebra and module structures.
In particular, this may apply to self-dual analogues
of
Item

(v)
and
Item

(viii)
above.

One can extract invariants

\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,

\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau)
, and
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,
from the elements

\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,

\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)
, and
\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

by performing
motivic integration
,
where the ordinary case is due to Joyce
[
30
]
.
These invariants live in a ring of motives,
and satisfy similar relations and wall-crossing formulae
as the ones mentioned above for the stack functions

\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,

\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)
, and
\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
.

From these, one can then obtain numerical invariants

\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in\mathbb{Q}
,
by taking the
Euler characteristics
of the invariants

\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
.
These are well-defined due to the
no-pole theorems
,

5.6.2
and
5.6.4
,
which state that these Euler characteristics are finite,
which is not true for the invariants
\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\tau)
and
\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
.
The self-dual no-pole theorem,
5.6.4
,
is one of the key results of this paper.
We also prove wall-crossing formulae
for these numerical invariants,
under a change of stability condition.

We also define
Donaldson–Thomas invariants
in type B/C/D,
for self-dual quivers with potentials,
as well as sheaves on a Calabi–Yau threefold,
in
Sections

6.5
and
7.4
,
as a self-dual analogue of the type A case developed by
Joyce–Song
[
32
]
and
Kontsevich–Soibelman
[
35
]
.
We expect that these invariants satisfy wall-crossing formulae,
but we hope to study them in more detail in future work.

1.1.5.

One limitation of the motivic method,
in its form in this paper,
is that wall-crossing formulae
are only true in one-dimensional categories,
or slightly more general situations,
such as the category of bundles over curves,
or representations of quivers without relations,
or sheaves on a surface of Kodaira dimension
\leqslant 0
.
In more general situations, the invariants should still be well-defined,
but they may no longer satisfy the wall-crossing formulae.

However, in future papers of this series,
we hope to explore other approaches to defining self-dual invariants,
which would possibly be analogues
of
Item

(v)
,
Item

(vi)
or
Item

(viii)
above.
For
Item

(v)
,
this paper defines
Donaldson–Thomas invariants
in type B/C/D,
for self-dual quivers with potentials,
as well as orthogonal or symplectic sheaves on a Calabi–Yau threefold,
and we hope to study them in more detail in future work.
Some work on
Item

(vi)
has already been done by Young
[
63
]
.
For
Item

(viii)
,
we also hope to construct an analogue in the self-dual setting.

1.1.6.

This paper is organized as follows.
In
Section

2
,
we present background material on exact categories
and stability conditions,
and we formulate a notion of moduli stacks for exact categories.
These moduli stacks will be
stacks in categories
,
and carry extra structure that will be helpful for our theory.
In
Section

3
,
we define
self-dual categories
,
and study stability conditions on self-dual categories.
We also define moduli stacks
of self-dual objects in a self-dual category.
In
Section

4
,
we define algebraic structures arising from moduli stacks
of exact categories and self-dual exact categories,
including
motivic Hall algebras
and

motivic Hall modules
.
In
Section

5
,
we present most of our main results.
We define self-dual motivic enumerative invariants,
prove their wall-crossing formulae under a change of stability condition,
and we state the
no-pole theorems
,
which allows us to take the Euler characteristic of the invariants,
obtaining numerical invariants.

In the sections that follow,
Sections

6
to
7
,
we discuss two main applications of our theory,
namely, self-dual representations of self-dual quivers,
and orthogonal or symplectic coherent sheaves
on a smooth, projective variety.

In
Appendix

A
,
we present background material on rings of motives,
and the theory of stack functions as in Joyce
[
29
]
.
In
Appendices

B
and
C
,
we study theory of stacks in categories and

2
-vector bundles,
and prove some basic results about them.
In
Appendices

D
and
E
,
we present the proofs of two of our main results,

Theorems

5.4.6
and
5.6.4
.

1.1.8. Notations and conventions.

Throughout this paper,

•

\mathbb{K}
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic
0
.

•

A
\mathbb{K}
-variety
is a
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
that is separated and of finite type.
We do not require a variety to be irreducible or reduced.
However, a
projective variety

is always assumed to be integral, i.e. irreducible and reduced.

are labelled with letters in brackets rather than numbers.
For the reader’s convenience,
we list all of them here,
together with the section numbers
where they are defined.

Section 2Exact categories and moduli stacks

A major branch of enumerative geometry is concerned with
studying moduli spaces of objects in
abelian categories
.
Here, we generalize this slightly to the setting of

exact categories
or
quasi-abelian categories
,
which are weaker than abelian categories,
but should still work reasonably well in the context of enumerative geometry.

The main reason for doing this is that
the abelian categories that we are interested in,
such as the category of coherent sheaves on a variety,
do not always have
self-dual structures
,
which we need in order to study enumerative invariants
for type B/C/D structure groups.
However, it is often the case that
we have a quasi-abelian subcategory of
the original abelian category that is self-dual,
so we will choose to work with this quasi-abelian subcategory instead.

2.1   Exact categories

2.1.1.

We start by recalling the definition of an exact category.
The following list of axioms is taken from
Keller
[
34
, Appendix A]
,
with some modifications.

Definition 0
.

An
exact category
is a pair
(\mathcal{A},\smash[b]{\textsf{{Ex}}})
,
where
\mathcal{A}
is an additive category,
and
Ex
is a class of sequences of maps

0\to E\overset{i}{\longrightarrow}G\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}F\to 0

(2.1)

in
\mathcal{A}
, called
short exact sequences
,
satisfying the following axioms.

We call morphisms that appear as the first (resp. second)
non-trivial arrow in a short exact sequence
an
inclusion
(resp. a
projection
).
Then,

(i)

Sequences of the form

0\to E\to E\oplus F\to F\to 0
,
called
split exact sequences
,
are short exact,
where the two non-trivial maps are the canonical ones.

(ii)

All short exact sequences are
kernel–cokernel pairs.
That is, in
Eq.

2.1
,
we always have
i=\ker(p)
and

p=\operatorname{coker}(i)
.

(iii)

Inclusions and projections
are closed under composition.

(iv)

Pushouts along inclusions exist,
and inclusions are closed under pushouts.
Dually, pullbacks along projections exist,
and projections are closed under pullbacks.

In this case,
we usually denote the exact category just by
\mathcal{A}
.

2.1.3. Relation to abelian categories.

Every abelian category is an exact category,
by taking
Ex
to be the class of
short exact sequences in that abelian category.

Moreover, any additive full subcategory of an abelian category
is an exact category,
where the notion of exactness is inherited from the
original abelian category.

Conversely, Quillen
[
47
, §2]
showed that
any essentially small exact category
\mathcal{A}

can be embedded in an abelian category
\hat{\mathcal{A}}
,
as a full, additive subcategory that is closed under extensions,
such that exact sequences in
\mathcal{A}
are precisely
those sequences that are exact in
\hat{\mathcal{A}}
.

For objects
E,F\in\mathcal{A}
, one can define the
Ext groups

\mathrm{Ext}^{n}(E,F)
for
n\geqslant 0
,
as in Neeman–Retakh
[
44
]
,
as the group of isomorphism classes of
n
-extensions.
The embedding
\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\hat{\mathcal{A}}
above
induces an isomorphism

\mathrm{Ext}^{n}(E,F)\simeq\mathrm{Ext}^{n}_{\smash{\hat{\mathcal{A}}}}(E,F)

at least for
n=0,1
.

2.1.4. The Grothendieck monoid.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an exact category
that is
essentially small
,
that is, the class of isomorphism classes of objects of
\mathcal{A}
form a set,
then we define the
Grothendieck monoid
of
\mathcal{A}

to be the abelian monoid
K_{+}(\mathcal{A})
,
which is the quotient of the free abelian monoid generated by
isomorphism classes
[E]
of objects
E\in\mathcal{A}
,
by the relations

[0]\sim 0\ ,\qquad[G]\sim[E]+[F]

for short exact sequences
Eq.

2.1
.

2.1.5.

We formulate the following set-up for exact categories,
which will be used in the rest of this paper.

Condition 0
(ExCat).

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an essentially small
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category,
with the following properties and extra data:

(i)

We assume that
\mathcal{A}
is
noetherian
.
This means that for any object
E\in\mathcal{A}
,
any increasing chain of inclusions

E_{0}\hookrightarrow E_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow E

must stabilize.

(ii)

We are given
a fixed quotient monoid
C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
of
K_{+}(\mathcal{A})
,
called the
class monoid
of
\mathcal{A}
.
For an object
E\in\mathcal{A}
,
write
\llbr E\rrbr\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
for the class of
E
in
C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
.
We assume that
\llbr E\rrbr=0
implies
E\simeq 0
.
Write
C(\mathcal{A})=C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})\setminus\{0\}
,
which is a sub-semigroup of
C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
.

Remark 2.1.7
.

In the condition
,
if
\mathcal{A}
also has a
self-dual structure

in the sense of
Section

3.1
below,
then the noetherian assumption on
\mathcal{A}

also implies that
\mathcal{A}
is
artinian
,
meaning that for any object
E\in\mathcal{A}
,
any decreasing chain of subobjects

E=E_{0}\hookleftarrow E_{1}\hookleftarrow\cdots

must stabilize.

2.2   Quasi-abelian categories

2.2.1.

Quasi-abelian categories are exact categories
satisfying certain extra properties.
A good reference for quasi-abelian categories
from the viewpoint of enumerative geometry and
stability conditions is Bridgeland
[
10
, §4]
,
who showed that quasi-abelian categories
can be viewed as slices in an abelian or triangulated category
with a stability condition,
consisting of objects with slopes in a certain interval.

Many constructions and results in this paper
work in the generality of exact categories.
However, the
no-pole theorems
,

5.6.2
and
5.6.4
below,
only hold for quasi-abelian categories,
as their proofs rely on
Lemma

2.2.4
below.

Definition 2.2.2
.

A
quasi-abelian category

is an exact category
(\mathcal{A},\smash[b]{\textsf{{Ex}}})

that has all kernels and cokernels,
such that
Ex

is precisely the class of all kernel–cokernel pairs,
that is, sequences
Eq.

2.1

with
i=\ker(p)
and
p=\operatorname{coker}(i)
.

2.2.3. Relation to abelian categories.

As in Schneiders
[
49
, §1.2.3]
,
for each quasi-abelian category
\mathcal{A}
,
there exist abelian categories
\mathcal{A}^{+}
and
\mathcal{A}^{-}
,
and exact fully faithful embeddings
\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{+}
and
\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{-}
,
such that
\mathcal{A}
is closed under extensions in both categories,
closed under taking subobjects in
\mathcal{A}^{+}
,
and closed under taking quotient objects in
\mathcal{A}^{-}
.

Lemma 2.2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\mathbb{K}
-linear quasi-abelian category,

E\in\mathcal{A}
an object,

n\geqslant 1
an integer, and

\iota\colon\mathbb{K}^{n}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{End}(E)
an inclusion of
\mathbb{K}
-algebras,
where
\mathbb{K}^{n}
has component-wise multiplication.
Then, there exists a unique decomposition

E\simeq E_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus E_{n}

(2.2)

up to isomorphisms,
such that for each
i
,
the
i
-th basis element
e_{i}\in\mathbb{K}^{n}

acts on
E_{i}
as the identity,
and acts on
E_{j}
as zero for
j\neq i
.

Proof 1
.

Define
E_{i}=\ker(\iota(1-e_{i}))
.
The subfunctor

\mathrm{Hom}(-,E_{i})\subset\mathrm{Hom}(-,E)\colon\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathbb{K}\textnormal{-}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Vect}}}

is the
1
-eigenspace of the action of
e_{i}
,
and
\mathrm{Hom}(-,E)
is the direct sum of such eigenspaces,
giving the desired decomposition by the Yoneda lemma.

\Box

2.2.5.

For convenience of referencing later,
we formulate a condition on exact categories,
which is stronger than
,
and requires the exact category to satisfy
the above splitting property,
which are satisfied by quasi-abelian categories.

Condition 0
(Spl).

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category,
with properties and extra data as in
,
such that
\mathcal{A}
satisfies the property described in

Lemma

2.2.4
above,
but
\mathcal{A}
is not necessarily quasi-abelian.

2.3   Categories of filtrations

Definition 2.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an exact category.

For an integer
n\geqslant 0
,
the
category of
n
-step filtrations
of
\mathcal{A}

is the exact category
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}

whose objects are sequences of inclusions

E_{\bullet}=\Bigl(\ 0=E_{0}\hookrightarrow E_{1}\hookrightarrow E_{2}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow E_{n}\ \Bigr)

(2.3)

in
\mathcal{A}
, and whose morphisms are commutative diagrams.
Exact sequences in
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}

are defined degree-wise.

Note that
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(0)}}\simeq\{0\}
,

\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(1)}}\simeq\mathcal{A}
,
and
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(2)}}
can be identified with
the category of short exact sequences in
\mathcal{A}
.

2.3.2. The class monoid.

If, moreover,
\mathcal{A}
is equipped with extra data as in
,
including a class monoid
C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
,
then we can also define

C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}})=C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})^{n}
,
and set

\llbr E_{\bullet}\rrbr=(\llbr E_{1}/E_{0}\rrbr,\,\llbr E_{2}/E_{1}\rrbr,\,\dotsc,\,\llbr E_{n}/E_{n-1}\rrbr)

(2.4)

for
E_{\bullet}\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}
as in
Eq.

2.3
,
where quotients denote cokernels of inclusions.
With this extra data, the
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category

\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}
now also satisfies
.

2.3.3. Projection functors.

For an integer
n\geqslant 0
,
let
[n]
denote the totally ordered set
\{0,1,\dotsc,n\}
.
For an order-preserving map
f\colon[m]\to[n]
,
there is an exact functor

p^{f}\colon\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(m)}}\ ,

(2.5)

sending a sequence
Eq.

2.3
to the sequence

p^{f}(E_{\bullet})=\Bigl(\ E_{f(1)}/E_{f(0)}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow E_{f(m)}/E_{f(0)}\ \Bigr)\ ,

(2.6)

where quotients denote cokernels of inclusions.

For an integer
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, we introduce shorthands

\displaystyle p^{\smash{(n)}}

\displaystyle=p^{(0,\mspace{2mu}n)\colon[1]\hookrightarrow[n]}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}\ ,

(2.7)

\displaystyle p_{i}

\displaystyle=p^{(i-1,\mspace{2mu}i)\colon[1]\hookrightarrow[n]}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}\ .

(2.8)

2.4   Stability conditions

2.4.1.

We define a notion of
stability conditions

on exact categories, based on ideas from
Rudakov
[
48
]
,
Bridgeland
[
10
]
,
Joyce
[
28
]
,
and many others.

Definition 0
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category, as in
.
Let
(T,\leqslant)
be a totally ordered set, and let

\tau\colon C(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow T

be a map of sets.
Write
\tau(E)
for
\tau(\llbr E\rrbr)

for an object
E\in\mathcal{A}
.

An object
E\in\mathcal{A}
is
\tau
-semistable
,
if for any inclusion
i\colon F\hookrightarrow E
,
with
0\neq F\neq E
, we have
\tau(F)\leqslant\tau(E)\leqslant\tau(E/F)
,
where
E/F
denotes the cokernel of
i
.

An object
E\in\mathcal{A}
is
\tau
-stable
,
if it is non-zero, and for any inclusion
i\colon F\hookrightarrow E
,
with
0\neq F\neq E
, we have
\tau(F)<\tau(E)<\tau(E/F)
.

We say that
\tau
is a
weak stability condition
on
\mathcal{A}
,
if the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

For any
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in C(\mathcal{A})

such that
\beta=\alpha+\gamma
, either

\tau(\alpha)\leqslant\tau(\beta)\leqslant\tau(\gamma)\ ,\quad\text{or}\quad\tau(\alpha)\geqslant\tau(\beta)\geqslant\tau(\gamma)\ .

(ii)

If
E,F\in\mathcal{A}
are non-zero and
\tau
-semistable,
and
\tau(E)>\tau(F)
, then

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(E,F)=0\ .

(iii)

Every object
E\in\mathcal{A}
has a

Harder–Narasimhan filtration
, that is a filtration

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathllap{0={}}E_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{k}\mathrlap{{}=E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{k}\hbox to0.0pt{ ,\hss}}

where each
E_{i-1}\hookrightarrow E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow F_{i}
is short exact,
and each
F_{i}
is non-zero and
\tau
-semistable, with

\tau(F_{1})>\tau(F_{2})>\cdots>\tau(F_{k})\ .

We say that
\tau
is a
stability condition
,
if it is a weak stability condition,
and moreover, the following conditions are satisfied:

(i
′
)

For any
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in C(\mathcal{A})

such that
\beta=\alpha+\gamma
, either

\tau(\alpha)<\tau(\beta)<\tau(\gamma)\ ,\quad\text{or}\quad\tau(\alpha)=\tau(\beta)=\tau(\gamma)\ ,\quad\text{or}\quad\tau(\alpha)>\tau(\beta)>\tau(\gamma)\ .

(iv)

For each
t\in T
, let
\mathcal{A}(t)\subset\mathcal{A}

be the full subcategory consisting of

\tau
-semistable objects
E\in\mathcal{A}

with
E\simeq 0
or
\tau(E)=t
.
Then
\mathcal{A}(t)
is an abelian category,
and the inclusion
\mathcal{A}(t)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}
is exact.
Moreover,
\mathcal{A}(t)
is
artinian
,
in that any decreasing sequence of subobjects

E=E_{0}\supset E_{1}\supset\cdots

in
\mathcal{A}(t)
must stabilize.

2.4.3. Remark.

2.4.1
can be simplified in the following two cases:

•

If
\mathcal{A}
is an abelian category,
then the condition
Item

(ii)
is automatic,
since one can take images.
If, moreover,
\mathcal{A}
is
\tau
-artinian

in the sense of Joyce
[
28
, §4]
,
then the conditions
Items

(iii)
to
(iv)

are also satisfied, as discussed there.

•

If
\mathcal{A}
is a
self-dual exact category

in the sense of
Section

3.1
,
then the artinian assumption in condition
Item

(iv)

is automatic, as discussed in
Remark

2.1.7
.

Theorem 2.4.4
.

In
2.4.1
,
if
\tau
is a weak stability condition,
then the Harder–Narasimhan filtration of an object
E\in\mathcal{A}

is unique up to a unique isomorphism.

Proof 2
.

This follows from a standard argument.
See, for example, Rudakov
[
48
, Theorem 2]

and Joyce
[
28
, Theorem 4.4]
.

\Box

Theorem 2.4.5
.

In
2.4.1
, if
\tau
is a stability condition,
then every
\tau
-semistable object
E\in\mathcal{A}

has a filtration

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathllap{0={}}E_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{\ell}\mathrlap{{}=E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\ell}\hbox to0.0pt{ $,$\hss}}

called a
\tau
-Jordan–Hölder filtration
,
where each
E_{i-1}\hookrightarrow E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow F_{i}
is short exact,
and each
F_{i}
is
\tau
-stable, with

\tau(F_{1})=\cdots=\tau(F_{\ell})
.
Moreover, the objects
F_{i}
are uniquely determined by
E

up to a permutation.

Proof 3
.

This is a standard result in abelian categories,
and follows from the ascending and descending chain conditions.
See, for example, Rudakov
[
48
, Theorem 3]

and Joyce
[
28
, Theorem 4.5]
.

\Box

2.5   Moduli of objects

2.5.1.

We define a notion of

moduli stacks of objects
in an exact category.
We take an axiomatic approach,
meaning that we do not attempt to construct such moduli stacks
from the category, but rather assume that they exist,
and we formulate the properties that we need from them.

We will use various notions of stacks in categories,
as in
Appendix

B
.

Condition 0
(Mod).

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category, as in
.

We assume that there is a
\mathbb{K}
-stack in categories
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
,
called a
categorical moduli stack of objects
in
\mathcal{A}
,
satisfying the following properties.

(i)

\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
is an exact
\mathbb{K}
-linear algebraic stack,
in the sense of
B.2.6
.

Write
\mathcal{M}=\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\simeq}
, and call it
the
moduli stack of objects
in
\mathcal{A}
,
which is an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack locally of finite type.
Write
\mathcal{M}^{I}=(\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{I})^{\simeq}
and

\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}}=(\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}})^{\simeq}
,
which are also algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stacks locally of finite type.
We further assume the following.

(ii)

There is an equivalence of
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact categories

\mathcal{A}\simeq\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbb{K})\ .

(2.9)

In particular, every object
E\in\mathcal{A}

defines a
\mathbb{K}
-point
E\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{K})
.

(iii)

There is a decomposition

\mathcal{M}=\coprod_{\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})}\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\ ,

(2.10)

where each
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\subset\mathcal{M}

is an open and closed substack,
such that
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{K})\subset\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{K})

consists precisely of those
\mathbb{K}
-points
E

with
\llbr E\rrbr=\alpha
.

2.5.3. Geometric functors.

Suppose we have a
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact functor

F\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
between
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact categories,
and suppose that
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
and
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{N}}
are categorical moduli stacks
of objects in
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, respectively, as in
.
We say that
F
is
geometric
,
if it extends to a morphism of exact linear algebraic stacks

F\colon\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}\longrightarrow\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{N}}

that is compatible with the identifications

\mathcal{A}\simeq\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbb{K})
and

\mathcal{B}\simeq\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{N}}(\mathbb{K})
.

Similarly, we can define whether a natural isomorphism
between two geometric functors is geometric.

For example, the symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathcal{A}
given by
\oplus

is geometric, since there is a canonical map

\oplus\colon\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}\times\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}\longrightarrow\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}\ ,

(2.11)

which, together with the unit
0\colon{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{K}}\to\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}

and various compatible
2
-morphisms,
exhibits
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
as a commutative monoid
in the
2
-category of
\mathbb{K}
-stacks in categories.
In particular, this also exhibits
\mathcal{M}

as a commutative monoid in the
2
-category of algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stacks.

2.5.4. Semistable loci.

We now formulate assumptions on stability conditions on
\mathcal{A}
,
such that the semistable locus in the moduli stack
\mathcal{M}

is well-behaved.

Condition 0
(Stab).

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category,
with a categorical moduli stack
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
, as in
.
Let
\tau
be a weak stability condition on
\mathcal{A}
.
We assume that the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

For each
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
,
there is an open substack

\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha}(\tau)\subset\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\ ,

such that for any
E\in\mathcal{A}
with
\llbr E\rrbr=\alpha
,
we have
E\in\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha}(\tau)(\mathbb{K})

if and only if
E
is
\tau
-semistable.

(ii)

For any
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
,
the substack
\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha}(\tau)\subset\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}

is of finite type.

(iii)

For any
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
U
of finite type
and any morphism
e\colon U\to\mathcal{M}
,
there exists a finite subset
S_{e}\subset C(\mathcal{A})
,
such that for any
x\in U(\mathbb{K})
,
the
\tau
-Harder–Narasimhan filtration of the object

E_{x}\in\mathcal{A}
corresponding to
e(x)

has stepwise quotients whose classes all belong to
S_{e}
.

The condition
2.5.5
is very weak:
it is true in all reasonable situations,
and is only used to avoid pathological cases.

2.6   Moduli of filtrations

2.6.1. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category,
with a categorical moduli stack
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
, as in
.

For an integer
n\geqslant 0
,
we define a
\mathbb{K}
-stack in categories
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(n)}}
by

\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(n)}}(U)=\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(U)^{\smash{(n)}}

(2.12)

for any
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
U
,
where the right-hand side denotes the category of
n
-step filtrations
in the exact category
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(U)
,
as in
Definition

2.3.1
.

The stack
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(n)}}

can be seen as a categorical moduli stack of objects in
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}
.
It is an exact
\mathbb{K}
-linear algebraic stack,
and satisfies
for
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}
,
as we will see below.

Define

\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}=(\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(n)}})^{\simeq}

to be the underlying algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack of
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(n)}}
.

2.6.2. Projection morphisms.

Recall from
2.3.3
the projections

p^{f}\colon\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}\to\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(m)}}

for an order-preserving map
f\colon[m]\to[n]
,
and
p_{i},p^{\smash{(n)}}\colon\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}\to\mathcal{A}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
.
These extend naturally to
morphisms of
\mathbb{K}
-stacks in categories

\displaystyle\pi^{f}\colon

\displaystyle\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(n)}}\longrightarrow\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(m)}}\ ,

(2.13)

\displaystyle\pi_{i},\pi^{\smash{(n)}}\colon

\displaystyle\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(n)}}\longrightarrow\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}\ ,

(2.14)

which, in turn, induce morphisms of
\mathbb{K}
-stacks

\displaystyle\pi^{f}\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(m)}}\ ,

(2.15)

\displaystyle\pi_{i},\pi^{\smash{(n)}}\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}\ .

(2.16)

2.6.3.
for filtrations.

Let us verify that
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(n)}}
satisfies

for
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}
.

We have natural identifications

\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(0)}}\simeq\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{K}
,
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(1)}}\simeq\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
,
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(2)}}\simeq\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}}
,
with the notation
(-)^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}}
as in
B.2.2
,
and

\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(n)}}\simeq\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(2)}}\underset{\pi^{\smash{(2)}},\,\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}},\,\pi_{1}\mspace{18mu}}{\times}\cdots\underset{\pi^{\smash{(2)}},\,\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}},\,\pi_{1}\mspace{18mu}}{\times}\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(2)}}

(2.17)

for
n\geqslant 2
,
where
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(2)}}
appears
(n-1)
times.
In particular, this shows that each

\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(n)}}
is an exact
\mathbb{K}
-linear algebraic stack.
Moreover, the projection morphisms
\pi^{f}
,
\pi_{i}
, and
\pi^{\smash{(n)}}

in
Eqs.

2.13
to
2.14

are morphisms of exact
\mathbb{K}
-linear algebraic stacks.

For
(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})^{n}\simeq C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}})
,
define an open and closed substack

\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}_{\alpha_{1},\,\dotsc,\,\alpha_{n}}\subset\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}

to be the preimage of

\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{n}}

under the morphism

\pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\pi_{n}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}\to\mathcal{M}^{n}
.

This verifies that
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(n)}}
satisfies
,
so that it qualifies as a categorical moduli stack for
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}
.

2.6.4. Semistable loci.

In the situation of
,
suppose that
\tau
is a weak stability condition on
\mathcal{A}
,
satisfying
2.5.5
.
Then, for
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
, we define an open substack

\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}}_{\alpha_{1},\,\dotsc,\,\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\subset\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}_{\alpha_{1},\,\dotsc,\,\alpha_{n}}

to be the preimage of

\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\times\cdots\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)

under the morphism

\pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\pi_{n}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}\to\mathcal{M}^{n}
.

2.6.5. Finiteness conditions.

We also formulate certain finiteness conditions on
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}
,
which will be needed later.
Compare also Joyce
[
26
, Assumption 8.1]
.

Condition 0
(Fin).

Suppose that we are in the situation of
.
We further assume the following:

(i)

For any
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
, the morphism

\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha_{1},\,\alpha_{2}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{1}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{2}}

is of finite type.

(ii)

For any
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
, the morphism

\pi^{\smash{(2)}}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha_{1},\,\alpha_{2}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}

is of finite type.

Remark 2.6.7
.

The condition
2.6.6

is equivalent to the morphism

\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}\to\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}

being of finite type.
However, the condition
2.6.6

does not imply that the morphism

\pi^{\smash{(2)}}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}\to\mathcal{M}

is of finite type, since for a given
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
,
there can be infinitely many pairs
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})

such that
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}=\alpha
.

Lemma 2.6.8
.

Suppose we are in the situation of
.

(i)

For any
n\geqslant 0,
the morphism

\pi^{\smash{(n)}}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}

is representable.

(ii)

Assume that
2.6.6
holds.
Then, for any
n\geqslant 0,
the morphism

\pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\pi_{n}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{n}

is of finite type.

(iii)

Assume that
2.6.6
holds.
Then, for any
n\geqslant 0,
the morphism

\pi^{\smash{(n)}}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}

is of finite type.

Proof 4
.

For
Item

(i)
, by a criterion in
Laumon–Moret-Bailly
[
39
, Corollary 8.1.1]
,
it is enough to verify that for any
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
U
,
the functor

p^{\smash{(n)}}\colon\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(U)^{\smash{(n)}}\longrightarrow\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(U)

induces injections of automorphism groups.
This is true since inclusions in an exact category
are monomorphisms.

For
Item

(ii)
, we use induction on
n
.
If
n=0,1
, the result is trivial.
If
n>1
, consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n-1)}}\times\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}\hbox to0.0pt{ ,\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{n}\times\pi_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\pi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\smash{(n-1)}}\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\pi_{n-1})\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2}}

(2.18)

where
\varphi_{n}
is the morphism
\pi^{f}
with

f=(0,\dotsc,n-1)\colon[n-1]\to[n]
.
By
2.6.6
,
the bottom arrow is of finite type,
and so is the map
\varphi_{n}\times\pi_{n}
.
But
(\pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\pi_{n-1})\times\mathrm{id}

is of finite type by induction hypothesis.
Since
\pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\pi_{n}
is the composition of these two arrows,
it is of finite type.

For
Item

(iii)
, a similar argument works,
using the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n-1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\hbox to0.0pt{ ,\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\smash{(n)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\smash{(n-1)}}\vphantom{{}^{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\smash{(2)}}}

(2.19)

where
\psi_{n}
is the morphism
\pi^{f}
with

f=(0,\dotsc,n-2,n)\colon[n-1]\to[n]
.

\Box

2.6.9.

In particular, if
2.6.6
holds,
and
\tau
is a weak stability condition on
\mathcal{A}

satisfying
2.5.5
,
then the semistable loci

\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}}_{\alpha_{1},\,\dotsc,\,\alpha_{n}}(\tau)

defined in
2.6.4

are of finite type.

2.7   The extension bundle

2.7.1.

We formulate another set of conditions on the exact category
\mathcal{A}

and its moduli stack
\mathcal{M}
,
which will be needed to prove wall-crossing formulae
for motivic enumerative invariants
in
Sections

5.5
to
5.6
below.
Namely, we assume the existence of
extension bundles
.
These conditions are quite restrictive,
and require
\mathcal{A}
to have homological dimension at most
1
,
although we can weaken this assumption in some cases,
as discussed in
Remark

2.7.7
below.

2.7.2. The Baer sum.

In the situation of
,
for
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
, consider the morphism

\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha_{1},\,\alpha_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{1}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{2}}\ .

(2.20)

The left-hand side is thought of as the stack of
short exact sequences
E_{1}\hookrightarrow E\twoheadrightarrow E_{2}

with
E_{1},E_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
of classes
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
.
Taking the Baer sum of two such sequences,
that is, taking the sum in
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(E_{2},E_{1})
,
as in Weibel
[
61
, Definition 3.4.4]
,
gives a morphism

+\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha_{1},\,\alpha_{2}}\underset{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{1}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{2}}}{\times}\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha_{1},\,\alpha_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha_{1},\,\alpha_{2}}\ ,

(2.21)

which is well-defined
since the limits and colimits involved in taking the Baer sum
are preserved by exact functors, as in
B.2.5
.
This gives
Eq.

2.20
the structure of an
abelian stack
,
in the sense of
C.1.2
.

2.7.3.

We now formulate the notion of an
extension bundle
,
using the language of
2
-vector bundles
and affine
2
-vector bundles,
in the sense of
Appendix

C
.

Condition 0
(Ext).

Suppose that we are in the situation of
.
We assume that for each
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
,
we have a
2
-vector bundle

\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\smash{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{1}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{2}}

(2.22)

of constant rank, called the
extension bundle
.
We assume that the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

\mathcal{A}
has homological dimension at most
1
,
in that for any
E_{1},E_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
,
we have
\mathrm{Ext}^{i}(E_{2},E_{1})=0
for all
i>1
.

(ii)

We have an isomorphism

\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\smash{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}}\simeq\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}

of abelian stacks over
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{1}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{2}}
.
The fibre of
\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{\smash{1/0}}_{\smash{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}}

over
(E_{1},E_{2})\in(\smash{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{1}}}\times\smash{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{2}}})(\mathbb{K})

is isomorphic to the
2
-vector space

\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E_{2},E_{1})=\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(E_{2},E_{1})\times[*/\mathrm{Ext}^{0}(E_{2},E_{1})]\ ,

and each short exact sequence

E_{1}\hookrightarrow E\twoheadrightarrow E_{2}
as a
\mathbb{K}
-point of

\smash{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}}

is identified with its class in
\mathrm{Ext}^{\smash{1/0}}(E_{2},E_{1})
.

Note that by
Remark

C.1.6
,
the
2
-vector bundle structure on
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}

is not extra data, but rather a property.

2.7.5. Functoriality.

Assume that we are in the situation of
.
Taking pushforwards and pullbacks
as in
B.2.5
,
we obtain the universal pushforward and pullback morphisms

\displaystyle(s\times\mathrm{id})^{*}\,\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\smash{\alpha_{1},\beta}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(t\times\mathrm{id})^{*}\,\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\smash{\alpha_{2},\beta}}\ ,

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}\times t)^{*}\,\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\smash{\alpha,\beta_{2}}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\mathrm{id}\times s)^{*}\,\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\smash{\alpha,\beta_{1}}}

of
2
-vector bundles on

\mathcal{M}^{I}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\smash{\beta}}
and

\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\times\mathcal{M}^{I}_{\smash{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}}
, respectively,
where
\smash{\mathcal{M}^{I}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}}
is the stack of morphisms

E_{1}\to E_{2}
with
E_{1},E_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
of classes

\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
,
and
s,t
are the source and target morphisms.
In plain words, this means that
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(-,-)

is contravariant in the first component,
and covariant in the second component.

These morphisms, in turn,
form short exact sequences of
2
-vector bundles

\displaystyle 0\to(\pi_{1}\times\mathrm{id})^{*}\,\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\smash{\alpha_{1},\beta}}\longrightarrow(\pi^{\smash{(2)}}\times\mathrm{id})^{*}\,\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\smash{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\beta}}\longrightarrow(\pi_{2}\times\mathrm{id})^{*}\,\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\smash{\alpha_{2},\beta}}\to 0\hbox to0.0pt{ ,\hss}

(2.23)

\displaystyle 0\to(\mathrm{id}\times\pi_{2})^{*}\,\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\smash{\alpha,\beta_{2}}}\longrightarrow(\mathrm{id}\times\pi^{\smash{(2)}})^{*}\,\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\smash{\alpha,\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}}}\longrightarrow(\mathrm{id}\times\pi_{1})^{*}\,\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\smash{\alpha,\beta_{1}}}\to 0

(2.24)

over

\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\beta}
and

\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\smash{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}}
, respectively.
In plain words, this means that there are six-term
long exact sequences of
\mathrm{Ext}
groups in both components.

2.7.6. The Euler form.

In the situation of
,
for
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
, define an integer

\chi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=-{\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\smash{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}}}\ .

(2.25)

By
Eqs.

2.23
to
2.24
,
this gives an
\mathbb{N}
-bilinear form

\chi\colon C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})\times C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\ ,

(2.26)

called the
Euler form
.

Remark 2.7.7
.

The condition
can be weakened
by only requiring
\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\smash{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}}

to be a
2
-vector bundle over the semistable locus

\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha_{2}}(\tau)
,
and only when
\tau(\alpha_{1})\leqslant\tau(\alpha_{2})
,
for a weak stability condition
\tau
on
\mathcal{A}
,
as we will do in
Section

7.3
below.
This will allow us to slightly relax the
homological dimension
1
condition,
and most results that require
will still hold in this case.

Lemma 2.7.8
.

In the situation of
,
consider the canonical morphism

q\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(3)}}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\underset{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{2}}}{\times}\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}\ .

(2.27)

Then
q
is an affine
2
-vector bundle,
as in
Definition

C.1.7
,
which is a torsor for
the
2
-vector bundle

(\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2})^{*}\,\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{\smash{1/0}}_{\alpha_{1},\mspace{2mu}\alpha_{3}}\to\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\times_{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{2}}}\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}
.

Proof 5
.

We need to show that
for objects
E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}\in\mathcal{A}
of classes
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}
,
the stack
\mathcal{F}_{\smash{a,b}}

of
3
-step filtrations with stepwise quotients
E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}
,
such that the induced elements

a\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E_{2},E_{1})(\mathbb{K})
and

b\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E_{3},E_{2})(\mathbb{K})
are fixed,
is a torsor for
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E_{3},E_{1})
.

Indeed, consider the short exact sequence

0\to\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E_{3},E_{1})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E_{3},E_{a})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E_{3},E_{2})\to 0

(2.28)

of
2
-vector spaces,
where
E_{a}
is the extension of
E_{2}
by
E_{1}
given by
a
.
Then
\mathcal{F}_{\smash{a,b}}
is the fibre
of the second non-trivial morphism at
b
,
so the action of
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E_{3},E_{1})
on
\mathcal{F}_{\smash{a,b}}

by translation is a torsor.

Repeating this argument in families
proves the lemma.

\Box

Section 3Self-dual categories and moduli stacks

We introduce the notion of
self-dual categories
,
which are categories that are equivalent to their own opposite categories.
We will be particularly interested in
self-dual exact or quasi-abelian categories,
as these will be the main setting for studying
enumerative geometry for type B/C/D structure groups.

Under this setting,
we will be interested in enumerative invariants
counting
self-dual objects
in self-dual categories.
These are objects that are isomorphic to their own duals,
and can be seen as generalized orthogonal and symplectic bundles,
since they are the self-dual objects in the category of vector bundles,
with the usual notion of the dual bundle.

3.1   Self-dual categories

Definition 3.1.1
.

A
self-dual category
is a triple
(\mathcal{A},D,\eta)
, where

•

\mathcal{A}
is a category.

•

D\colon\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}

is an equivalence,
called the
dual functor
.
We often denote this functor by
(-)^{\vee}
.

•

\eta\colon D^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\circ D\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.1097pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\Rightarrow}}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{A}}

is a natural isomorphism,
such that for any
E\in\mathcal{A}
, we have

\eta_{E^{\vee}}=(\eta_{E}^{\vee})^{-1}\colon E^{\vee\vee\vee}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\longrightarrow}}E^{\vee}\ .

(3.1)

By abuse of language, we sometimes say that

\mathcal{A}
is a self-dual category
when the extra data
D,\eta
are clear from the context.

If, in addition,
\mathcal{A}
is equipped with
certain types of additional structure,
such as the structure of a
\mathbb{K}
-linear category,
an exact category,
or a quasi-abelian category, etc.,
and if such structure is respected by
D
and
\eta
,
meaning that
D
and
\eta
are
1
- and
2
-morphisms
in the
2
-category of such categories with additional structure,
then we say that
(\mathcal{A},D,\eta)

is, for example, a
self-dual
\mathbb{K}
-linear category
, etc.

Definition 3.1.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},D,\eta)
be a self-dual category.
A
self-dual object
in
\mathcal{A}

is a pair
(E,\phi)
, where

•

E\in\mathcal{A}
is an object.

•

\phi\colon E\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}E^{\vee}
is an isomorphism in
\mathcal{A}
,
such that
\phi^{\vee}=\phi\circ\eta_{E}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\vee}\hbox to0.0pt{ .\hss}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\vee\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textstyle\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textstyle\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textstyle\sim}

(3.2)

Let
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
denote the groupoid
whose objects are self-dual objects in
\mathcal{A}
,
and morphisms are isomorphisms of self-dual objects.

3.1.3. Module structure.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a self-dual additive category.
Then we have a natural action of
\mathcal{A}
on
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
,
given by the functor

\oplus^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\colon\mathcal{A}^{\simeq}\times\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\ ,

(3.3)

where
\mathcal{A}^{\simeq}
is the underlying groupoid of
\mathcal{A}
,
and
\oplus^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
sends
F\in\mathcal{A}
and
(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}

to the self-dual object
(F\oplus E\oplus F^{\vee},\tilde{\phi})
,
with
\tilde{\phi}\colon F\oplus E\oplus F^{\vee}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}F^{\vee}\oplus E^{\vee}\oplus F^{\vee\vee}
given by

\tilde{\phi}=\left(\,\begin{matrix}&&\mathrm{id}_{F}\\
&\phi\\
\eta_{F}^{-1}\end{matrix}\,\right)\ .

This establishes
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
as a module
for the monoidal groupoid
(\mathcal{A}^{\simeq},\oplus)
.

In fact, this construction will still work if we replace
the additive structure on
\mathcal{A}
by an arbitrary monoidal structure
compatible with the self-dual structure,
but this will not be needed for our purposes.

3.2   Examples

3.2.1. Example. Vector spaces.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be the
\mathbb{K}
-linear abelian category of

\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces of finite dimension,
and let
D=(-)^{\vee}\colon\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}

be the operation of taking the dual vector space.
Choose a sign
\varepsilon=\pm 1
,
and define a natural isomorphism

\eta\colon(-)^{\vee\vee}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.1097pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\Rightarrow}}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{A}}
by

\eta_{V}=\varepsilon\cdot\mathrm{ev}_{V}^{-1}\colon V^{\vee\vee}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\longrightarrow}}V

for
V\in\mathcal{A}
,
where
\mathrm{ev}_{V}\colon V\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}V^{\vee\vee}

is the evaluation map.
Then
(\mathcal{A},D,\eta)
is a self-dual
\mathbb{K}
-linear abelian category.

By definition, a self-dual object in
\mathcal{A}

is a pair
(V,\phi)
, where
V
is a vector space, and

\phi\colon V\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}V^{\vee}

is an isomorphism, such that
\phi^{\vee}=\phi\circ\eta_{V}
.
Equivalently, when
\varepsilon=1
(resp.
-1
),

\phi
is a non-degenerate
symmetric (resp. antisymmetric) bilinear form on
V
.
Therefore,
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
can be identified with the groupoid of
finite-dimensional orthogonal (resp. symplectic) vector spaces,
where the morphisms are linear isomorphisms that preserve the bilinear forms.

3.2.2. Example. Self-dual quivers.

Let
Q
be a
self-dual quiver
,
that is, a quiver with an involution
\sigma\colon Q\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}Q^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}
,
where
Q^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}
is the opposite quiver of
Q
.
For the precise definition,
see
Section

6
below.

Let
\mathcal{A}=\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}Q)
be the
\mathbb{K}
-linear abelian category of
finite-dimensional representations of
Q
over
\mathbb{K}
.
One can define a self-dual structure
(-)^{\vee}\colon\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}

by sending each representation of
Q

to the representation with the dual vector spaces and dual linear maps.
This also involves choosing signs when defining

\eta\colon(-)^{\vee\vee}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.1097pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\Rightarrow}}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{A}}
, as in the previous example.

Self-dual objects in
\mathcal{A}
are called

self-dual representations
of
Q
,
and our enumerative invariants will be counting
these self-dual objects.

Enumerative invariant theories of quiver representations
can sometimes be seen as a model for
such theories for coherent sheaves on varieties,
which can be thought of as generalized
\mathrm{GL}(n)
-bundles.
We will argue in
Section

6.6
that
self-dual quivers can also be thought of
as a model for enumerative invariant theories of
G
-bundles,
for
G=\mathrm{O}(n)
or
\mathrm{Sp}(n)
.

3.2.3. Example. Vector bundles.

Let
X
be a
\mathbb{K}
-variety,
and let
\mathcal{A}=\smash[b]{\textsf{{Vect}}}(X)

be the
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category
of vector bundles on
X
of finite rank,
or equivalently, locally free sheaves on
X
of finite rank.

Define a self-dual structure
(-)^{\vee}\colon\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}

by sending a vector bundle to its dual vector bundle.
Choose a sign
\varepsilon=\pm 1
,
and define a natural isomorphism
\eta\colon(-)^{\vee\vee}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.1097pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\Rightarrow}}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{A}}

to be
\varepsilon
times the usual identification.

A self-dual object in
\mathcal{A}
is a pair
(E,\phi)
,
where
E
is a vector bundle on
X
,
and
\phi\colon E\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}E^{\vee}
is an isomorphism,
satisfying
\phi^{\vee}=\phi\circ\eta_{E}
.
Equivalently,
\phi
is a symmetric (resp. antisymmetric) bilinear form on
E
,
when
\varepsilon=+1
(resp.
-1
).
This is the same as an orthogonal (resp. symplectic) vector bundle on
X
,
or a principal bundle on
X
for the group
\mathrm{O}(n)
(resp.
\mathrm{Sp}(n)
),
where
n
is the rank of the vector bundle.

See
Section

7.1
below for further discussion
of this example in the case when
X
is a curve.

3.2.4. Non-example. Coherent sheaves.

Let
X
be a smooth, projective
\mathbb{K}
-variety,
and let
\mathcal{A}=\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(X)
be the abelian category of coherent sheaves on
X
.
Then, when
\dim X>0
,

\mathcal{A}
is not a self-dual category,
since
\mathcal{A}
is noetherian in the sense of
2.1.5
,
but not artinian, as there is an infinite decreasing chain

\mathcal{O}_{X}\supset\mathcal{O}_{X}(-1)\supset\mathcal{O}_{X}(-2)\supset\cdots
in
\mathcal{A}

that does not stabilize.

However, the derived category of coherent sheaves on
X

does have many self-dual structures, as in the next example.

3.2.5. Example. Derived category of coherent sheaves.

Let
X
be a smooth, projective
\mathbb{K}
-variety,
and let
\mathcal{C}=\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(X)
be the derived category
of coherent sheaves on
X
.

Define
(-)^{\vee}\colon\mathcal{C}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\mathcal{C}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}

to be the derived dual functor

(-)^{\vee}=\mathbb{R}\mathcal{H}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{om}(-,L)\ ,

where
L\to X
is a line bundle, possibly with a shift.
For example, taking
L\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X}
gives the ordinary dual functor,
and taking
L\simeq\omega_{X}[n]
,
where
\omega_{X}
is the canonical bundle of
X
, and
n=\dim X
,
gives the Serre/Verdier duality functor.
As before, choose a sign
\varepsilon=\pm 1

to identify the double dual of a sheaf to itself.

Using Bayer’s
[
3
]
notion of

polynomial Bridgeland stability conditions
,
it is possible to construct quasi-abelian subcategories

\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{C}
that are self-dual under the duality functor above.
Self-dual objects in
\mathcal{A}

can then be seen as generalized orthogonal or symplectic bundles.
We will discuss this example further in
Section

7.2
below.

3.3   Self-dual filtrations

3.3.1. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a self-dual exact category,
and let
n\geqslant 0
be an integer.
Then the category of filtrations
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}
,
as in
Definition

2.3.1
,
can be equipped with an induced self-dual structure.
Namely, for a filtration

E_{\bullet}=(0=E_{0}\hookrightarrow E_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow E_{n}=E)
,
define its dual filtration
E_{\bullet}^{\vee}
by

0=(E/E_{n})^{\vee}\hookrightarrow(E/E_{n-1})^{\vee}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow(E/E_{0})^{\vee}=E^{\vee}\ .

(3.4)

A
self-dual filtration
of length
n
is
a self-dual object
(E_{\bullet},\phi_{\bullet})\in\mathcal{A}^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}
.
We also say that
E_{\bullet}
is a self-dual filtration of
the self-dual object
(E_{n},\phi_{n})\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
.

In this case, if we write
F_{i}=E_{i}/E_{i-1}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
,
then there are induced isomorphisms
F_{i}\simeq F_{n+1-i}^{\vee}
,
defined via the isomorphism

(E/E_{n-i})^{\vee}/(E/E_{n+1-i})^{\vee}\simeq(E_{n+1-i}/E_{n-i})^{\vee}

given by the third isomorphism theorem,
which holds in an exact category by
2.1.3
.
In particular, if
n=2k+1
is odd,
then
F_{k+1}
has an induced self-dual structure,
and becomes a self-dual object in
\mathcal{A}
.

3.3.2. Projection functors.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a self-dual exact category.
Recall from
2.3.3
the projection functors

\smash{p^{f}}\colon\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}\to\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(m)}}
for order-preserving maps

f\colon[m]\to[n]
.

Now, equip each set
[n]
with the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action given by

(-)^{\vee}\colon[n]\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}[n]
,
i\mapsto n-i
.
Then, if an order-preserving map
f\colon[m]\to[n]

is also
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant,
then
\smash{p^{f}}
is compatible with the self-dual structures
on
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(m)}}
, inducing a functor

p^{f,\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\colon\mathcal{A}^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{{\smash{(m)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\ .

(3.5)

In particular, taking
f
to be the map

(0,n)\colon[1]\to[n]
or the map
(i-1,i)\colon[1]\to[2i-1]
,
similarly as in
2.3.3
,
we obtain the projection functors

\displaystyle p^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}

\displaystyle\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\ ,

(3.6)

\displaystyle p_{i}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}

\displaystyle\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{{\smash{(2i-1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\ .

(3.7)

3.3.3. Self-dual extensions.

We are particularly interested in
self-dual filtrations of length
n=3
, of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathllap{0={}}E_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{3}\mathrlap{{}=E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{3}\hbox to0.0pt{ ,\hss}}

(3.8)

where the vertical arrows are cokernels of the horizontal arrows,
so that
F_{i}\simeq E_{i}/E_{i-1}
for
i=1,2,3
.
As in
3.3.1
,

E
has an induced self-dual structure
(E,\phi)
,

F_{2}
has an induced self-dual structure
(F_{2},\psi)
,
and we have a natural isomorphism
F_{3}\simeq F_{1}^{\vee}
.

In this case, we say that the self-dual object
(E,\phi)

is a
self-dual extension

of the self-dual object
(F_{2},\psi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}

by the object
F_{1}\in\mathcal{A}
.

3.3.4. Isotropic subobjects.

Let
(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
, and let
F\in\mathcal{A}
.
An inclusion
i\colon F\hookrightarrow E
is said to be
isotropic
,
if the composition

F\overset{i}{\longrightarrow}E\overset{\phi}{\longrightarrow}E^{\vee}\overset{i^{\vee}}{\longrightarrow}F^{\vee}

is zero.
We also say that
F
is an
isotropic subobject
of
(E,\phi)
.
In this case, writing

F^{\perp}=\ker\Bigl(\ E\overset{\phi}{\longrightarrow}E^{\vee}\overset{i^{\vee}}{\longrightarrow}F^{\vee}\ \Bigr)\ ,

(3.9)

we have a filtration of length
3
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\perp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\vee}\hbox to0.0pt{ ,\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

(3.10)

where
j
is induced by
i
.
A standard argument shows that

A
has an induced self-dual structure
(A,\psi)
,
such that the above diagram is a self-dual extension
of
(A,\psi)
by
F
.

Moreover, all self-dual extensions arise in this way.
In other words, given any self-dual extension
Eq.

3.8
,
the inclusion
E_{1}\hookrightarrow E
is isotropic,
E_{2}\simeq E_{1}^{\perp}
,
and the induced self-dual filtration
Eq.

3.10

coincides with the original one.

3.3.5. The Grothendieck monoid module.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a self-dual exact category,
and recall from
2.1.4
the
Grothendieck monoid
K_{+}(\mathcal{A})
of
\mathcal{A}
,
which now has a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action

(-)^{\vee}\colon K_{+}(\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}K_{+}(\mathcal{A})

given by the self-dual structure of
\mathcal{A}
.

Define a
K_{+}(\mathcal{A})
-module
K_{+}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})

to be the free
K_{+}(\mathcal{A})
-module generated by
isomorphism classes of objects
(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
,
modulo the relations

[(E,\phi)]\sim[F_{1}]+[(F_{2},\psi)]

for self-dual extensions
Eq.

3.8
,
where ‘
+
’ denotes the module action.

To avoid confusion, we will denote the action of

K_{+}(\mathcal{A})
on
K_{+}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
by

(\alpha,\theta)\longmapsto\bar{\alpha}+\theta\ ,

instead of writing
\alpha+\theta
,
where
\alpha\in K_{+}(\mathcal{A})
and
\theta\in K_{+}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
.
This notation hints that the class
\bar{\alpha}+\theta

should be thought of as a composition of
the classes
\alpha
,
\alpha^{\vee}
, and
\theta
.

For an object
E_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}
of class
\alpha
,
and a self-dual object
(E_{\theta},\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
of class
\theta
,
the class
\bar{\alpha}+\theta
is represented by the self-dual object

E_{\alpha}\oplus^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(E_{\theta},\phi)
defined in
3.1.3
.
In particular, we have

\bar{\alpha}+\theta=\overline{\alpha^{\vee}}+\theta

(3.11)

for any
\alpha\in K_{+}(\mathcal{A})
and
\theta\in K_{+}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
.

3.3.6.

We now formulate a set-up for
self-dual exact categories, similar to
,
which will be used in the rest of this paper.

Condition 0
(SdCat).

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a self-dual
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category,
with properties and extra data as in
.
We also require the following:

(i)

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
K_{+}(\mathcal{A})

via the self-dual structure of
\mathcal{A}

descends to a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action

(-)^{\vee}\colon C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\longrightarrow}}C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})\ .

(3.12)

(ii)

We are given a
C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
-module
C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
,
equipped with a surjection of
K_{+}(\mathcal{A})
-modules

K_{+}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\to C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
.
For
(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
, write
\llbr E,\phi\rrbr\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})

for its class.
We assume that there is a map of sets

j\colon C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})\ ,

(3.13)

such that
j(\llbr E,\phi\rrbr)=\llbr E\rrbr

for all
(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
.

In this case, denote the action of
C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
on
C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
by

(\alpha,\theta)\longmapsto\bar{\alpha}+\theta\ ,

as in
3.3.5
.
For
E_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}
and
(E_{\theta},\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}

with
\llbr E_{\alpha}\rrbr=\alpha
and
\llbr E_{\theta},\phi\rrbr=\theta
,
the class
\bar{\alpha}+\theta
is represented by the self-dual object

E_{\alpha}\oplus^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(E_{\theta},\phi)
defined in
3.1.3
.
In particular,
Eq.

3.11

also holds for the
C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
-action on
C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
.

3.3.8.
for filtrations.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a self-dual exact category, as in
.
Then, the category of filtrations
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}
,
equipped with the induced self-dual structure as in
3.3.1
,
also satisfies
,
with the extra data
C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}})
given by

C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}})=\begin{cases}C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})^{n/2}&\text{if $n$ is even ,}\\
C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})^{(n-1)/2}\times C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})&\text{if $n$ is odd ,}\end{cases}

(3.14)

where a self-dual filtration
(E_{\bullet},\phi_{\bullet})\in\mathcal{A}^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}

is assigned the class

(\llbr E_{1}/E_{0}\rrbr,\ldots,\penalty\llbr E_{n/2}/E_{n/2-1}\rrbr)
if
n
is even,
or
(\llbr E_{1}/E_{0}\rrbr,\ldots,\llbr E_{(n-1)/2}/E_{(n-3)/2}\rrbr,\llbr E_{(n+1)/2}/E_{(n-1)/2},\psi\rrbr)

if
n
is odd, where
\psi
is the induced self-dual structure.

3.3.9.

We can also define a self-dual version of the Ext functors.
They will be related to self-dual extensions
in
3.7.6
below.

Definition 0
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},D,\eta)
be a self-dual exact category.
Let
(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
, and let
F\in\mathcal{A}
.
For every
i\geqslant 0
, define

\mathrm{Ext}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}i}}(E,F)=\mathrm{Ext}^{i}(E,F)\oplus\mathrm{Ext}^{i}(F^{\vee},F)^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}},

(3.15)

where
(-)^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
denotes the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-invariant part,
with the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
\mathrm{Ext}^{i}(F^{\vee},F)
given by

\mathrm{Ext}^{i}(F^{\vee},F)\overset{(-)^{\vee}}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{Ext}^{i}(F^{\vee},F^{\vee\vee})\overset{(\eta_{F})_{*}}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{Ext}^{i}(F^{\vee},F).

(3.16)

3.4   Self-dual stability conditions

3.4.1.

We define a notion of
self-dual stability conditions
on
self-dual exact categories,
which are stability conditions in the sense of
Section

2.4

that are compatible with the self-dual structure.

Definition 0
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a self-dual exact category,
as in
.
Then, a
self-dual (weak) stability condition
on
\mathcal{A}

consists of the following data:

•

A totally ordered set
T
,
equipped with an order-reversing involution
t\mapsto-t
,
fixing a unique element
0\in T
.

•

A (weak) stability condition
\tau\colon C(\mathcal{A})\to T
,
as in
2.4.1
,
such that
\tau(\alpha)=-\tau(\alpha^{\vee})

for all
\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A})
.

In this case, we have the following basic observations:

•

A non-zero object
E\in\mathcal{A}
is semistable with
\tau(E)=t
,
if and only if
E^{\vee}
is semistable with
\tau(E^{\vee})=-t
.

•

If
(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
and
E
is non-zero,
then
\tau(E)=0
.

3.4.3. Semistable and stable self-dual objects.

Let
\tau
be a self-dual weak stability condition on
\mathcal{A}
, as above.
Recall from
3.3.4
the notion of isotropic subobjects.

•

An object
(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
is
\tau
-semistable
,
if for any isotropic subobject
F\subset E

with
0\neq F\neq E
, we have
\tau(F)\leqslant 0\leqslant\tau(E/F)
.

•

An object
(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
is
\tau
-stable
,
if for any isotropic subobject
F\subset E

with
0\neq F\neq E
, we have
\tau(F)<0<\tau(E/F)
.

We will see in
Theorem

3.4.5
that
a self-dual object is semistable if and only if
its underlying object is semistable.
However, this is not true for stable self-dual objects,
which are characterized by
3.4.6
below.

Also, note that the object
(0,0)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
is considered
a stable self-dual object,
although the object
0\in\mathcal{A}
is not stable as an ordinary object.
This might seem strange, but it is justified by

3.4.6
below.

3.4.4. Self-dual Harder–Narasimhan filtrations.

Let
\tau
be a self-dual weak stability condition on
\mathcal{A}
,
and let
(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
.

A
self-dual
\tau
-Harder–Narasimhan filtration
of
(E,\phi)

is a self-dual filtration of
(E,\phi)
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathllap{0={}}E_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{k}^{\perp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{k-1}^{\perp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{1}^{\perp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{0}^{\perp}\mathrlap{{}=E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{k}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}^{\vee}\hbox to0.0pt{ ,\hss}}

(3.17)

where the horizontal maps are inclusions,
the vertical maps are cokernels of the horizontal maps,
each
F_{i}
is non-zero and
\tau
-semistable,

A
is self-dual
\tau
-semistable with the induced self-dual structure,
and

\tau(F_{1})>\cdots>\tau(F_{k})>0\ .

(3.18)

Each inclusion
E_{i}\hookrightarrow E
is then automatically isotropic,
with each
E_{i}^{\perp}
as in
3.3.4
.

Theorem 3.4.5
.

Let
\tau
be a self-dual weak stability condition on
\mathcal{A}
,
and let
(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
.

Then
(E,\phi)
has a unique self-dual

\tau
-Harder–Narasimhan filtration, up to a unique isomorphism.
It coincides with the
\tau
-Harder–Narasimhan filtration of
E

as an ordinary object,
upon deleting the middle term
A
if it is zero.

In particular,
a self-dual object
(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
is
\tau
-semistable,
if and only if the underlying object
E\in\mathcal{A}
is
\tau
-semistable.

Proof 6
.

Let
E_{\bullet}
be the Harder–Narasimhan filtration of
E

an ordinary object.
Then its dual filtration
E^{\vee}_{\bullet}
,
as in
3.3.1
,
is a Harder–Narasimhan filtration of
E^{\vee}
.
Identifying
E
with
E^{\vee}
using
\phi
,
these two filtrations must coincide,
giving
E_{\bullet}
the structure of a self-dual filtration.
Inserting an extra middle term
0
if necessary,
the self-dual filtration
E_{\bullet}
has the form
Eq.

3.17
.
The middle term
A
is semistable as an ordinary object,
and hence, it is also semistable as a self-dual object by definition.
This proves the existence part of the theorem.

For uniqueness, it suffices to show that
every self-dual Harder–Narasimhan filtration
is also an ordinary Harder–Narasimhan filtration,
upon deleting the middle term if it is zero.
It is then enough to show that
any semistable self-dual object
is semistable as an ordinary object.
