This is because otherwise,
its ordinary Harder–Narasimhan filtration
would have the form described above,
inducing a destabilizing isotropic subobject of
A
.
This argument also proves the last statement of the theorem.

\Box

3.4.6. Characterizing stable self-dual objects.

The following result gives a convenient description of
stable self-dual objects.
Note that this requires
\tau
to be a stability condition,
rather than just a weak stability condition as above.

Theorem 0
.

Let
\tau
be a self-dual stability condition on
\mathcal{A}
,
and
(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
a
\tau
-stable self-dual object.
Then there is a decomposition

(E,\phi)\simeq(E_{1},\phi_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus(E_{m},\phi_{m})\ ,

(3.19)

unique up to permuting the factors,
where
(E_{i},\phi_{i})\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}

are pairwise non-isomorphic self-dual objects,
such that each
E_{i}
is a
\tau
-stable object in
\mathcal{A}
.

In particular, we have

\mathrm{Aut}(E,\phi)\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{m}.

(3.20)

Proof 7
.

Consider a
\tau
-Jordan–Hölder filtration of
E
,

0=F_{0}\hookrightarrow F_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow F_{\ell}=E\ ,

as in
Theorem

2.4.5
.
If
\ell\leqslant 1
, then we are already done.
Otherwise, let
G_{1},\dotsc,G_{\ell}
denote the stepwise quotients.
The dual filtration

0=F_{\ell}^{\perp}\hookrightarrow F_{\ell-1}^{\perp}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow F_{0}^{\perp}=E

is also a
\tau
-Jordan–Hölder filtration of
E
,
where
F_{i}^{\perp}\simeq(E/F_{i})^{\vee}
,
and we identified
E\simeq E^{\vee}
via
\phi
.
The stepwise quotients of this filtration are
G_{\ell}^{\vee},\dotsc,G_{1}^{\vee}
.

Since
(E,\phi)
is
\tau
-stable,
the map
F_{1}\hookrightarrow E
cannot factor through
F_{1}^{\perp}
.
Thus, the composition
F_{1}\hookrightarrow E\twoheadrightarrow E/F_{1}^{\perp}\simeq F_{1}^{\vee}

is non-zero, and hence is an isomorphism.
This defines a self-dual structure on
F_{1}
.

Now, the inclusion
F_{1}^{\vee}\simeq F_{1}\hookrightarrow E

splits the short exact sequence

F_{1}^{\perp}\hookrightarrow E\twoheadrightarrow F_{1}^{\vee}
, so that

E\simeq F_{1}\oplus F_{1}^{\perp}
.
We can then identify
E^{\vee}\simeq F_{1}^{\vee}\oplus F_{1}^{\perp\vee}
.
Since
\phi\colon E\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}E^{\vee}
sends
F_{1}
into
F_{1}^{\vee}
,
and
F_{1}^{\perp}
into
F_{1}^{\perp\vee}
, it follows that

\smash{\phi|_{F_{1}}}
and
\smash{\phi|_{F_{1}^{\perp}}}
must be isomorphisms onto

F_{1}^{\vee}
and
F_{1}^{\perp\vee}
, respectively.
They define self-dual structures on
F_{1}
and
F_{1}^{\perp}
, and we have

(E,\phi)\simeq(F_{1},\phi|_{F_{1}})\oplus(F_{1}^{\perp},\phi|_{F_{1}^{\perp}})\ ,

with
F_{1}
stable as an object of
\mathcal{A}
.
Repeating the process with
F_{1}^{\perp}
in place of
E
,
we will eventually obtain a desired decomposition.

To see that the
(E_{i},\phi_{i})
are pairwise non-isomorphic,
suppose the contrary,
so
\psi\colon(E_{i},\phi_{i})\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}(E_{j},\phi_{j})
for some
i\neq j
.
Then the map
\mathrm{id}_{E_{i}}+\sqrt{-1}\cdot\psi\colon E_{i}\to E

gives an isotropic subobject, a contradiction.

The uniqueness follows from the uniqueness part of

Theorem

2.4.5
, as any such decomposition
gives rise to a
\tau
-Jordan–Hölder filtration of
E
.

Finally, for the last statement,
suppose that
\psi\colon E\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}E
is an isomorphism.
Then we must have
\psi(E_{i})=E_{i}
for all
i
.
Since
\mathrm{Aut}(E_{i})\simeq\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}
,
we have
\mathrm{Aut}(E_{i},\phi_{i})\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{2}
,
as a scalar automorphism of
E_{i}
preserves
\phi_{i}

if and only if it squares to the identity.
This completes the proof.

\Box

3.5   Moduli of self-dual objects

3.5.1.

We formulate a notion of the

moduli stack of self-dual objects

in a
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category
\mathcal{A}
,
parallel to the approach of
Section

2.5
.

Condition 0
(SdMod).

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a self-dual
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category,
as in
,
and let
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
be a categorical moduli stack of
\mathcal{A}
,
as in
.
We further assume that:

(i)

There is a self-dual structure
(\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}},D,\eta)
on
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
,
in the sense of
Definition

B.3.1
,
extending the self-dual structure of
\mathcal{A}
.

In this case,
we define the
moduli stack of self-dual objects
in
\mathcal{A}

to be the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-fixed locus

\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}=\mathcal{M}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\ ,

(3.21)

as in
B.3.2
.
It is an algebraic stack
locally of finite type, and has affine stabilizers,
as follows from
Lemma

B.3.4

Item

(i)
.

We also assume that
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
satisfies the following condition:

(ii)

There is a decomposition

\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}=\coprod_{\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\vphantom{{}^{0}}}\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}\ ,

(3.22)

where each
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}\subset\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}

is an open and closed substack,
such that
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\mathbb{K})\subset\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathbb{K})

consists of
\mathbb{K}
-points
(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}

with
\llbr(E,\phi)\rrbr=\theta
.

3.5.3. Module structure.

Under the condition
,
the functor
\oplus^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\colon\mathcal{A}^{\simeq}\times\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\to\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}

in
3.1.3

extends naturally to a map

\oplus^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\colon\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\ ,

(3.23)

exhibiting
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
as a module for the monoid
\mathcal{M}
.

3.5.4. Semistable loci.

Under the condition
,
let
\tau
be a self-dual weak stability condition on
\mathcal{A}
,
satisfying
2.5.5
.
For each
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
, define an open substack

\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{ss}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\subset\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}

as the preimage of
\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{j(\theta)}(\tau)

under the forgetful morphism
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\to\mathcal{M}
,
with
j
as in
Eq.

3.13
.
This can be thought of as the locus of

\tau
-semistable self-dual objects of class
\theta
,
by
Theorem

3.4.5
.

If, moreover,
\tau
satisfies
2.5.5
,
then
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{ss}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
is of finite type,
as follows from
Lemma

B.3.4

Item

(ii)
.

3.6   Moduli of self-dual filtrations

3.6.1. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a self-dual
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category,
with a categorical moduli stack
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
, as in
.
Let
n\geqslant 0
be an integer.

Recall from
2.6.1

the moduli stack
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(n)}}
of
n
-step filtrations in
\mathcal{A}
.
It is now equipped with a self-dual structure,
in the sense of
Definition

B.3.1
,
given by the induced self-dual structures on
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(U)^{\smash{(n)}}
,
as in
3.3.1
, for all
\mathbb{K}
-schemes
U
.
Therefore,
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\smash{(n)}}
satisfies

as a moduli stack for the self-dual category
\mathcal{A}^{\smash{(n)}}
.

In particular, the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-fixed locus

\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}=(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}

is an algebraic stack locally of finite type,
called the
moduli stack of self-dual
n
-step filtrations
in
\mathcal{A}
.

3.6.2. Projection morphisms.

Recall from
3.3.2

the projection functors

p^{f,\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\colon\mathcal{A}^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\penalty\to\mathcal{A}^{{\smash{(m)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}

for an order-preserving
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant map
f\colon[m]\to[n]
,
and the total projection and middle projection functors

p^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\colon\mathcal{A}^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\to\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}

and
p_{i}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\colon\mathcal{A}^{{\smash{(2i-1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\to\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
.
These extend naturally to
morphisms of algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stacks

\displaystyle\pi^{f,\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(m)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\ ,

(3.24)

\displaystyle\pi^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\ ,

(3.25)

\displaystyle\pi_{i}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(2i-1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\ .

(3.26)

On the other hand, recall from
2.6.2

the projection morphisms

\pi^{f}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}\to\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(m)}}
and

\pi^{{\smash{(n)}}},\pi_{i}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}\to\mathcal{M}
,
with notations explained there.
Composing with the forgetful morphism

\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\to\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}
,
we also obtain projection morphisms

\displaystyle\pi^{f}\colon\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(m)}}}\ ,

(3.27)

\displaystyle\pi^{{\smash{(n)}}},\pi_{i}\colon\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}\ .

(3.28)

3.6.3. Semistable loci.

In the situation of
,
suppose that
\tau
is a self-dual weak stability condition on
\mathcal{A}
,
satisfying
2.5.5
.
Then, for
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
,
we define an open substack

\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(2n+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{ss}}}}_{\alpha_{1},\,\dotsc,\,\alpha_{n},\mspace{2mu}\theta}(\tau)\subset\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2n+1)}}_{\alpha_{1},\,\dotsc,\,\alpha_{n},\mspace{2mu}\theta}

to be the preimage of

\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\times\cdots\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{ss}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

under the morphism

\pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\pi_{n}\times\pi_{n+1}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\colon\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(2n+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\to\mathcal{M}^{n}\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
.

Lemma 3.6.4
.

Suppose we are in the situation of
.

(i)

For any
n\geqslant 0,
the morphism

\pi^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\colon\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}

is representable.

(ii)

Assume that
2.6.6
holds.
Then, for any
n\geqslant 0,
the morphism

\pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\pi_{n}\times\pi_{n+1}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\colon\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(2n+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{n}\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}

(3.29)

is of finite type.

(iii)

Assume that
2.6.6
holds.
Then, for any
n\geqslant 0,
the morphism

\pi^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\colon\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}

(3.30)

is of finite type.

Proof 8
.

Part
Item

(i)

follows from an analogous argument as in the proof of

Lemma

2.6.8

Item

(i)
.

For
Item

(ii)
,
consider the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2n+1)}}
as above,
and the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
\mathcal{M}^{2n+1}

sending
(E_{1},\dotsc,E_{2n+1})
to

(E_{2n+1}^{\vee},\dotsc,E_{1}^{\vee})
.
Then the morphism

\pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\pi_{2n+1}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2n+1)}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{2n+1}

(3.31)

is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant,
and the morphism
Eq.

3.29

is the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-fixed locus in
Eq.

3.31
.
But
Eq.

3.31
is of finite type
by
Lemma

2.6.8

Item

(ii)
.
Therefore, by
Lemma

B.3.4

Item

(ii)
,
the morphism
Eq.

3.29

is of finite type.

For
Item

(iii)
,
the morphism
\pi^{\smash{(n)}}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}\to\mathcal{M}

is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant,
and is of finite type by
Lemma

2.6.8

Item

(iii)
,
and the result follows from

Lemma

B.3.4

Item

(ii)
.

\Box

3.6.5.

In particular, if
2.6.6
holds,
and
\tau
is a self-dual weak stability condition on
\mathcal{A}

satisfying
2.5.5
,
then the semistable loci

\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(2n+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{ss}}}}_{\alpha_{1},\,\dotsc,\,\alpha_{n},\mspace{2mu}\theta}(\tau)

defined in
3.6.3

are of finite type.

3.7   The self-dual extension bundle

3.7.1.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a self-dual
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category,
with a categorical moduli stack
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
, as in
.
We also assume that
\mathcal{A}
has an extension bundle,
as in
, parametrizing extensions of objects in
\mathcal{A}
.
We aim to study the self-dual analogue of this problem,
and we study the bundle whose fibres
parametrize self-dual extensions of a self-dual object
by another object in
\mathcal{A}
.

Lemma 3.7.2
.

In the situation of
,
suppose that
is satisfied.
Then there is an isomorphism of
2
-vector bundles

\sigma^{*}\,\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\simeq\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\smash{\alpha_{2}^{\vee},\alpha_{1}^{\vee}}}\ ,

(3.32)

where
\sigma\colon\smash{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{1}}}\times\smash{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{2}}}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\smash{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{2}^{\vee}}}\times\smash{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{1}^{\vee}}}

sends
(E_{1},E_{2})
to
(E_{2}^{\vee},E_{1}^{\vee})
.

In particular, we have isomorphisms of
2
-vector spaces

\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E_{2},E_{1})\simeq\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E_{1}^{\vee},E_{2}^{\vee})

for all
E_{1},E_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
, and the Euler form satisfies

\chi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\chi(\alpha_{2}^{\vee},\alpha_{1}^{\vee})

(3.33)

for all
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof 9
.

The natural
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}

in
3.6.1

is compatible with the automorphism
\sigma
.
It is an automorphism of module stacks,
because taking the opposite category of a
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category
is compatible with scaling morphisms
and taking Baer sums of extensions.

\Box

3.7.3.

In particular, for any
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
,
the isomorphism
Eq.

3.32
induces a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action
on the
2
-vector bundle

(\mathrm{id}\times D)^{*}\,\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{\smash{1/0}}_{\smash{\alpha,\alpha^{\vee}}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}
,
whose fibre at
E
is the
2
-vector space
\mathrm{Ext}^{\smash{1/0}}(E^{\vee},E)
.
Consider the fixed locus of this action,

(\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{\smash{1/0}}_{\smash{\alpha,\alpha^{\vee}}})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\ ,

which is a
2
-vector bundle over
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}
.

We will need the following compatibility condition
between the extension bundle and the self-dual structure.

Condition 0
(SdExt).

In the situation of
,
suppose that
is satisfied.
We further assume that for all
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
,
the
2
-vector bundle

(\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{\smash{1/0}}_{\smash{\alpha,\alpha^{\vee}}})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}

defined above has constant rank.

3.7.5. The self-dual Euler form.

In the situation of
, write

\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,0)=-{\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{\smash{1/0}}_{\smash{\alpha,\alpha^{\vee}}})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}}\ .

(3.34)

Define a map of sets

\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\colon C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})\times C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathbb{Z}
,
called the
self-dual Euler form
, by

\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,\theta)=\chi(\alpha,j(\theta))+\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,0)

(3.35)

for
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
,
where
j
is the map in
Eq.

3.13
.
One can deduce the relation

\chi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})+\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\theta)=\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{2},\theta)+\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1},\bar{\alpha}_{2}+\theta)

(3.36)

for all
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
.

3.7.6.

We can now show that the family of self-dual extensions is,
roughly speaking,
parametrized by the self-dual Ext groups in
3.3.9
.

Theorem 0
.

In the situation of
,
let
(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
be a self-dual object,
and let
F\in\mathcal{A}
be an object.

Then the stack of self-dual extensions of
(E,\phi)
by
F

is an affine
2
-vector bundle over the
2
-vector space

\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)
, which is a torsor for the trivial
2
-vector bundle

\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)\times\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(F^{\vee},F)^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)\ .

(3.37)

Proof 10
.

Consider the
2
-vector bundle

\mathcal{E}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)

whose fibre over
a\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)(\mathbb{K})

is given by
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(F^{\vee},G_{a})
,
where
G_{a}
is the extension of
E
by
F
given by
a
.
Precisely,
\mathcal{E}
is the moduli stack of

3
-step filtrations with stepwise quotients
F,E,F^{\vee}
.

Define

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{E}\underset{\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)^{2}}{\times}\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F),

where the morphism
\mathcal{E}\to\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)^{2}

sends a filtration

0\hookrightarrow F\hookrightarrow G\hookrightarrow H
to
(a,a^{\prime})
,
where
a
is the class of
G
as an extension of
E
by
F
,
and
a^{\prime}
is the image of the class of
H
in
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(F^{\vee},G)

under the pushforward map to
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(F^{\vee},E)
,
identified with
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)
using the self-dual structure of
E
.
The morphism
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)\to\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)^{2}
is the diagonal morphism.

Regard
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)^{2}
as a trivial
2
-vector bundle over
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)
.
It follows from
Lemma

C.1.8

that
\mathcal{F}
is an affine
2
-vector bundle over
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)
,
and is a torsor for the trivial
2
-vector bundle with fibre
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(F^{\vee},F)
.

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
\mathcal{E}
by taking the dual filtration
induces a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
\mathcal{F}
,
and the morphism
\mathcal{F}\to\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)
is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-invariant.
The fixed locus
\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
is
the moduli stack of self-dual extensions of
E
by
F
.

Moreover, this
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
\mathcal{F}
is affine linear.
Indeed, for any
a\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)(\mathbb{K})
,
the fibre
\mathcal{F}_{a}
is
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(F^{\vee},G_{a})_{a}
,
where the last subscript
a
denotes taking the fibre over

a\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(F^{\vee},E)(\mathbb{K})
.
The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action identifies this with

\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(G_{a}^{\vee},F)_{a}
,
and reinterprets its elements as
3
-step filtrations,
giving elements of
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(F^{\vee},G_{a})_{a}
again,
but note that now the roles of the two
a
’s have changed.
These two steps are affine linear by

Lemmas

2.7.8
and
3.7.2
.

Therefore,
\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
is an affine
2
-vector bundle
over
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)
,
and is a torsor for the trivial
2
-vector bundle
with fibre
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(F^{\vee},F)^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
.

\Box

3.7.8.

We also have a family version of
3.7.6
.

Theorem 0
.

In the situation of
,
let
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
.
Then the morphism

\pi_{1}\times\pi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{2}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{{\smash{(3)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}}_{\alpha,\theta}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}

(3.38)

factors as an affine
2
-vector bundle of rank
-\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,0)

followed by a
2
-vector bundle of rank
-\chi(\alpha,j(\theta)),

so that the total rank is
-\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,\theta)
.

Proof 11
.

Let
u\colon\smash{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\smash{j(\theta)}}
be the forgetful morphism
that forgets the self-dual structure.
Consider the
2
-vector bundle

\mathcal{V}=(\mathrm{id}\times u)^{*}\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\smash{\alpha,j(\theta)}}

on
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\times\smash{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}}
,
whose fibre over
(F,(E,\phi))
is
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)
.
Using
\mathcal{V}
in place of
\mathrm{Ext}^{1/0}(E,F)
in the proof of
3.7.6
,
a similar argument shows that
there is an affine
2
-vector bundle
\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{V}
,
together with an affine linear
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action,
such that the fixed locus
\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}

can be identified with
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{{\smash{(3)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}}_{\alpha,\theta}
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
is a torsor for the
2
-vector bundle

p^{*}(\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}_{\smash{\alpha,\alpha^{\vee}}})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\to\mathcal{V}
,
where
p\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}
is the projection.
This shows that the rank of
\smash{\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}}
is as stated.

\Box

Section 4Motivic algebraic structures

In this
Section
,
we introduce the algebraic structures
that arise in the enumerative geometry in self-dual categories,
including
motivic Hall algebras
,

motivic Hall modules
,
and their corresponding Lie algebras and
twisted modules
.
These structures will be closely related to
motivic enumerative invariants
that we will study in
Section

5
.

4.1   Involutive algebraic structures

4.1.1.

To fix notation and terminology,
we introduce several types of algebraic structures
that arise in the enumerative geometry in self-dual categories.

Throughout this section,
we assume that
\mathbb{k}
is a field with
\operatorname{char}\mathbb{k}\neq 2
.

4.1.2. Involutive algebras.

Let
A
be an associative
\mathbb{k}
-algebra.
An
involution
of
A
is a
\mathbb{k}
-linear map

(-)^{\vee}\colon A\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\longrightarrow}}A\ ,

such that
(-)^{\vee\vee}=\mathrm{id}_{A}
, and
(xy)^{\vee}=y^{\vee}x^{\vee}

for all
x,y\in A
.
An algebra
A
equipped with an involution is called
an
involutive algebra
over
\mathbb{k}
.

In this case, define the subgroup of

isometric elements
in
A
to be

A^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}=\{x\in A\mid xx^{\vee}=1\}\ .

(4.1)

When
A
is finite-dimensional,

A^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}
is a linear algebraic group over
\mathbb{k}
.

4.1.3. Involutive Lie algebras.

Let
L
be a Lie algebra over
\mathbb{k}
.
An
involution
of
L
is a
\mathbb{k}
-linear map

(-)^{\vee}\colon L\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\longrightarrow}}L\ ,

such that
(-)^{\vee\vee}=\mathrm{id}_{L}
, and
[x,y]^{\vee}=-[x^{\vee},y^{\vee}]

for all
x,y\in L
.
A Lie algebra
L
equipped with an involution is called
an
involutive Lie algebra
over
\mathbb{k}
.

In this case, define the subspace of

isometric elements
in
L
to be

L^{+}=\{x\in L\mid x^{\vee}=-x\}\ ,

(4.2)

which is a Lie subalgebra of
L
.

4.1.4. Twisted modules.

Let
L
be an involutive Lie algebra over
\mathbb{k}
.
A
twisted module
for
L
is a
\mathbb{k}
-vector space
M
,
together with a bilinear map
\mathbin{\heartsuit}\colon L\otimes M\to M
,
such that for any
x,y\in L
and
m\in M
, we have

\displaystyle x^{\vee}\mathbin{\heartsuit}m

\displaystyle=-x\mathbin{\heartsuit}m\ ,

(4.3)

\displaystyle x\mathbin{\heartsuit}(y\mathbin{\heartsuit}m)-y\mathbin{\heartsuit}(x\mathbin{\heartsuit}m)

\displaystyle=[x,y]\mathbin{\heartsuit}m-[x^{\vee},y]\mathbin{\heartsuit}m\ .

(4.4)

Here, the module structure is
twisted
by the involution,
in the sense that we have an extra term
on the right-hand side of
Eq.

4.4
,
compared to the usual Jacobi identity.

Equivalently, a twisted module for
L
is just
a module
M
for the Lie algebra
L^{+}
.
Namely,
given a twisted module as above, for
x\in L^{+}
and
m\in M
,
defining

x\cdot m=\frac{1}{2}\,x\mathbin{\heartsuit}m

(4.5)

equips
M
with the structure of a Lie algebra module for
L^{+}
.
On the other hand, given an
L^{+}
-module
M
,
for any
x\in L
, defining

x\mathbin{\heartsuit}m=(x-x^{\vee})\cdot m

(4.6)

recovers the twisted module structure.

4.2   Motivic Hall algebras

4.2.1.

We summarize the construction of
motivic Hall algebras

on the space of stack functions,
due to Joyce
[
27
]
.
See
Appendix

A
for an introduction to stack functions.

Throughout this section,
we assume that
\mathcal{A}
is a
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category,
with a moduli stack
\mathcal{M}
, satisfying

and
2.6.6
.

Definition 4.2.2
.

In the situation of
and
2.6.6
,
define an operation

{*}=(\pi^{\smash{(2)}})_{!}\circ(\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2})^{*}\colon\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})\otimes\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})\longrightarrow\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})\ ,

(4.7)

where the composition is through
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}})
.
This is well-defined by

Theorem

A.2.5
,
Lemma

2.6.8
, and
2.6.6
.

Let
\delta_{0}\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})
be the characteristic function
of the point
0\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{K})
,
represented by the morphism
0\colon\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{K}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}
.

This defines the
motivic Hall algebra

(\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}),*,\delta_{0})
,
which is an associative
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra, as stated below.

Theorem 4.2.3
.

In the situation of
and
2.6.6
,
for any
f,g,h\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}),
we have

\displaystyle\delta_{0}*f=f=f*\delta_{0}\ ,

(4.8)

\displaystyle(f*g)*h=f*(g*h)\ .

(4.9)

Therefore, the operation
*
defines
an associative algebra structure on
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})
.

Moreover, for any
f_{1},\dotsc,f_{n}\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}),
we have

f_{1}*\cdots*f_{n}=(\pi^{\smash{(n)}})_{!}\circ(\pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\pi_{n})^{*}(f_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes f_{n})\ ,

(4.10)

where
f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{n}\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})^{\otimes n}\simeq\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{n}),

and the composition is through

\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}}).

Proof 12
.

This is essentially

[
27
, Theorem 5.2]
.

\Box

Remark 4.2.4
.

As in
[
27
, §5.4]
,
if
2.6.6
is also satisfied,
then it is possible to define the motivic Hall algebra structure on the space

\dot{\mathrm{LSF}}(\mathcal{M})\subset\mathrm{LSF}(\mathcal{M})

of local stack functions supported on a finite union of the substacks
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}
.

4.2.5.

Now, we also assume that
\mathcal{A}
has a self-dual structure,
with a self-dual moduli stack
\mathcal{M}
as in
.
We show that the motivic Hall algebra
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})

is an involutive algebra in this case,
in the sense of
4.1.2
.

Theorem 0
.

In the situation of
and
2.6.6
,
define a map

(-)^{\vee}=D^{*}\colon\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})\ ,

(4.11)

where
D\colon\mathcal{M}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\mathcal{M}
is the self-dual structure.
Then, for any
f,g\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})
, we have

(f*g)^{\vee}=g^{\vee}*f^{\vee}\ ,

(4.12)

and
(-)^{\vee}
defines an involution of
the motivic Hall algebra
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})
.

Proof 13
.

There is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\hbox to0.0pt{ ,\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\times D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}\times\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\smash{(2)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\smash{(2)}}}

where the vertical morphisms are isomorphisms.
Therefore, by
Theorem

A.2.5

Item

(iii)
,
we have

\displaystyle(f*g)^{\vee}

\displaystyle=D^{*}\,(\pi^{\smash{(2)}})_{!}\,(\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2})^{*}\,(f\otimes g)

\displaystyle=(\pi^{\smash{(2)}})_{!}\,D^{*}\,(\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2})^{*}\,(f\otimes g)

\displaystyle=(\pi^{\smash{(2)}})_{!}\,(\pi_{2}\times\pi_{1})^{*}\,(D\times D)^{*}\,(f\otimes g)

\displaystyle=(\pi^{\smash{(2)}})_{!}\,(\pi_{2}\times\pi_{1})^{*}\,(f^{\vee}\otimes g^{\vee})

\displaystyle=g^{\vee}*f^{\vee}.

\Box

4.2.7. The Hall Lie algebra.

In particular,
in the situation of
and
2.6.6
,
the motivic Hall algebra
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})
is also a Lie algebra,
with the Lie bracket given by

[f,g]=f*g-g*f

(4.13)

for
f,g\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})
.

Moreover, in the situation of
and
2.6.6
,
this Lie algebra is also an involutive Lie algebra,
with the involution given by
Eq.

4.11
.

4.3   Motivic Hall modules

4.3.1.

Now, let
\mathcal{A}
be a self-dual
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category,
with a self-dual moduli stack
\mathcal{M}
as in
,
so that we have a moduli stack
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
of self-dual objects in
\mathcal{A}
.
We construct on the space
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})

a structure of a module over the motivic Hall algebra
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})
,
which we call the
motivic Hall module
.

Definition 4.3.2
.

In the situation of
and
2.6.6
,
define an operation

{\diamond}=(\pi^{{\smash{(3)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}})_{!}\circ(\pi_{1}\times\pi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{2})^{*}\colon\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})\otimes\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})\longrightarrow\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})\ ,

(4.14)

where the composition is through
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(3)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}})
.
This is well-defined by

Theorem

A.2.5
,
Lemma

3.6.4
, and
2.6.6
.

This defines the
motivic Hall module

structure on
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
, as stated below.

Theorem 4.3.3
.

In the situation of
and
2.6.6
,
for any
f,g\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})

and
h\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{0}\diamond h

\displaystyle=h\ ,

(4.15)

\displaystyle(f*g)\diamond h

\displaystyle=f\diamond(g\diamond h)\ .

(4.16)

Therefore, the operation
\diamond

equips
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
with the structure of a module
over the motivic Hall algebra
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})
.

Moreover, for any
f_{1},\dotsc,f_{n}\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})

and
h\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}),
writing

f_{1}\diamond\cdots\diamond f_{n}\diamond h=f_{1}\diamond({\cdots\diamond(f_{n-1}\diamond(f_{n}\diamond h))\cdots})\ ,

we have

f_{1}\diamond\cdots\diamond f_{n}\diamond h=(\pi^{{\smash{(2n+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}})_{!}\circ(\pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\pi_{n}\times\pi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{n+1})^{*}(f_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes f_{n}\boxtimes h)\ ,

(4.17)

where
f_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes f_{n}\boxtimes h\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{n}\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}),

and the composition is through

\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(2n+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}).

Proof 14
.

Equation
Eq.

4.15
,
follows from the fact that
\delta_{0}

is supported on
\{0\}\subset\mathcal{M}
,
and that
\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}

restricts to an isomorphism

\pi_{1}^{-1}(\{0\})\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\{0\}\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
.

To prove
Eq.

4.16
, consider the diagram below,
where subscripts
\alpha,\beta,\theta
are added to help with understanding,
indicating classes in
C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
and
C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
,
but one could also write down the diagram without them.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\times\mathcal{M}_{\beta}\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha,\beta}\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\alpha+\beta}\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\times\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(3)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\beta,\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(5)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\alpha,\beta,\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(3)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\alpha+\beta,\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\bar{\beta}+\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(3)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\alpha,\bar{\beta}+\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\bar{\alpha}+\bar{\beta}+\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2})\times\mathrm{id}}
rep.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\smash{(2)}}\times\mathrm{id}}
f.t.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathllap{\mathrm{id}\times\pi^{{\smash{(3)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}}\kern 4.09723pt\mathrlap{\text{f.t.}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathllap{\mathrm{id}\times(\pi_{1}\times\pi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{2})}\kern 4.09723pt\mathrlap{\text{rep.}}}
rep.
f.t.
rep.
f.t.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\urcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathllap{\text{f.t.}}\kern 4.09723pt\mathrlap{\pi^{{\smash{(3)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathllap{\text{rep.}}\kern 4.09723pt\mathrlap{\pi_{1}\times\pi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\times\pi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{2}}
rep.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{{\smash{(3)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}}
f.t.

(4.18)

In the diagram, ‘rep.’ means ‘representable’ and ‘f.t.’ means ‘finite type’.
These properties follow from

Lemmas

2.6.8
and
3.6.4
,
applied to the outer eight morphisms;
the other four morphisms are pullbacks of them.
By
Theorem

A.2.5
, this gives rise to a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{matrix}\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha})\otimes\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}_{\beta})\\
{}\otimes\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta})\end{matrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha,\beta})\otimes\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha+\beta})\otimes\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha})\otimes\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(3)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\beta,\theta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(5)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\alpha,\beta,\theta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(3)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\alpha+\beta,\theta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha})\otimes\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\bar{\beta}+\theta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(3)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\alpha,\bar{\beta}+\theta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\bar{\alpha}+\bar{\beta}+\theta})\hbox to0.0pt{ ,\hss}}

(4.19)

where the maps are pushforwards and pullbacks,
depending on the direction of the arrows.
The commutativity of the outer square is precisely
the content of
Eq.

4.16
.

\Box

Remark 4.3.4
.

As in
Remark

4.2.4
,
if
2.6.6
is also satisfied,
then it is also possible to define
the motivic Hall module structure on the space

\dot{\mathrm{LSF}}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})\subset\mathrm{LSF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})

of local stack functions supported on a finite union of the substacks

\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash{\theta}}
,
which is a module over the algebra in
Remark

4.2.4
.

4.3.5. The Hall twisted module.

In the situation of
and
2.6.6
,
the motivic Hall module
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})

can also be equipped with the structure of a twisted module,
in the sense of
4.1.4
,
for the Hall Lie algebra
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})

defined in
4.2.7
, given by

f\mathbin{\heartsuit}h=f\diamond h-f^{\vee}\diamond h

(4.20)

for
f\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})
and
h\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
.

Note that although the operation
\diamond

already makes
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
into an (untwisted) module
for the Hall Lie algebra
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})
,
the twisted module structure is more analogous
to the Hall Lie bracket,
as one can think of
Eq.

4.20

as a commutator of
f
and
h
.
In our applications to enumerative invariants,
we will often find that properties enjoyed by the Hall Lie bracket,
but not the associative product,
will have analogues for the twisted module structure,
but not the usual module structure.

4.4   Motivic integration

4.4.1.

We study the
motivic integration
of stack functions,
in the sense of
A.2.7
,
and in particular, its relation to the motivic Hall algebra and module.
This will be helpful in studying motivic enumerative invariants,
which will be defined as motivic integrals of certain stack functions.

Throughout, we assume that

\mathcal{A}
is a self-dual
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category,
with a self-dual moduli stack
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
as in
.
We also assume the condition
,
restricting the homological dimension of
\mathcal{A}
.

Let
{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}
be the completed ring of motives
as in
Definition

A.1.2
.

4.4.2. The algebra
\Lambda(\mathcal{A})
.

In the situation of
4.4.1
,
following a similar construction in

[
27
, Definition 6.3]
,
we define an associative
{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra

\Lambda(\mathcal{A})
as follows.

As an
{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}
-module, set

\Lambda(\mathcal{A})=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})}{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}\cdot\lambda_{\alpha}\ ,

(4.21)

where
\lambda_{\alpha}
is a basis element.
Define the multiplication

*\colon\Lambda(\mathcal{A})\otimes_{\smash{{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}}}\Lambda(\mathcal{A})\to\Lambda(\mathcal{A})
by

\lambda_{\alpha_{1}}*\lambda_{\alpha_{2}}=\mathbb{L}^{-\chi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})}\cdot\lambda_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}

(4.22)

for all
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
,
where
\chi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
is the Euler form defined in
2.7.6
.
The associativity of this product follows from
the bilinearity of
\chi
.
The element
\lambda_{0}\in\Lambda(\mathcal{A})
is the multiplication identity.

The self-dual structure of
\mathcal{A}
equips
the algebra
\Lambda(\mathcal{A})
with an involution,
in the sense of
4.1.2
,
given by
\lambda_{\alpha}^{\vee}=\lambda_{\smash{\alpha^{\vee}}}
for all
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
.

Note that we have an isomorphism

\Lambda(\mathcal{A})\simeq\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A}))

(4.23)

of
{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}
-modules,
where
C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
is regarded as a discrete space.
It is sometimes helpful to think of
\Lambda(\mathcal{A})
in this way,
as some kind of discretized Hall algebra.

4.4.3. The module
\Lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
.

We define a
\Lambda(\mathcal{A})
-module
\Lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
as follows.
As an
{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}
-module, set

\Lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})=\bigoplus_{\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\vphantom{{}^{0}}}{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}\cdot\lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash{\theta}}\,.

(4.24)

Define an operation

\diamond\colon\Lambda(\mathcal{A})\otimes_{\smash{{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}}}\Lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\to\Lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
by

\lambda_{\alpha}\diamond\lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash{\theta}}=\mathbb{L}^{-\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,\theta)}\cdot\lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash{\bar{\alpha}+\theta}}

(4.25)

for all
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
,
where
\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,\theta)
is the self-dual Euler form
defined in
3.7.5
.
The associativity follows from the relation
Eq.

3.36
.

Similarly, we also have an isomorphism

\Lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\simeq\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}))

(4.26)

of
{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}
-modules,
where
C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
is regarded as a discrete space.

4.4.4. Explicit coefficients.

We can describe the multiplication in

\Lambda(\mathcal{A})
and its action on
\Lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
more explicitly.
For
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
and
\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
, write

\displaystyle\chi(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\chi(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})\ ,

(4.27)

\displaystyle\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n},\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}{}\bigl(\chi(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})+\chi(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\vee})\bigr)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{i},\rho)\ .

(4.28)

Then we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha_{1}}*\cdots*\lambda_{\alpha_{n}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}^{-\chi(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})}\cdot\lambda_{\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}}\ ,

(4.29)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha_{1}}\diamond\cdots\diamond\lambda_{\alpha_{n}}\diamond\lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}^{-\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n},\rho)}\cdot\lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho}\ ,

(4.30)

which can be verified using the bilinearity of
\chi

and the relations
Eq.

3.35
–
Eq.

3.36

of
\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
.

4.4.5. The integration maps.

We now define the motivic integration maps

\displaystyle\Psi\colon

\displaystyle\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Lambda(\mathcal{A})\ ,

\displaystyle\Psi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\colon

\displaystyle\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})

by setting

\displaystyle\Psi(f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})}\lambda_{\alpha}\cdot\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}}f|_{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}}\ ,

(4.31)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(h)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\vphantom{{}^{0}}}\lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash{\theta}}\cdot\int_{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}}h|_{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}}

(4.32)

for
f\in\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{M})
and
h\in\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
,
where the integrals are defined in
A.2.7
.

Using the isomorphisms

Eqs.

4.23
and
4.26
,
these maps can also be interpreted as pushing forward
along the natural maps

\mathcal{M}\to C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\to C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
.

Theorem 4.4.6
.

In the setting of
4.4.1
,
let
f,g\in\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{M})

and
h\in\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
.
Then

\displaystyle\Psi(f*g)

\displaystyle=\Psi(f)*\Psi(g)\ ,

(4.33)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(f\diamond h)

\displaystyle=\Psi(f)\diamond\Psi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(h)\ .

(4.34)

Proof 15
.

Equation
Eq.

4.33

is essentially
[
27
, Theorem 6.4]
,
but we provide a more direct proof here,
as our setting is slightly different,
and the same strategy will help us prove

Eq.

4.34
as well.

To prove
Eq.

4.33
,
without loss of generality, we may assume that

f
and
g
are supported in
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{1}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{2}}
,
respectively,
for some
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
.
By
, the morphism

\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{1}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{2}}

is a
2
-vector bundle of rank
-\chi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
.
Therefore, by
Theorem

C.1.10
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}}f*g

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}}(\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2})^{*}(f\boxtimes g)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}^{-\chi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})}\cdot\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{1}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{2}}}(f\boxtimes g)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}^{-\chi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})}\cdot\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{1}}}f\cdot\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{2}}}g\ ,

which implies
Eq.

4.33
.

The proof of
Eq.

4.34
is analogous,
where we use
3.7.8
in place of
.

\Box

4.5   Motivic integration, Lie algebras and twisted modules

4.5.1. The setting.

We continue to develop other versions of the structures
presented in
Section

4.4
,
involving Lie algebras and twisted modules,
as in
4.1.4
.

These versions will be helpful in defining
numerical enumerative invariants in
Section

5.6
below.
The idea is that we wish to define numerical enumerative invariants
as Euler characteristics of certain motivic integrals.
For the Euler characteristic to be well-defined, that is, not infinite,
these integrals should live in the subspace
{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}\subset{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}
.
However, the space of stack functions with this property
is not closed under the Hall algebra product or the Hall module action,
but only under the Hall Lie bracket and the twisted module action.
The integration map in this setting is described in

Theorem

4.5.8
below.

Throughout,
we assume that we are in the situation of
4.4.1
,
with the conditions
and
.

We also define enlarged versions of the rings of motives,

\prescript{*}{}{{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}={\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}[\mathbb{L}^{1/2}]\ ,\qquad\prescript{*}{}{{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}}_{\mathbb{K}}={\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}[\mathbb{L}^{1/2}]\ ,

(4.35)

and we write

\prescript{*}{}{\Lambda}(\mathcal{A})=\Lambda(\mathcal{A})\underset{\phantom{{}^{0}}{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}}{\otimes}\prescript{*}{}{{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}}_{\mathbb{K}}\ ,\qquad\prescript{*}{}{\Lambda}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})=\Lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\underset{\phantom{{}^{0}}{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}}{\otimes}\prescript{*}{}{{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}}_{\mathbb{K}}\ .

(4.36)

4.5.2. The Lie algebra version.

We describe a Lie algebra version
of the structures discussed in
Section

4.4
.

For each
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
, define an element

\tilde{\lambda}_{\alpha}\in\prescript{*}{}{\Lambda}(\mathcal{A})
by

\tilde{\lambda}_{\alpha}=\frac{\mathbb{L}^{-\chi(\alpha,\alpha)/2}}{\mathbb{L}^{1/2}-\mathbb{L}^{-1/2}}\cdot\lambda_{\alpha}\ .

(4.37)

Also, for
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
, write

\bar{\chi}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\chi(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1})-\chi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\ .

(4.38)

One can verify using

Eqs.

4.22
and
4.37
that

[\tilde{\lambda}_{\alpha_{1}}\,,\,\tilde{\lambda}_{\alpha_{2}}]=\frac{\mathbb{L}^{\bar{\chi}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})/2}-\mathbb{L}^{-\bar{\chi}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})/2}}{\mathbb{L}^{1/2}-\mathbb{L}^{-1/2}}\cdot\tilde{\lambda}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}\ .

(4.39)

Note that the coefficient in
Eq.

4.39

has no poles when
\mathbb{L}=1
. As a consequence,
the
\prescript{*}{}{{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}
-submodule

\prescript{*}{}{\Lambda}^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})\subset\prescript{*}{}{\Lambda}(\mathcal{A})\ ,

spanned over
\prescript{*}{}{{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}
by the elements
\tilde{\lambda}_{\alpha}

for
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
,
is a Lie subalgebra,
although it need not be an associative subalgebra.

The Lie algebras
\prescript{*}{}{\Lambda}(\mathcal{A})
and
\prescript{*}{}{\Lambda}^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})

are equipped with involutions,
in the sense of
4.1.3
,
given by
\lambda_{\alpha}\mapsto\lambda_{\alpha^{\vee}}
and
\tilde{\lambda}_{\alpha}\mapsto\tilde{\lambda}_{\alpha^{\vee}}
,
respectively. These two involutions are compatible,
since
\chi(\alpha,\alpha)=\chi(\alpha^{\vee},\alpha^{\vee})

by
Eq.

3.33
.

4.5.3. The twisted module.

We also consider the operation

\displaystyle{\mathbin{\heartsuit}}\colon\Lambda(\mathcal{A})\times\Lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}),

(4.40)

\displaystyle(x,m)

\displaystyle\longmapsto x\diamond m-x^{\vee}\diamond m\ ,

exhibiting
\Lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})

as a twisted module over the involutive Lie algebra
\Lambda(\mathcal{A})
,
in the sense of
4.1.4
.
This also equips
\prescript{*}{}{\Lambda}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})

with a twisted module structure over
\prescript{*}{}{\Lambda}(\mathcal{A})
.

For each
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
, define an element

\tilde{\lambda}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}\in\prescript{*}{}{\Lambda}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
by

\tilde{\lambda}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}=\mathbb{L}^{-\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(j(\theta),0)/2}\cdot\lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}\ ,

(4.41)

with
j
as in
Eq.

3.13
.
For
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
, write

\bar{\chi}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,\theta)=\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha^{\vee},\theta)-\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,\theta)\ .

(4.42)

One can verify using
Eqs.

4.25
and
4.41
that

\tilde{\lambda}_{\alpha}\mathbin{\heartsuit}\tilde{\lambda}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}=\frac{\mathbb{L}^{\bar{\chi}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,\theta)/2}-\mathbb{L}^{-\bar{\chi}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,\theta)/2}}{\mathbb{L}^{1/2}-\mathbb{L}^{-1/2}}\cdot\tilde{\lambda}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\bar{\alpha}+\theta}\ .

(4.43)

Since the coefficient has no poles when
\mathbb{L}=1
,
the
\prescript{*}{}{{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}
-submodule

\prescript{*}{}{\Lambda}^{{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}},\mspace{2mu}\circ}(\mathcal{A})\subset\prescript{*}{}{\Lambda}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\ ,

spanned over
\prescript{*}{}{{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}
by the elements
\tilde{\lambda}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}

for
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
,
is a twisted module for the Lie algebra
\prescript{*}{}{\Lambda}^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})

with the
\mathbin{\heartsuit}
action,
although it is not necessarily closed under
the
\diamond
action by
\prescript{*}{}{\Lambda}^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
.

4.5.4. The numeric Lie algebra.

Let
{\smash{\widetilde{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}
be the ring of motives as in
Definition

A.1.2
.
We define a Lie algebra
\Omega(\mathcal{A})
over
{\smash{\widetilde{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}
,
as the free
{\smash{\widetilde{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}
-module

\Omega(\mathcal{A})=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})}{\smash{\widetilde{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}\cdot\omega_{\alpha}\ ,

(4.44)

equipped with the Lie bracket given by

[\omega_{\alpha_{1}}\,,\,\omega_{\alpha_{2}}]=\bar{\chi}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\cdot\omega_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}

(4.45)

for all
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
,
where
\bar{\chi}
is given by
Eq.

4.38
.
One can verify that this defines a Lie algebra structure on
\Omega(\mathcal{A})
.
The Lie algebra
\Omega(\mathcal{A})

is equipped with an involution,
in the sense of
4.1.3
,
given by
\omega_{\alpha}\mapsto\omega_{\alpha^{\vee}}
for all
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
.

Define a map of Lie algebras over
{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}
,

\displaystyle\Xi\colon\quad\prescript{*}{}{\Lambda}^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Omega(\mathcal{A})\ ,

(4.46)

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})}a_{\alpha}\cdot\tilde{\lambda}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})}a_{\alpha}\cdot\omega_{\alpha}\ ,

where each
a_{\alpha}\in{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}
.
That
\Xi
respects Lie brackets
can be seen by comparing
Eq.

4.39
with
Eq.

4.45
.

4.5.5. The numeric twisted module.

Define a free
{\smash{\widetilde{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}
-module

\Omega^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})=\bigoplus_{\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\vphantom{{}^{0}}}{\smash{\widetilde{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}\cdot\omega^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}\ ,

(4.47)

and define an action of
\Omega(\mathcal{A})

on
\Omega^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
by

\omega_{\alpha}\mathbin{\heartsuit}\omega^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}=\bar{\chi}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,\theta)\cdot\omega^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\bar{\alpha}+\theta}

(4.48)

for all
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
,
where
\bar{\chi}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
is given by
Eq.

4.42
.
One can verify that this defines a twisted module
of the involutive Lie algebra
\Omega(\mathcal{A})
.

Define a map of
{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}
-modules,

\displaystyle\Xi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\colon\quad\prescript{*}{}{\Lambda}^{{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}},\mspace{2mu}\circ}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Omega^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\ ,

(4.49)

\displaystyle\sum_{\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\vphantom{{}^{0}}}a_{\theta}\cdot\tilde{\lambda}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\vphantom{{}^{0}}}a_{\theta}\cdot\omega^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}\ ,

where each
a_{\theta}\in{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}
.
The maps
\Xi
and
\Xi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
respect the
\mathbin{\heartsuit}
action,
as can be seen by comparing
Eq.

4.43
with
Eq.

4.48
.

4.5.6. Explicit coefficients.

We also describe iterated Lie brackets in
\Omega(\mathcal{A})

and their action on
\Omega^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
explicitly.
For classes
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
, write

\tilde{\chi}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})=\prod_{j=2}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\bar{\chi}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})\ .

(4.50)

Then, in
\Omega(\mathcal{A})
, we have

[[\dotsc[\omega_{\alpha_{1}}\,,\,\omega_{\alpha_{2}}],\dotsc],\,\omega_{\alpha_{n}}]=\tilde{\chi}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})\cdot\omega_{\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}}\ .

(4.51)

Similarly, for classes
\alpha_{1,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{1,m_{1}};\dotsc;\alpha_{n,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n,m_{n}}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})

and
\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
, write

\tilde{\chi}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{1,m_{1}};\dotsc;\alpha_{n,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n,m_{n}};\rho)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\chi}(\bar{\alpha}_{i,1},\dotsc,\bar{\alpha}_{i,m_{i}})\cdot{}\\
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{\chi}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\biggl(\alpha_{i,1}+\cdots+\alpha_{i,m_{i}}\,,\ \sum_{j=i+1}^{n}{}(\bar{\alpha}_{j,1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{j,m_{j}})+\rho\biggr)\ .

(4.52)

Then, in
\Omega^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
, we have

\bigl[\bigl[\omega_{\alpha_{1,1}},\dotsc\bigr],\omega_{\alpha_{1,m_{1}}}\bigr]\mathbin{\heartsuit}\cdots\mathbin{\heartsuit}\bigl[\bigl[\omega_{\alpha_{n,1}},\dotsc\bigr],\omega_{\alpha_{n,m_{n}}}\bigr]\mathbin{\heartsuit}\omega^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}=\\
\tilde{\chi}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{1,m_{1}};\dotsc;\alpha_{n,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n,m_{n}};\rho)\cdot\omega^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}\ ,

(4.53)

where
\theta=(\bar{\alpha}_{1,1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{1,m_{1}})+\cdots+(\bar{\alpha}_{n,1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n,m_{n}})+\rho
.

4.5.7. The integration maps.

Recall the integration maps
\Psi,\Psi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}

from
4.4.5
. Consider the subspaces

\displaystyle\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}^{\circ}(\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Psi^{-1}

\displaystyle\bigl(\Lambda^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)

\displaystyle\subset\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{M})\ ,

(4.54)

\displaystyle\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}^{\circ}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(\Psi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})^{-1}

\displaystyle\bigl(\Lambda^{{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}},\mspace{2mu}\circ}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)

\displaystyle\subset\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})\ ,

(4.55)

where
\Lambda^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})\subset\Lambda(\mathcal{A})
is the

{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}
-subalgebra spanned by
the elements
\lambda_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
,
and
\Lambda^{{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}},\mspace{2mu}\circ}(\mathcal{A})\subset\Lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
is the

{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}
-submodule spanned by
the elements
\lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}
for
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
.

It then follows from
Theorem

4.4.6

that
\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}^{\circ}(\mathcal{M})
is an involutive Lie subalgebra
of the Hall Lie algebra
\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{M})
as in
4.2.7
,
and that
\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}^{\circ}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
is a twisted module
for
\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}^{\circ}(\mathcal{M})
under the action
\mathbin{\heartsuit}
in
4.3.5
.
Moreover,
Theorem

4.4.6
implies the following.

Theorem 4.5.8
.

In the situation of
4.5.1
,
consider the integration maps

\displaystyle\Xi\circ\Psi\colon

\displaystyle\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}^{\circ}(\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Omega(\mathcal{A})\ ,

(4.56)

\displaystyle\Xi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\circ\Psi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\colon

\displaystyle\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}^{\circ}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Omega^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\ .

(4.57)

Then
Eq.

4.56
is a
morphism of Lie algebras,
and
Eq.

4.57

is compatible with the twisted module structures on both sides,
in the sense that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}^{\circ}(\mathcal{M})\underset{\mathbb{Q}}{\otimes}\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}^{\circ}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega(\mathcal{A})\underset{\mathbb{Q}}{\otimes}\Omega^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}^{\circ}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Xi\circ\Psi)\otimes(\Xi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\circ\Psi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbin{\heartsuit}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbin{\heartsuit}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\circ\Psi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}

commutes.

\square

Section 5Motivic enumerative invariants

In this
Section
,
we state our main constructions of motivic enumerative invariants,
including the following different versions:

•

The
stack function
version

\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha})

and
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,
\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta})
.

•

The
motivic
version

\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,

\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}
,
obtained from the stack function invariants
via motivic integration.

•

The
numeric
version

\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,

\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\textnormal{na\"{\i}}}}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\textnormal{na\"{\i}}}}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in\mathbb{Q}
,
obtained from the motivic invariants
by taking the Euler characteristic.

We also prove
wall-crossing formulae

for each version of invariants,
under a change of the stability condition.

5.1   Stack function invariants

In the following, we use the notion of
stack functions

due to Joyce
[
29
]
.
See
Appendix

A
for an introduction to stack functions.

5.1.1. The stack functions
\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)
.

Let us recall Joyce’s construction

[
28
, Definition 8.1]

of the stack functions

\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
representing motives that count

\tau
-semistable objects of a fixed class
\alpha
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category,
with a categorical moduli stack
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
,
satisfying
and
2.6.6
.
Let
\tau
be a weak stability condition on
\mathcal{A}
,
satisfying
.

For each
\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A})
, define an element

\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})

(5.1)

to be the characteristic function of the open substack

\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha}(\tau)\subset\mathcal{M}
,
that is,
\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)=[(\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha}(\tau),\,i)]
,
where
i\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha}(\tau)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}
is the inclusion.

Define an element

\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})
by

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[47.83347pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-4.52083pt]
&n>0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}\,,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tau(\alpha_{n})\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}\hskip 5.0pt\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}\cdot\delta_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)*\cdots*\delta_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\ ,

(5.2)

as in
[
28
, Definition 8.1]
,
where
*
is the multiplication in the motivic Hall algebra.
The sum in
Eq.

5.2
has only finitely many non-zero terms
by
2.5.5
and
[
28
, Proposition 4.9]
,
whose proof can be adapted to exact categories.
Formally inverting
Eq.

5.2
,
as in
[
28
, Theorem 8.2]
,
we have

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[47.83347pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-4.52083pt]
&n>0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}\,,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tau(\alpha_{n})\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}\hskip 5.0pt\frac{1}{n!}\cdot\epsilon_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)*\cdots*\epsilon_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\ ,

(5.3)

where the sum has only finitely many non-zero terms.

5.1.2.

In more compact terms, for any
t\in T
,
if we define
\delta(\tau;t),\epsilon(\tau;t)\in\mathrm{LSF}(\mathcal{M})
by

\displaystyle\delta(\tau;t)=\delta_{0}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[31.88898pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha)=t\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)\ ,\hskip 20.00003pt\epsilon(\tau;t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[31.88898pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha)=t\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)\ ,

where
\delta_{0}
is as in
Definition

4.2.2
, then

\displaystyle\epsilon(\tau;t)

\displaystyle=\log\delta(\tau;t)\ ,

(5.4)

\displaystyle\delta(\tau;t)

\displaystyle=\exp\epsilon(\tau;t)\ ,

(5.5)

where ‘
\log
’ and ‘
\exp
’ are interpreted as
formal power series using the multiplication
*
.

Remark 5.1.3
.

In the situation above,
if we drop the assumption
2.5.5
,
then there are still well-defined elements

\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)\in\mathrm{LSF}(\mathcal{M})
,
defined in the same way as above.
However, the elements
\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)

may not be well-defined, even in
\mathrm{LSF}(\mathcal{M})
,
as the sum in
Eq.

5.2
may not converge
in the sense of
A.2.8
;
a counterexample is given by trivial stability
on the category of coherent sheaves on a curve.

5.1.4. The stack functions
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,
\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
.

We now define self-dual versions of the

\delta
and
\epsilon
stack functions,
which represent motives that count

\tau
-semistable self-dual objects of a fixed class
\theta
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a self-dual
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category,
with a self-dual moduli stack
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
,
satisfying
and
2.6.6
.
Let
\tau
be a self-dual weak stability condition on
\mathcal{A}
,
satisfying
.

For each
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
,
define an element

\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})

(5.6)

to be the characteristic function
of the open substack
\smash{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{ss}}}_{\theta}(\tau)}\subset\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
.

Define an element

\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
by

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[47.83347pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-4.52083pt]
&n\geqslant 0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A}),\,\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tau(\alpha_{n})=0\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}\binom{-1/2}{n}\cdot\delta_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\diamond\cdots\diamond\delta_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\diamond\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tau)\ ,

(5.7)

where
\diamond
is the multiplication in the motivic Hall module,
and there are only finitely many non-zero terms
by the proof of
[
28
, Proposition 4.9]
.
Formally inverting
Eq.

5.7
, we have

\displaystyle\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[47.83347pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-4.52083pt]
&n\geqslant 0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A}),\,\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tau(\alpha_{n})=0\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}\frac{1}{2^{n}\,n!}\cdot\epsilon_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\diamond\cdots\diamond\epsilon_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\diamond\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tau)\ ,

(5.8)

where only finitely many terms are non-zero.
Indeed,
Eq.

5.8
follows from the following.

5.1.5.

Define elements
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\tau),\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\tau)\in\mathrm{LSF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
by

\displaystyle\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\tau)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[31.88898pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\vphantom{{}^{0}}\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\ ,\hskip 20.00003pt\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\tau)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[31.88898pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\vphantom{{}^{0}}\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\ ,

then
Eqs.

5.7
to
5.8

translate to

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\delta(\tau;0)^{-1/2}\diamond\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\tau)\ ,

(5.9)

\displaystyle\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\exp\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\,\epsilon(\tau;0)\Bigr)\diamond\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\tau)\ ,

(5.10)

where
(-)^{-1/2}
and ‘
\exp
’ are interpreted
as formal power series using the multiplication
*
.
Now, using
Eqs.

5.4
to
5.5
,
it is straightforward to see that

Eqs.

5.9
and
5.10

are equivalent.

Remark 5.1.6
.

The choice of the coefficients
\binom{-1/2}{n}

in the definition of

\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
in
Eq.

5.7

is justified by the fact that
it is the unique choice
only depending on
n
, such that
the
no-pole theorem
,

5.6.4
below, holds.

Remark 5.1.7
.

As in
Remark

5.1.3
,
if we drop the assumption
2.5.5
,
then there are still well-defined elements

\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in\mathrm{LSF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
,
but the elements
\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

may not be well-defined in
\mathrm{LSF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
.

5.2   Wall-crossing: Preparation

5.2.1.

We now study the change of the stack function invariants

\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)
,

\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,
\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

when we alter the weak stability condition
\tau
.
We express the change in terms of
wall-crossing formulae
.
This was done for ordinary invariants in
Joyce
[
30
, §5]
,
and we now give a parallel theory for self-dual invariants.

Throughout this section,

\mathcal{A}
will be a self-dual
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact category,
with a self-dual moduli stack
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
,
satisfying
and
,
with the understanding that for results that
do not involve the self-dual structure of
\mathcal{A}
,
the category
\mathcal{A}
need not be self-dual,
and only needs to satisfy
and
.

Definition 5.2.2
.

Let
\tau
and
\tilde{\tau}
be weak stability conditions on
\mathcal{A}
.
As in
[
30
, Definition 3.16]
,
we say that
\tilde{\tau}

dominates

\tau
,
if
\tau(\alpha)\leqslant\tau(\beta)

implies
\tilde{\tau}(\alpha)\leqslant\tilde{\tau}(\beta)

for any
\alpha,\beta\in C(\mathcal{A})
.

5.2.3.

Here is the first version of wall-crossing formulae,
which is the special case when one stability condition dominates another.
We assume the setting of
5.2.1
.

Theorem 0
.

Let
\tau,\tilde{\tau}
be self-dual weak stability conditions on
\mathcal{A}

satisfying
2.5.5
,
and suppose that
\tilde{\tau}
dominates
\tau
.

Then for any
\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A})

and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}(\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[59.11185pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &n>0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}\,,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{n}),\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})>\cdots>\tau(\alpha_{n})\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}\delta_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)*\cdots*\delta_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\ ,

(5.11)

\displaystyle\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[59.11185pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &n\geqslant 0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A}),\,\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\vphantom{{}^{0}}\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{n})=0,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})>\cdots>\tau(\alpha_{n})>0\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}\delta_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\diamond\cdots\diamond\delta_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\diamond\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tau)

(5.12)

in
\mathrm{LSF}(\mathcal{M})
and
\mathrm{LSF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
, respectively,
where the sums may be infinite,
but they converge in the sense of
A.2.8
.

Proof 16
.

Equation
Eq.

5.11

was proved in
[
30
, Theorem 5.11]
,
but we repeat the argument here,
fixing a gap in the original argument using
Theorem

A.1.9
.

First, the
*
and
\diamond
products are well-defined by

Remarks

4.2.4
and
4.3.4
.
Every finite type substack of
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}
or
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}

only intersects the support of finitely many terms in the sum,
by
2.5.5
.

By
Theorem

2.4.4
, the morphism

\displaystyle\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[50.6673pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &n>0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}\,,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{n}),\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})>\cdots>\tau(\alpha_{n})\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(n)}},{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}}_{\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha}(\tilde{\tau})\ ,

(5.13)

given by
\pi^{\smash{(n)}}
on each component,
is bijective on
\mathbb{K}
-points,
and induces isomorphisms of stabilizer groups at
\mathbb{K}
-points.
Applying
Theorem

A.1.9
Zariski locally,
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[50.6673pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &n>0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}\,,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{n}),\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})>\cdots>\tau(\alpha_{n})\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}(\pi^{\smash{(n)}})_{!}\,[\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(n)}},{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}}_{\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}}(\tau)]=\delta_{\alpha}(\tilde{\tau})

(5.14)

in
\mathrm{LSF}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha})
.
Now,
Eq.

5.11
follows from

Eq.

5.14
and
Theorem

4.2.3
.

For
Eq.

5.12
,
we use a similar strategy.
By
Theorem

3.4.5
, the morphism

\displaystyle\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[50.6673pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &n\geqslant 0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A}),\,\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\vphantom{{}^{0}}\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{n})=0,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})>\cdots>\tau(\alpha_{n})>0\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(2n+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{ss}}}}_{\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n},\rho}(\tau)\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{ss}}}_{\theta}(\tilde{\tau})\ ,

(5.15)

given by
\pi^{{\smash{(2n+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}
on each component,
is bijective on
\mathbb{K}
-points,
and induces isomorphisms of stabilizer groups at
\mathbb{K}
-points.
Applying
Theorem

A.1.9
Zariski locally,
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[50.6673pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &n\geqslant 0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A}),\,\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\vphantom{{}^{0}}\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{n})=0,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})>\cdots>\tau(\alpha_{n})>0\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}(\pi^{{\smash{(2n+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}})_{!}\,[\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(2n+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{ss}}}}_{\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n},\rho}(\tau)]=\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tilde{\tau})

(5.16)

in
\mathrm{LSF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta})
.
Now,
Eq.

5.12
follows from

Eq.

5.16
and
Theorem

4.3.3
.

\Box

5.2.5.

Formally inverting the dominant wall-crossing formulae

Eqs.

5.11
to
5.12
,
we obtain the following ‘anti-dominant’ wall-crossing formulae,
which is a step towards the general case.

Theorem 0
.

Let
\tau,\tilde{\tau}
be self-dual weak stability conditions on
\mathcal{A}

satisfying
2.5.5
,
and suppose that
\tilde{\tau}
dominates
\tau
.

Then for any
\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A})

and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
, the equations

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[47.83347pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &n>0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}\,,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{n}),\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{i})>\tau(\alpha_{i+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n})\text{ for all }i=1,\dotsc,n-1\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}(-1)^{n-1}\,\delta_{\alpha_{1}}(\tilde{\tau})*\cdots*\delta_{\alpha_{n}}(\tilde{\tau})\ ,

(5.17)

\displaystyle\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[47.83347pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &n\geqslant 0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A}),\,\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{n})=0,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{i})>0\text{ for all }i=1,\dotsc,n\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}(-1)^{n}\,\delta_{\alpha_{1}}(\tilde{\tau})\diamond\cdots\diamond\delta_{\alpha_{n}}(\tilde{\tau})\diamond\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tilde{\tau})

(5.18)

hold whenever the sums
converge in the sense of
A.2.8
.

Proof 17
.

Equation
Eq.

5.17

was proved in
[
30
, Theorem 5.12]
,
by formally inverting
Eq.

5.11
.

One can also prove
Eq.

5.18

using a similar strategy.
Let
A_{\theta}
denote the right-hand side of
Eq.

5.18
.
Expanding it using
Eq.

5.11
and
Eq.

5.12
,
and collecting like terms, we obtain

\displaystyle A_{\theta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[59.11185pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &n\geqslant 0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A}),\,\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{n})=0\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}C(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})\cdot\delta_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\diamond\cdots\diamond\delta_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\diamond\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tau)\ ,

(5.19)

where

\displaystyle C(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[84.4455pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &m\geqslant 0,\ 0=a_{0}<\cdots<a_{m}\leqslant n\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{j})>\tau(\alpha_{j+1})\text{ for all }j\in\{1,\dotsc,n\}\setminus\{a_{1},\dotsc,a_{m}\},\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{a_{i}})>0\text{ for all }i=1,\dotsc,m\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}(-1)^{m}\ ,

where we define
\tau(\alpha_{n+1})=0
for convenience.

To prove that this step is valid,
we have to show that the sum
Eq.

5.19

is
locally finite
, that is, after restricting to any finite type substack,
only finitely many terms in the sum should be non-zero,
even before collecting like terms.
To prove this, it is enough to show that
each term in
Eq.

5.18

becomes a locally finite sum of terms in
Eq.

5.19
.
By
2.5.5
for
\tau
,
each
