\delta_{\alpha_{i}}(\tilde{\tau})
and
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tilde{\tau})

in
Eq.

5.18
split into locally finite sums.
By
2.6.6
and
Lemma

3.6.4
,
the sum is locally finite
after taking the
\diamond
product.

Now, the remaining task is to show that
C(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})=0

unless
n=0
.
Indeed, let us fix the classes
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}
,
with
n>0
. Define sets

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\{1,\dotsc,n\}\ ,

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\{j\in I\mid\tau(\alpha_{j})>\tau(\alpha_{j+1})\}\ ,

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\{j\in I\mid\tau(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{j})>0\}\ .

Then

\displaystyle C(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset I\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
I\setminus I_{1}\subset J\subset I_{2}\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}(-1)^{|J|}\ ,

which is non-zero precisely when
I_{2}=I\setminus I_{1}
.
Assume that this is the case.
Then if
1\in I_{1}
, then
1\notin I_{2}
,
so
\tau(\alpha_{1})\leqslant 0
, and so
\tau(\alpha_{2})<0
.
Continuing, we see that
0\geqslant\tau(\alpha_{1})>\tau(\alpha_{2})>\cdots
,
contradicting with the definition that
\tau(\alpha_{n+1})=0
.
Similarly, if
1\in I_{2}
, then

0<\tau(\alpha_{1})\leqslant\tau(\alpha_{2})\leqslant\cdots
,
contradicting again with
\tau(\alpha_{n+1})=0
.

Therefore, the only non-zero term in
Eq.

5.19

is the term with
n=0
,
which gives
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
, as desired.

\Box

5.3   Wall-crossing: The general case

5.3.1.

We are now ready to prove the general wall-crossing formulae
for the stack function invariants

\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)
,

\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,
\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
.
The strategy is to compose the
dominant wall-crossing formulae
Eqs.

5.11
to
5.12

with the anti-dominant wall-crossing formulae

Eqs.

5.17
to
5.18
.
The ordinary case was done by Joyce
[
30
, §5]
,
while the self-dual case is new.
The main result is
Theorem

5.3.5
.

Throughout, we assume the same setting as in
5.2.1
,
that is, we assume
and
.

Definition 5.3.2
.

In the setting of
5.2.1
,
let
\tau,\tilde{\tau}
be weak stability conditions on
\mathcal{A}
.
For classes
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A})
, define

\displaystyle\hskip-10.00002ptS(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1,&\tau(\alpha_{i})>\tau(\alpha_{i+1})\text{ and }\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{i})\leqslant\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{i+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n})\\
-1,&\tau(\alpha_{i})\leqslant\tau(\alpha_{i+1})\text{ and }\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{i})>\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{i+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n})\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right\},\hskip-10.00002pt

\displaystyle\hskip-10.00002ptS^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1,&\tau(\alpha_{i})>\tau(\alpha_{i+1})\text{ and }\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{i})\leqslant 0\\
-1,&\tau(\alpha_{i})\leqslant\tau(\alpha_{i+1})\text{ and }\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{i})>0\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right\},\hskip-10.00002pt

where we set
\tau(\alpha_{n+1})=0
.
Write
\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}
.
Define

U(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})=\\
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[55.80571pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-1.50694pt]
&0=a_{0}<\cdots<a_{m}=n,\ 0=b_{0}<\cdots<b_{l}=m.\\[-1.50694pt]
&\text{Define }\beta_{1},\dotsc,\beta_{m}\text{ by }\beta_{i}=\alpha_{a_{i-1}+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{a_{i}}\,.\\[-1.50694pt]
&\text{Define }\gamma_{1},\dotsc,\gamma_{l}\text{ by }\gamma_{i}=\beta_{b_{i-1}+1}+\cdots+\beta_{b_{i}}\,.\\[-1.50694pt]
&\text{We require }\tau(\beta_{i})=\tau(\alpha_{j})\text{ for all }a_{i-1}<j\leqslant a_{i}\,,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\text{and }\tilde{\tau}(\gamma_{i})=\tilde{\tau}(\alpha)\text{ for all }i=1,\dotsc,l\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}\frac{(-1)^{l-1}}{l}\cdot\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{l}S(\beta_{b_{i-1}+1},\dotsc,\beta_{b_{i}};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\biggr)\cdot\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{1}{(a_{i}-a_{i-1})!}\biggr)\ ,\hskip-20.00003pt\\[-21.52771pt]
\mathstrut

(5.28)

U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})=\\
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[55.80571pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-1.50694pt]
&0=a_{0}<\cdots<a_{m}\leqslant n,\ 0=b_{0}<\cdots<b_{l}\leqslant m.\\[-1.50694pt]
&\text{Define }\beta_{1},\dotsc,\beta_{m}\text{ by }\beta_{i}=\alpha_{a_{i-1}+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{a_{i}}\,.\\[-1.50694pt]
&\text{Define }\gamma_{1},\dotsc,\gamma_{l}\text{ by }\gamma_{i}=\beta_{b_{i-1}+1}+\cdots+\beta_{b_{i}}\,.\\[-1.50694pt]
&\text{We require }\tau(\beta_{i})=\tau(\alpha_{j})\text{ for all }a_{i-1}<j\leqslant a_{i}\,,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{j})=0\text{ for all }j>a_{m}\,,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\text{and }\tilde{\tau}(\gamma_{i})=0\text{ for all }i=1,\dotsc,l\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}\binom{-1/2}{l}\cdot\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{l}S(\beta_{b_{i-1}+1},\dotsc,\beta_{b_{i}};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\biggr)\cdot S^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\beta_{b_{l}+1},\dotsc,\beta_{m};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot{}\\[-47.36096pt]
\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{1}{(a_{i}-a_{i-1})!}\biggr)\cdot\frac{1}{2^{n-a_{m}}\,(n-a_{m})!}\ .\hskip-20.00003pt\\
\mathstrut

(5.29)

Here,
Definitions

5.3.2
and
5.28
are as in Joyce
[
30
, §4.1]
;

Definitions

5.3.2
and
5.29
are new.

Definition 5.3.3
.

In the setting of
5.2.1
,
let
\tau,\tilde{\tau}
be weak stability conditions on
\mathcal{A}
,
satisfying
2.5.5
.

As in
[
30
, Definition 5.1]
,
we say that
the change from
\tau
to
\tilde{\tau}
is locally finite
,
if for any
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
,
any finite type
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
U
, and any morphism
f\colon U\to\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}
,
there only exist finitely many choices of
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A})
,
with
\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}
,

S(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\neq 0
,
and
f^{-1}(\pi^{{\smash{(n)}}}(\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(n)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}}_{\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}}(\tau)))\neq\varnothing
.

We say that
the change from
\tau
to
\tilde{\tau}
is globally finite
,
if for any
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
,
there only exist finitely many choices of
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A})
,
with
\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}
,

S(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\neq 0
,
and
\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha_{i}}(\tau)\neq\varnothing
for all
i
.

In both cases, it is also true that for any
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
,
there only exist finitely many choices of
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A})

and
\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
,
with
\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho
,

S^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\neq 0
,
such that the corresponding semistable moduli stacks
(pulled back along
f
in the first case) are non-empty.
This is because

S^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\neq 0
if and only if

S(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n},\rho,\alpha_{n}^{\vee},\dotsc,\alpha_{1}^{\vee};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\neq 0
, where
\rho
is omitted if it is zero.

For example, when considering coherent sheaves on a higher dimensional variety,
the change from the trivial stability condition to Gieseker stability
may fail to be locally finite. But, as in Joyce
[
30
, Proposition 5.7]
,
the change from
purity
to Gieseker stability is locally finite.
Note, however, that Gieseker stability is not self-dual.

5.3.4.

From now on, we assume the following setting.
In the setting of
5.2.1
,
including the conditions
and
,
let
\tau,\tilde{\tau},\hat{\tau}
be self-dual weak stability conditions on
\mathcal{A}

satisfying
2.5.5
.
Suppose that
\tau
and
\tilde{\tau}
are dominated by
\hat{\tau}
,
and that the change from
\hat{\tau}
to
\tilde{\tau}
is locally finite,
in the sense of
Definition

5.3.3
.

We state the general wall-crossing formulae
for the stack function invariants as follows.

Theorem 5.3.5
.

In the setting of
5.3.4
,
for any
\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A})
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
,

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}(\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[42.22275pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &n>0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}S(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\delta_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)*\cdots*\delta_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\ ,

(5.30)

\displaystyle\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[42.22275pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &n\geqslant 0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A}),\,\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}S^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\delta_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\diamond\cdots\diamond\delta_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\diamond\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tau)\ ,

(5.31)

where the sums converge in the sense of
A.2.8
.
If, moreover,
\tau
and
\tilde{\tau}
satisfy
2.5.5
, then

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[42.22275pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &n>0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}U(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\epsilon_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)*\cdots*\epsilon_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\ ,

(5.32)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[42.22275pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &n\geqslant 0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A}),\,\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\epsilon_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\diamond\cdots\diamond\epsilon_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\diamond\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tau)\ ,

(5.33)

where the sums converge.

Proof 18
.

Equation
Eq.

5.30

was shown in
[
30
, Theorem 5.2]
,
as a result of composing

Eq.

5.17
with
Eq.

5.11
.

For
Eq.

5.31
,
we use a similar strategy by expanding
the left-hand side using

Eq.

5.18
,
which expresses everything in terms of
\hat{\tau}
,
and then expanding everything again using

Eq.

5.11
and
Eq.

5.12
.
Collecting like terms, this gives

\displaystyle\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[42.22275pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &n\geqslant 0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A}),\,\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}C(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})\cdot\delta_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\diamond\cdots\diamond\delta_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\diamond\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tau)\ ,

(5.34)

where

\displaystyle C(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[84.4455pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &m\geqslant 0,\ 0=a_{0}<\cdots<a_{m}\leqslant n\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{j})>\tau(\alpha_{j+1})\text{ for all }j\in\{1,\dotsc,n\}\setminus\{a_{1},\dotsc,a_{m}\},\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{a_{i}})>0\text{ for all }i=1,\dotsc,m\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}(-1)^{m}\ ,

where we write
\tau(\alpha_{n+1})=0
as before.
This step is valid by a similar argument
as in the proof of
5.2.5
,
involving the local finiteness condition.

Define sets

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\{1,\dotsc,n\}\ ,

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\{j\in I\mid\tau(\alpha_{j})>\tau(\alpha_{j+1})\}\ ,

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\{j\in I\mid\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{j})>0\}\ .

Then

\displaystyle C(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset I\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
I\setminus I_{1}\subset J\subset I_{2}\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}(-1)^{|J|}\ ,

which is non-zero precisely when
I_{2}=I\setminus I_{1}
,
in which case it is equal to
(-1)^{|I_{2}|}
.
This agrees with the definition of
S^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})
.

Equation
Eq.

5.32

was shown in
[
30
, Theorem 5.2]
,
as a result of composing

Eq.

5.30
with

Eqs.

5.2
and
5.3
.
Similarly,
Eq.

5.33

follows from
Eq.

5.31

by composing it with

Eqs.

5.7
,
5.3
, and
5.8
.

\Box

Remark 5.3.6
.

In
Theorem

5.3.5
,
although
\hat{\tau}
does not appear anywhere
in the wall-crossing formulae,
it is important that it exists,
satisfies
2.5.5
,
and satisfies the condition
that the change from
\hat{\tau}
to
\tilde{\tau}
is locally finite,
as these conditions ensure that the wall-crossing formulae
in
Theorem

5.3.5
converge.

5.4   Wall-crossing: Lie algebras and twisted modules

5.4.1.

Having obtained the general wall-crossing formulae,

Theorem

5.3.5
,
we now show that wall-crossing formulae for the

\epsilon
and
\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
functions
can be expressed solely in terms of
the Lie algebra structure on
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})
,
defined in
4.2.7
,
and the twisted module structure on
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
,
defined in
4.3.5
.
The main result is
Theorem

5.4.6
.

Our running assumptions will now include

,
,
and self-dual weak stability conditions
\tau,\tilde{\tau},\hat{\tau}

satisfying conditions in
5.3.4
,
with
\hat{\tau}
satisfying
2.5.5
,
and
\tau,\tilde{\tau}
satisfying
.

5.4.2.

The ordinary case of this result
was proved in
[
30
, Theorem 5.4]
,
and we state it below.

Theorem 0
.

In the setting of
5.4.1
,
for classes
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A}),

consider the free Lie algebra
F_{n}
over
\mathbb{Q},
generated by symbols

e_{1},\dotsc,e_{n}
. Let
U(F_{n})
be its universal enveloping algebra,
whose multiplication is denoted by
*
.
Then the element

\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}U(\alpha_{\sigma(1)},\dotsc,\alpha_{\sigma(n)};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot e_{\sigma(1)}*\cdots*e_{\sigma(n)}\in U(F_{n})

(5.35)

lies in the subspace
F_{n}\subset U(F_{n}),

where
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
is the symmetric group.
Consequently, there exist coefficients

\tilde{U}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\in\mathbb{Q}\ ,

such that
Eq.

5.35
can be rewritten as

\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\tilde{U}(\alpha_{\sigma(1)},\dotsc,\alpha_{\sigma(n)};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\bigl[\dotsc\bigl[\bigl[e_{\sigma(1)}\,,\,e_{\sigma(2)}\bigr],\dotsc\bigr],e_{\sigma(n)}\bigr]\ .

(5.36)

In particular, for any
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A}),

we have the wall-crossing formula

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[50.6673pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-9.04166pt]
&n>0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}\tilde{U}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\bigl[\dotsc\bigl[\bigl[\epsilon_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau),\epsilon_{\alpha_{2}}(\tau)\bigr],\dotsc\bigr],\epsilon_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\bigr]\ ,

where the Lie brackets are as in
4.2.7
,
and the sum converges in the sense of
A.2.8
.

Note that the coefficients
\tilde{U}({\cdots})
above
are not necessarily unique.

5.4.4.

We now state the self-dual counterpart of
5.4.2
,
as
Theorem

5.4.6
below,
expressing wall-crossing formulae for the
\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
functions
in terms of the Lie algebra structure on
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})

and the twisted module structure on
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
.

Definition 0
.

Let
n\geqslant 0
be an integer,
and let
L_{n}
be the free Lie algebra over
\mathbb{Q}
, generated by symbols

e_{1},e_{1}^{\vee},\dotsc,e_{n},e_{n}^{\vee}
.
Define an involution
(-)^{\vee}
on
L_{n}

by exchanging
e_{i}
and
e_{i}^{\vee}
for all
i,

and sending
[x,y]
to
[y^{\vee},x^{\vee}]
for all
x,y\in L_{n}
.

Let
L_{n}^{+}\subset L_{n}
be the subalgebra as in
4.1.3
.
Define a map
(-)^{+}\colon L_{n}\to L_{n}^{+}
by
x\mapsto x-x^{\vee}
.

Let
P_{n}
be the set of sequences
x=(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})

of elements of
L_{n}
,
such that for some
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, we have

x_{\sigma(i)}\in\{e_{i},e_{i}^{\vee}\}
for all
i
.

Let
Q_{n}
be the set of lists
y=(y_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k,\,1\leqslant j\leqslant m_{i}}
,
where
k\geqslant 0
and
m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k}\geqslant 1
are integers,
with
m_{1}+\cdots+m_{k}=n
,
such that each
y_{i,j}
is equal to some
e_{l}
or
e_{l}^{\vee}
,
where
l\in\{1,\dotsc,n\}
,
and for each such
l
, there is a unique
y_{i,j}
that is equal to
e_{l}
or
e_{l}^{\vee}
.

Theorem 5.4.6
.

In the setting of
5.4.1
,
for any
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A}),

the element

\sum_{x\in P_{n}}U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{\sigma(1)},\dotsc,\alpha_{\sigma(n)};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot x_{1}*\cdots*x_{n}\in U(L_{n})

(5.38)

lies in
U(L_{n}^{+}),

where notations are as in
5.4.4
.
Consequently,
there exist coefficients

\tilde{U}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{1,m_{1}};\dotsc;\alpha_{k,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{k,m_{k}};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\in\mathbb{Q}\ ,

for all lists of classes
\alpha_{1,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{1,m_{1}};\dotsc;\alpha_{n,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n,m_{n}}\in C(\mathcal{A}),

such that
Eq.

5.38
can be rewritten as

\sum_{y\in Q_{n}}\tilde{U}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{y};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\bigl[\bigl[y_{1,1},\dotsc\bigr],y_{1,m_{1}}\bigr]^{+}*\cdots*\bigl[\bigl[y_{k,1},\dotsc\bigr],y_{k,m_{k}}\bigr]^{+}\ ,

(5.39)

where
\alpha_{y}
is the list obtained from
y

by replacing each
e_{l},e^{\vee}_{l}
with
\alpha_{l},\alpha^{\vee}_{l},
respectively.

In particular, for any
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}),

we have the wall-crossing formula

\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tilde{\tau})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[84.4455pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-9.04166pt]
&n\geqslant 0;\,m_{1},\dotsc,m_{n}>0;\\[-1.50694pt]
&\alpha_{1,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{1,m_{1}};\dotsc;\alpha_{n,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n,m_{n}}\in C(\mathcal{A});\,\rho\in\smash{C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})}\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=(\bar{\alpha}_{1,1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{1,m_{1}})+\cdots+(\bar{\alpha}_{n,1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n,m_{n}})+\rho\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}\tilde{U}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{1,m_{1}};\dotsc;\alpha_{n,1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n,m_{n}};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot{}\\[4.30554pt]
\bigl[\bigl[\epsilon_{\alpha_{1,1}}(\tau),\dotsc\bigr],\epsilon_{\alpha_{1,m_{1}}}(\tau)\bigr]\mathbin{\heartsuit}\cdots\mathbin{\heartsuit}\bigl[\bigl[\epsilon_{\alpha_{n,1}}(\tau),\dotsc\bigr],\epsilon_{\alpha_{n,m_{n}}}(\tau)\bigr]\mathbin{\heartsuit}\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tau)\ ,

(5.40)

where the Lie brackets and the operation
\mathbin{\heartsuit}

are defined in
4.2.7
and
4.3.5
,
and the sum converges in the sense of
A.2.8
.

The proof will be given in
Appendix

D
.
Again, note that the coefficients
\tilde{U}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}({\cdots})
above
are not necessarily unique.

5.5   Motivic invariants

5.5.1.

We now define
motivic enumerative invariants

in a self-dual category, which is one of the main goals of this paper.
This is done by applying
motivic integration
,
as in
A.2.7
, to the stack function invariants.
We will define the following types of motivic invariants:

•

Motivic invariants
\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\tau),\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}
,
defined as the integral of the
\delta
and
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
functions,
representing motives of the semistable moduli stacks.

•

Motivic invariants
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau),\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}
,
defined as the integral of the
\epsilon
and
\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
functions,
representing weighted motives of the semistable moduli stacks.
They have the additional property that they
satisfy the
no-pole theorems
,

5.6.2
and
5.6.4
,
which allow us to define their Euler characteristics,
as we will do in
Section

5.6
below.

The invariants
\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\tau)
and
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau)
were defined in
Joyce
[
30
, Definitions 6.1 and 6.7]
,
while the invariants
\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
and
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
are new.

Throughout, we assume the conditions

and
2.6.6
.

5.5.2. Definition.

Let
\tau
be a self-dual weak stability condition on
\mathcal{A}
,
satisfying
.
For classes
\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A})
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
,
define invariants

\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\tau)\ ,\quad\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\ ,\quad\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau)\ ,\quad\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\ \in\ {\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}

by the motivic integrals

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}}\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)\ ,

\displaystyle\qquad\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}}\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)\ ,

(5.41)

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}}\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\ ,

\displaystyle\qquad\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}}\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\ ,

(5.42)

as in
A.2.7
,
where the integrands were defined in
Section

5.1
.

5.5.3.

One can deduce the relations

\displaystyle\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[47.83347pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-4.52083pt]
&n>0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}\,,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tau(\alpha_{n})\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}\cdot\mathbb{L}^{-\chi(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})}\cdot\mathrm{I}_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\cdots\mathrm{I}_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\ ,

(5.43)

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[47.83347pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-4.52083pt]
&n>0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}\,,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tau(\alpha_{n})\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{n!}\cdot\mathbb{L}^{-\chi(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})}\cdot\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\cdots\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\ ,

(5.44)

\displaystyle\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[47.83347pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-4.52083pt]
&n\geqslant 0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A}),\,\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tau(\alpha_{n})=0\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}\binom{-1/2}{n}\cdot\mathbb{L}^{-\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n},\rho)}\cdot\mathrm{I}_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\cdots\mathrm{I}_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\cdot\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tau)\ ,

(5.45)

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[47.83347pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-4.52083pt]
&n\geqslant 0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A}),\,\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tau(\alpha_{n})=0\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}\frac{1}{2^{n}\,n!}\cdot\mathbb{L}^{-\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n},\rho)}\cdot\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\cdots\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\cdot\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tau)\ ,

(5.46)

where
\chi(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})
and

\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n},\rho)

are given by
Eqs.

4.27
and
4.28
.
These relations follow from
Eq.

5.2
,

Eq.

5.3
,

Eq.

5.7
, and

Eq.

5.8
, respectively,
using
Theorem

4.4.6
and

Eqs.

4.29
to
4.30
.

5.6   Numerical invariants

5.6.1.

Next, we define
numerical enumerative invariants

by taking the Euler characteristics
of the
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}
and
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
invariants in
Section

5.5
,
coming from the
\epsilon
and
\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
functions
defined in
Section

.
The fact that this is well-defined relies on the

no-pole theorems

5.6.2
and
5.6.4
,
which do not hold for the
\delta,\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
functions.
We will define the following invariants:

•

Numerical invariants

\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}_{\alpha}(\tau),\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in\mathbb{Q}
,
defined as the Euler characteristics of the

\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}
and
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
invariants.

•

Numerical invariants

\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\textnormal{na\"{\i}}}}_{\alpha}(\tau),\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\textnormal{na\"{\i}}}}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in\mathbb{Q}
,
which only differ from the
\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}
and
\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
invariants
by a sign.

Throughout, we assume the conditions

and
2.6.6
,
as in
5.5.1
.
We also assume that
\mathcal{A}
satisfies
,
which is important for the no-pole theorems.

5.6.2. The no-pole theorem, ordinary case.

Joyce
[
28
, Theorem 8.7]

proved the following
no-pole theorem
for the
stack function
\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)
and the invariant
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
stating that the motive
(\mathbb{L}-1)\cdot\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau)

has no poles at
\mathbb{L}=1
.

Theorem 0
.

In the situation of
5.6.1
,
let
\tau
be a weak stability condition on
\mathcal{A}

satisfying
.
Then for any
\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A})
, the element

\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha})

has pure virtual rank
1
, in the sense of
Section

A.3
.
In particular, we have

(\mathbb{L}-1)\cdot\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau)\in{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}\ .

(5.51)

5.6.4. The no-pole theorem, self-dual case.

We state the following no-pole theorem
for the stack function
\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

and the invariant
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
.
This is one of the main results in this paper,
and the proof will be given in
Appendix

E
.

Theorem 0
.

In the situation of
5.6.1
,
let
\tau
be a self-dual weak stability condition on
\mathcal{A}

satisfying
.
Then for any
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
, the element

\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta})

has pure virtual rank
0
, in the sense of
Section

A.3
.
In particular, we have

\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}\ .

(5.52)

5.6.6. The Euler characteristic.

Let
\chi\colon{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}\to\mathbb{Q}
be the Euler characteristic map,
which can be defined first on
K_{0}(\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{K}})
,
then extended to
{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}
by setting
\chi(\mathbb{L})=1
.
The map
\chi
factors through the ring
{\smash{\widetilde{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}

in
Definition

A.1.2
.

The symbol
\chi
here is not to be confused with
the Euler form
\chi(-,-)
defined in
2.7.6
,
which will also appear in the following.

Alternatively,
\chi
can be taken to be any map
with
\chi(\mathbb{L})=1
,
as discussed in Joyce
[
29
, Examples 6.2–6.4]
,
and the theory should still work,
but we do not make this generalization here.

5.6.7. Numerical invariants.

In the situation of
5.6.1
,
let
\tau
be a self-dual weak stability condition on
\mathcal{A}

satisfying
.

For classes
\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A})
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
,
define the numerical enumerative invariants

\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}_{\alpha}(\tau)\in\mathbb{Q}
and

\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in\mathbb{Q}

as the Euler characteristics,

\displaystyle\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\chi((\mathbb{L}-1)\cdot\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau))=\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}}(\mathbb{L}-1)\,\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)\,d\chi\ ,

(5.53)

\displaystyle\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\chi(\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau))=\int_{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}}\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\,d\chi\ .

(5.54)

These are well-defined by the no-pole theorems,

5.6.2
and
5.6.4
.

Define the
naïve Donaldson–Thomas invariants

\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\textnormal{na\"{\i}}}}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,

\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\textnormal{na\"{\i}}}}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in\mathbb{Q}
by

\displaystyle\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\textnormal{na\"{\i}}}}_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle=-(-1)^{\chi(\alpha,\alpha)}\cdot\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}_{\alpha}(\tau)\ ,

(5.55)

\displaystyle\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\textnormal{na\"{\i}}}}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(j(\theta),0)}\cdot\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\ .

(5.56)

Remark 5.6.8
.

We use the name
naïve Donaldson–Thomas invariants
,
because it is only conceptually correct for smooth moduli stacks.
In general, one needs to introduce a weighting by the

Behrend function
,
as in Joyce–Song
[
32
]
,
to obtain the correct definition of the Donaldson–Thomas invariants.
In the smooth case, the Behrend function is a constant function
equal to
\pm 1
, hence the signs in

Eqs.

5.55
to
5.56
.
We hope to explore the weighted version in the future.

5.6.9. Wall-crossing.

We also write down wall-crossing formulae

Theorem 0
.

In the situation of

\displaystyle\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}_{\alpha}(\tilde{\tau})

where

and

and
4.28
,

\bar{\chi}
in place of
\chi
.

\tilde{\chi}({\cdots})

4.50
and

.

Proof 19
.

Note that we have the relations

\displaystyle((\mathbb{L}-1)\cdot\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau))\cdot\omega_{\alpha}

(5.61)

\displaystyle\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\cdot\omega^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}

\displaystyle\sim\Xi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\circ\Psi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau))\ ,

(5.62)

in
\Omega(\mathcal{A})
and

Section 6Self-dual quivers

In this
Section
, we study enumerative invariants counting

self-dual representations
of a
self-dual quiver
,
which is one of the main examples of our theory.

The notion of a self-dual quiver was introduced by
Derksen and Weyman
[
14
]
,
and studied by Young
[
62
,
63
]

in the context of enumerative geometry.
They are a reasonable notion of a
G
-quiver
,
where
G
is a type B/C/D algebraic group.
Self-dual representations of a self-dual quiver
are thus analogous to principal
G
-bundles for such
G
.

We start by giving some basic definitions,
and then we verify the various assumptions needed for our theory.
We also provide an algorithm to compute our invariants,
and we carry out the computation in some examples.

6.1   Self-dual quivers

6.1.1. Quivers.

A
quiver
is a quadruple
(Q_{0},Q_{1},s,t)
,
where
Q_{0}\,,Q_{1}
are finite sets,
thought of as the sets of vertices and arrows,
and
s,t\colon Q_{1}\to Q_{0}
are maps,
sending each arrow to its source and target.

A
representation
of
Q
over
\mathbb{K}
is the data

E=((E_{i})_{i\in Q_{0}}\,,(e_{a})_{a\in Q_{1}})
,
where each
E_{i}
is a finite-dimensional
\mathbb{K}
-vector space,
and each
e_{a}\colon E_{s(a)}\to E_{t(a)}
is a linear map.
A
morphism
of representations
h\colon E\to F

is a collection of maps
(h_{i}\colon E_{i}\to F_{i})_{i\in Q_{0}}
,
such that
f_{a}\circ h_{s(a)}=h_{t(a)}\circ e_{a}

for all
a\in Q_{1}
.

Let
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}Q)
denote the
\mathbb{K}
-linear
abelian category of
Q
-representations over
\mathbb{K}
.
Here,
\mathbb{K}Q
is the
path algebra
of
Q
,
whose finite-dimensional representations
are the same as
Q
-representations over
\mathbb{K}
.

For the extra data as in
, define

C^{\circ}(Q)=\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}\ ,

and for each representation
E
of
Q
,
define
\llbr E\rrbr=\smash{(\dim E_{i})_{i\in Q_{0}}}\in C^{\circ}(Q)
.
Let
C(Q)=C^{\circ}(Q)\setminus\{0\}
.

The
opposite quiver
of
Q
is the quiver

Q^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}=(Q_{0},Q_{1},t,s)
,
which is the quiver obtained from
Q

by inverting the directions of all arrows.

6.1.2. Quivers with relations.

We take the following notion of a
quiver with relations

from Joyce
[
31
, §6]
.

A
quiver with relations
over
\mathbb{K}
is a tuple

\smash{\breve{Q}}=(Q_{0},Q_{1},Q_{2},s,t,p,q,r)
,
where
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},s,t)
is a quiver;

Q_{2}
is a finite set, interpreted as the set of relations;

p,q\colon Q_{2}\to Q_{0}
are maps,
indicating the source and target of paths on which the relations are to be imposed;

r\colon Q_{2}\to\mathbb{K}Q
is a map,
such that for any
b\in Q_{2}
, we have
r(b)\in\mathbb{K}Q_{p(b),q(b)}
,
the linear subspace in
\mathbb{K}Q
spanned by paths
from
p(b)
to
q(b)
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{K}Q
denote the two-sided ideal
generated by
r(b)
for
b\in Q_{2}
.

A
representation
of such a quiver with relations
is a finite-dimensional representation
of the
\mathbb{K}
-algebra
\mathbb{K}\breve{Q}=\mathbb{K}Q/I
.
Let
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q})
denote the

\mathbb{K}
-linear abelian category of representations of
\breve{Q}
.
It can be seen as a full subcategory of
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}Q)
.

The
opposite quiver with relations
of
\breve{Q}

is
\breve{Q}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}=(Q_{0},Q_{1},Q_{2},t,s,q,p,r^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}})
,
where
r^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\colon Q_{2}\to\mathbb{K}Q^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}

is the composition of
r
with the isomorphism of vector spaces
\mathbb{K}Q\simeq\mathbb{K}Q^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}

sending a path to its reverse path.

6.1.3. Self-dual quivers.

A
self-dual quiver

is a quadruple
(Q,\sigma,u,v)
, where

•

Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},s,t)
is a quiver.

•

\sigma=(\sigma_{i}\colon Q_{i}\to Q_{i})_{i=0,1}

consists of two maps,
such that
\sigma_{i}^{2}=\mathrm{id}_{\smash{Q_{i}}}
for
i=0,1
,

\sigma_{0}\circ s=t\circ\sigma_{1}
,
and
\sigma_{0}\circ t=s\circ\sigma_{1}
.
This is called a
contravariant involution
of
Q
,
and can be seen as an isomorphism
\sigma\colon Q\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}Q^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}
.
We also write these maps as
(-)^{\vee}\colon Q_{i}\to Q_{i}
for
i=0,1
.

•

u\colon Q_{0}\to\{\pm 1\}
and
v\colon Q_{1}\to\{\pm 1\}
are functions,
such that
u_{i}=u_{\smash{i^{\vee}}}
for all
i\in Q_{0}
,
and
v_{a}\,v_{\smash{a^{\vee}}}=u_{s(a)}\,u_{t(a)}

for all
a\in Q_{1}
.

6.1.4. Self-dual quivers with relations.

A
self-dual quiver with relations

is a quintuple
(\breve{Q},\sigma,u,v,w)
, where

•

\breve{Q}=(Q_{0},Q_{1},Q_{2},s,t,p,q,r)
is a quiver with relations.

•

\sigma=(\sigma_{i}\colon Q_{i}\to Q_{i})_{i=0,1,2}

consists of three maps,
such that
\sigma_{i}^{2}=\mathrm{id}_{\smash{Q_{i}}}
for all
i
,

\sigma_{0}\circ s=t\circ\sigma_{1}
,

\sigma_{0}\circ t=s\circ\sigma_{1}
,

\sigma_{0}\circ p=q\circ\sigma_{2}
,
and
\sigma_{0}\circ q=p\circ\sigma_{2}
.
These are also written as
(-)^{\vee}\colon Q_{i}\to Q_{i}
for
i=0,1,2
.

•

u\colon Q_{0}\to\{\pm 1\}
,
v\colon Q_{1}\to\{\pm 1\}
,
and
w\colon Q_{2}\to\{\pm 1\}
are functions,
such that
u,v
satisfy the conditions above,
and
w_{b}\,w_{b^{\vee}}=u_{p(b)}\,u_{q(b)}

for all
b\in Q_{2}
.
Furthermore, if
(-)^{\vee}\colon\mathbb{K}Q\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\mathbb{K}Q^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}

is the map that sends a path to its image under
\sigma
,
multiplied by the product of
v_{a}
for all edges
a
in that path,
then we require that
r(b)^{\vee}=w_{b}\cdot r^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}(b^{\vee})

for all
b\in Q_{2}
.

In particular, a self-dual quiver is also
a self-dual quiver with relations with
Q_{2}=\varnothing
.

6.1.5. The self-dual structure.

Let
(\breve{Q},\sigma,u,v,w)
be a self-dual quiver with relations.
Define a self-dual structure
on the
\mathbb{K}
-linear abelian category
\smash{\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q})}
as follows.

For a
\breve{Q}
-representation

E=(\smash{(E_{i})_{i\in Q_{0}}},\smash{(e_{a})_{a\in Q_{1}}})
,
define its dual representation

E^{\vee}=(\smash{(E^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in Q_{0}}},\smash{(e^{\prime}_{a})_{a\in Q_{1}}})

by

E^{\prime}_{i}=(E_{i^{\vee}})^{\vee}\ ,\qquad e^{\prime}_{a}=v_{a}\cdot(e_{a^{\vee}})^{\vee}\ ,

(6.1)

where the duals
(-)^{\vee}
outside the brackets
are the usual vector space duals.
Define the natural isomorphism
\eta_{E}\colon E^{\vee\vee}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}E
by

(\eta_{E})_{i}=u_{i}\cdot\mathrm{ev}_{i}^{-1}

(6.2)

for all
i\in Q_{0}
,
where
\mathrm{ev}_{i}\colon E_{i}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}E_{i}^{\vee\vee}

is the evaluation isomorphism.
This defines a self-dual structure on
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q})
.

A
self-dual representation
of
\breve{Q}

is a self-dual object in
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q})
.
If
(E,\phi)
is such a self-dual object,
then
E_{i^{\vee}}\simeq(E_{i})^{\vee}
for all
i\in Q_{0}
.
In particular, if
i=i^{\vee}
,
this isomorphism equips
E_{i}
with
an orthogonal or symplectic structure,
depending on the sign
u_{i}
.

Define

C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(Q)=\biggl\{\,\theta\in C^{\circ}(Q)\biggm|\begin{aligned} &\theta_{i}=\theta_{\smash{i^{\vee}}}\text{ for all }i,\\[-2.15277pt]
&2\mid\theta_{i}\text{ if }i=i^{\vee}\text{ and }u_{i}=-1\end{aligned}\,\biggr\}\ .

(6.3)

This is the set of all self-dual dimension vectors,
where the second line of the condition is due to the
non-existence of odd-dimensional symplectic vector spaces.

6.1.6. Stability functions.

We recall the notion of
stability functions

on a quiver, which is a class of stability conditions,
although not all stability conditions arise in this way.

Let
Q
be a quiver, possibly with relations.
A
stability function
on
Q
is a function

\tau\colon Q_{0}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}\ .

Given such a stability function, we can define
a stability condition on
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}Q)
,
denoted also by
\tau\colon C(Q)\to\mathbb{Q}
,
by setting

\tau(\alpha)=\frac{\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}\tau(i)\,\alpha_{i}}{\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}\alpha_{i}}

(6.4)

for all
\alpha\in C(Q)
.

Similarly, if
\breve{Q}
is a quiver with relations,
a
stability function
on
\breve{Q}

is a stability function on its underlying quiver,
and this defines a stability condition on
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q})
.

Now, suppose that
(Q,\sigma,u,v)
is a self-dual quiver.
We say that a stability function
\tau
on
Q
is
self-dual
, if

\tau(i^{\vee})=-\tau(i)

(6.5)

for all
i\in Q_{0}
.
In this case, the corresponding stability condition on
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}Q)

is a self-dual stability condition.

6.2   Verifying the assumptions

6.2.1.

Next, we verify the various assumptions
needed for our enumerative invariant theory.
These include the following:

•

and

for
\mathcal{A}=\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q})
,
where
\breve{Q}
is a quiver with relations.

•

for
\mathcal{A}=\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q})
,
where
\breve{Q}
is a self-dual quiver with relations.

•

for
\mathcal{A}=\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}Q)
,
where
Q
is a quiver, with no relations.

•

for
\mathcal{A}=\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}Q)
,
where
Q
is a self-dual quiver, with no relations.

We also verify

•

for any weak stability condition
\tau

on
\mathcal{A}=\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q})
, where
\breve{Q}
is a quiver with relations.

In particular, in the case of self-dual quivers with no relations,
all assumptions mentioned above are satisfied,
and hence, all the results in
Sections

4
to
5
apply.

When there are relations, the condition
,
and hence
, fails,
but all the invariants in
Section

5
are still well-defined,
as their definitions do not depend on this.
However, in this case,
the wall-crossing formulae may no longer hold.

6.2.2. Defining the moduli stacks.

Let
\breve{Q}
be a quiver with relations over
\mathbb{K}
,
and let
\mathcal{A}=\smash{\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q})}
.
We verify the conditions
and
.

For
, a categorical moduli stack
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
of objects in
\mathcal{A}

was constructed in
[
26
, §10.2]
,
and can be described as follows.
For a
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
U
, the exact category
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(U)

is the category of pairs
(E,\rho)
,
with
E
a locally free sheaf on
U
of finite rank,
and
\rho\colon\mathbb{K}\breve{Q}\to\mathrm{End}(E)

is a map of
\mathbb{K}
-algebras.
It was shown in
[
26
, §§10.2–10.4]
that
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
is
an exact
\mathbb{K}
-stack,
and that
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}}

are algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stacks locally of finite type.
To see that
\mathcal{M}^{I}
is algebraic, note that it
can be identified with the moduli stack for
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q}^{\prime})

for a certain quiver with relations
\breve{Q}^{\prime}
.
This verifies
.

The condition
is automatically true,
since all moduli stacks in question are already of finite type.

Now, assume that
\breve{Q}
is equipped with a self-dual quiver structure

(\breve{Q},\sigma,u,v,w)
,
and we verify
.

We have constructed in
6.1.5

an induced self-dual structure on
\mathcal{A}
.
One can define a self-dual structure on
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(U)

for any
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
U
analogously,
using the description of
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(U)
as above.
This defines a self-dual structure on
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}

and verifies
.

Note that by the above construction, there are
universal families

of representations of
\breve{Q}
over
\mathcal{M}
,
consisting of universal vector bundles
\mathcal{U}_{i}\to\mathcal{M}

for
i\in Q_{0}
,
and morphisms
\mathcal{U}_{s(a)}\to\mathcal{U}_{t(a)}

for
a\in Q_{1}
,
satisfying the relations specified by
Q_{2}
.

6.2.3. Explicit description, ordinary case.

The moduli stack
\mathcal{M}
of representations
of a quiver
Q
without relations
has the following explicit description.
For each
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(Q)
, we have a canonical isomorphism

\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\simeq[V_{\alpha}/G_{\alpha}]\ ,

(6.6)

where

\displaystyle V_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\prod_{a\in Q_{1}}\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{K}^{\alpha_{s(a)}},\mathbb{K}^{\alpha_{t(a)}})\ ,

(6.7)

\displaystyle G_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in Q_{0}}\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{K}^{\alpha_{i}})\ ,

(6.8)

and the (right) action of
G_{\alpha}
on
V_{\alpha}
is given as follows.
For
g=(g_{i})_{i\in Q_{0}}\in G_{\alpha}
and
e=(e_{a})_{a\in Q_{1}}\in V_{\alpha}
, define

g\cdot e=\smash{(e^{\prime}_{a})_{a\in Q_{1}}}
, where

\smash{e^{\prime}_{a}}=g_{\smash{t(a)}}^{\smash{-1}}\circ e_{a}\circ g_{s(a)}
.

6.2.4. Explicit description, self-dual case.

Now, let
(Q,\sigma,u,v)
be a self-dual quiver with no relations.
Similarly, there is an explicit description of
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
, as follows.

For convenience in notation,
we choose total orders
\leqslant
on
Q_{0}
and
Q_{1}
,
so that we can write

\displaystyle Q_{0}

\displaystyle=Q_{0}^{+}\sqcup Q_{0}^{-}\sqcup Q_{0}^{\mathbin{\vartriangle}}\sqcup Q_{0}^{{\mathbin{\vartriangle}}\vee}\ ,

(6.9)

\displaystyle Q_{1}

\displaystyle=Q_{1}^{+}\sqcup Q_{1}^{-}\sqcup Q_{1}^{\mathbin{\vartriangle}}\sqcup Q_{1}^{{\mathbin{\vartriangle}}\vee}\ ,

(6.10)

where

\displaystyle Q_{0}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\{i\in Q_{0}\mid i=i^{\vee},\,u_{i}=\pm 1\}\ ,

(6.11)

\displaystyle Q_{0}^{\mathbin{\vartriangle}}

\displaystyle=\{i\in Q_{0}\mid i<i^{\vee}\}\ ,

(6.12)

\displaystyle Q_{1}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\{a\in Q_{1}\mid a=a^{\vee},\,u_{t(a)}\,v_{a}=\pm 1\}\ ,

(6.13)

\displaystyle Q_{1}^{\mathbin{\vartriangle}}

\displaystyle=\{a\in Q_{1}\mid a<a^{\vee}\}\ .

(6.14)

This is purely for notational reasons,
and the underlying theory will not depend on this choice.

For each
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(Q)
, we have a canonical isomorphism

\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}\simeq[V^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}/G_{\theta}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}]\ ,

(6.15)

where

\displaystyle V^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}

\displaystyle=\prod_{a\in Q_{1}^{\mathbin{\vartriangle}}}{}\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{K}^{\theta_{s(a)}},\mathbb{K}^{\theta_{t(a)}})\times\prod_{a\in Q_{1}^{+}}{}\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathbb{K}^{\theta_{t(a)}})\times\prod_{a\in Q_{1}^{-}}{}{\wedge}^{2}(\mathbb{K}^{\theta_{t(a)}}),

(6.16)

\displaystyle G^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in Q_{0}^{\mathbin{\vartriangle}}}{}\mathrm{GL}(\theta_{i})\times\prod_{i\in Q_{0}^{+}}{}\mathrm{O}(\theta_{i})\times\prod_{i\in Q_{0}^{-}}{}\mathrm{Sp}(\theta_{i}),

(6.17)

and it is not difficult to write down
the action of
G^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}
on
V^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}
,
so that the quotient produces the expected moduli stack.

6.2.5. Extension bundles.

We now verify

for quivers without relations,
and

for self-dual quivers without relations.

Let
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},s,t)
be a quiver,
and let
E,F\in\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}Q)
.
Define
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces

\displaystyle A^{0}(E,F)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i\in Q_{0}}\mathrm{Hom}(E_{i}\,,F_{i})\ ,

(6.18)

\displaystyle A^{1}(E,F)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{a\in Q_{1}}\mathrm{Hom}(E_{s(a)}\,,F_{t(a)})\ .

(6.19)

Define a map
d\colon A^{0}(E,F)\to A^{1}(E,F)
as follows.
For each
\gamma=(\gamma_{i})_{i\in Q_{0}}\in A^{0}(E,F)
,
define
d\gamma\in A^{1}(E,F)
by

(d\gamma)_{a}=\gamma_{t(a)}\circ e_{a}-f_{a}\circ\gamma_{s(a)}

(6.20)

for all
a\in Q_{1}
,
where
e_{a}\,,f_{a}
are structure maps of
E,F
.

As in, for example, Crawley-Boevey
[
11
, p. 8]
,
the cohomology of the two-term complex

\cdots\to 0\to A^{0}(E,F)\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}A^{1}(E,F)\to 0\to\cdots

(6.21)

computes
\mathrm{Ext}^{0}(E,F)
and
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(E,F)
,
and this can be described as follows.

For each
\alpha=(\alpha_{a})_{a\in Q_{1}}\in A^{1}(E,F)
,
we define a representation
G_{\alpha}\in\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}Q)
as follows.
For each
i\in Q_{0}
, let
(G_{\alpha})_{i}=F_{i}\oplus E_{i}
.
For each
(i\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}j)\in Q_{1}
, define the structure map

(g_{\alpha})_{a}\colon F_{i}\oplus E_{i}\to F_{j}\oplus E_{j}
by

(g_{\alpha})_{a}=\begin{pmatrix}f_{a}&\alpha_{a}\\
0&e_{a}\end{pmatrix},

(6.22)

where
e_{a}\,,f_{a}
are structure maps of
E
and
F
.
We have a short exact sequence

0\to F\longrightarrow G_{\alpha}\longrightarrow E\to 0

(6.23)

in
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}Q)
,
where the maps are defined in the obvious way.
This defines an extension of
E
by
F
.
Moreover, two elements
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in A^{1}(E,F)

give rise to isomorphic extensions,
if and only if
\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha=d\gamma
for some
\gamma\in A^{0}(E,F)
.
Indeed, if this is the case, then the map

\eta\colon G_{\alpha}\to G_{\alpha^{\prime}}
given by

\eta_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{id}_{F_{i}}&\gamma_{i}\\
0&\mathrm{id}_{E_{i}}\end{pmatrix}

(6.24)

is an isomorphism that is compatible with the inclusions from
F

and the projections to
E
. The condition that
d\gamma=\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha

ensures that

\eta_{t(a)}\circ(g_{\alpha})_{a}=(g_{\alpha^{\prime}})_{a}\circ\eta_{s(a)}

(6.25)

for all
a\in Q_{1}
.
Conversely, all such isomorphisms must be of this form.
Therefore, the cokernel of
d
parametrizes all extensions of
E
by
F
,
and the kernel of
d
parametrizes all automorphisms of such extensions
that induce identities on
E
and
F
.

From the above argument, we conclude that the
2
-vector space

[A^{1}(E,F)/A^{0}(E,F)]\simeq\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(E,F)\times[*/\mathrm{Ext}^{0}(E,F)]

(6.26)

is the moduli stack of extensions of
E
by
F
,
where we used the map
d\colon A^{0}(E,F)\to A^{1}(E,F)
.

All the above is true in families as well.
Indeed, we can define vector bundles
A^{0},A^{1}\to\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}

whose fibres at
(F,E)
are
A^{0}(E,F)
and
A^{1}(E,F)
,
which can be constructed from the universal families.
Then, define a morphism
d\colon A^{0}\to A^{1}

similarly as above.
The
2
-vector bundle

\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}=[A^{1}/A^{0}]\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}

(6.27)

is isomorphic to
\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}\to\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}
,
the moduli stack of all extensions.
This verifies
.
The Euler form
\chi(-,-)
is given
for dimension vectors
\alpha,\beta\in C^{\circ}(Q)
by

\displaystyle\chi(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}\alpha_{i}\,\beta_{i}-\sum_{a\in Q_{1}}\alpha_{s(a)}\,\beta_{t(a)}\ .

(6.28)

6.2.6. Self-dual extension bundles.

Now, let
(Q,\sigma,u,v)
be a self-dual quiver without relations.
The condition
follows from the fact that
each
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}
is connected.

The self-dual Euler form, as in
3.7.5
,
is given by

\displaystyle\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,\theta)

\displaystyle=\chi(\alpha,j(\theta))+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in Q_{0}^{\pm}}\alpha_{i}\,(\alpha_{i}-u_{i})+\sum_{i\in Q_{0}^{\mathbin{\vartriangle}}}\alpha_{i^{\vee}}\,\alpha_{i}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt{}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{a\in Q_{1}^{\pm}}\alpha_{t(a)}\,(\alpha_{t(a)}+u_{t(a)}\,v_{a})-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{a\in Q_{1}^{\mathbin{\vartriangle}}}\alpha_{s(a)^{\vee}}\,\alpha_{t(a)}

(6.29)

for
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(Q)
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(Q)
,
with
j
as in
Eq.

3.13
.
See also Young
[
63
, Eq. (7)]
for a similar expression.

Proposition 6.2.7
.

Let
\breve{Q}
be a quiver with relations,
and let
\tau
be any weak stability condition on
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q})
.
Then
\tau
satisfies
.

Proof 20
.

First, let us verify
2.5.5

for a quiver
Q
with no relations.
It was shown in the proof of
[
8
, Lemma 3.2.2]

that for any
\alpha,\beta\in C(Q)
,
there is a closed substack of
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}

consisting of representations of class
\alpha

that have sub-representations of class
\beta
.
The semistable locus can be obtained from
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}

by removing these substacks for all
\beta
satisfying

\alpha-\beta\in C(Q)
and
\tau(\beta)>\tau(\alpha-\beta)
.
Therefore, we see that the semistable locus is open.

For a quiver with relations
\breve{Q}
,
a representation of
\breve{Q}
is
\tau
-semistable
if and only if it is
\tau
-semistable as a representation of
Q
.
This verifies
2.5.5
in general.

2.5.5
is automatically true,
as each
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}
is already of finite type.

2.5.5

is also automatically true, because each element of
C(Q)

can only be written as a sum of elements of
C(Q)
in finitely many ways.

\Box

6.2.8. Conclusion.

By the above discussion,
for any self-dual quiver with relations
\breve{Q}
,
and any self-dual weak stability condition
\tau
on
\mathcal{A}=\smash{\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q})}
,
we have well-defined invariants

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}(\tau),

\displaystyle\ \epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha})\ ,

\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\tau),

\displaystyle\ \mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle\in{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}\ ,

\displaystyle\quad\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}_{\alpha}(\tau),

\displaystyle\ \mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\textnormal{na\"{\i}}}}_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{Q}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau),

\displaystyle\ \epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta})\ ,

\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau),

\displaystyle\ \mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle\in{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}\ ,

\displaystyle\quad\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau),

\displaystyle\ \mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\textnormal{na\"{\i}}}}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{Q}\ ,

for all
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\breve{Q})
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\breve{Q})
.

If, moreover,
\breve{Q}
has no relations,
then the above invariants satisfy wall-crossing formulae
under a change of the self-dual weak stability condition
\tau
,
as in
Theorems

5.3.5
,
5.5.5
, and
5.6.9
.

6.3   An algorithm for computing invariants

6.3.1.

For a self-dual quiver
Q
without relations,
we provide an algorithm to compute the invariants

\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,

\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,

\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,

\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,

\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,

\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,

\mathrm{DT}_{\alpha}(\tau)
, and

\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,
defined above.
This will allow us to explicitly compute these invariants
for any given self-dual quiver,
and any given self-dual weak stability condition.

Note that we have dropped the superscript ‘
naï
’
for the DT invariants, since when there are no relations,
the
\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\textnormal{na\"{\i}}}}
and
\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\textnormal{na\"{\i}}}}}}
invariants
coincide with the correct definition of DT invariants
in
Section

6.5
below.

Algorithm 6.3.2
.

Let
Q
be a self-dual quiver,
and
\tau
be a self-dual weak stability condition
on
\mathcal{A}=\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}Q)
.
Let
\alpha\in C(Q)
be a dimension vector,
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(Q)
a self-dual dimension vector.

One can compute the invariants

\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,

\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,

\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,

\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,
and hence the invariants

\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,

\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,

\mathrm{DT}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,

\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
, as follows.

(i)

Compute the invariants

\smash{\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(0)}

and
\smash{\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(0)}
,
for the trivial stability condition
0
.

This is done using the explicit descriptions

Eqs.

6.6
and
6.15
,
together with
Theorem

A.1.8
.
Explicitly, using the notations of
Eq.

6.6
,
and using
Eq.

A.13
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(0)

\displaystyle=\mu(V_{\alpha})\cdot\mu([*/G_{\alpha}])

\displaystyle=\prod_{a\in Q_{1}}\mathbb{L}^{\alpha_{s(a)}\,\alpha_{t(a)}}\cdot\prod_{i\in Q_{0}}\ \prod_{j=0}^{\alpha_{i}-1}\frac{1}{\mathbb{L}^{\alpha_{i}}-\mathbb{L}^{j}}\ .

(6.30)

Similarly, using the notations of
Eq.

6.15
,
and using
Eqs.

A.13
,
A.14
, and
A.15
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(0)

\displaystyle=\mu(V^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta})\cdot\mu([*/G^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}])

\displaystyle=\prod_{a\in Q_{1}^{\mathbin{\vartriangle}}}\mathbb{L}^{\theta_{s(a)}\,\theta_{t(a)}}\cdot\prod_{a\in Q_{1}^{\pm}}\mathbb{L}^{\theta_{t(a)}\,(\theta_{t(a)}\,+\,u_{t(a)}\,v_{a})/2}\cdot{}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\biggl({}\prod_{i\in Q_{0}^{\mathbin{\vartriangle}}}\ \prod_{j=0}^{\theta_{i}-1}\frac{1}{\mathbb{L}^{\theta_{i}}-\mathbb{L}^{j}}\biggr)\cdot\biggl({}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in Q_{0}^{\pm}:\xmathstrut[0]{.3}\\
\theta_{i}\text{ even}\end{subarray}}{}\mathbb{L}^{u_{i}\,\theta_{i}/2}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{\theta_{i}/2-1}\frac{1}{\mathbb{L}^{\theta_{i}}-\mathbb{L}^{2j}}\biggr)\cdot{}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\biggl({}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in Q_{0}^{+}:\xmathstrut[0]{.3}\\
\theta_{i}\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}{}\mathbb{L}^{-(\theta_{i}-1)/2}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{(\theta_{i}-3)/2}\frac{1}{\mathbb{L}^{\theta_{i}-1}-\mathbb{L}^{2j}}\biggr)\ .

(6.31)

(ii)

Use wall-crossing to compute the invariants

\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\tau)

and
\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
.

This can be done using
Eqs.

5.47
and
5.49
.
Since the trivial stability condition
0
dominates
\tau
,
these formulae can be simplified,
similarly to
5.2.5
.
Explicitly, for
\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A})
and

\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[47.83347pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &n>0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}\,,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{i})>\tau(\alpha_{i+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n})\text{ for all }i=1,\dotsc,n-1\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}(-1)^{n-1}\cdot\mathbb{L}^{-\chi(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})}\cdot\mathrm{I}_{\alpha_{1}}(0)\cdots\mathrm{I}_{\alpha_{n}}(0)\ ,

(6.32)

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[47.83347pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &n\geqslant 0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A}),\,\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{i})>0\text{ for all }i=1,\dotsc,n\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}(-1)^{n}\cdot\mathbb{L}^{-\chi(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n},\rho)}\cdot\mathrm{I}_{\alpha_{1}}(0)\cdots\mathrm{I}_{\alpha_{n}}(0)\cdot\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(0)\ .

(6.33)

(iii)

Compute the invariants

\smash{\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau)}

and
\smash{\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)}
.

This can be done using the relations

Eqs.

5.43
and
5.45
,
as we now have explicit expressions for the invariants

\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\tau)
and
\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
.

(iv)

Compute the invariants

\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,

\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,

\mathrm{DT}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,

\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
.

We have now obtained explicit expressions for

(\mathbb{L}-1)\cdot\smash{\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau)}

and
\smash{\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)}
,
which are rational functions in the variable
\mathbb{L}

that have no poles at
\mathbb{L}=1
.
Setting
\mathbb{L}=1
gives the invariants

\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}_{\alpha}(\tau)
and
\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,
and adding appropriate signs gives the corresponding
naïve DT invariants.

6.4   Examples

We now apply
Algorithm

6.3.2

to several examples of self-dual quivers,
and compute the invariants explicitly.

6.4.1. The point quiver.

Let
Q=(\bullet)
be the point quiver,
equipped with the unique contravariant involution.
Let
\varepsilon=\pm 1
be a sign,
and let
u_{1}=\varepsilon
,
where
1
is the label of the unique vertex of
Q
.
Consider the self-dual quiver
(Q,\sigma,u,v)
,
where
v
is trivial.

Then, self-dual representations of
Q

can be seen as orthogonal (or symplectic) vector spaces,
when
\varepsilon=+1
(or
-1
).

We use the trivial stability condition
0
,
and we will omit the stability condition in all notations.

Remark 6.4.2
.

This setup in
6.4.1

is equivalent to the following situations.

(i)

As in
3.2.1
,
let
\mathcal{A}
be the self-dual abelian category of
finite-dimensional
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces,
with a sign
\varepsilon=\pm 1

introduced into the natural isomorphism

\eta_{V}\colon V^{\vee\vee}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}V
.
Then
\mathcal{A}\simeq\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}Q)
.

(ii)

Let
Q^{\prime}=(\bullet\to\bullet)
be the
A_{2}
quiver,
equipped with a contravariant involution
that exchanges the two vertices.
We assign the sign
+1
to both vertices,
and the sign
\varepsilon=\pm 1
to the edge.
We use the stability function
(1,-1)
.
Then, a self-dual representation of
Q
is of the form

V\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}V^{\vee},

where
V
is a vector space, and
f=\varepsilon f^{\vee}
.
Such a representation is semistable if and only if

f
is an isomorphism, since otherwise,
it would be destabilized by the sub-representation
(\ker f\to 0)
.
Therefore, we see that semistable self-dual representations
are equivalent to orthogonal or symplectic vector spaces.

(iii)

Consider the
\mathbb{K}
-linear
quasi-abelian category
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Vect}}}(\mathbb{P}^{1})

of vector bundles on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
as in
3.2.3
.
Choose a sign
\varepsilon=\pm 1

as the way to identify a vector bundle and its double dual.
Consider the usual slope stability on
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Vect}}}(\mathbb{P}^{1})
.
Then, a self-dual vector bundle of rank
r

is the same as a vector bundle equipped with a
non-degenerate, (anti-)symmetric bilinear form,
which can then be identified with
a principal
\mathrm{O}(r)
- or
\mathrm{Sp}(r)
-bundle.

It is well-known that every vector bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{1}

can be decomposed into a direct sum of line bundles
of the form
\mathcal{O}(d)
, for
d\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Therefore, a semistable vector bundle of slope
0

must have the form
\mathcal{O}\otimes V
,
where
V
is a finite-dimensional vector space.
Since the underlying vector bundle of a
semistable self-dual vector bundle is semistable of slope
0

(
Theorem

3.4.5
),
it must be of this form, and its self-dual structure
gives rise to a self-dual structure on
V
.
This shows that orthogonal or symplectic vector bundles on
\mathbb{P}^{1}

are equivalent to orthogonal or symplectic vector spaces.

6.4.3. Orthogonal vector spaces.

In the situation of
6.4.1
,
set
\varepsilon=+1
.
Using
Algorithm

6.3.2

to compute the self-dual invariants,
we obtain
Table

6.1
.

\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{d}

\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{d}
by setting
\mathbb{L}=1
,
and the invariants
\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{d}
are given by

\smash{(-1)^{\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(d,0)}}\cdot\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{d}
,
where
\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(d,0)=d(d-1)/2
.

Table 6.1:
Invariants for orthogonal vector spaces

From the numerical evidence, we make the following conjecture.

Conjecture 6.4.4
.

In the situation of
6.4.1
, with
\varepsilon=1
,
for any integer
n\geqslant 0
, we have

\displaystyle\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{2n}

\displaystyle=\binom{\,1/4\,}{n}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{2n}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\,\binom{\,1/4\,}{n}\ ,

(6.34)

\displaystyle\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{2n+1}

\displaystyle=\binom{-1/4}{n}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{2n+1}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\,\binom{-1/4}{n}\ .

Equivalently, we have the generating series

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}_{n}}\,q^{n}

\displaystyle=(1+q)^{1/4}\ ,

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}_{n}}\,q^{n}

\displaystyle=(1-q)^{1/4}\ ,

(6.35)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash[b]{\textsf{{B}}}_{n}}\,q^{n}

\displaystyle=(1+q)^{-1/4}\ ,

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash[b]{\textsf{{B}}}_{n}}\,q^{n}

\displaystyle=(1-q)^{-1/4}\ ,

where we replaced the dimension vectors by their
Dynkin types
\smash[b]{\textsf{{B}}}_{n}
and
\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}_{n}
.

6.4.5. Symplectic vector spaces.

In the situation of
6.4.1
,
now set
\varepsilon=-1
.
By
Eq.

A.15
, for any
n\geqslant 0
, we have

\mu([*/\mathrm{Sp}(2n)])=\mu([*/\mathrm{O}(2n+1)])\ .

Using
Algorithm

6.3.2
, in each step,
we obtain identical expressions as in the odd orthogonal case.
Therefore, if we write
\smash{\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\smash[b]{\textsf{{C}}}_{n}}}
, etc.,
for the symplectic invariants for
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
, then

\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash[b]{\textsf{{C}}}_{n}}=\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash[b]{\textsf{{B}}}_{n}}\ ,\qquad\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash[b]{\textsf{{C}}}_{n}}=\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash[b]{\textsf{{B}}}_{n}}\ ,\qquad\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash[b]{\textsf{{C}}}_{n}}=\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash[b]{\textsf{{B}}}_{n}}

for all
n\geqslant 0
.
In particular, if
6.4.4
is true,
then we have the generating series

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash[b]{\textsf{{C}}}_{n}}\,q^{n}=(1+q)^{-1/4}\ ,\qquad\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash[b]{\textsf{{C}}}_{n}}\,q^{n}=(1-q)^{-1/4}\ .

(6.36)

We make the remark that the types B and C
invariants being equal is probably related to the fact that
these types are Langlands dual to each other.

6.4.6. Loop quivers.

We now consider the more general situation of loop quivers.

Let
m\geqslant 0
be an integer,
and let
Q
be the
m
-loop quiver
,
the quiver with a unique vertex and
m
edges.
Equip
Q
with the contravariant involution
\sigma

that fixes all the edges.

For convenience, we denote the self-dual quiver
(Q,\sigma,u,v)

using the notation
m^{u}_{v}
,
where
m
is the number of loops,

u
is a sign, and
v
is a list of
m
signs.
For example,
1^{+}_{-}
is the
1
-loop quiver
whose vertex sign is
+1
and edge sign is
-1
.

Consider the trivial stability condition
0
,
and omit it from all notations.

Table 6.2:
The invariants
\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{d}

6.4.7.

Applying
Algorithm

6.3.2
,
we obtain numerical results listed in
Table

6.2
.
The table only shows the invariants
\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{d}
,
as they only differ from
\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{d}
by a sign.
Also, we have placed some of the orthogonal and symplectic invariants
in the same column when they coincide,
but it is understood that
the symplectic invariants are undefined when
d
is odd.

Remark 6.4.8
.

In
Table

6.2
,
one can see clear patterns in the first few rows,
but the numbers become more chaotic further down.
For one-loop quivers, these numbers are,
up to signs, binomial coefficients
with
-3/4
,
-1/4
or
1/4
on the top.
However, in general,
we have not been able to find a pattern
when there are at least two loops.

For stability conditions satisfying some
genericity
conditions,
we expect that there are integral invariants
lying behind these rational invariants,
which are integers and are sometimes called
BPS invariants
,
which encode the dimension of the space of BPS states.
In the ordinary (non-self-dual) case,
one has a
multiple cover formula

\mathrm{DT}_{d}=\sum_{n\mid d}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\,\mathrm{BPS}_{d/n}\ ,

(6.37)

as in Joyce–Song
[
32
, §6.2]
,
where
\mathrm{BPS}_{d/n}
denotes the integral invariant.
We expect a similar relation to be true for self-dual invariants as well,
where the right-hand side should involve
both self-dual and ordinary versions of integral invariants.
Such self-dual integral invariants are
possibly related to discussions in Young
[
63
]
.
The zero-loop and one-loop invariants having a simple pattern
is possibly due to the vanishing of these integral invariants in those cases.

6.4.9. The
\tilde{A}_{1}
quiver.

Finally, we consider the
\tilde{A}_{1}
quiver,
which is related to coherent sheaves on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
as will be discussed in
Section

6.6
.

Let
Q=(\bullet\rightrightarrows\bullet)

be the quiver with two vertices and
two arrows pointing in the same direction,
called the
\tilde{A}_{1}
quiver
.
Let
\sigma
be the contravariant involution of
Q

that exchanges the two vertices but fixes the edges.

We use the simplified notation
\tilde{A}_{1}^{u,v}

to denote the self-dual quiver
(Q,\sigma,u,v)
.
For example,
\tilde{A}_{1}^{+,++}
means that we assign the sign
+1

to all vertices and edges.
Note that both vertices must have the same sign.

We use the self-dual stability function
\tau=(1,-1)
,
and exclude it from the notations.

6.4.10.

Applying
Algorithm

6.3.2
,
we obtain numerical results that suggest the following.

Conjecture 0
.

In the situation of
6.4.9
,
we should have the following.

(i)

For
\tilde{A}_{1}^{+,++}
and
\tilde{A}_{1}^{-,--}
,
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{{(n,n)}}\,q^{n/2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-q\,\mathbb{L})^{1/2}}{(1-q^{1/2})(1-q^{1/2}\,\mathbb{L})}\ ,

(6.38)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{(n,n)}\,q^{n/2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+q^{1/2})^{1/2}}{(1-q^{1/2})^{3/2}}\ ,

(6.39)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{(n,n)}\,q^{n/2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-q^{1/2})^{1/2}}{(1+q^{1/2})^{3/2}}\ .

(6.40)

(ii)

For
\tilde{A}_{1}^{+,+-}
and
\tilde{A}_{1}^{-,+-}
,
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{(n,n)}\,q^{n/2}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{(n,n)}\,q^{n/2}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{(n,n)}\,q^{n/2}=\frac{(1+q^{1/2})^{1/2}}{(1-q^{1/2})^{1/2}}\ .

(6.41)

(iii)

For
\tilde{A}_{1}^{+,--}
and
\tilde{A}_{1}^{-,++}
,
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{(n,n)}\,q^{n/2}

\displaystyle=(1-q\,\mathbb{L}^{-1})^{1/2}\ ,\hskip 30.00005pt

(6.42)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{(n,n)}\,q^{n/2}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{(n,n)}\,q^{n/2}

\displaystyle=(1-q)^{1/2}\ .

(6.43)

6.5   Donaldson–Thomas invariants

6.5.1.

We discuss a self-dual version of the theory of

Donaldson–Thomas invariants

for
quivers with potentials
,
as a self-dual analogue of the ordinary case studied in
Joyce–Song
[
32
, Chapter 7]
.

6.5.2. Quivers with potentials.

For a quiver
Q
, a
potential
for
Q

is a
\mathbb{K}
-linear combination of oriented cycles in
Q
.
Given such a potential
W
,
we define a set
Q_{2}
of relations on
Q

(see
6.1.2
),
by setting
Q_{2}=Q_{1}
, and for each
b\in Q_{2}
,
we set
p(b)=t(b)
,
q(b)=s(b)
,
and
r(b)=\partial_{b}W
,
where for an oriented cycle
C
in
Q
,

\partial_{b}C
is the sum over all occurrences of
b
in
C

of the path obtained by removing that occurrence of
b
from
C
.
Such a pair
(Q,W)
is called a
quiver with potential
,
and
\breve{Q}=(Q,Q_{2},p,q,r)
is its
associated quiver with relations.
We also refer to representations of
\breve{Q}

as
representations of
(Q,W)
.

If, moreover,
Q
is equipped with a self-dual structure
(
6.1.3
),
then a potential
W
for
Q
is called
self-dual

if
W=W^{\vee}
, where
W^{\vee}
is the potential obtained
from
W
by replacing each cycle
C
with
C^{\vee}
.
In this case, we have a self-dual quiver with relations
\breve{Q}

(
6.1.4
),
with the data
Q_{2},p,q,r
as above,
the involution on
Q_{2}
given by that on
Q_{1}
,
and the sign function
w\colon Q_{2}\to\{\pm 1\}
given by
w=v
.
Such a pair
(Q,W)
is called a
self-dual quiver with potential
,
and
\breve{Q}
is its associated self-dual quiver with relations.
In particular, this defines a self-dual structure
on the
\mathbb{K}
-linear abelian category of representations of
(Q,W)
.

6.5.3. Definition.

Let
(Q,W)
be a self-dual quiver with potential,
and write
\mathcal{A}
for the self-dual
\mathbb{K}
-linear abelian category
of representations of its associated
self-dual quiver with relations
\breve{Q}
.

For a self-dual weak stability condition
\tau
on
\mathcal{A}
,
and classes
\alpha\in C(Q)
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(Q)
,
define
Donaldson–Thomas invariants

\mathrm{DT}_{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in\mathbb{Q}
by

\displaystyle\mathrm{DT}_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathcal{M}}{}(\mathbb{L}-1)\cdot\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)\cdot\nu_{\mathcal{M}}\,d\chi\ ,

(6.44)

\displaystyle\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}{}\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\cdot\nu_{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\,d\chi\ ,

(6.45)

where
\nu_{\mathcal{M}}
and
\nu_{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}
are the
Behrend functions

for
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
, as in Behrend
[
5
]

or Joyce–Song
[
32
, Chapter 4]
.
The ordinary case
Eq.

6.44

is due to
[
32
, Definition 7.15]
.
These invariants are well-defined by the no-pole theorems,

5.6.2
and
5.6.4
.

Note that when
W=0
,
the theory is reduced to that of quivers with no relations,
and these invariants coincide with the
naïve Donaldson–Thomas invariants
defined in
6.2.8
,
since the Behrend functions are constant in this case,
due to the stacks
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
being smooth.

6.5.4. Wall-crossing.

Let
(Q,W)
be a self-dual quiver with potential,
and let
\tau,\tilde{\tau}
be self-dual weak stability conditions
on the category
\mathcal{A}
above.
Joyce–Song
[
32
, Theorem 7.17]

showed that the invariants
\mathrm{DT}_{\alpha}(\tau)
and
\mathrm{DT}_{\alpha}(\tilde{\tau})

satisfy the wall-crossing formula
Eq.

5.58

with
\mathrm{DT}
in place of
\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\textnormal{na\"{\i}}}}
,
with the coefficients
\bar{\chi}({\cdots})
,
\tilde{\chi}({\cdots})

given by those of
Q
as a quiver without relations.
A key ingredient in their proof is a set of identities

[
32
, Theorem 7.11]

involving the Behrend function
\nu_{\mathcal{M}}
.

We expect that similar identities and wall-crossing formulae
should hold for the self-dual invariants
\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,
and we state the expected properties as follows.

Conjecture 0
.

Let
(Q,W)
be a self-dual quiver with potential.
Then the Donaldson–Thomas invariants defined above satisfy
the wall-crossing formula
Eq.

5.60

for any two self-dual weak stability conditions,
with the coefficients

\bar{\chi}({\cdots})
,
\tilde{\chi}({\cdots})
,

\bar{\chi}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}({\cdots})
,
\tilde{\chi}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}({\cdots})

given by those of
Q
as a self-dual quiver without relations.

Results in
6.2.8
imply that
the conjecture is true when there are no relations,
that is, when
W=0
.

6.6   Relation to coherent sheaves

6.6.1.

The theory of enumerative invariants of coherent sheaves
is closely related to that of quiver representations.
For a smooth, projective variety
X
over
\mathbb{K}
,
consider the bounded derived category
\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(X)

of coherent sheaves on
X
.
A result of Bondal
[
9
]
states that
for some special choices of
X
,
there is an equivalence of triangulated categories

\Phi\colon\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(X)\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\longrightarrow}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q})\ ,

(6.46)

where
\breve{Q}
is a quiver with relations.
When
X=\mathbb{P}^{n}
, one may take

Q=\Biggl(
\Biggr)\ ,
\scriptstyle 0
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle n-1
\scriptstyle n
\scriptstyle\varphi_{1,0}
\scriptstyle\varphi_{1,1}
\scriptstyle\vdots
\scriptstyle\varphi_{1,n}
\cdots
\scriptstyle\varphi_{n,0}
\scriptstyle\varphi_{n,1}
\scriptstyle\vdots
\scriptstyle\varphi_{n,n}

(6.47)

with
(n+1)
vertices and
n(n+1)
arrows,
with the relations

\smash{\varphi_{i+1,j}\,\varphi_{i,k}}=\smash{\varphi_{i+1,k}\,\varphi_{i,j}}

for
i\in\{1,\dotsc,n-1\}
and
j,k\in\{0,\dotsc,n\}
.
In particular, when
X=\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we have

Q=\Bigl(
\Bigr)\ ,
\scriptstyle 0
\scriptstyle 1

with no relations.
This is the
\tilde{A}_{1}
quiver discussed in
6.4.9
.

6.6.2.

The functor
\Phi
in

is constructed using a
strong exceptional collection

in the triangulated category
\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(X)
, as in
[

]
.
When
X=\mathbb{P}^{n}
, such a collection may be taken to be

\bigl(\mathcal{O},\ \mathcal{O}(1),\ \dotsc,\ \mathcal{O}(n)\bigr)\ ,

and for a complex
E\in\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(X)
,
its image under
\Phi
is given by

\Phi(E)_{i}=\mathbb{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{O}(n-i),E)

(6.48)

for
i=0,\dotsc,n
,
where for an object
V\in\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q})
,
we denote by
V_{i}
the complex assigned to the vertex
i
.

For example, when
X=\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
using Serre duality to compute the
\mathbb{R}\mathrm{Hom}
functor,
one obtains

\displaystyle\mathclap{\,\dotsc\dotsc\mathrlap{\ ,}}

(6.49)

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathcal{O}(-2))

\displaystyle\mathclap{=}

\displaystyle\bigl(\,\mathbb{K}^{2}[-1]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{K}[-1]\,\bigr)\ ,

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathcal{O}(-1))

\displaystyle\mathclap{=}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0\,\bigr)\ ,

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathcal{O}(0))

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathcal{O}(2))
