6.6.3. Self-dual structures.

We now define and compare
self-dual structures on the categories

\smash{\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(X)}
and
\smash{\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q})}
.

For coherent sheaves, we take an object
L\in\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(X)

such that

\mathbb{R}\mathcal{H}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{om}_{X}(L,L)\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X}\ .

For example,
L
may be a line bundle, or a shift of a line bundle.
We define the dual operation on the triangulated category
\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(X)
by

E^{\vee}=\mathbb{R}\mathcal{H}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{om}_{X}(E,L)

(6.50)

for a complex of sheaves
E
.
Then
E^{\vee\vee}
is canonically isomorphic to
E
.
We may insert a sign
\pm 1
into this identification,
which will correspond to the orthogonal and symplectic cases.

On the other hand, when
\breve{Q}
is the quiver with relations
given by
Eq.

6.47
,
we define a contravariant involution of
\breve{Q}

by sending the vertex
i
to the vertex
n-i
for
i=0,\dotsc,n
,
and sending the arrow
\smash{\varphi_{i,j}}

to the arrow
\smash{\varphi_{n+1-i,j}}

for
i=1,\dotsc,n
and
j=0,\dotsc,n
.
We define a dual operation on the triangulated category
\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q})

to be induced by the dual operation on the abelian category
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q})

induced by the contravariant involution of
\breve{Q}
described above.

Using the functor
\Phi
,
the quiver-induced dual operation on
\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}\breve{Q})

corresponds to the choice

L=\mathcal{O}(-1)[1]

for the dual operation on
\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(\mathbb{P}^{n})
.

The quiver-induced dual operation

When
X=\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
the quiver-induced dual operation is shown in
Fig.

6.1
.
The left-hand side plots complexes of sheaves on
\mathbb{P}^{1}

r
and degree
d
,
while the right-hand side plots complexes of representations of
Q=\tilde{A}_{1}

using the dimension vector
(d_{0},d_{1})
.
The two-way arrows indicate points that are exchanged by the dual operation.
The shaded region indicates the usual heart of
\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}Q)

, and the corresponding region in
\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(\mathbb{P}^{1})
.
The coordinate systems are arranged so that the phase angles
give rise to Bridgeland stability conditions in both categories,
namely, slope stability for coherent sheaves,
and the stability function
(1,-1)
for quiver representations.

Section 7Orthogonal and symplectic sheaves

We now apply our theory of enumerative invariants
to the following two cases.

•

We consider the self-dual category
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Vect}}}(X)

of vector bundles on a smooth algebraic curve
X
,
whose self-dual objects will be
principal bundles on the curve
for the orthogonal or symplectic groups.

•

More generally, we also study self-dual
quasi-abelian subcategories in the derived category

\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(X)
of coherent sheaves on
a smooth, projective variety
X
,
where the moduli of self-dual objects
can be seen as a compactification
of the moduli of orthogonal or symplectic bundles on the variety.

These two cases will be discussed in
Sections

7.1
and
7.2
,
respectively.
Then, in
Section

7.3
,
we discuss a modified version of our theory
that works for surfaces of Kodaira dimension
\leqslant 0
,
and in
Section

7.4
,
we discuss a possible generalization to
Calabi–Yau threefolds,
for which we can define self-dual Donaldson–Thomas invariants.

7.1   Orthogonal and symplectic bundles on curves

7.1.1. Principal bundles.

Recall that for a
\mathbb{K}
-variety
X

and a linear algebraic group
G
over
\mathbb{K}
,
a
principal
G
-bundle
on
X

is a morphism
\pi\colon P\to X
of
\mathbb{K}
-schemes
with a right
G
-action on
P
,
such that
\pi
is
G
-invariant and étale locally isomorphic to
the trivial
G
-bundle.

As in Serre
[
51
, §5.6]
,
a principal
G
-bundle on
X
,
where
G=\mathrm{O}(n)
with
n\geqslant 0
,
or
\mathrm{Sp}(n)
with
n\geqslant 0
even,
is equivalent to a vector bundle
E\to X
of rank
n
,
equipped with a non-degenerate bilinear form

\phi\in H^{0}(X;\operatorname{Sym}^{2}E^{\vee})
when
G=\mathrm{O}(n)
,
or
\phi\in H^{0}(X;{\wedge^{2}}\,E^{\vee})
when
G=\mathrm{Sp}(n)
.

In other words, orthogonal or symplectic bundles
are self-dual objects in the self-dual category of vector bundles on
X
,
as in
3.2.3
.

7.1.2. The setting.

We now specialize ourselves to the following situation.

Let
X
be a smooth, projective curve over
\mathbb{K}
,
and let
\mathcal{A}=\smash[b]{\textsf{{Vect}}}(X)

be the
\mathbb{K}
-linear quasi-abelian category of
vector bundles on
X
of finite rank.

Fix a line bundle
L\to X
.
Define a self-dual structure
(-)^{\vee}\colon\mathcal{A}\simeq\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}

by sending a vector bundle
E
to the vector bundle

E^{\vee}=\mathcal{H}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{om}(E,L)\ ,

and choosing a sign
\varepsilon=\pm 1
,
so that the natural isomorphism
\eta\colon(-)^{\vee\vee}\simeq\mathrm{id}

is given by
\varepsilon
times the usual identification.
In particular, when
L=\mathcal{O}_{X}
, the self-dual objects of
\mathcal{A}

can be identified with principal orthogonal or symplectic bundles,
depending on the sign
\varepsilon
, as in
7.1.1
.

For the extra data in
, we take

C(X)=\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\times\mathbb{Z}\ ,\qquad C^{\circ}(X)=C(X)\cup\{(0,0)\}\ ,

and for
E\in\mathcal{A}
, define
\llbr E\rrbr=(r,d)
,
where
r=\operatorname{rank}(E)
and
d=\deg(E)
.

For the extra data in
, we may take

C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(X)=\begin{cases}\mathbb{N}&\text{if }\varepsilon=+1\ ,\\
2\mathbb{N}&\text{if }\varepsilon=-1\ ,\end{cases}

(7.1)

recording the rank of a self-dual vector bundle.
The degree of a self-dual vector bundle
(E,\phi)

is determined by its rank,
\deg E=\operatorname{rank}E\cdot\deg L/2
.

7.1.3. Remark.

In the special case when
L=\mathcal{O}_{X}
and
\varepsilon=+1
,
one can use a more refined version,

\displaystyle C^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(X)

\displaystyle=\bigl\{(\operatorname{rank}(E),w_{1}(E),w_{2}(E))\bigm|(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\bigr\}

(7.2)

\displaystyle\subset\mathbb{Z}\oplus H^{1}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\oplus H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ ,

where
H^{i}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
denotes étale cohomology,
isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\smash{2g}}
when
i=1
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
when
i=2
,
where
g
is the genus of
X
,
and
w_{i}(E)
denotes the
i
-th Stiefel–Whitney class of
(E,\phi)
,
as in Laborde
[
38
]
.
However, we will not use this in the following,
as the simpler and coarser version
Eq.

7.1

is sufficient for our theory to work.

7.1.4. Verifying the assumptions.

In the setting of
7.1.2
,
we verify the conditions

,
,
,

, and
.

Define a categorical moduli stack
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
of objects in
\mathcal{A}
as follows.
For a
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
U
,
let
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(U)
be the exact category of locally free sheaves on
X\times U

of finite rank.
By faithfully flat descent of locally free sheaves

[
22
, Proposition 1.10]
,

\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
satisfies the descent property,
and is an exact stack.
To show that
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
is algebraic,
we notice that
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}}

are open substacks of the moduli stacks of coherent sheaves on
X

and of exact sequences of coherent sheaves on
X
, respectively,
as in Joyce
[
26
, §9]
.
It was shown there that the latter two moduli stacks are algebraic.
That
\mathcal{M}^{I}
is algebraic follows from
García-Prada
et al.

[
20
, §4.1]
.
This verifies
.

For
,
we need to define a self-dual structure on the exact category
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(U)

for any
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
U
.
Define
(-)^{\vee}\colon\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(U)\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(U)^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}

sending a locally free sheaf
E\to X\times U

to
\mathcal{H}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{om}(E,L\boxtimes\mathcal{O}_{U})
,
and identify
E^{\vee\vee}
with
E
using the sign
\varepsilon
.

The condition
follows from Joyce
[
26
, Theorem 9.7]
.

For
,
the condition
2.7.4
holds since

X
is a smooth, projective curve.
To construct the extension bundle,
let
\mathcal{U}\to X\times\mathcal{M}
be the universal locally free sheaf,
and define a perfect complex
\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}\to\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}
by

\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}=(\operatorname{\smash[b]{\mathrm{pr}}}_{2,3})_{*}\,\mathcal{H}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{om}\bigl(\operatorname{\smash[b]{\mathrm{pr}}}_{1,3}^{*}(\mathcal{U}),\operatorname{\smash[b]{\mathrm{pr}}}_{1,2}^{*}(\mathcal{U})\bigr)\ ,

(7.3)

where the
\operatorname{\smash[b]{\mathrm{pr}}}_{i,j}
are projections from
X\times\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}

to the product of its
i
-th and
j
-th factors,
and we are using the derived pushforward functor.
Then
\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}
is concentrated in degrees
0
and
1
.
By
Theorem

C.1.5
,
we obtain a
2
-vector bundle

\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}\ ,

as the realization of
\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}[1]
,
which can be identified with
\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}\to\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}
.
Indeed, for any affine
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
U
,
and any morphism
U\to\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}
classifying locally free sheaves

E,F\to X\times U
,
the groupoid
\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}(U)

is the groupoid of sections of

(\operatorname{\smash[b]{\mathrm{pr}}}_{2})_{*}\,\mathcal{H}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{om}_{X\times U}(F,E)[1]
over
U
,
which is the groupoid of objects of the
2
-vector space

\pi_{*}\,\mathcal{H}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{om}_{X\times U}(F,E)[1]
,
where
\pi\colon X\times U\to{*}
is the projection,
since the derived pushforward respects composition.
On the other hand,
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}(U)
is the groupoid of extensions
of
F
by
E
on
X\times U
,
which is the groupoid of objects of the same
2
-vector space.
This shows that
\mathcal{E}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{xt}^{1/0}\simeq\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}

as
2
-vector bundles over
\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}
.
We have the Euler pairing

\chi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(1-g)\,r_{1}r_{2}-r_{1}d_{2}+r_{1}d_{2}

(7.4)

for classes
\alpha_{i}=(r_{i},d_{i})\in C^{\circ}(X)
for
i=1,2
,
which follows from the Riemann–Roch formula

\chi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\int_{X}\operatorname{ch}(E_{2}^{\vee})\operatorname{ch}(E_{1})\operatorname{td}(X),

(7.5)

for objects
E_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
of classes
\alpha_{i}
for
i=1,2
,
where the integrand is an element of the Chow ring of
X
;

\operatorname{td}(X)=1+(1/2)\,c_{1}(T_{X})
is the Todd class of
X
,
where
T_{X}
is the tangent bundle of
X
;

\operatorname{ch}(E_{1})=r_{1}+c_{1}(E_{1})
, and

\operatorname{ch}(E_{2}^{\vee})=r_{2}-c_{1}(E_{2})
.

For the condition
,
one computes the self-dual Euler pairing

\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,\theta)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\chi(\alpha,j(\theta))+(1-g)\,\binom{r+1}{2}+(r+1)\,d&\text{if }\varepsilon=+1\ ,\\[4.30554pt]
\displaystyle\chi(\alpha,j(\theta))+(1-g)\,\binom{r}{\,2\,}+(r-1)\,d&\text{if }\varepsilon=-1\ ,\end{cases}

(7.6)

for classes
\alpha=(r,d)\in C^{\circ}(X)

and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(X)
.
Indeed, when
\varepsilon=+1
, we have

\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,\theta)=\chi(\alpha,j(\theta))+\int_{X}{}\operatorname{ch}(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(E))\operatorname{td}(X)\ ,

(7.7)

for
E\in\mathcal{A}
of class
\alpha
.
By the splitting principle,
we may assume that
E\simeq L_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus L_{r}

is a sum of line bundles, so that

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(E))

\displaystyle=\binom{r+1}{2}\ ,

(7.8)

\displaystyle c_{1}(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(E))

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant j\leqslant r}{}(c_{1}(L_{i})+c_{1}(L_{j}))=(r+1){}\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{1}(L_{i})\ .

(7.9)

These give the desired expression.
A similar technique works when
\varepsilon=-1
,
where we use
\wedge^{2}(E)
instead of
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(E)
.

7.1.5. Stability conditions.

In the setting of
7.1.2
,
define a self-dual stability condition
\tau\colon C(X)\to\mathbb{Q}
,
called the
slope stability condition
, by

\tau((r,d))=d/r-\deg L/2

(7.10)

for
(r,d)\in C(X)
.
We verify the condition
for this stability condition.

Indeed, we may assume that
\deg L=0
,
since shifting
\tau
by a constant does not affect
.
Now,
2.5.5
follow from Huybrechts–Lehn

[
24
, Proposition 2.3.1 and Theorem 3.3.7]
.
For
2.5.5
, let
E\in\mathcal{M}(U)
be such a family.
We may assume that
U
is connected,
so there exists
(r,d)\in C(\mathcal{A})
such that

\llbr E_{x}\rrbr=(r,d)
for all
x
.
By
[
24
, Lemma 1.7.6]
,
there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that

E_{x}
is
m
-regular for all
x\in U
.
By
[
24
, Lemma 1.7.2 (i)]
,
each
E_{x}
admits a surjection from
\mathcal{O}(-m)^{\oplus n}
for some
n
.
This means that the slopes of the Harder–Narasimhan factors of
E_{x}

are bounded below by
t=\tau(\llbr\mathcal{O}(-m)\rrbr)
.
Hence, they are also bounded above by
d-(r-1)t
.
Therefore, we can take
S_{E}
to be the set of classes with rank
\leqslant r

and with slope between
t
and
d-(r-1)t
.

7.1.6. Conclusion.

By the above arguments,
all results in
Sections

4
to
5

apply to the situation of
7.1.2
,
with the stability condition
\tau
defined above.
However, there are no meaningful wall-crossing formulae in this case,
as we do not have other interesting stability conditions
to compare with.

7.2   Orthogonal and symplectic sheaves

7.2.1.

Let
X
be a smooth, projective variety over
\mathbb{K}
.
As was mentioned in
3.2.4
,
the category
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(X)
of coherent sheaves on
X

does not admit self-dual structures in general.

On the other hand, the derived category
\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(X)

does admit self-dual quasi-abelian subcategories
for which our theory can apply.
We will call the self-dual objects in these self-dual categories

orthogonal sheaves
or
symplectic sheaves
.
For curves, one example of such a quasi-abelian subcategory
is the category of vector bundles on the curve,
and we recover the situation of
Section

7.1
.
However, in general, such quasi-abelian subcategories
are not contained in the usual heart
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(X)
,
and self-dual objects will be complexes of coherent sheaves in general.

Note that this notion of orthogonal or symplectic sheaves
is different from the approach of
Fernandez Herrero
et al.

[
17
]
,
who defined a different compactification
of the moduli of principal
G
-bundles for
connected reductive groups
G
.

We discuss two versions of this construction,
using
polynomial Bridgeland stability

and
Bridgeland stability
, respectively, in

7.2.2
and
7.2.5
below.
We will show that the assumptions
and
2.6.6

are satisfied in both cases,
so that our invariants in
Section

5
are defined
for self-dual weak stability conditions satisfying
.
However, since
fails when
\dim X>1
,
we do not expect the wall-crossing formulae to hold for these invariants,
except in the case of certain surfaces,
which will be discussed in
Section

7.3
below.

7.2.2. Polynomial Bridgeland stability conditions.

Let
X
be a smooth, projective variety over
\mathbb{K}
, of dimension
n
.
Let
A^{*}(X)
be the Chow ring of
X
,
and
\mathrm{Num}(X)
the group of cycles in
A^{*}(X)
modulo numerical equivalence.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{\smash{\mathrm{b}}}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(X)
be the bounded derived category
of coherent sheaves on
X
.
Choose an object
L\in\mathcal{C}
such that

L[-\ell]
is a line bundle for some
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Define a dual operation
(-)^{\vee}\colon\mathcal{C}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\mathcal{C}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}
by

(-)^{\vee}=\mathbb{R}\mathcal{H}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{om}(-,L)\ .

(7.11)

Typical choices of
L
include
\mathcal{O}_{X}
and
\omega_{X}[n]
,
giving the usual duality and Serre/Verdier duality, respectively.
Choose a sign
\varepsilon=\pm 1
, and use it to identify

E^{\vee\vee}
with
E
for all
E\in\mathcal{C}
.

Write
\beta=c_{1}(L[-\ell])/2\in A^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
,
and let
\omega\in A^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
be an ample class.
Consider the
polynomial Bridgeland stability condition

on
\mathcal{C}
, in the sense of Bayer
[
3
]
,
given by the polynomial central charge

Z^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}(E,m)=\mathrm{i}^{n-\ell}\cdot\int_{X}\exp(-\beta-\mathrm{i}m\omega)\cdot\operatorname{ch}(E)\ ,

(7.12)

where
E\in\mathcal{C}
, and
m
is a real parameter.
We will consider the limit
m\gg 0
.
Here, we introduced the factor
\mathrm{i}^{n}

so that the central charge can be self-dual.
Indeed, we have

Z^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}(E^{\vee},m)=\overline{Z^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}(E,m)\vphantom{{}^{0}}}

(7.13)

for all
E\in\mathcal{C}
, where
(-)^{\vee}
is as in
Eq.

7.11
,
and the bar denotes complex conjugation.
As in Bayer
[
3
, Theorem 3.2.2]
,
choose the
perversity function

p\colon\{0,\dotsc,n\}\to\mathbb{Z}
to be given by

p(d)=\lfloor(n-\ell-d-1)/2\rfloor
for all
d
.
Then we have a phase function

\phi^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}(E,m)
for
Z^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}(E^{\vee},m)

in the sense of
[
3
, §§2.2–2.3]
,
defined for
m\gg 0
and all
E\in\mathcal{C}
with
\operatorname{ch}E\neq 0
,
such that
Z^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}(E,m)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\cdot\smash{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}(E,m)}}

for
m\gg 0
, and

\phi^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}(E^{\vee},m)=-\phi^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}(E,m)

(7.14)

for all such
E
.
We have quasi-abelian full subcategories
\mathcal{P}^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}(I)\subset\mathcal{C}
,
closed under extensions,
for all intervals
I
in the set of phase functions
such that
I\cap(I+1)=\varnothing
,
and we have
\mathcal{P}^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}(I)^{\vee}=\mathcal{P}^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}(-I)
for all such intervals
I
.
In particular, the quasi-abelian subcategory

\mathcal{A}_{L}=\mathcal{P}^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}\bigl(\mathopen{]}-1/2,1/2\mathclose{[}\bigr)\subset\mathcal{C}

(7.15)

is self-dual, and contains the self-dual abelian category
\mathcal{P}^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}(0)
.

Alternatively, the category
\mathcal{A}_{L}
can be described as follows.
Let
\smash{\mathcal{A}^{p^{\prime}}}
be the abelian category of

perverse coherent sheaves
,
as in Bayer
[
3
, §3.1]
,
with the perversity function
p^{\prime}\colon\{0,\dotsc,n\}\to\mathbb{Z}
given by

p(d)=\lfloor(n-\ell-d)/2\rfloor
for all
d
.
Then
\smash{\mathcal{A}^{p^{\prime}}}=\mathcal{P}^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}(\mathopen{]}-1/2,1/2])\subset\mathcal{C}
,
and we have

\mathcal{A}_{L}=\smash{\mathcal{A}^{p^{\prime}}}\cap(\smash{\mathcal{A}^{p^{\prime}}})^{\vee}

(7.16)

in
\mathcal{C}
. In particular, this shows that

\mathcal{A}_{L}
only depends on
L
, and justifies its notation.

There is a self-dual stability condition

\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}\colon C(\mathcal{A}_{L})\longrightarrow T

induced by the polynomial Bridgeland stability condition
Z^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}
,
where
T
is the totally ordered set of phase functions.

7.2.3. Verifying the assumptions.

We sketch how to verify the conditions
and
2.6.6

for the self-dual
\mathbb{K}
-linear quasi-abelian category

\mathcal{A}_{L}
defined above.

For the extra data in
and
, take

\displaystyle C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A}_{L})

\displaystyle=\{\operatorname{ch}(E)\mid E\in\mathcal{A}_{L}\}

\displaystyle\subset\mathrm{Num}(X)_{\mathbb{Q}}\ ,

(7.17)

\displaystyle C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}_{L})

\displaystyle=\{\operatorname{ch}(E)\mid(E,\phi)\in(\mathcal{A}_{L})^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\}

\displaystyle\subset\mathrm{Num}(X)_{\mathbb{Q}}\ ,

(7.18)

with
\llbr E\rrbr=\operatorname{ch}(E)\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A}_{L})

for
E\in\mathcal{A}_{L}
,
and
\llbr E,\phi\rrbr=\operatorname{ch}(E)\in\smash{C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}_{L})}

for
(E,\phi)\in\smash{(\mathcal{A}_{L})^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}
.

For
, we define
a categorical moduli stack
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
of objects in
\mathcal{A}_{L}
as follows.
By Lieblich
[
41
]

or Toën–Vaquié
[
60
, Corollary 3.21]
,
there is an algebraic stack
{\smash{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}}
, locally of finite type,
parametrizing objects
E\in\mathcal{C}
with
\mathrm{Ext}^{<0}(E,E)=0
.
We have an open substack
\mathcal{M}\subset{\smash{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}}

of objects in
\mathcal{A}_{L}
,
which can be defined via
Eq.

7.16
,
since being perverse coherent is an open condition.
For any
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
U
, now define
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(U)

to be the full subcategory of
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Perf}}}(X\times U)

spanned by objects in
\mathcal{M}(U)
,
with the induced exact structure.
Then
\mathcal{M}^{I}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}}

are also algebraic stacks locally of finite type,
since the former admits a representable, finite type morphism
to
\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}
, and the latter an open immersion into
\mathcal{M}^{I}
.

For
,
we need to define a self-dual structure on
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(U)

for any
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
U
.
Define
(-)^{\vee}\colon\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(U)\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(U)^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}

sending an object
E\in\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}(U)\subset\smash[b]{\textsf{{Perf}}}(X\times U)
to

\mathbb{R}\mathcal{H}\mspace{-2mu}\mathit{om}(E,L\boxtimes\mathcal{O}_{U})
,
and identify
E^{\vee\vee}
with
E
using the sign
\varepsilon
.

For
2.6.6
,
the morphism
\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}\to\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}

is the classical truncation of a
derived vector bundle
,
by a similar argument as in
7.1.4
,
and hence is of finite type.

7.2.4. Invariants.

In the situation of
7.2.2
,
for any self-dual weak stability condition
\tau

on
\mathcal{A}_{L}
satisfying
,
by results in

Sections

5.1
,
5.5
, and
5.6
,
we have well-defined invariants

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}(\tau),

\displaystyle\ \epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}_{\alpha})\ ,

\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\tau),

\displaystyle\ \mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle\in{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}\ ,

\displaystyle\quad\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{Q}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau),

\displaystyle\ \epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta})\ ,

\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau),

\displaystyle\ \mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle\in{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}\ ,

\displaystyle\quad\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{Q}\ ,

for all
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A}_{L})

and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}_{L})
.
Also, when changing between weak stability conditions on
\mathcal{A}_{L}
,
the
\delta,\epsilon,\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}},\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
invariants
satisfy wall-crossing formulae in
Theorem

5.3.5
,
as long as the involved weak stability conditions
satisfy the assumptions given there.
However, the other invariants are not expected to satisfy
wall-crossing formulae discussed in

Sections

5.5
to
5.6
,
due to the failure of
and
.

In particular, if one can verify

for the self-dual stability condition

\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}
in
7.2.2
,
then these invariants will be defined for
\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}
.
This is known when
\dim X\leqslant 2
,
where the case of surfaces follows from
Bayer
[
3
, Lemma 4.2]
.

7.2.5. Bridgeland stability conditions.

We continue to use the notations

X
,
\mathcal{C}
,
L
,
\beta
,
\omega
,
\varepsilon

from
7.2.2
,
and we mention
Bridgeland stability conditions

on
\mathcal{C}
, as in Bridgeland
[
10
]
,
that are self-dual with respect to the dual operation
(-)^{\vee}

in
Eq.

7.11
, depending on
L
and
\varepsilon
.
Bridgeland stability conditions are
typically difficult to construct,
and are not known to exist for general
X
with
\dim X>2
.

We follow Bayer–Macrì–Toda
[
4
]
,
who constructed Bridgeland stability conditions
for
X
a curve, a surface, or a threefold
satisfying the conjectural Bogomolov–Gieseker inequality,

[
4
, Conjecture 3.2.7]
.

Consider the central charge

Z_{L,\omega}(E)=\mathrm{i}^{n-\ell}\cdot\int_{X}\exp(-\beta-\mathrm{i}\omega)\cdot\operatorname{ch}(E)\ ,

(7.19)

where
E\in\mathcal{C}
.
For
X
as above,
Bayer–Macrì–Toda
[
4
]

showed that
Z_{L,\omega}
defines a Bridgeland stability condition on
\mathcal{C}
.
Arguing as in
7.2.2
,
with a suitable choice of phase function,
this Bridgeland stability condition is self-dual, in that

\mathcal{P}_{L,\omega}(t)^{\vee}=\mathcal{P}_{L,\omega}(-t)

(7.20)

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
where
\mathcal{P}_{L,\omega}(t)\subset\mathcal{C}
is the full subcategory
of semistable objects of phase
t
.
In particular, the quasi-abelian subcategory

\mathcal{A}_{L,\omega}=\mathcal{P}_{L,\omega}\bigl(\mathopen{]}-1/2,1/2\mathclose{[}\bigr)\subset\mathcal{C}

(7.21)

is self-dual, and contains the self-dual abelian category
\mathcal{P}_{L,\omega}(0)
.
Moreover,
Z_{L,\omega}
induces a self-dual stability condition

\tau_{L,\omega}
on
\mathcal{A}_{L,\omega}
.

7.2.6. Verifying the assumptions.

We now sketch the proof of
and
2.6.6

for
\mathcal{A}_{L,\omega}
, as well as
for
\tau_{L,\omega}
,
in the setting of
7.2.5
,
where
X
is a curve, a surface, or a threefold
satisfying
[
4
, Conjecture 3.2.7]
.

Consider the stack
{\smash{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}}
in

7.2.3
.
There is an open substack
\mathcal{M}\subset{\smash{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}}

of objects of
\mathcal{A}_{L,\omega}
,
and open substacks

\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha}(\tau_{L,\omega})\subset\mathcal{M}
of finite type,
consisting of
\tau_{L,\omega}
-semistable objects
with Chern character
\alpha
.
The case of surfaces is by
Toda
[
58
, §4]
and Piyaratne–Toda
[
46
, §1.1]
,
and the case of threefolds
by Piyaratne–Toda
[
46
, Corollary 4.21]
;
see also Alper–Halpern-Leistner–Heinloth

[
1
, Examples 7.22 and 7.29]
,
Halpern-Leistner
[
23
, Example 6.2.8]
.
Then, arguing as in
7.2.3

proves
and
2.6.6
,
and
for
\tau_{L,\omega}

also follows from this description.

Therefore, similarly to
7.2.4
,
we have well-defined enumerative invariants for
any self-dual weak stability condition
\tau

on
\mathcal{A}_{L,\omega}
satisfying
,
and in particular, for the self-dual stability condition

\tau_{L,\omega}
.

7.3   Surfaces of Kodaira dimension
\leqslant
0

7.3.1.

We now consider a special case of
orthogonal and symplectic sheaves
discussed in
Section

7.2
above,
where
X
is an algebraic surface with Kodaira dimension
\leqslant 0
.
In this case, the invariants defined in
Section

7.2

above will satisfy wall-crossing formulae,
similarly to the case of curves in
Section

7.1
,
since we have a weaker version of the assumptions

and
.
This is a self-dual analogue of results in
Joyce
[
30
, §6.4]
.

By standard results on classification of algebraic surfaces,
as described in, for example,
Iskovskikh–Shafarevich
[
25
]
,
such a surface
X
either has Kodaira dimension
-\infty
,
so it is a rational surface or a ruled surface,
or it has Kodaira dimension
0
,
so it is a K3 surface, an abelian surface, an Enriques surface,
or a hyperelliptic surface.

7.3.2. The setting.

Let
X
be an algebraic surface
of Kodaira dimension
\leqslant 0
.
Choose a line bundle
L
on
X
,
a sign
\varepsilon=\pm 1
,
and an ample class
\omega\in\smash{A^{1}}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}

as in
7.2.2
.
Consider the self-dual full subcategory

\mathcal{A}_{L}^{+}=\mathcal{P}^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}\bigl(\mathopen{]}-1/4,1/4\mathclose{[}\bigr)\subset\mathcal{A}_{L}\ ,

(7.22)

which consists of all objects
E\in\mathcal{A}_{L}

with
\operatorname{rank}E>0
,
so in particular, it is independent of
\omega
.
Choose the data
C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A}_{L}^{+})
and
C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}_{L}^{+})

as the corresponding subsets of
C(\mathcal{A}_{L})
and
C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}_{L})
,
respectively, as in
7.2.3
,
and we use the moduli stacks constructed there.

7.3.3.

The main property of this class of surfaces that we will use
is the following weaker version of
.

Proposition 0
.

In the setting of
7.3.2
,
let
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C(\mathcal{A}_{L}^{+})
,
and let
\mathcal{M}^{>}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})\subset\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{1}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{2}}

be the open substack of pairs
(E_{1},E_{2})
such that
the minimal slope of
E_{1}
with respect to
\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}

is greater than the maximal slope of
E_{2}
.

Then, for any
(E_{1},E_{2})\in\mathcal{M}^{>}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
,
we have
\mathrm{Ext}^{2}(E_{2},E_{1})=0
.
Moreover, the morphism

\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{1}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\alpha_{2}}\ ,

(7.23)

restricted to the preimage of

\mathcal{M}^{>}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})

is a
2
-vector bundle of rank
-\chi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
,
where

\chi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\int_{X}\alpha_{1}\,\alpha_{2}^{\vee}\,\operatorname{td}(X)\ .

(7.24)

Proof 21
.

As in Joyce
[
30
, Lemma 6.18]
,
we have either
c_{1}(K_{X})\cdot\omega<0
or
c_{1}(K_{X})=0
,
where
K_{X}
is the canonical bundle of
X
.
One can use this to verify that
the operation
-\otimes K_{X}
on
\mathcal{A}_{L}^{+}

does not increase the
\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}
-slope,
and an argument as in

[
30
, Propositions 6.19 and 6.20]

shows that
\mathrm{Ext}^{2}(E_{2},E_{1})=0

for such
(E_{1},E_{2})
.
As in
7.2.3
,
the morphism
\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2}
is the classical truncation
of a derived vector bundle,
and the vanishing of
\mathrm{Ext}^{2}
implies that
it is a
2
-vector bundle over
\mathcal{M}^{>}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
.

\Box

7.3.5.

Similarly, we have a weaker version of
.
For
\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A}_{L}^{+})
,
let
\mathcal{M}^{>}_{\alpha}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})\subset\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}
be the open substack of objects
E

such that the minimal slope of
E
is greater than
0
.
Then
\mathrm{Ext}^{2}(E^{\vee},E)=0
for such
E
,
and the morphism
\pi_{1}\colon(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(2)}}_{\smash{\alpha,\alpha^{\vee}}})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}

restricted to the preimage of
\mathcal{M}^{>}_{\alpha}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})

is a
2
-vector bundle of rank
-\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,0)
,
with the notation
\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
as in
3.7.5
, where

\chi^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,\theta)=\chi(\alpha,j(\theta))+\int_{X}\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\alpha)\,\operatorname{td}(X)\ ,

(7.25)

where
\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\alpha)
is the Chern character of either

\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(E)
or
{\wedge^{2}}(E)
, depending on the sign
\varepsilon
,
for any object
E
with Chern character
\alpha
.

7.3.6. Wall-crossing for
\mathrm{I}
and
\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
.

Using the properties in
7.3.3
to
7.3.5

in place of the conditions
and
,
the relation
Eq.

4.33

still holds if
f\boxtimes g
is supported on

\mathcal{M}^{>}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
for some
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C(\mathcal{A}_{L}^{+})
,
and
Eq.

4.34
holds if

f\boxtimes h
is supported on the preimage of

\mathcal{M}^{>}_{\smash{\alpha,j(\theta)}}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
in
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}

for some
\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A}_{L}^{+})

and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}_{L}^{+})\setminus\{0\}
,
or on
\mathcal{M}^{>}_{\alpha}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{0}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{0}
.

We argue that these properties are enough for the wall-crossing formulae
in
Theorem

5.3.5
to hold for the invariants

\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
and
\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
,
similarly to the arguments in Joyce
[
30
, §6.4]
.

Let
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in A^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
be ample classes.
We claim that the change from

\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega_{1}}
to
\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega_{2}}

is globally finite, in the sense of
Definition

5.3.3
.
Indeed, this was shown in Joyce
[
30
, Theorem 5.16]

for slope stability and Gieseker stability,
a key ingredient being the
Bogomolov inequality

\Delta(\alpha)\geqslant 0
for classes
\alpha
admitting semistable objects,
where
\Delta(\alpha)=\int_{X}{}\bigl(\operatorname{ch}_{1}(\alpha)^{2}-2\operatorname{ch}_{0}(\alpha)\operatorname{ch}_{2}(\alpha)\bigr)
.
The same inequality holds for polynomial Bridgeland stability
by Li–Qin
[
40
, Theorem 1.1]
,
and a similar argument works in our case as well.

Joyce
[
30
, Theorem 6.21]
then showed that
the wall-crossing formulae for the
\mathrm{I}
and
\mathrm{I}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
invariants
hold for Gieseker stability, when changing
between ample classes
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in A^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
.
The strategy of the proof is to consider the family

\omega(t)=(1-t)\omega_{1}+t\omega_{2}
for
t\in[0,1]
,
and then identify a finite set of walls
W\subset[0,1]
,
so that all the involved invariants
are unchanged when no wall is crossed.
When crossing each wall
t\in W
,
the
\mathrm{I}
invariant at
t
can be expressed in terms of
the
\mathrm{I}
invariants at
t-\epsilon
and
t+\epsilon
for small
\epsilon>0
,
as in
Eq.

5.47
, despite the failure of
,
since all the Hall products involved
are covered by
7.3.3
,
so that
Eq.

4.33
holds for them.
Consequently, the wall-crossing formula
Eq.

5.47

is valid when crossing each wall, and hence globally.

The same strategy also works for
polynomial Bridgeland stability,
and for the self-dual invariants as well.
Note that there is no extra global finiteness requirements
for the self-dual invariants.
We have thus obtained the following result.

Theorem 0
.

In the situation of
7.3.2
,
let
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in A^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
be ample classes.
Then the wall-crossing formulae

Eqs.

5.47
and
5.49
hold
with
\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{\smash{L,\omega_{1}}},\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{\smash{L,\omega_{2}}}

in place of
\tau,\tilde{\tau}
.

\square

7.3.8. Wall-crossing for
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}
and
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
.

The methods in
7.3.6

do not work for the
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}
and
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
invariants directly,
since the Hall algebra and module operations
involved in defining the
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
functions
are not covered by
7.3.3
to
7.3.5
.

We can work around this problem following
Joyce
[
30
, Definition 6.22]
.
Namely, we redefine the invariants

\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
and
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})

by the relations
Eqs.

5.43
and
5.45
,
instead of integrating the
\epsilon
and
\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
functions.
Using this alternative definition, it immediately follows that
the wall-crossing formulae hold for these invariants.

Theorem 0
.

In the situation above,
let
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in A^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
be ample classes.
Then the wall-crossing formulae

Eqs.

5.48
and
5.50
hold
for the
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}
and
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
invariants redefined above,
with
\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{\smash{L,\omega_{1}}},\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{\smash{L,\omega_{2}}}

in place of
\tau,\tilde{\tau}
.

\square

However, since we have changed the definition of

\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
and
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
,
the no-pole theorems may no longer hold,
except when
X
is a del Pezzo surface, as discussed below.

7.3.10. Del Pezzo surfaces.

Now, assume that
X
is a
del Pezzo surface
,
also known as a
2
-dimensional Fano variety,
which means that the anti-canonical bundle
\smash{K_{X}^{-1}}
is ample.
By standard results on classification of algebraic surfaces,

X
is either
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
or a blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
at
d
points, where
0\leqslant d\leqslant 9
.

In this case, results in
7.3.3
to
7.3.5

can be strengthened to hold for

\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\geqslant}}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
in place of

\mathcal{M}^{\smash{>}}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
,
where
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\geqslant}}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})

consists of pairs
(E_{1},E_{2})
such that
the minimal slope of
E_{1}

is greater than or equal to the maximal slope of
E_{2}
.
This is because the operation
-\otimes K_{X}
on
\mathcal{C}

strictly increases the (polynomial) slope,
by the proof of
7.3.3
.
Consequently, the relation
Eq.

4.33

holds if
f\boxtimes g
is supported on

\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\geqslant}}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
,
and
Eq.

4.34
holds if

f\boxtimes h
is supported on the preimage of

\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\geqslant}}_{\smash{\alpha,j(\theta)}}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
in
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}

or on
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\geqslant}}_{\alpha}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{0}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{0}
.
These relations cover all the Hall algebra and Hall module operations
involved in defining the
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
functions,
and as a result, the modified invariants

\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
and
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})

coincide with the unmodified version,
defined via integrating the
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
functions.
See also Joyce
[
30
, Proposition 6.23]

for the case of Gieseker stability.
We have thus obtained the following:

Theorem 0
.

For
X
a del Pezzo surface,
the no-pole theorems,
5.6.2
and
5.6.4
,
hold for the
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}
and
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
invariants
defined above, and they
satisfy the wall-crossing formulae
Eqs.

5.48
and
5.50

with
\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{\smash{L,\omega_{1}}},\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{\smash{L,\omega_{2}}}

in place of
\tau,\tilde{\tau}
, for ample classes
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in A^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
.

The numerical invariants

\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}_{\alpha}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
and
\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})

are well-defined, and satisfy the wall-crossing formulae

Eqs.

5.57
and
5.59

with
\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{\smash{L,\omega_{1}}},\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{\smash{L,\omega_{2}}}

in place of
\tau,\tilde{\tau}
.

\square

7.3.12. Surfaces of Kodaira dimension 0.

On the other hand, if
X
is a surface of Kodaira dimension
0
,
that is, a K3 surface, an abelian surface, an Enriques surface,
or a hyperelliptic surface,
we have
c_{1}(K_{X})=0
,
so that
\operatorname{td}_{1}(X)=-c_{1}(K_{X})/2=0
.
It follows from
Eqs.

7.24
and
7.25

that we have the relations

\bar{\chi}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=0\ ,\qquad\bar{\chi}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha,\theta)=0

(7.26)

for all
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A}_{L}^{+})

and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}_{L}^{+})
,
where
\bar{\chi}
and
\bar{\chi}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
are as in

Eqs.

4.38
and
4.42
.
This means that the algebra
\Lambda(\mathcal{A}_{L}^{+})
in
4.4.2

is commutative, and the module
\Lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}_{L}^{+})
in
4.4.3

satisfies
x\cdot m=x^{\vee}\cdot m

for all
x\in\Lambda(\mathcal{A}_{L}^{+})
and
m\in\Lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}_{L}^{+})
.
Consequently, the Lie bracket on
\Lambda(\mathcal{A}_{L}^{+})

and the
\mathbin{\heartsuit}
action on
\Lambda^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}_{L}^{+})
vanish.
The wall-crossing formulae
Eqs.

5.48
and
5.50

together with
5.4.2
and
5.4.6

imply that the invariants

\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
and
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})

are independent of
\omega
.
See also Joyce
[
30
, Theorem 6.24]

for the case of Gieseker stability.
We state this as the following:

Theorem 0
.

For
X
a surface of Kodaira dimension
0
,
the invariants
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
and
\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{J}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})

defined in
7.3.8
are independent of
the ample class
\omega
.

\square

7.4   Donaldson–Thomas invariants

7.4.1.

Let
X
be a
Calabi–Yau threefold
,
that is, a smooth, projective threefold over
\mathbb{K}

with trivial canonical bundle,
K_{X}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X}
.
Joyce–Song
[
32
]
and
Kontsevich–Soibelman
[
35
]

defined
Donaldson–Thomas invariants

\mathrm{DT}_{\alpha}(\tau)\in\mathbb{Q}
, where
\tau
is a Gieseker stability condition
or a slope stability condition on

\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(X)
, and
\alpha
is a Chern character.
These invariants are defined by

\mathrm{DT}_{\alpha}(\tau)=-\int_{\mathcal{M}}{}(\mathbb{L}-1)\cdot\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)\cdot\nu_{\mathcal{M}}\,d\chi\ ,

(7.27)

where
\nu_{\mathcal{M}}
is the
Behrend function
,
as in Behrend
[
5
]
,
and
\int(-)\,d\chi
denotes applying the Euler characteristic map,
as in
5.6.6
, to the motivic integral
defined in
A.2.7
.
Joyce–Song showed that these invariants satisfy nice properties,
including wall-crossing formulae analogous to
Eq.

5.58
,
and
deformation invariance
,
meaning the invariants are unchanged under deformations of
X
.

In this setting,
the Euler form
\bar{\chi}(-,-)
in
Eq.

4.38

is replaced by

\bar{\chi}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\int_{X}\alpha_{1}\,\alpha_{2}^{\vee}\,\operatorname{td}(X)\ ,

(7.28)

so that for objects
E_{1},E_{2}\in\smash[b]{\textsf{{Coh}}}(X)

of Chern character
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
, respectively, we have

\displaystyle\bar{\chi}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{3}{}(-1)^{i}\dim\mathrm{Ext}^{i}(E_{2},E_{1})

\displaystyle=\bigl(\dim\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(E_{1},E_{2})-\dim\mathrm{Ext}^{0}(E_{1},E_{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt{}-\bigl(\dim\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(E_{2},E_{1})-\dim\mathrm{Ext}^{0}(E_{2},E_{1})\bigr)\ ,

where
\mathrm{Ext}^{i}(E_{1},E_{2})\simeq\mathrm{Ext}^{3-i}(E_{2},E_{1})^{\vee}

by Serre duality, and since
X
is Calabi–Yau.

In the situation of
Section

7.2
above,
for
X
a Calabi–Yau threefold, we can also define
Donaldson–Thomas invariants counting
both ordinary and self-dual objects in
\mathcal{A}_{L}
,
for the self-dual polynomial Bridgeland stability conditions

\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}
in
7.2.2
,
as well as invariants counting ordinary and self-dual objects
in
\mathcal{A}_{L,\omega}
, for the self-dual Bridgeland stability condition

\tau_{L,\omega}
in
7.2.5
.
We expect that these invariants should satisfy
wall-crossing formulae and deformation invariance,
and we hope to study these properties in future work.

7.4.2. Definition.

Let
X
be a Calabi–Yau threefold,
and use the notations in

7.2.2
,
7.2.3
, and
7.2.4
.
Fix a choice of
L
, and an ample class
\omega\in\smash{A^{1}}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
,
and we assume that the polynomial Bridgeland stability condition

\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}
satisfies
the openness and boundedness conditions
.

Then, for classes
\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A}_{L})
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}_{L})
,
define
Donaldson–Thomas invariants

\displaystyle\mathrm{DT}_{\alpha}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathcal{M}}{}(\mathbb{L}-1)\cdot\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})\cdot\nu_{\mathcal{M}}\,d\chi\ ,

(7.29)

\displaystyle\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}{}\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})\cdot\nu_{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\,d\chi\ ,

(7.30)

where
\nu_{\mathcal{M}}
and
\nu_{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}
are the Behrend functions
for
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
, as in
[
5
]
.
These are well-defined by the no-pole theorems,

5.6.2
and
5.6.4
.

Similarly, for
X
a threefold satisfying the
Bogomolov–Gieseker inequality in
[
4
]
,
choose the data
L,\omega
,
and for classes
\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A}_{L,\omega})
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}_{L,\omega})
,
define

\displaystyle\mathrm{DT}_{\alpha}(\tau_{L,\omega})

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathcal{M}}{}(\mathbb{L}-1)\cdot\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau_{L,\omega})\cdot\nu_{\mathcal{M}}\,d\chi\ ,

(7.31)

\displaystyle\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau_{L,\omega})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}{}\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau_{L,\omega})\cdot\nu_{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\,d\chi\ .

(7.32)

Note that
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
now denote moduli stacks for

\mathcal{A}_{L,\omega}
and
\mathcal{A}_{L,\omega}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
, instead of

\mathcal{A}_{L}
and
\mathcal{A}_{L}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
.
Invariants similar to
Eq.

7.31

for Bridgeland stability conditions
were already studied in the work of
Feyzbakhsh–Thomas
[
18
,
19
]
.

We formulate some expected properties of these invariants
in the following conjecture.

Conjecture 7.4.3
.

Let
X
be a Calabi–Yau threefold,
and fix a choice of
L
as above.

(i)

For any ample class
\omega\in\smash{A^{1}}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
,
the polynomial Bridgeland stability condition

\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega}
on
\mathcal{A}_{L}
satisfies
the openness and boundedness conditions
,
so that the Donaldson–Thomas invariants

\mathrm{DT}_{\alpha}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})
and
\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{L,\omega})

are defined.

(ii)

For ample classes
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\smash{A^{1}}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}

that are sufficiently close,
the Donaldson–Thomas invariants
defined above satisfy wall-crossing formulae
analogous to
Eqs.

5.58
and
5.60
,
with
\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{\smash{L,\omega_{1}}},\tau^{\smash{\mathrm{P}}}_{\smash{L,\omega_{2}}}

in place of
\tau,\tilde{\tau}
.
If, moreover,
X
satisfies the Bogomolov–Gieseker inequality,
then the same holds for the Bridgeland stability conditions

\tau_{L,\omega_{1}},\tau_{L,\omega_{2}}
.

(iii)

If
H^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})=0
,
then the Donaldson–Thomas invariants
defined above are unchanged under deformations of
X
,
which also induce unique deformations of
L
.

Appendix A
Rings of motives

A.1   Motives of algebraic stacks

A.1.1.

We briefly summarize the formalism of motives
(in the naïve sense of the word) of algebraic stacks,
following Joyce
[
29
, §4]
.

In the following, an
algebraic stack
over
\mathbb{K}

will refer to a stack on the site of
\mathbb{K}
-schemes
with the étale topology, with a smooth atlas.

Definition A.1.2
.

Let
\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{K}}
be the set of isomorphism classes of
\mathbb{K}
-varieties.
Define an abelian group
K_{0}(\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{K}})

as the quotient of the free abelian group
generated by symbols
\mu(X)
for

X\in\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{K}}
, by the relations

\mu(X)\sim\mu(Z)+\mu(X\setminus Z)

(A.1)

for closed subvarieties
Z\subset X
.

Define multiplication on
K_{0}(\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{K}})
by

\mu(X)\cdot\mu(Y)=\mu(X\times Y)\ .

(A.2)

This gives
K_{0}(\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{K}})
the structure of a commutative ring.

Denote
\mathbb{L}=\mu(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{K}}^{1})\in K_{0}(\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{K}})
.
We also define the following versions of rings of motives. Define

\displaystyle\mathrm{M}_{\mathbb{K}}

\displaystyle=K_{0}(\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{K}})\,[\mathbb{L}^{-1}]\otimes\mathbb{Q}\ ,

(A.3)

\displaystyle{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=K_{0}(\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{K}})\,[\mathbb{L}^{-1},\,(\mathbb{L}^{n}+\mathbb{L}^{n-1}+\cdots+1)^{-1}]\otimes\mathbb{Q}\ ,

(A.4)

\displaystyle{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}

\displaystyle=K_{0}(\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{K}})\,[\mathbb{L}^{-1},\,(\mathbb{L}-1)^{-1},\,(\mathbb{L}^{n}+\mathbb{L}^{n-1}+\cdots+1)^{-1}]\otimes\mathbb{Q}\ ,

(A.5)

\displaystyle{\smash{\widetilde{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}

\displaystyle={\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}/(\mathbb{L}-1)\ ,

(A.6)

with
\mathbb{L}^{n}+\mathbb{L}^{n-1}+\cdots+1
inverted for all
n\geqslant 0

in
Eqs.

A.4
to
A.5
.

A.1.3.

For example, one can deduce that

\displaystyle\mu(\mathbb{A}^{n})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}^{n}\ ,

(A.7)

\displaystyle\mu(\mathbb{P}^{n})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}^{n}+\mathbb{L}^{n-1}+\cdots+1\ ,

(A.8)

\displaystyle\mu(\mathrm{GL}(n))

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}{}(\mathbb{L}^{n}-\mathbb{L}^{i})\ ,

(A.9)

etc., in
K_{0}(\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{K}})
.

Definition A.1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack, locally of finite type.
We say that
\mathcal{X}

has affine stabilizers
,
if the stabilizer groups of all
\mathbb{K}
-points of
\mathcal{X}

are affine algebraic groups over
\mathbb{K}
.

Theorem A.1.5
.

Let
\mathrm{St}_{\mathbb{K}}
denote the set of
isomorphism classes of algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stacks
of finite type with affine stabilizers.
There is a unique map

\mu\colon\mathrm{St}_{\mathbb{K}}\longrightarrow{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}

extending the map
\mu\colon\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{K}}\to K_{0}(\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{K}})

in
Definition

A.1.2
,
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

If
\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{St}_{\mathbb{K}}
and

\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathcal{X}
is a closed substack, then

\mu(\mathcal{X})=\mu(\mathcal{Z})+\mu(\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{Z})\ .

(A.10)

(ii)

For any two
\mathbb{K}
-stacks
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\in\mathrm{St}_{\mathbb{K}}
,
we have

\mu(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})=\mu(\mathcal{X})\cdot\mu(\mathcal{Y})\ .

(A.11)

(iii)

For any special
\mathbb{K}
-group
G
acting on a
\mathbb{K}
-variety
X
,
we have

\mu(X)=\mu([X/G])\cdot\mu(G)\ ,

(A.12)

where
[X/G]
is the quotient stack.

See Joyce
[
29
, Theorem 4.10]
.
Here, a
special group
is an algebraic group
G

such that every principal
G
-bundle on a scheme is Zariski locally trivial,
as in Serre
[
51
, §4.1]
.
For example, the groups
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{a}}
,
\mathrm{GL}(n)
, and
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
are special;
finite products of special groups are special.
The group
\mathrm{O}(n)
is not special when
n>0
.

Example A.1.6
.

Here are some examples which will be useful later.
For
n\geqslant 0
, we have

\displaystyle\mu([*/\mathrm{GL}(n)])

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{\mathbb{L}^{n}-\mathbb{L}^{i}}\ ,

(A.13)

\displaystyle\mu([*/\mathrm{O}(2n)])

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}^{n}\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{\mathbb{L}^{2n}-\mathbb{L}^{2i}}\ ,

(A.14)

\displaystyle\mathllap{\mu([*/\mathrm{O}(2n+1)])={}}\mu([*/\mathrm{Sp}(2n)])

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}^{-n}\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{\mathbb{L}^{2n}-\mathbb{L}^{2i}}\ ,

(A.15)

where the cases for orthogonal groups are due to
Dhillon and Young
[
15
, Theorem 3.7]
.
Notice that when
r>0
, the group
\mathrm{O}(r)
is not special, and we have

\mu([*/\mathrm{O}(r)])\neq\mu(\mathrm{O}(r))^{-1}\ .

In particular, when
r=1
, we have

\mu([*/\mathbb{Z}_{2}])=1\neq 1/2
.

Next, we prove some properties of the motive of a stack.

Theorem A.1.7
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic stack of finite type
with affine stabilizers.

(i)

Let
\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
be a vector bundle of rank
n
. Then

\mu(\mathcal{Y})=\mu(\mathcal{X})\cdot\mathbb{L}^{n}\ .

(A.16)

(ii)

Let
\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
be a principal
G
-bundle,
where
G
is a special
\mathbb{K}
-group. Then

\mu(\mathcal{Y})=\mu(\mathcal{X})\cdot\mu(G)\ .

(A.17)

Proof 22
.

By a result of Kresch
[
37
, Proposition 3.5.9]
,
we may decompose
\mathcal{X}
into a finite disjoint union of
quotient stacks of the form
[X/\mathrm{GL}(m)]
,
where
X
is a
\mathbb{K}
-variety.
So we may assume that
\mathcal{X}=[X/\mathrm{GL}(m)]
.

For
Item

(i)
,
let
Y=X\times_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{Y}
, so that
\mathcal{Y}\simeq[Y/\mathrm{GL}(m)]
,
and
Y\to X
is a vector bundle of rank
n
.
Since all vector bundles are Zariski trivial,
we can deduce that
\mu(Y)=\mu(X)\cdot\mathbb{L}^{n}
.
Dividing both sides by
\mu(\mathrm{GL}(m))
,
we obtain that
\mu(\mathcal{Y})=\mu(\mathcal{X})\cdot\mathbb{L}^{n}
.

For
Item

(ii)
,
an analogous argument will work.

\Box

Theorem A.1.8
.

Let
G
be an affine algebraic group over
\mathbb{K}
,
acting linearly on a
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
\mathbb{A}^{n}
.
Then the motive of the quotient
[\mathbb{A}^{n}/G]
can be expressed as

\mu([\mathbb{A}^{n}/G])=\mathbb{L}^{n}\cdot\mu([*/G])\ .

(A.18)

Proof 23
.

We can regard
[\mathbb{A}^{n}/G]
as a vector bundle over
[*/G]

of rank
n
.
The theorem then follows from

Theorem

A.1.7

Item

(i)
.

\Box

Note that, as shown in
Example

A.1.6
,
the motive
\mu([*/G])
is not always equal to
\mu(G)^{-1}
,
unless
G
is special.

Theorem A.1.9
.

Let
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
be algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stacks of finite type,
and let
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a morphism.
Suppose that
f
induces a bijection of
\mathbb{K}
-points
and induces isomorphisms of stabilizer groups at all
\mathbb{K}
-points.

Then, there are decompositions into disjoint reduced locally closed substacks

\mathcal{X}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{X}_{i}\ ,\qquad\mathcal{Y}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{Y}_{i}\ ,

such that for each
i=1,\dotsc,n,

the restriction
f|_{\mathcal{X}_{i}}\colon\mathcal{X}_{i}\to\mathcal{Y}_{i}

is an isomorphism.

In particular,
if
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
have affine stabilizers, then

\mu(\mathcal{X})=\mu(\mathcal{Y})\ .

Proof 24
.

Taking reductions,
we may assume that
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are reduced.

Let
|\mathcal{X}|
be the underlying topological space of
\mathcal{X}
,
as in
[
39
, §5]
.
By
[
39
, Theorem 5.9.4]
,
the image
f(|\mathcal{X}|)\subset|\mathcal{Y}|
is constructible.
But since it contains all
\mathbb{K}
-points,
it must be the whole of
|\mathcal{Y}|
,
so
f
is surjective.

Therefore, by noetherian induction, it is enough to show that
there is an open substack
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{X}
,
such that
f|_{\mathcal{U}}
is an isomorphism onto an open substack of
\mathcal{Y}
.

Choose an open morphism
U\to\mathcal{X}

from an irreducible
\mathbb{K}
-variety
U
, with dense image.
For example, this could be a smooth covering of a dense, open substack of
\mathcal{X}
.
Shrinking
\mathcal{X}
if necessary, we assume that

U\to\mathcal{X}
is surjective.

Let us show that
f
induces isomorphisms of all stabilizer groups,
including at points other than
\mathbb{K}
-points.
Let
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{Y}}\to\mathcal{Y}

be the inertia stacks.
These morphisms are representable and of finite type.
Let
V=U\times_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{X}}
and

W=U\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{Y}}
.
Then the induced morphism of finite type algebraic spaces

p\colon V\to W
satisfies the property that, for any
u\in U(\mathbb{K})
,
the morphism of fibres
p_{u}\colon V_{u}\to W_{u}
is an isomorphism.
By generic flatness, shrinking
U
, and hence
\mathcal{X}
, if necessary,
we can assume that
V
and
W
are flat over
U
.
By
[
52
, Tag
05X0
]
,
p
is flat, and hence étale.
Since it is bijective on
\mathbb{K}
-points, it is an isomorphism,
which implies the claim.

By
[
52
, Tag
04YY
]
,
f
is representable by algebraic spaces.
Shrinking
U
again, we may assume that
f
is flat,
and that
f
is a reduced morphism, as in
[
21
, Definition 6.8.1]
,
since morphisms whose source is reduced
are generically reduced
[
52
, Tag
054Z
]
.
Now, fibres of
f
over
\mathbb{K}
-points
have to be either empty or
\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{K}
, so
f
is étale.
On the other hand, by
[
52
, Tag
0DUC
]
,
f
is universally injective,
so it is an open immersion,
and hence an isomorphism.

\Box

A.2   Stack functions

A.2.1.

We briefly summarize the theory of stack functions,
developed in
[
29
]
.
Stack functions on an algebraic stack
\mathcal{X}

are a version of motives relative to
\mathcal{X}
.
There are two versions of stack functions,
the representable version
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{X})
which includes only
representable morphisms to
\mathcal{X}
,
and the completed version
\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{X})
which includes all morphisms.

Definition A.2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack with affine stabilizers.
Consider pairs
(\mathcal{R},\rho)
,
where
\mathcal{R}
is an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack of finite type,
and
\rho\colon\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{X}
is a representable morphism.
The space of
stack functions
on
\mathcal{X}
is the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space

\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{X})=\Bigl(\bigoplus_{(\mathcal{R},\rho)}\mathbb{Q}\cdot[(\mathcal{R},\rho)]\Bigr)\Big/{\sim}\ ,

(A.19)

where we take the direct sum over
all isomorphism classes of such pairs
(\mathcal{R},\rho)
,
and
\sim
is generated by the relations

[(\mathcal{R},\rho)]\sim[(\mathcal{S},\rho|_{\mathcal{S}})]+[(\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{S},\rho|_{\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{S}})]

(A.20)

for closed substacks
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{R}
.

When the map
\rho
is clear from the context,
the generator
[(\mathcal{R},\rho)]
will be denoted by
[\mathcal{R}]
.

One can define multiplication on
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{X})
by

[\mathcal{R}]\cdot[\mathcal{S}]=[\mathcal{R}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{S}]\ .

(A.21)

If
\mathcal{X}
is of finite type, then this gives

\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{X})
the structure of a
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra.
Otherwise, the multiplication may not have a unit,
as
[\mathcal{X}]
will not be a valid element of
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{X})
.

A.2.3.

We also define a completed version
\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{X})
,
as in
[
29
, Definition 4.11]
,
where this was denoted by
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{X},\Upsilon,\Lambda)
.

Definition 0
.

Define
\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{X})

to be the quotient of
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{X})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}
,
as an
{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}
-module,
by the following relations:

•

For an algebraic stack
\mathcal{R}
of finite type
with affine stabilizers, a morphism
\rho\colon\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{X}
,
and a
\mathbb{K}
-variety
U
,
consider the composition
\mathcal{R}\times U\to\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{X}
,
where the first map is the projection.
Then we have

[\mathcal{R}\times U]\sim\mu(U)\cdot[\mathcal{R}]\ .

(A.22)

•

For a special algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-group
G
,
a
\mathbb{K}
-variety
U
acted on by
G
,
and a morphism
\rho\colon[U/G]\to\mathcal{X}
,
consider the composition
U\to[U/G]\to\mathcal{X}
,
where the first map is the quotient map.
Then we have

[U/G]\sim\mu(G)^{-1}\cdot[U]\ .

(A.23)

As in
[
29
, Theorem 4.13]
,
multiplication on
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{X})

induces a multiplication map on
\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{X})
.
We have a natural
\mathbb{Q}
-linear map

\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{X})\longrightarrow\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{X})

(A.24)

compatible with the multiplication maps.

As in
[
29
, Definition 4.11]
,
one could also use non-representable morphisms to
\mathcal{X}

to define
\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{X})
, and the result would be the same.

By definition and
[
29
, Proposition 4.15]
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{SF}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{K})

\displaystyle\simeq K_{0}(\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{K}})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\ ,

(A.25)

\displaystyle\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{K})

\displaystyle\simeq{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}\ .

(A.26)

Theorem A.2.5
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
and
g\colon\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Z}

be morphisms of algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stacks with affine stabilizers.

(i)

There is a pushforward map

f_{!}\colon\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{X})\longrightarrow\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{Y})\ ,

given on generators by

f_{!}\,[(\mathcal{R},\rho)]=[(\mathcal{R},f\circ\rho)]\ .

Moreover, we have
g_{!}\circ f_{!}=(g\circ f)_{!}
.

If
f
is representable, then there is also a pushforward map

f_{!}\colon\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{X})\longrightarrow\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{Y})\ .

Moreover, we have
g_{!}\circ f_{!}=(g\circ f)_{!}

if
f
and
g
are both representable.

(ii)

If
f
is of finite type, then there are pullback maps

\displaystyle f^{*}\colon\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{Y})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{X})\ ,

\displaystyle f^{*}\colon\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{Y})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{X})\ ,

given on generators by

f^{*}[(\mathcal{R},\rho)]=[(\mathcal{R}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{X},\rho^{\prime})]\ ,

where
\rho^{\prime}
is the induced morphism.
Moreover, we have
f^{*}\circ g^{*}=(g\circ f)^{*}

if
f
and
g
are both of finite type.

(iii)

Given a
2
-pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}\hbox to0.0pt{ $,$\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}

are algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stacks with affine stabilizers,
if
h
is of finite type,
then there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{X}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{Y})\hbox to0.0pt{ $.$\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{\prime})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h^{\prime})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{*}}

If, moreover,
f
is representable,
then there is a similar commutative diagram for
\mathrm{SF}(-)
.

This follows from
[
29
, Theorem 3.5 and §4.3]
.
The pushforward map
f_{!}
was denoted by
f_{*}
there.

A.2.6. Relation to the category of spans.

In the modern language of six-functor formalisms,
as in, for example, Scholze
[
50
]
,

Theorem

A.2.5
can be reformulated as follows:

Let
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Span}}}(\smash[b]{\textsf{{St}}}_{\mathbb{K}};\mathrm{ft},\mathrm{rep})

be the category of spans in the category

\smash[b]{\textsf{{St}}}_{\mathbb{K}}
of algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stacks with affine stabilizers,
where morphisms from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
are
spans
\mathcal{X}\leftarrow\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Y}
,
where the left arrow is of finite type
and the right arrow is representable.
Composition of spans are given by pullbacks.
Then
\mathrm{SF}
defines a functor

\mathrm{SF}\colon\smash[b]{\textsf{{Span}}}(\smash[b]{\textsf{{St}}}_{\mathbb{K}};\mathrm{ft},\mathrm{rep})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}\textnormal{-}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}\ ,

(A.27)

where
\mathbb{Q}\textnormal{-}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}
is the category of
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces,
sending a span
\mathcal{X}\xleftarrow{f}\mathcal{Z}\xrightarrow{g}\mathcal{Y}

to the map
g_{!}\circ f^{*}\colon\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{Y})
.

Similarly, let
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Span}}}(\smash[b]{\textsf{{St}}}_{\mathbb{K}};\mathrm{ft})

be the category of spans in
\smash[b]{\textsf{{St}}}_{\mathbb{K}}

whose left leg is of finite type and right leg is arbitrary.
Then
\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}
defines a functor

\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}\colon\smash[b]{\textsf{{Span}}}(\smash[b]{\textsf{{St}}}_{\mathbb{K}};\mathrm{ft})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}\textnormal{-}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}\ .

(A.28)

A.2.7. Motivic integration.

For an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack
\mathcal{X}
of finite type with affine stabilizers,
pushing forward along the structure morphism
\mathcal{X}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{K}

defines a map

\int_{\mathcal{X}}\colon\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{X})\longrightarrow{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}\ ,

(A.29)

called
motivic integration
.
Composing with the map
Eq.

A.24
gives a map

\int_{\mathcal{X}}\colon\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{X})\longrightarrow{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}\ .

(A.30)

A.2.8. Local stack functions.

There is another variant of stack functions,
called
local stack functions
,
which allows infinite linear combinations of stack functions
as long as they are locally finite.

Definition 0
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack with affine stabilizers.
Following
[
29
, Definition 3.9]
, define

\displaystyle\mathrm{LSF}(\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{X}}\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{U})\ ,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{LSF}}(\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{X}}\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{SF}}}(\mathcal{U})\ ,

where we take the limit over all finite type open substacks
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{X}
,
with respect to restriction along inclusions.

For an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack with affine stabilizers
\mathcal{R}
,
not necessarily of finite type,
and for a finite type morphism
\rho\colon\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{X}
, there is an element

[(\mathcal{R},\rho)]\in\mathrm{LSF}(\mathcal{X})\ ,

as in
[
29
, Definition 3.9]
.

For an infinite collection of elements
(f_{i}\in\mathrm{LSF}(\mathcal{X}))_{i\in I}
,
and another element
f\in\mathrm{LSF}(\mathcal{X})
, we say that

\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}=f\ ,

or that the infinite sum
converges
to
f
,
if for any finite type open substack
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{X}
,
there are only finitely many
i\in I
such that
f_{i}|_{\mathcal{U}}
is non-zero,
and we have the finite sum
\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}|_{\mathcal{U}}=f|_{\mathcal{U}}
.

Similarly, one can define convergence in
\widehat{\mathrm{LSF}}(\mathcal{X})
.

A.3   Virtual rank projections

A.3.1.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack with affine stabilizers.
Joyce
[
29
, Definition 5.13]

defined
virtual rank projections

\Pi^{\mathrm{vi}}_{n}\colon\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{X})\longrightarrow\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{X})

(A.31)

for all integers
n\geqslant 0
, inducing a decomposition

\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{X})=\bigoplus_{n\geqslant 0}\mathrm{SF}_{\smash{(n)}}(\mathcal{X})\ ,

(A.32)

where
\Pi^{\mathrm{vi}}_{n}
acts as the identity on
\mathrm{SF}_{\smash{(n)}}(\mathcal{X})
,
and as zero on
\mathrm{SF}_{\smash{(m)}}(\mathcal{X})
for
m\neq n
.
We say that elements of
\mathrm{SF}_{\smash{(n)}}(\mathcal{X})
have
(
pure
)
virtual rank
n
.

Roughly speaking, a stack function
[\mathcal{R}]\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{X})

has virtual rank
n

if stabilizer groups in
\mathcal{R}
look like tori
T
of rank
n
.
In general, such an element
[\mathcal{R}]
is decomposed into pieces
with virtual ranks between the minimum
central rank

(i.e. rank of the centre)
and the maximal rank of stabilizer groups in
\mathcal{R}
.
For example,
[*/\mathrm{GL}(n)]
has mixed virtual ranks
1,\dotsc,n
.

As in
[
29
, Proposition 5.14]
,
the virtual rank projections
\Pi^{\mathrm{vi}}_{n}
commute with
the pushforward maps
f_{!}

for all representable morphisms
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
of algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stacks
with affine stabilizers. In other words, we have

f_{!}(\mathrm{SF}_{\smash{(n)}}(\mathcal{X}))\subset\mathrm{SF}_{\smash{(n)}}(\mathcal{Y})

(A.33)

for all
n\geqslant 0
.

A.3.2.

By results in Joyce
[
29
, §6]
,
the virtual rank decomposition satisfies
the following important property related to motivic integration.

Theorem 0
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack with affine stabilizers,
and let
f\in\mathrm{SF}_{(n)}(\mathcal{X})
be a stack function
of pure virtual rank
n
. Then we have

\int_{\mathcal{X}}f\in(\mathbb{L}-1)^{-n}\cdot{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\circ}\ ,

(A.34)

with notations as in
Eqs.

A.4
and
A.30
.

Appendix B
Stacks in categories

In this
Appendix
, we study the notion of
an
algebraic stack in categories
.
This should be seen as a geometric incarnation of a category,
whereas an ordinary algebraic stack
is a geometric incarnation of a groupoid.
From such a stack in categories,
one can obtain an underlying ordinary stack
by forgetting the non-invertible arrows;
one can also extract an underlying category
by forgetting the geometric structure.

The notion of stacks in categories in similar contexts
was discussed in Joyce
[
26
, §7]

and Behrend–Ronagh
[
7
]
.

In the following, we always use the term
2
-category

to mean a
(2,1)
-category, where
all
2
-morphisms are invertible.

B.1   Stacks in categories

Definition B.1.1
.

Let
Cat
be the
2
-category of small categories,
whose morphisms are functors
and
2
-morphisms are natural isomorphisms.
Let
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Gpd}}}\subset\smash[b]{\textsf{{Cat}}}
be the full subcategory of small groupoids.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a site.
A
stack in categories
on
\mathcal{C}

is a
2
-functor

\mathcal{X}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\longrightarrow\smash[b]{\textsf{{Cat}}}

(B.1)

that satisfies the
2
-sheaf property,
meaning that it takes coverings in
\mathcal{C}
to
2
-limits in
Cat
.
In particular, if
\mathcal{C}=\smash[b]{\textsf{{Sch}}}_{\mathbb{K}}

is the site of
\mathbb{K}
-schemes, equipped with the étale topology,
then
\mathcal{X}
will be called a
\mathbb{K}
-stack in categories
.

For any such
\mathcal{X}
,
composing with the limit-preserving
2
-functor

(-)^{\simeq}\colon\smash[b]{\textsf{{Cat}}}\to\smash[b]{\textsf{{Gpd}}}

forgetting the non-invertible arrows
defines the
underlying stack

\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\to\smash[b]{\textsf{{Gpd}}}
,
with a natural inclusion
\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}\to\mathcal{X}
.

Similarly, composing with the
2
-functor

(-)^{I}=\smash[b]{\textsf{{Fun}}}(I,-)\colon\smash[b]{\textsf{{Cat}}}\to\smash[b]{\textsf{{Cat}}}
,
where
I=(\bullet\to\bullet)
,
assigns to every stack in categories
\mathcal{X}

another stack in categories
\mathcal{X}^{I}
,
which is the
stack of arrows
in
\mathcal{X}
.
There are the source and target projections
s,t\colon\mathcal{X}^{I}\to\mathcal{X}
.
If
\mathcal{X}
is a stack in groupoids, then
\mathcal{X}^{I}\simeq\mathcal{X}
.

B.1.2. Relative groupoids.

A morphism
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}

of stacks in categories is called a
relative groupoid
,
if the induced morphism

\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}\underset{\mathcal{Y}}{\times}\mathcal{Y}^{\simeq}

is an isomorphism.

Lemma 0
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a site,
and
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
a morphism
of stacks in categories on
\mathcal{C}
that is a relative groupoid.
Let
\mathcal{W}
be a stack in groupoids on
\mathcal{C}
,
and let
g\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a morphism.

Then the natural morphism

\mathcal{W}\underset{\mathcal{Y}^{\simeq}}{\times}\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}\longrightarrow\mathcal{W}\underset{\mathcal{Y}}{\times}\mathcal{X}

(B.2)

is an isomorphism.
In particular,
\mathcal{W}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{X}
is a stack in groupoids.

Proof 25
.

We have

\mathcal{W}\underset{\mathcal{Y}^{\simeq}}{\times}\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}\simeq\mathcal{W}\underset{\mathcal{Y}^{\simeq}}{\times}\Bigl(\mathcal{X}\underset{\mathcal{Y}}{\times}\mathcal{Y}^{\simeq}\Bigr)\simeq\mathcal{W}\underset{\mathcal{Y}}{\times}\mathcal{X}\ .

\Box

Definition B.1.4
.

A
\mathbb{K}
-stack in categories
\mathcal{X}
is
representable
,
if
\mathcal{X}\simeq\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}
is representable by an algebraic space.

A morphism of
\mathbb{K}
-stacks in categories

f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
is
representable
,
if for any
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
U
and any morphism
U\to\mathcal{Y}
,
the
2
-pullback
U\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{X}
is representable.
Equivalently by
B.1.2
,
f
is a relative groupoid
and the morphism
\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}\to\mathcal{Y}^{\simeq}
is representable.

Definition B.1.5
.

An
algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack in categories

is a
\mathbb{K}
-stack in categories
\mathcal{X}
, satisfying the following conditions.

(i)

The
\mathbb{K}
-stack
\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}
is an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack.

(ii)

The
\mathbb{K}
-stack
(\mathcal{X}^{I})^{\simeq}
is an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack.

Lemma B.1.6
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack in categories.
Then

(i)

The morphisms

\displaystyle\Delta\colon\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}\underset{\mathbb{K}}{\times}\mathcal{X}\ ,

\displaystyle(s,t)\colon\mathcal{X}^{I}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}\underset{\mathbb{K}}{\times}\mathcal{X}

are representable.

(ii)

\mathcal{X}^{I}
is an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack in categories.

Proof 26
.

For
Item

(i)
,
it is clear that the morphisms are relative groupoids,
and it remains to show that the corresponding morphisms
of underlying stacks in groupoids are representable.
The representability of
\Delta\colon\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}\to\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}\times\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}

follows from the algebraicity of
\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}
.
To show that
(s,t)\colon(\mathcal{X}^{I})^{\simeq}\to\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}\times\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}

is representable, by
[
39
, Corollary 8.1.1]
,
it suffices to show that
it induces injections on stabilizer groups,
which is true by definition.

For
Item

(ii)
,
it remains to show that
((\mathcal{X}^{I})^{I})^{\simeq}
is an algebraic stack.
One can express it as an iterated fibre product

((\mathcal{X}^{I})^{I})^{\simeq}\simeq\Bigl((\mathcal{X}^{I})^{\simeq}\underset{\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}}{\times}(\mathcal{X}^{I})^{\simeq}\Bigr)\underset{(\mathcal{X}^{I})^{\simeq}}{\times}\Bigl((\mathcal{X}^{I})^{\simeq}\underset{\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}}{\times}(\mathcal{X}^{I})^{\simeq}\Bigr)\ ,

(B.3)

as it parametrizes commutative squares in
\mathcal{X}
.
Since
\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}
and
(\mathcal{X}^{I})^{\simeq}
are algebraic,
so is
((\mathcal{X}^{I})^{I})^{\simeq}
.

\Box

Definition B.1.7
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack in categories,
and let
S
be an algebraic space over
\mathbb{K}
.
The category
\mathcal{X}(S)

is called the category of
S
-points
in
\mathcal{X}
.

For any two objects
x,y\in\mathcal{X}(S)
, we may form the fibre product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}^{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\hbox to0.0pt{ ,\hss}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x,y)}

(B.4)

where the algebraic
S
-space

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)

is called the
mapping space
from
x
to
y
.

Unwinding the definitions, we see that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)
represents the functor
that assigns to any
S
-scheme
U

the set
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{X}(U)}(\pi^{*}x,\pi^{*}y)
,
where
\pi\colon U\to S
is the structure map.
In particular, we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)(S)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{X}(S)}(x,y)
.
This endows
\mathcal{X}(S)
with the structure of
a category enriched in algebraic
S
-spaces.

By
B.1.2
, we also have

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)\simeq S\underset{\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}\times\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}}{\times}(\mathcal{X}^{I})^{\simeq}\ ,

(B.5)

which is now a fibre product of algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stacks.

Example B.1.8
.

Let
A
be a finite-dimensional associative
\mathbb{K}
-algebra.
One can construct an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack in categories
[*/A]
as follows.

Let
[*/A]^{\mathrm{pre}}\colon\smash[b]{\textsf{{Sch}}}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\to\smash[b]{\textsf{{Cat}}}

be the
2
-functor which sends a
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
U

to the category of
trivial
A
-bundles
on
U
,
that is, the category with a unique object,
whose endomorphism ring is
\mathrm{Hom}(U,A)
,
the set of morphisms from
U
to
A
,
with a ring structure induced by that of
A
.
Define
[*/A]
to be the stackification of
[*/A]^{\mathrm{pre}}
.

For a
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
U
, the category
[*/A](U)

is the category of
principal
A
-bundles
on
U
.
When
U
is a
\mathbb{K}
-variety, a principal
A
-bundle on
U

can be defined as a vector bundle on
U
with a right
A
-action,
such that the fibres over
\mathbb{K}
-points are isomorphic to
A
as right
A
-modules.

There is a unique
\mathbb{K}
-point in
[*/A]
,
with endomorphism ring isomorphic to
A
.
The morphism
*\to[*/A]
is the
universal principal
A
-bundle
.

We observe that

[*/A]^{\simeq}\simeq[*/A^{\times}]\ ,\qquad([*/A]^{I})^{\simeq}\simeq[A/(A^{\times})^{2}]\ ,

where
A^{\times}
is the group of invertible elements in
A
,
regarded as an algebraic group,
and
(A^{\times})^{2}
acts on
A
by

(a,b)\cdot c=a^{-1}cb
,
where
a,b\in A^{\times}
and
c\in A
.
This shows that
[*/A]
is an
algebraic
stack in categories.

In particular, if we denote by
*\in[*/A](\mathbb{K})
the unique object, then

\mathrm{Hom}_{[*/A]}(*,*)\simeq\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{K}\underset{[*/A^{\times}]^{2}}{\times}[A/(A^{\times})^{2}]\simeq A\ .

(B.6)

However, notice that by
B.1.2
, one has

\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{K}\underset{[*/A]}{\times}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{K}\simeq A^{\times}\ ,

(B.7)

instead of
A
, since fibre products do not see
non-invertible
2
-morphisms.

B.2   Additive, exact, and linear stacks

We now define additive, exact, and linear stacks,
which are stacks in categories with extra structures.

B.2.1. Additive stacks.

Let
AddCat
be the
2
-category of small additive categories,
whose morphisms are additive functors,
and
2
-morphisms are natural isomorphisms.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a site.
An
additive stack
on
\mathcal{C}

is a
2
-functor
\mathcal{X}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\to\smash[b]{\textsf{{AddCat}}}

that satisfies the
2
-sheaf property.

Since every additive category has a symmetric monoidal structure
given by the direct sum,
which is preserved by additive functors,
every additive stack
\mathcal{X}
carries two morphisms

\displaystyle 0\colon*\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}\ ,

\displaystyle{\oplus}\colon\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}\ ,

which establish
\mathcal{X}
as a commutative monoid object
in the
2
-category of stacks in categories,
or in other words, a stack that takes values in
the
2
-category of symmetric monoidal categories
and symmetric monoidal functors.

An
additive
