\mathbb{K}
-stack

is an additive stack on the site
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Sch}}}_{\mathbb{K}}

of
\mathbb{K}
-schemes, with the étale topology.

An
additive algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack

is an additive
\mathbb{K}
-stack
\mathcal{X}

which is also an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack in categories.

For an additive algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack
\mathcal{X}
,
and two
\mathbb{K}
-points
x,y\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{K})
,
the mapping space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)

in
Definition

B.1.7

carries the structure of an abelian group algebraic space over
\mathbb{K}
.
This gives the additive category
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{K})

an enrichment over abelian group algebraic spaces over
\mathbb{K}
.

B.2.2. Exact stacks.

Let
ExCat
be the
2
-category of small exact categories,
whose morphisms are exact functors,
and
2
-morphisms are natural isomorphisms.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a site.
An
exact stack
on
\mathcal{C}

is a
2
-functor
\mathcal{X}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\to\smash[b]{\textsf{{ExCat}}}

that satisfies the
2
-sheaf property.

Every exact stack
\mathcal{X}

is also an additive
\mathbb{K}
-stack.

The
2
-functor

(-)^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}}\colon\smash[b]{\textsf{{ExCat}}}\longrightarrow\smash[b]{\textsf{{ExCat}}}

sending an exact category
\mathcal{D}

to the category whose objects are exact sequences in
\mathcal{D}
,
and morphisms are commutative diagrams,
assigns to each exact stack
\mathcal{X}

another exact stack
\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}}
.

An
exact
\mathbb{K}
-stack

is an exact stack on the site
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Sch}}}_{\mathbb{K}}

of
\mathbb{K}
-schemes, with the étale topology.

B.2.3. Exact algebraic stacks.

An
exact algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack

is an exact
\mathbb{K}
-stack
\mathcal{X}
, satisfying the following conditions:

•

\mathcal{X}
is an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack in categories.

•

The
\mathbb{K}
-stack
(\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}})^{\simeq}

is an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack.

Lemma 0
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an exact algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack.
Then

(i)

The morphisms

d_{1},\ d_{2}\colon\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}^{I}\ ,

sending an exact sequence to its constituting arrows,
are representable.

(ii)

\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}}
is an exact algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack.

Proof 27
.

For
Item

(i)
,
it is enough to verify that these morphisms
induce injections of stabilizer groups of the
underlying stacks in groupoids,
which is true since every exact sequence consists of
a monomorphism and an epimorphism.

For
Item

(ii)
,
we need to show that
((\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}})^{I})^{\simeq}

and
((\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}})^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}})^{\simeq}

are algebraic stacks.
The proof is similar to that of

Lemma

B.1.6

Item

(ii)
,
using different iterated fibre products,
which we omit here.

\Box

B.2.5. Internal pushforwards and pullbacks.

Pushforwards along inclusions exist in an exact category,
and are preserved by exact functors,
as in, for example,
[
34
, Appendix A]
.
Therefore, given an exact stack
\mathcal{X}
,
there is a pushforward morphism

\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}}\underset{\pi_{1},\,\mathcal{X},\,s}{\times}\mathcal{X}^{I}\longrightarrow(\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}})^{I}\ ,

(B.8)

where
\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}}
parametrizes the inclusion,

\mathcal{X}^{I}
parametrizes the other map,
and
(\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}})^{I}
parametrizes the result,
which is a square where two opposite edges are inclusions.
The map
\pi_{1}\colon\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}}\to\mathcal{X}
sends an exact sequence to its first object.

Dually, pullbacks along projections exist,
and there is a pullback morphism

\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}}\underset{\pi_{2},\,\mathcal{X},\,t}{\times}\mathcal{X}^{I}\longrightarrow(\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{ex}}})^{I}\ ,

(B.9)

where
\pi_{2}
sends an exact sequence to its last object.

B.2.6. Linear stacks.

Finally, we define a notion of linear algebraic stacks,
which are the
\mathbb{K}
-linear category version of
stacks in categories.
See also Behrend–Ronagh
[
7
, §1.2]

for a similar concept.

Definition 0
.

A
linear algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack

is an additive algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack
\mathcal{X}
,
such that

(i)

The algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stacks
\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}
and
(\mathcal{X}^{I})^{\simeq}

are locally of finite type.

(ii)

There is a
[*/\mathbb{A}^{1}]
-action on
\mathcal{X}
,
compatible with the additive structures.

Here,
[*/\mathbb{A}^{1}]
is regarded as a commutative monoid object
in the
2
-category of
\mathbb{K}
-stacks in categories.
We require that the action is
compatible

with the additive structures,
which means that the action morphism
[*/\mathbb{A}^{1}]\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}

is bilinear, in that for any
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
S
and any
x,y\in\mathcal{X}(S)
,
the induced action of

\mathrm{End}_{\smash{[*/\mathbb{A}^{1}](S)}}(*)\simeq\mathcal{O}_{S}(S)
on

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{X}(S)}(x,y)

is bilinear, establishing
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{X}(S)}(x,y)

as an
\mathcal{O}_{S}(S)
-module.

For
\mathbb{K}
-points
x,y\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{K})
,
the abelian group algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)

is locally of finite type and acted on by
\mathbb{A}^{1}
,
so it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Therefore, the category
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{K})
has the structure
of a
\mathbb{K}
-linear category.

In particular, stabilizer groups of
\mathbb{K}
-points in
\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}

are linear algebraic groups,
which also justifies the terminology.

A
morphism of linear algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stacks

is a
[*/\mathbb{A}^{1}]
-equivariant morphism of the underlying additive
\mathbb{K}
-stacks.
A
2
-morphism
between such morphisms is
a
[*/\mathbb{A}^{1}]
-equivariant
2
-morphism
of the underlying morphisms of additive
\mathbb{K}
-stacks.

An
exact linear algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack

is an exact algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack
that is also a linear algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack.
A
morphism of exact linear algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stacks

is a
[*/\mathbb{A}^{1}]
-equivariant morphism of the underlying exact
\mathbb{K}
-stacks.
A
2
-morphism
between such morphisms is
a
[*/\mathbb{A}^{1}]
-equivariant
2
-morphism
of the underlying morphisms of exact
\mathbb{K}
-stacks.

B.3   Self-dual stacks

We also define a self-dual category version of
stacks in categories, called
self-dual stacks
.

Definition B.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a site, and let
\mathcal{X}
be a stack in categories on
\mathcal{C}
.

The
opposite stack
of
\mathcal{X}
is given by the
2
-functor

\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\overset{\mathcal{X}}{\longrightarrow}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Cat}}}\overset{(-)^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}}{\longrightarrow}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Cat}}}\ .

This defines an automorphism
of the
2
-category of stacks in categories on
\mathcal{C}
.
Note that the
2
-functor
(-)^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\colon\smash[b]{\textsf{{Cat}}}\to\smash[b]{\textsf{{Cat}}}

reverses the direction of natural isomorphisms.

A
self-dual structure
on
\mathcal{X}

consists of an isomorphism
D\colon\mathcal{X}\simeq\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}
,
and a
2
-morphism
\eta\colon D^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\circ D\simeq\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{X}}
,
such that
D\circ\eta=\eta^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\circ D\colon D\circ D^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\circ D\simeq D
.
Equivalently, a self-dual structure on
\mathcal{X}

is given by a compatible way to choose
a self-dual structure on the category

\mathcal{X}(U)
for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
.

A
self-dual stack
on
\mathcal{C}

is a stack in categories on
\mathcal{C}
together with a self-dual structure.
We denote this data as a triple
(\mathcal{X},D,\eta)
.

A
self-dual
\mathbb{K}
-stack
,
self-dual algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack
, etc.,
is a
\mathbb{K}
-stack, or algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack, etc., together with a self-dual structure.
When the stack has additional structure,
such as an additive or exact structure,
we require that the morphism
D
respects these structures.

B.3.2. The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-fixed locus.

Let
(\mathcal{X},D,\eta)
be a self-dual stack on
\mathcal{C}
.
There is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action
(\mathcal{X}^{\simeq},f,\zeta)
on
\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}
,
where
f
is the composition

f\colon\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}\overset{D}{\longrightarrow}(\mathcal{X}^{\simeq})^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\overset{i}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}\ ,

where
i
is the identification sending each arrow to its inverse,
and
\zeta\colon f^{2}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.1097pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\Rightarrow}}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}}
is induced from
\eta
. Define

\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}=(\mathcal{X}^{\simeq})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}

(B.10)

as the fixed locus of this
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action,
as a
2
-limit in the category of stacks in categories.

Explicitly, for any
U\in\mathcal{C}
, we have

\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(U)\simeq\mathcal{X}(U)^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\ ,

(B.11)

where the right-hand side is the groupoid of self-dual objects,
as in
Definition

3.1.2
.
In particular, for a self-dual
\mathbb{K}
-stack
\mathcal{X}
,
the
\mathbb{K}
-points in
\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
are the self-dual
\mathbb{K}
-points in
\mathcal{X}
,
i.e. self-dual objects in
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{K})
.

Example B.3.3
.

Let
n\geqslant 0
be an integer,
and let
\mathrm{Mat}(n)
be the
\mathbb{K}
-algebra of
n\times n
matrices over
\mathbb{K}
.
Let
\mathcal{X}=[*/\mathrm{Mat}(n)]
, as in
Example

B.1.8
.
Then
\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}=[*/\mathrm{Mat}(n)^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}]
.
Define a self-dual structure on
\mathcal{X}

by the isomorphism
D\colon\mathcal{X}\simeq\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}
induced by the map

(-)^{\mathrm{t}}\colon\mathrm{Mat}(n)\simeq\mathrm{Mat}(n)^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}

sending a matrix to its transpose.
A
2
-morphism
\eta\colon D^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\circ D\simeq\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{X}}

is then given by an invertible element in the centre of
\mathrm{Mat}(n)
,
which must be of the form
\varepsilon\cdot I_{n}
for
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}
,
where
I_{n}
is the identity matrix.
The condition that
D\circ\eta=\eta^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\circ D

means that
\varepsilon\cdot I_{n}=(\varepsilon\cdot I_{n})^{-\mathrm{t}}
,
or
\varepsilon=\pm 1
.

We have
\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}\simeq[*/\mathrm{GL}(n)]
.
The morphism
f\colon\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}\to\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}
induced by
D

is given by the morphism of algebraic groups

(-)^{-\mathrm{t}}\colon\mathrm{GL}(n)\to\mathrm{GL}(n)
,
taking a matrix to the inverse of its transpose.
We have
\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\simeq[*/\mathrm{O}(n)]
when
\varepsilon=1
,
and when
\varepsilon=-1
, we have

\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\simeq[*/\mathrm{Sp}(n)]
if
n
is even, or
\varnothing
if
n
is odd.

Lemma B.3.4
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},D,\eta)
be a self-dual linear algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack. Then,

(i)

\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
is an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack locally of finite type,
and has affine stabilizers

(
Definition

A.1.4
).

(ii)

The natural morphism
\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\to\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}
is of finite type.

Proof 28
.

Consider the pullback of algebraic stacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}\times\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}\hbox to0.0pt{ ,\hss}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{id},\,D)}

(B.12)

where
\mathcal{Y}
can be seen as the fixed locus of
D

as a
\mathbb{Z}
-action instead of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action.
There is an induced morphism
j\colon\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\to\mathcal{Y}
.
We claim that
j
is a closed immersion.

Indeed, let
U
be any affine
\mathbb{K}
-scheme,
and let
x\colon U\to\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}
be a morphism.
Write
x^{\vee}=D\circ x
, and write

H=\mathrm{Isom}_{\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}}(x,x^{\vee})=U\underset{\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}}{\times}\mathcal{Y}\ ,

which is an algebraic
U
-space, such that for any
U
-scheme
f\colon V\to U
,
its
V
-points parametrize isomorphisms between

x\circ f
and
x^{\vee}\circ f
in
\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}(V)
.
There is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
H

induced by
D
and
\eta
. Write
H^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\subset H
for the fixed locus,
which is now a closed subspace of
H
,
since
H
is separated. We claim that

H^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\simeq U\underset{\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}}{\times}\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\ .

(B.13)

This is because for any
U
-scheme
f\colon V\to U
,
the
V
-points of both sides parametrize
isomorphisms
\phi\colon x\circ f\simeq x^{\vee}\circ f

such that
\phi^{\vee}=\phi\circ\eta
, where
\phi^{\vee}=D(\phi)
.
This shows that
j
is a closed immersion.

To prove
Item

(i)
,
since
\mathcal{Y}
is locally of finite type, so is
\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
.
For
(x,\phi)\in\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathbb{K})
,
with
x\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{K})
and
\phi
as above,
the stabilizer group
\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(x,\phi)
is affine,
since it can be identified with
\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{X}}(x)^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}
,
where
(-)^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}
is as in
4.1.2
,
and the involution on
\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{X}}(x)
is given by

e\mapsto\phi^{-1}\circ e^{\vee}\circ\phi
.

For
Item

(ii)
,
the morphism
\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}
above is of finite type,
and so is
\mathcal{X}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}\to\mathcal{X}^{\simeq}
.

\Box

Appendix C
2-vector bundles

C.1.1.

We define a notion of
2
-vector bundles
,
also known as
vector bundle stacks
in the literature.
For a
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
U
, a
2
-vector bundle over
U

is an algebraic stack that looks like a quotient stack

[E^{1}/E^{0}]\longrightarrow U\ ,

where
E^{1}
and
E^{0}
are vector bundles over
U
,
and
E^{0}
acts on
E^{1}
via a morphism of vector bundles
d\colon E^{0}\to E^{1}
.
This can be thought of as the
total space
of the two-term complex

E^{\bullet}[1]
, generalizing the notion of the total space of a vector bundle.

In fact, there is a general construction of total spaces of perfect complexes
in derived algebraic geometry (see Toën
[
59
, p. 46]
),
which produces, in general, derived stacks.
For two-term complexes,
the derived stacks obtained this way are classical
1
-stacks,
and our goal here is to describe this stack classically.

A
2
-vector bundle over a point is a
2
-vector space
.
There is a notion of a
2
-vector space that appeared in
Baez–Crans
[
2
, §3]
,
which is essentially equivalent to ours,
except that we have assumed all vector spaces to be finite-dimensional.
However, one should be aware that there are
very different notions of
2
-vector spaces in the literature,
most notably that of Kapranov–Voevodsky
[
33
]
.

2
-vector bundles were perhaps first studied by Deligne
[
13
, §1.4]
,
under the name of
Picard stacks
,
in a more general setting of abelian sheaves.
They were also discussed under the name of
vector bundle stacks

in Behrend–Fantechi
[
6
, §§1–2]
.

C.1.2. Abelian stacks.

A
2
-group
is a small monoidal groupoid
\mathcal{G}
,
such that for any object
x\in\mathcal{G}
,
left and right multiplication by
x
are equivalences.

An
abelian
2
-group
is a symmetric monoidal groupoid
that is also a
2
-group.

Let
2\textnormal{-}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Ab}}}
denote the

2
-category of abelian
2
-groups,
whose morphisms are symmetric monoidal functors,
and
2
-morphisms are monoidal natural isomorphisms.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a site.
An
abelian stack
over
\mathcal{C}
is a
2
-functor

\mathcal{X}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}\to 2\textnormal{-}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Ab}}}
,
which is also a stack in groupoids over
\mathcal{C}
.
This defines the
2
-category of abelian stacks over
\mathcal{C}
.

An abelian stack
\mathcal{X}
naturally comes with morphisms

\displaystyle 0\colon*

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}\ ,

\displaystyle{+}\colon\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}\ ,

establishing
\mathcal{X}
as an abelian group object
in stacks in groupoids over
\mathcal{C}
,
in a suitable
2
-categorical sense.

When
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Sch}_{\mathcal{S}}
,
the category of schemes over a
\mathbb{K}
-stack
\mathcal{S}
,
with the étale topology,
an abelian stack over
\mathcal{C}
is called an
abelian
\mathcal{S}
-stack
.

Deligne defined a
Picard stack

to be an abelian stack that is
strictly commutative

[
13
, Definition 1.4.5]
,
and showed that this condition ensures that
any Picard stack is a quotient stack of abelian sheaves

[
13
, Lemma 1.4.13]
.

Definition C.1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack.

A
module
\mathcal{S}
-stack

is an abelian stack
\mathcal{X}
over
\mathcal{S}
,
together with an
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{S}}
-action
that respects the abelian stack structures.
This means that the action morphism
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{S}}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}

is bilinear.

A
morphism of module
\mathcal{S}
-stacks

is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{S}}
-equivariant morphism of abelian stacks.
A
2
-morphism
between such morphisms
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{S}}
-equivariant
2
-morphism of
the underlying morphisms of abelian stacks.

Definition C.1.4
.

Let
U
be a
\mathbb{K}
-scheme.

Let
E^{0},E^{1}\to U
be vector bundles of finite rank,
and let
d\colon E^{0}\to E^{1}
be a morphism of vector bundles.
We refer to this data as a
two-term complex
on
U
,
and regard it as a complex of
\mathcal{O}_{U}
-modules,
where
E^{0}
lies in degree
-1
, and
E^{1}
lies in degree
0
.
We may form the relative quotient stack

[E^{1}/E^{0}]\longrightarrow U\ ,

which is a module
U
-stack.
See also
[
13
, §1.4.11]
.

A
2
-vector bundle
over
U

is a module
U
-stack

\pi\colon\mathcal{E}\longrightarrow U\ ,

such that it is locally isomorphic to module stacks of the form
[E^{1}/E^{0}]

over open subsets of
U
.
In this case, the underlying stack in groupoids of
\mathcal{E}

is an algebraic
U
-stack.

A
morphism of
2
-vector bundles
over
U

is a morphism of the underlying module stacks,
and a
2
-morphism
between such morphisms
is a
2
-morphism between the morphisms of module stacks.

The
rank
of a
2
-vector bundle
\mathcal{E}

is its relative dimension as a smooth morphism of algebraic stacks.
This defines a locally constant function
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{E}\colon U\to\mathbb{Z}
.

A
2
-vector space
is a
2
-vector bundle over
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{K})
.
We see that a
2
-vector space can always be written as

E^{1}\times[*/E^{0}]

for finite-dimensional vector spaces
E^{0}
,
E^{1}
.
A morphism of
2
-vector spaces from
E^{1}\times[*/E^{0}]

to
F^{1}\times[*/F^{0}]

is of the form
\alpha\times\beta
,
where
\alpha\colon E^{1}\to F^{1}
is a linear map,
and
\beta\colon[*/E^{0}]\to[*/F^{0}]

is induced by a linear map
E^{0}\to F^{0}
.

Finally, for an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack
\mathcal{S}
,
a
2
-vector bundle
over
\mathcal{S}

is a module
\mathcal{S}
-stack
\mathcal{E}
,
such that for any morphism
U\to\mathcal{S}
from a
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
U
,
the pullback
U\times_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{E}
is a
2
-vector bundle over
U
.

Theorem C.1.5
.

Let
U
be a
\mathbb{K}
-scheme.

(i)

For two-term complexes
E^{\bullet},F^{\bullet}
of vector bundles on
U
,
there is a canonical isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{O}_{U})}(E^{\bullet},F^{\bullet})\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Hom}_{U}([E^{1}/E^{0}],[F^{1}/F^{0}])\ ,

(C.1)

where
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{O}_{U})
is the derived category of
\mathcal{O}_{U}
-modules,
and the right-hand side is the set of
2
-isomorphism classes
of morphisms of
2
-vector bundles over
U
.

Moreover, this induces an equivalence of
2
-categories
between the full subcategory of the derived
\infty
-category
\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}(\mathcal{O}_{U})

spanned by such two-term complexes, which is a
2
-category,
and the
2
-category of
2
-vector bundles over
U
.

(ii)

Let
E^{\bullet}\to F^{\bullet}\to G^{\bullet}

be an exact triangle of two-term complexes of vector bundles on
U
. Then

[F^{1}/F^{0}]\underset{[G^{1}/G^{0}]}{\times}U\simeq[E^{1}/E^{0}]\ ,

(C.2)

where the map
U\to[G^{1}/G^{0}]
is the zero section. We say that the sequence

0\to[E^{1}/E^{0}]\longrightarrow[F^{1}/F^{0}]\longrightarrow[G^{1}/G^{0}]\to 0

(C.3)

is a
short exact sequence
of
2
-vector bundles.

Proof 29
.

Part
Item

(i)

was shown in Deligne
[
13
, Proposition 1.4.15]
;
see also Tatar
[
55
, Theorem 6.4]
.

For
Item

(ii)
,
we use the consequence of
[
55
, Theorem 6.4]

that there is an equivalence of
2
-categories
between
\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}^{[-1,0]}(U)
,
the full subcategory of the derived
\infty
-category
\smash[b]{\textsf{{D}}}(U)
of abelian sheaves
spanned by two-term complexes in degrees
-1
and
0
,
and the
2
-category of Picard
U
-stacks,
which is a full subcategory of the
2
-category of abelian
U
-stacks.
In particular, this construction preserves
2
-pullbacks.
It then remains to show that
2
-pullbacks in the
2
-category of Picard
U
-stacks
coincide with
2
-pullbacks in the
2
-category of all stacks.
Equivalently,
2
-pullbacks of
Picard categories

[
13
, Definition 1.4.2]
, which are certain
2
-groups,
coincide with
2
-pullbacks of groupoids,
which can be verified by an explicit description of such pullbacks.

\Box

Remark C.1.6
.

A
2
-vector bundle is determined by its underlying abelian stack.
In other words, any abelian stack automorphism of a
2
-vector bundle

\mathcal{E}\to U
is a
2
-vector bundle automorphism,
since Zariski locally, we may assume that
\mathcal{E}\simeq[E^{1}/E^{0}]

with
E^{0},E^{1}
trivial,
and that the given automorphism
[E^{1}/E^{0}]\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}[E^{1}/E^{0}]

is given by a morphism of two-term complexes,
so that it is determined by its value on
respective global bases of
E^{0},E^{1}
.

Definition C.1.7
.

Let
U
be a
\mathbb{K}
-scheme.

An
affine
2
-vector bundle
over
U

is a morphism of algebraic stacks

\pi\colon\mathcal{E}\to U\ ,

such that there is a
2
-vector bundle
\mathcal{E}_{0}\to U
,
such that
\mathcal{E}
is an
\mathcal{E}_{0}
-torsor
.
This means that there is an
\mathcal{E}_{0}
-action on
\mathcal{E}
as
U
-stacks,
such that there is an
open cover
(U_{i}\subset U)_{i\in I}
,
and
\smash{\mathcal{E}_{0}|_{U_{i}}}
-equivariant isomorphisms of
U_{i}
-stacks

\phi_{i}\colon\smash{\mathcal{E}|_{U_{i}}}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\smash{\mathcal{E}_{0}|_{U_{i}}}
.
In particular, each transition morphism

\phi_{j}\circ\phi_{i}^{-1}\colon\smash{\mathcal{E}_{0}|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}}\to\smash{\mathcal{E}_{0}|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}}

is a translation by a section
U_{i}\cap U_{j}\to\mathcal{E}_{j}
.

A
morphism
of affine
2
-vector bundles
\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F}
over
U
,
or an
affine linear morphism
,
is a morphism of stacks
f\colon\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{F}
over
U
,
such that there is a morphism of
2
-vector bundles

f_{0}\colon\mathcal{E}_{0}\to\mathcal{F}_{0}
over
U
,
an open cover
(U_{i}\subset U)_{i\in I}
,
and trivializations of torsors

\phi_{i}\colon\smash{\mathcal{E}|_{U_{i}}}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\smash{\mathcal{E}_{0}|_{U_{i}}}
and

\psi_{i}\colon\smash{\mathcal{F}|_{U_{i}}}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\smash{\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{U_{i}}}
,
such that
\psi_{i}\circ f\circ\phi_{i}^{-1}=\smash{f_{0}|_{U_{i}}}
for all
i
.

A
2
-morphism
of such morphisms is
a
2
-morphism between the underlying morphisms of stacks,
such that there exists a
2
-morphism between the corresponding
morphisms of
2
-vector bundles,
which can be identified with the given
2
-morphism
using some local trivialization.

Finally, for an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack
\mathcal{S}
,
we define
affine
2
-vector bundles
over
\mathcal{S}

similarly as above, with
U
replaced by
\mathcal{S}
,
and open covers of
U
replaced by smooth open covers of
\mathcal{S}
by
\mathbb{K}
-schemes.
Morphisms and
2
-morphisms of affine
2
-vector bundles over
\mathcal{S}

are defined likewise.

Lemma C.1.8
.

In the situation of

Theorem

C.1.5

Item

(ii)
,
let
\alpha\colon U\to[G^{1}/G^{0}]
be any section. Then

[F^{1}/F^{0}]\underset{[G^{1}/G^{0}]}{\times}U

is an affine
2
-vector bundle over
U
,
and is an
[E^{1}/E^{0}]
-torsor.

Proof 30
.

We claim that
\alpha
locally lifts to a section of
[F^{1}/F^{0}]
.
Indeed, locally, we may assume that
U
is affine,
that all vector bundles in question are trivial,
and that
F^{1}\to G^{1}
is surjective.
Then
\alpha
factors through
G^{1}
,
since
\alpha
can be represented as a
G^{0}
-torsor
T\to U

together with a
G^{0}
-equivariant morphism
T\to G^{1}
.
However, such a
G^{0}
-torsor must be trivial,
since if
m
is the rank of
G^{0}
, then

H^{1}_{\smash{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}}(U,(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{a}})^{m})\simeq H^{1}(U,\mathcal{O}_{U}^{\oplus m})\simeq 0\ ,

where the first two terms are étale and Zariski cohomology, respectively,
and they are the same because
(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{a}})^{m}
is a special group.
Therefore,
T
has a section, through which

\alpha
lifts to
G^{1}
, and upon shrinking
U
, lifts to
F^{1}
.

Therefore, locally, we can use the result of

Theorem

C.1.5

Item

(ii)

by pretending that this lift is the zero section of
[F^{1}/F^{0}]
.
This shows that the fibre product locally looks like
[E^{1}/E^{0}]
,
and transition morphisms are translations by sections.

\Box

Remark C.1.9
.

Theorems

C.1.5
and
C.1.8

can be generalized to the case when
U
is replaced by an algebraic stack
\mathcal{S}
,
without much difficulty.
For example, a fibre product of
\mathcal{S}
-stacks can be seen as
a fibre product of stacks over the site
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Sch}}}_{\mathcal{S}}
,
which can be given object-wise by those lemmas.

Theorem C.1.10
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-stack of finite type with affine stabilizers,
and let
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{X}
be a
2
-vector bundle of rank
n
,
or an affine
2
-vector bundle of rank
n
.
Then their motives satisfy

\mu(\mathcal{E})=\mu(\mathcal{X})\cdot\mathbb{L}^{n}\ .

(C.4)

Proof 31
.

As in the proof of
Theorem

A.1.7
,
we may assume that
\mathcal{X}=[X/\mathrm{GL}(m)]

for some
\mathbb{K}
-variety
X
.
Let
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
be the pullback of
\mathcal{E}
to
X
.
Then
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
is a principal
\mathrm{GL}(m)
-bundle over
\mathcal{E}
.
By
Theorem

A.1.7
, we have

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle=\mu(X)\cdot\mu(\mathrm{GL}(m))^{-1}\ ,

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{E})

\displaystyle=\mu(\tilde{\mathcal{E}})\cdot\mu(\mathrm{GL}(m))^{-1}\ .

Therefore, it is enough to prove that

\mu(\tilde{\mathcal{E}})=\mu(X)\cdot\mathbb{L}^{n}\ .

(C.5)

Shrinking
X
, we may assume that
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}

has the form
[E^{1}/E^{0}]
,
where
E^{0},E^{1}\to X
are trivial vector bundles.
Let
r,s
be the ranks of
E^{0},E^{1}
, respectively.
Then
E^{1}
is a principal
(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{a}})^{r}
-bundle over
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
.
Since
(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{a}})^{r}
is special, we have

\displaystyle\mu(\tilde{\mathcal{E}})

\displaystyle=\mu(E^{1})\cdot\mu((\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{a}})^{r})^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\mu(X)\cdot\mathbb{L}^{s})\cdot\mathbb{L}^{-r}

\displaystyle=\mu(X)\cdot\mathbb{L}^{s-r}\ ,

(C.6)

as required.

\Box

Appendix D
Proof of a combinatorial result

This appendix is dedicated to the proof of
Theorem

5.4.6
,
through a complicated combinatorial argument.
We also believe that with some slight modifications,
our argument can also provide a purely combinatorial proof of

[
30
, Theorem 5.4]
,
which was claimed there, but an alternative proof was given there instead.

D.1   The setup

D.1.1.

Throughout this section, let
I
be a finite set,
and let

C_{I}=\{e_{i}\,,e_{i}^{\vee}\mid i\in I\}

(D.1)

be a set of symbols.
We define a map
(-)^{\vee}\colon C_{I}\to C_{I}

by sending
e_{i}
to
e_{i}^{\vee}

and
e_{i}^{\vee}
to
e_{i}
for all
i\in I
.

Let
A_{I}
be the free associative algebra over
\mathbb{Q}

generated by elements of
C_{I}
,
where we denote the multiplication by
*
.
There is an involution
(-)^{\vee}\colon A_{I}\to A_{\smash{I}}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}
,
given by

(x_{1}*\cdots*x_{k})^{\vee}=x_{\smash{k}}^{\vee}*\cdots*x_{1}^{\vee}

(D.2)

for
x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k}\in C_{I}
.
This equips
A_{I}
with the structure of an involutive algebra,
as in
4.1.2
.

Let
L_{I}
be the free Lie algebra over
\mathbb{Q}

generated by elements of
C_{I}
.
Let
L_{\smash{I}}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}
be the opposite Lie algebra of
L_{I}
,
i.e. the Lie algebra with the same underlying vector space
and with Lie bracket
[x,y]_{L_{\smash{I}}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}}=[y,x]_{L_{I}}
.
There is an involution
(-)^{\vee}\colon L_{I}\to L_{\smash{I}}^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}
,
defined inductively by

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto x^{\vee}

\displaystyle\quad\text{for }x\in C_{I}\ ,

(D.3)

\displaystyle{}[x,y]

\displaystyle\mapsto[y^{\vee},x^{\vee}]

\displaystyle\quad\text{for }x,y\in L_{I}\ .

(D.4)

This equips
L_{I}
with the structure of an involutive Lie algebra,
as in
4.1.3
.

There is a natural inclusion of vector spaces
L_{I}\hookrightarrow A_{I}
,
which identifies
A_{I}
with the universal enveloping algebra of
L_{I}
.

D.1.2.

Define linear subspaces

\displaystyle L_{\smash{I}}^{+}

\displaystyle=\{x\in L_{I}\mid x=-x^{\vee}\}\ ,

(D.5)

\displaystyle L_{\smash{I}}^{-}

\displaystyle=\{x\in L_{I}\mid x=x^{\vee}\}\ .

(D.6)

Then
L_{I}=L_{\smash{I}}^{+}\oplus L_{\smash{I}}^{-}
,
and this makes
L_{I}
into a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded Lie algebra.
In other words, we have

[L_{\smash{I}}^{+},L_{\smash{I}}^{\pm}]\subset L_{\smash{I}}^{\pm}
and

[L_{\smash{I}}^{-},L_{\smash{I}}^{\pm}]\subset L_{\smash{I}}^{\mp}
.
In particular,
L_{\smash{I}}^{+}\subset L_{I}
is a Lie subalgebra,
and there is a natural embedding

U(L_{\smash{I}}^{+})\hookrightarrow A_{I}

(D.7)

of associative algebras,
where the left-hand side is the universal enveloping algebra of
L_{\smash{I}}^{+}
.
This is important,
as our main goal is to show that the wall-crossing formula in
Theorem

5.4.6
,
which was originally expressed in terms of the

A_{I}
-module structure given by the operation
\diamond
,
using the coefficients
U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}({\cdots})
,
can actually be expressed solely in terms of the

L_{\smash{I}}^{+}
-module structure given by the operation
\mathbin{\heartsuit}
,
using the coefficients
\tilde{U}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}({\cdots})
.

Define linear maps

(-)^{+}\colon L_{I}\to L_{\smash{I}}^{+}
,
(-)^{-}\colon L_{I}\to L_{\smash{I}}^{-}
by

\displaystyle x^{+}

\displaystyle=x-x^{\vee},

(D.8)

\displaystyle x^{-}

\displaystyle=x+x^{\vee}.

(D.9)

We have the relations

\displaystyle(x^{+})^{-}

\displaystyle=(x^{-})^{+}=0\ ,

(D.10)

\displaystyle{}[x,y]^{+}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigl([x^{+},y^{+}]+[x^{-},y^{-}]\bigr)\ ,

(D.11)

\displaystyle{}[x,y]^{-}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigl([x^{+},y^{-}]+[x^{-},y^{+}]\bigr)

(D.12)

for
x,y\in L_{I}
.

D.1.3.

Write
n=|I|
.
Define a set

P_{I}=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}\sigma\colon\{1,\dotsc,n\}\to I\\[-4.52083pt]
\text{bijective}\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\bigl\{x=(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})\bigm|x_{i}\in\{e_{\sigma(i)}\,,e_{\smash{\sigma(i)}}^{\vee}\}\text{ for all }i\bigr\}\ ,

(D.13)

as a subset of
C_{\smash{I}}^{n}
.

Let
K_{I}=\mathbb{Z}^{C_{I}}
be the free abelian group generated by elements of
C_{I}
,
and let
K_{\smash{I}}^{+}=\mathbb{N}^{C_{I}}\setminus\{0\}\subset K_{I}
.

Definition D.1.4
.

A
self-dual weak stability condition
on
I

is a map
\tau\colon K_{\smash{I}}^{+}\to T
,
where
T
is a totally ordered set,
equipped with a distinguished element
0\in T
,
and an order-reversing involution
t\mapsto-t
,
fixing the element
0
, such that

(i)

For any
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in K_{\smash{I}}^{+}
,
such that
\beta=\alpha+\gamma
, either

\tau(\alpha)\leqslant\tau(\beta)\leqslant\tau(\gamma)\ ,\quad\text{or}\quad\tau(\alpha)\geqslant\tau(\beta)\geqslant\tau(\gamma)\ .

(ii)

For any
\alpha\in K_{\smash{I}}^{+}
,

\tau(\alpha^{\vee})=-\tau(\alpha)\ .

D.2   Wall-crossing to trivial stability

The goal of this subsection is to prove
Theorem

D.2.2
below.

D.2.1.

Let
\tau
be a self-dual weak stability condition on
I
,
as in
Definition

D.1.4
. Define elements

\displaystyle T(I;\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}U(e_{\sigma(1)},\dotsc,e_{\sigma(n)};\tau,0)\cdot e_{\sigma(1)}*\cdots*e_{\sigma(n)}\ ,

(D.14)

\displaystyle\bar{T}(I;\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in P_{I}}U(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,0)\cdot x_{1}*\cdots*x_{n}\ ,

(D.15)

\displaystyle T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in P_{I}}U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,0)\cdot x_{1}*\cdots*x_{n}

(D.16)

in the algebra
A_{I}
, where the coefficients

U({\cdots})
and
U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}({\cdots})
are defined as in

Eq.

5.28
and
Eq.

5.29
.

Theorem D.2.2
.

We have

\displaystyle T(I;\tau)

\displaystyle\in L_{I}\ ,

(D.17)

\displaystyle\bar{T}(I;\tau)

\displaystyle\in L_{\smash{I}}^{-}\ ,

(D.18)

\displaystyle\smash{T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I;\tau)

\displaystyle\in U(L_{\smash{I}}^{+})\ .

(D.19)

The proof will be given at the end of this subsection.

From now on, we take
I=\{1,\dotsc,n\}
.
For a subset
J=\{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{k}\}\subset I
,
where
k\geqslant 1
and
i_{1}>\cdots>i_{k}
, define elements

F(J),\bar{F}(J)\in L_{I}
and
G(J)\in L_{\smash{I}}^{+}
by

\displaystyle F(J)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}\,B_{k-1}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}:\\[-1.50694pt]
\sigma(1)=1\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}[[\dotsc[e_{i_{1}}\,,e_{i_{\sigma(2)}}],\dotsc],e_{i_{\sigma(k)}}]\ ,

(D.20)

\displaystyle\bar{F}(J)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}\,B_{k-1}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}:\\[-1.50694pt]
\sigma(1)=1\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}[[\dotsc[e_{i_{1}}\,,e_{i_{\sigma(2)}}^{-}],\dotsc],e_{i_{\sigma(k)}}^{-}]\ ,

(D.21)

\displaystyle G(J)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\,\Bigl(B_{k}-B_{k}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\Bigr)\Bigr)\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}:\\[-1.50694pt]
\sigma(1)=1\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}[[\dotsc[e_{i_{1}}^{\mp}\,,e_{i_{\sigma(2)}}^{-}],\dotsc],e_{i_{\sigma(k)}}^{-}]\ ,

(D.22)

where
B_{k}
denotes the
k
-th Bernoulli number,
and
B_{k}(-)
denotes the
k
-th Bernoulli polynomial.
The sign ‘
\mp
’ is ‘
+
’ if and only if
k
is odd.

Note that
F(J)=\bar{F}(J)=0
whenever
k>2
is even,
and
G(J)=0
whenever
k>1
is odd.

For
x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k}\in A_{I}
, we denote

\displaystyle s_{k}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}}x_{\sigma(1)}*\cdots*x_{\sigma(k)}\ ,

(D.23)

\displaystyle\bar{s}_{k}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{k}\,k!}\,s_{k}(x_{1}^{-},\dotsc,x_{k}^{-})\ .

(D.24)

Lemma D.2.3
.

We have combinatorial identities

\displaystyle e_{n}*s_{n-1}(e_{1},\dotsc,e_{n-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset I:\\[-1.50694pt]
n\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}s_{n-|J|+1}\bigl(F(J),\ e_{i}:i\in I\setminus J\bigr)\ ,

(D.25)

\displaystyle e_{n}*\bar{s}_{n-1}(e_{1},\dotsc,e_{n-1})

\displaystyle=

(D.26)

\displaystyle\hskip-60.00009pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset I:\\[-1.50694pt]
n\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}\bar{s}_{n-|J|+1}\bigl(\bar{F}(J),\ e_{i}:i\in I\setminus J\bigr)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset I:\\[-1.50694pt]
n\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}\bar{s}_{n-|J|}\bigl(e_{i}:i\in I\setminus J\bigr)*G(J)\ .\hskip 20.00003pt

(D.27)

Proof 32
.

For
Eq.

D.25
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant k\leqslant n
, write

E_{i,k}=\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}:\\[-4.52083pt]
\sigma(i)=n\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}e_{\sigma(1)}*\cdots*e_{\sigma(k)}\ .

(D.28)

For
k=1,\dotsc,n
, using the invariance under the

\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}
-action permuting the elements
e_{1},\dotsc,e_{n-1}
,
we find that

\frac{1}{\binom{n-1}{k-1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset I:\\[-4.52083pt]
n\in J,\,|J|=k\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}F(J)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}{}(-1)^{i-1}\binom{k-1}{i-1}\cdot\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}\,B_{k-1}\cdot(k-1)!\,E_{i,k}\ .

(D.29)

Simplifying this, we obtain

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset I:\\[-4.52083pt]
n\in J,\,|J|=k\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}F(J)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}{}\frac{(-1)^{k-i}\,(n-1)!}{(n-k)!\,(k-i)!\,(i-1)!}\,B_{k-1}\cdot E_{i,k}\ .

(D.30)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset I:\\[-1.50694pt]
n\in J,\,|J|=k\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}s_{n-k+1}\bigl(F(J),\ e_{i}:i\in I\setminus J\bigr)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n-k+1}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{n-k}{}\sum_{i=1}^{k}{}\frac{(-1)^{k-i}\,(n-1)!}{(n-k)!\,(k-i)!\,(i-1)!}\,B_{k-1}\cdot E_{i+j,n}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}{}\sum_{j=i-n+k}^{i}{}\frac{(-1)^{k-j}\,(n-1)!}{(n-k+1)!\,(k-j)!\,(j-1)!}\,B_{k-1}\cdot E_{i,n}\ .

(D.31)

The left-hand side of
Eq.

D.25
is just
E_{1,n}
,
so it suffices to prove that for any
i=1,\dotsc,n
,

\sum_{k=1}^{n}{}\sum_{j=i-n+k}^{i}{}\frac{(-1)^{k-j}\,(n-1)!}{(n-k+1)!\,(k-j)!\,(j-1)!}\,B_{k-1}=\begin{cases}1,&i=1\ ,\\
0,&i>1\ .\end{cases}

(D.32)

Setting
p=j-1
and
q=k-j
, the above reduces to

\sum_{p=0}^{i-1}{}\sum_{q=0}^{n-i}{}\frac{(-1)^{q}\,(n-1)!}{(n-p-q)!\,p!\,q!}\,B_{p+q}=\begin{cases}1,&i=1\ ,\\
0,&i>1\ .\end{cases}

(D.33)

This follows from taking
x=0
in
Lemma

D.4.3
below.

For
Eq.

D.27
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant k\leqslant n
, write

E_{i,k}^{\pm}=\frac{1}{2^{k-1}\,(n-1)!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}:\\[-4.52083pt]
\sigma(i)=n\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}e_{\sigma(1)}^{-}*\cdots*e_{\sigma(i)}^{\pm}*\cdots*e_{\sigma(k)}^{-}\ .

(D.34)

Similarly to the previous case, we find that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset I:\\[-1.50694pt]
n\in J,\,|J|=k\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}\bar{F}(J)^{-}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}{}\frac{(-1)^{k-i+1}\,2^{k-2}\,(n-1)!}{(n-k)!\,(k-i)!\,(i-1)!}\,B_{k-1}\cdot E_{i,k}^{(-1)^{k}}\ ,

(D.35)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset I:\\[-1.50694pt]
n\in J,\,|J|=k\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}G(J)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}{}\frac{(-1)^{k-i}\,2^{k-1}\,(n-1)!}{k\,(n-k)!\,(k-i)!\,(i-1)!}\,B^{\prime}_{k}\cdot E_{i,k}^{(-1)^{k-1}}\ ,

(D.36)

where
B^{\prime}_{k}=B_{k}-B_{k}(1/2)
.
Proceeding as before, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset I:\\[-1.50694pt]
n\in J,\,|J|=k\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}\bar{s}_{n-k+1}\bigl(\bar{F}(J),\ e_{i}:i\in I\setminus J\bigr)

(D.37)

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{}\sum_{j=i-n+k}^{i}{}\frac{(-1)^{k-j}\,2^{k-2}\,(n-1)!}{(n-k+1)!\,(k-j)!\,(j-1)!}\,B_{k-1}\cdot E_{i,n}^{(-1)^{k}}\ ,

(D.38)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset I:\\[-1.50694pt]
n\in J,\,|J|=k\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}\bar{s}_{n-k}\bigl(e_{i}:i\in I\setminus J\bigr)*G(J)

(D.39)

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt=\sum_{i=n-k+1}^{n}{}\frac{(-1)^{n-i+1}\,2^{k-1}\,(n-1)!}{k\,(n-k)!\,(n-i)!\,(i+k-n-1)!}\,B^{\prime}_{k}\cdot E_{i,n}^{(-1)^{k-1}}\ .

(D.40)

The left-hand side of
Eq.

D.27
is just

(1/2)\,(E_{1,n}^{+}+E_{1,n}^{-})
.
Collecting the coefficients of each
E_{i,n}^{\pm}
,
we see that to prove
Eq.

D.27
, it is enough to prove that
for any
i=1,\dotsc,n
and
\varepsilon=\pm 1
,

\sum_{k=1}^{n}{}\varepsilon^{k-1}\cdot\sum_{j=i-n+k}^{i}{}\frac{(-1)^{k-j}\,2^{k-2}\,(n-1)!}{(n-k+1)!\,(k-j)!\,(j-1)!}\,B_{k-1}\\
{}+\sum_{k=n-i+1}^{n}{}\varepsilon^{k}\cdot\frac{(-1)^{n-i+1}\,2^{k-1}\,(n-1)!}{k\,(n-k)!\,(n-i)!\,(i+k-n-1)!}\,B^{\prime}_{k}=\begin{cases}1,&i=1\text{ and }\varepsilon=1\ ,\\
0,&i>1\text{ or }\varepsilon=-1\ .\end{cases}

(D.41)

The case
\varepsilon=1

follows from taking
x=0
in
Lemma

D.4.4
below.
The case
\varepsilon=-1

follows from taking
x=0
in
Lemma

D.4.5
below.

\Box

D.2.4.

For
J\subset I
, we denote

I\mathbin{/}J=(I\setminus J)\sqcup\{J\}\ .

For any element
x\in A_{J}
, there is a homomorphism

\displaystyle A_{I\mathbin{/}J}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow A_{I}\ ,

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\longmapsto y\,|_{e_{J}\mapsto x}\ ,

defined by mapping
e_{i}
to
e_{i}
,
and
e_{i}^{\vee}
to
e_{i}^{\vee}
, for
i\in I\setminus J
,
and mapping
e_{J}
to the image of
x
in
A_{I}
,
and
e_{J}^{\vee}
to the image of
x^{\vee}
in
A_{I}
.

If, moreover,
x\in L_{J}
, then this map sends the subspace
L_{I\mathbin{/}J}

to
L_{I}
, preserving the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading.
In particular, this map sends the subalgebra
U(L_{\smash{I\mathbin{/}J}}^{+})

into
U(L_{\smash{I}}^{+})
.

If
\tau
is a self-dual weak stability condition on
I

such that the restriction of
\tau
to
K^{+}(J)
is a constant map,
then
\tau
induces a self-dual weak stability condition on
I\mathbin{/}J
.

To avoid nested subscripts,
for
I,J,\tau,x
as above, we denote

\displaystyle T(I,J;\tau;x)

\displaystyle=T(I\mathbin{/}J;\tau)\,|_{e_{J}\mapsto x}\ ,

(D.42)

\displaystyle T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I,J;\tau;x)

\displaystyle=T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I\mathbin{/}J;\tau)\,|_{e_{J}\mapsto x}\ .

(D.43)

We also define auxiliary coefficients

U^{\prime}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[47.83347pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &0=a_{0}<\cdots<a_{m}=n.\\[-1.50694pt]
&\text{Define }\beta_{1},\dotsc,\beta_{m}\text{ by }\beta_{i}=\alpha_{a_{i-1}+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{a_{i}}\,.\\[-1.50694pt]
&\text{We require }\tau(\beta_{i})=\tau(\alpha_{j})\text{ for all }a_{i-1}<j\leqslant a_{i}\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}S(\beta_{1},\dotsc,\beta_{m};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{1}{(a_{i}-a_{i-1})!}\biggr)\ ,\hskip-20.00003pt\\[-17.22217pt]
\mathstrut

(D.44)

U^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})=\\
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[47.83347pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &0=a_{0}<\cdots<a_{m}\leqslant n.\\[-1.50694pt]
&\text{Define }\beta_{1},\dotsc,\beta_{m}\text{ by }\beta_{i}=\alpha_{a_{i-1}+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{a_{i}}\,.\\[-1.50694pt]
&\text{We require }\tau(\beta_{i})=\tau(\alpha_{j})\text{ for all }a_{i-1}<j\leqslant a_{i}\,,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\text{and }\tau(\alpha_{j})=0\text{ for all }j>a_{m}\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}S^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\beta_{1},\dotsc,\beta_{m};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{1}{(a_{i}-a_{i-1})!}\biggr)\cdot\frac{1}{2^{n-a_{m}}\,(n-a_{m})!}\ .\hskip-20.00003pt\\[-17.22217pt]
\mathstrut

(D.45)

In other words, in
Eq.

D.44
,
we take the sum of all terms in
Eq.

5.28
with
l=1
;
in
Eq.

D.45
,
we take the sum of all terms in
Eq.

5.29
with
l=0
.

Define

\displaystyle T^{\prime}(I;\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}U^{\prime}(e_{\sigma(1)},\dotsc,e_{\sigma(n)};\tau,0)\cdot e_{\sigma(1)}*\cdots*e_{\sigma(n)}\ ,

(D.46)

\displaystyle\bar{T}^{\prime}(I;\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in P_{I}}U^{\prime}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,0)\cdot x_{1}*\cdots*x_{n}\ ,

(D.47)

\displaystyle T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I;\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in P_{I}}U^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,0)\cdot x_{1}*\cdots*x_{n}\ ,

(D.48)

as elements of
A_{I}
.
By definition, we have the relations

\displaystyle T(I;\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}I=I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{l}:\\[-1.50694pt]
I_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for any }i\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}\frac{(-1)^{l-1}}{l}\cdot T^{\prime}(I_{1};\tau)*\cdots*T^{\prime}(I_{l};\tau)\ ,

(D.49)

\displaystyle\bar{T}(I;\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}I=I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{l}:\\[-1.50694pt]
I_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for any }i\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}\frac{(-1)^{l-1}}{l}\cdot\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{1};\tau)*\cdots*\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{l};\tau)\ ,

(D.50)

\displaystyle T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}I=I_{0}\sqcup I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{l}:\\[-1.50694pt]
I_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for }i=1,\dotsc,l\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}\binom{-1/2}{l}\cdot\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{1};\tau)*\cdots*\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{l};\tau)*T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I_{0};\tau)\ .\hskip 10.00002pt

(D.51)

For
J\subset I
as above, a self-dual weak stability condition
\tau

on
I
that is constant on
J
,
and an element
x\in A_{I}
, we denote

\displaystyle T^{\prime}(I,J;\tau;x)

\displaystyle=T^{\prime}(I\mathbin{/}J;\tau)\,|_{e_{J}\mapsto x}\ ,

(D.52)

\displaystyle\bar{T}^{\prime}(I,J;\tau;x)

\displaystyle=\bar{T}^{\prime}(I\mathbin{/}J;\tau)\,|_{e_{J}\mapsto x}\ ,

(D.53)

\displaystyle T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I,J;\tau;x)

\displaystyle=T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I\mathbin{/}J;\tau)\,|_{e_{J}\mapsto x}\ ,

(D.54)

as is similar to the above.

Lemma D.2.5
.

Let
0\leqslant l<m\leqslant n
with
m\geqslant l+2
.
Let
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
be two self-dual weak stability conditions on
I
,
satisfying

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\tau_{1}(e_{1})\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\tau_{1}(e_{l})<\tau_{1}(e_{l+1})=\cdots=\tau_{1}(e_{m-1})<\tau_{1}(e_{m})<\tau_{1}(e_{m+1})\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\tau_{1}(e_{n})\ ,\\
&\tau_{2}(e_{1})\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\tau_{2}(e_{l})<\tau_{2}(e_{l+1})=\cdots=\tau_{2}(e_{m-1})=\tau_{2}(e_{m})<\tau_{2}(e_{m+1})\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\tau_{2}(e_{n})\ ,\end{aligned}

where each
‘
\leqslant
’
sign is
‘
=
’
for
\tau_{1}

if and only if the corresponding
‘
\leqslant
’
sign is
‘
=
’
for
\tau_{2}
.
Then

\displaystyle T^{\prime}(I;\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{l+1,\dotsc,m\}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}T^{\prime}(I,J;\tau_{2};F(J))\ ,

(D.55)

\displaystyle T(I;\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{l+1,\dotsc,m\}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}T(I,J;\tau_{2};F(J))\ ,

(D.56)

where
F(J)
is given by
Eq.

D.20
.

Proof 33
.

First, let us prove
Eq.

D.55
.
By the definitions,
both sides of
Eq.

D.55

lie in the subspace of
A_{I}
spanned by the elements

e_{\sigma(1)}*\cdots*e_{\sigma(n)}
,
where
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
such that
\tau_{2}(e_{\sigma(1)})\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\tau_{2}(e_{\sigma(n)})
.
Therefore, it suffices to prove that for each of these monomials,
its coefficients on both sides are equal.

Let
I_{0}=\{l+1,\dotsc,m\}
.
Then
Eq.

D.55

can be rewritten as

T^{\prime}(I,I_{0};\tau_{2};T^{\prime}(I_{0};\tau_{1}))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset I_{0}:\\[-4.52083pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}T^{\prime}\bigl(I,I_{0};\tau_{2};T^{\prime}(I_{0}\,,J;\tau_{2};F(J))\bigr)\ ,

(D.57)

by an elementary combinatorial argument.
Therefore, it is enough to show that

T^{\prime}(I_{0};\tau_{1})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset I_{0}:\\[-4.52083pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}T^{\prime}(I_{0}\,,J;\tau_{2};F(J))\ ,

(D.58)

which is precisely
Eq.

D.55

with
I=I_{0}
.
Thus, we may ease the notation by setting
l=0
,
m=n
,
and
I_{0}=I
.
Expanding both sides of
Eq.

D.58
,
we see that it is equivalent to

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}:\\[-1.50694pt]
\sigma(1)=n\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\cdot e_{\sigma(1)}*\cdots*e_{\sigma(n)}=\\[-4.30554pt]
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset I_{0}:\\[-1.50694pt]
n\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\mspace{9mu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}\sigma\colon\{1,\dotsc,n-|J|+1\}\to I\mathbin{/}J\\[-1.50694pt]
\text{bijective}\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{(n-|J|+1)!}\cdot e_{\sigma(1)}*\cdots*e_{\sigma(n-|J|+1)}\,\bigg|_{e_{J}\mapsto F(J)}\,,

(D.59)

which is precisely
Eq.

D.25
.
Therefore, we have proved
Eq.

D.55
.

For
Eq.

D.56
,
using
Eq.

D.49
,
we see that the right-hand side of
Eq.

D.56
equals

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{l+1,\dotsc,m\}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\mspace{9mu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}I=I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{k}:\\[-1.50694pt]
I_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for any }i,\\[-1.50694pt]
J\subset I_{j}\text{ for some }j\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k}\cdot T^{\prime}(I_{1};\tau_{2})*\cdots*T^{\prime}(I_{j}\,,J;\tau_{2};F(J))*{}

\displaystyle\mathllap{\cdots*T^{\prime}(I_{k};\tau_{2})}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}I=I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{k}:\\[-1.50694pt]
I_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for any }i.\\[-1.50694pt]
\mathclap{\text{Let }j\text{ satisfy }m\in I_{j}}\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\mspace{9mu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{l+1,\dotsc,m\}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k}\cdot T^{\prime}(I_{1};\tau_{2})*\cdots*T^{\prime}(I_{j}\,,J;\tau_{2};F(J))*{}

\displaystyle\mathllap{\cdots*T^{\prime}(I_{k};\tau_{2})}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}I=I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{k}:\\[-1.50694pt]
I_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for any }i.\\[-1.50694pt]
\mathclap{\text{Let }j\text{ satisfy }m\in I_{j}}\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}T^{\prime}(I_{1};\tau_{1})*\cdots*T^{\prime}(I_{k};\tau_{1})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle T(I;\tau_{1})\ ,

(D.60)

where the second equal sign uses the fact that

T^{\prime}(I_{i};\tau_{1})=T^{\prime}(I_{i};\tau_{2})
if
i\neq j
, by the definitions.

\Box

Lemma D.2.6
.

Let
0\leqslant l<m\leqslant n
and
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}

satisfy the assumptions of
Lemma

D.2.5
.
Then

\displaystyle\bar{T}^{\prime}(I;\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{l+1,\dotsc,m\}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}\bar{T}^{\prime}(I,J;\tau_{2};F(J))\ ,

(D.61)

\displaystyle\bar{T}(I;\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{l+1,\dotsc,m\}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}\bar{T}(I,J;\tau_{2};F(J))\ ,

(D.62)

where
F(J)
is given by
Eq.

D.20
.

Proof 34
.

We observe that for any
x=(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})\in P_{I}
, we have

\displaystyle U^{\prime}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,0)

\displaystyle=U^{\prime}(x_{n}^{\vee},\dotsc,x_{1}^{\vee};\tau,0)\ ,

(D.63)

\displaystyle U(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,0)

\displaystyle=U(x_{n}^{\vee},\dotsc,x_{1}^{\vee};\tau,0)\ ,

(D.64)

which follow from the definition of these coefficients.
Therefore, if we write

\displaystyle T^{\prime}(x;\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}U^{\prime}(x_{\sigma(1)},\dotsc,x_{\sigma(n)};\tau,0)\cdot x_{\sigma(1)}*\cdots*x_{\sigma(n)}\ ,

(D.65)

\displaystyle T(x;\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}U(x_{\sigma(1)},\dotsc,x_{\sigma(n)};\tau,0)\cdot x_{\sigma(1)}*\cdots*x_{\sigma(n)}\ ,

(D.66)

and write
x^{\vee}=(x_{n}^{\vee},\dotsc,x_{1}^{\vee})
, then

\displaystyle T^{\prime}(x^{\vee};\tau)

\displaystyle=T^{\prime}(x,\tau)^{\vee}\ ,

(D.67)

\displaystyle T(x^{\vee};\tau)

\displaystyle=T(x,\tau)^{\vee}\ .

(D.68)

To prove
Eq.

D.61
,
note that both sides are self-dual by the above observation,
so it is enough to prove that the coefficients of monomials

x_{1}*\cdots*x_{n}
that involve
e_{m}
(rather than
e_{m}^{\vee}
)
are equal on both sides.
We divide such monomials into
2^{n-1}
classes,
according to whether they involve
e_{i}
or
e_{i}^{\vee}

for
i\in\{1,\dotsc,n\}\setminus\{m\}
.
For each of these classes, let
I^{\prime}\subset I
be the set of
i\in I

such that
e_{i}^{\vee}
is involved in that class.
Let
\xi\colon K_{I}\to K_{I}
be the automorphism exchanging
e_{i}
and
e_{i}^{\vee}

for all
i\in I^{\prime}
.
Applying
Lemma

D.2.5

to the weak stability condition
\alpha\mapsto\tau(\xi(\alpha))
,
we see that the coefficients of these monomials
on both sides of
Eq.

D.61

are equal to the coefficients of the corresponding monomials
in
Eq.

D.55

using the modified weak stability condition.
This proves
Eq.

D.61
.

Finally,
Eq.

D.62

follows from an analogous argument using
Eq.

D.56
.

\Box

Lemma D.2.7
.

Let
0\leqslant l<m\leqslant n
with
m\geqslant l+2
.
Let
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
be two self-dual weak stability conditions on
I
,
satisfying

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 0&\leqslant\tau_{1}(e_{1})\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\tau_{1}(e_{l})<\tau_{1}(e_{l+1})=\cdots=\tau_{1}(e_{m-1})<\tau_{1}(e_{m})<\tau_{1}(e_{m+1})\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\tau_{1}(e_{n})\ ,\\
0&\leqslant\tau_{2}(e_{1})\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\tau_{2}(e_{l})<\tau_{2}(e_{l+1})=\cdots=\tau_{2}(e_{m-1})=\tau_{2}(e_{m})<\tau_{2}(e_{m+1})\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\tau_{2}(e_{n})\ ,\end{aligned}

where each
‘
\leqslant
’
sign is
‘
=
’
for
\tau_{1}

if and only if the corresponding
‘
\leqslant
’
sign is
‘
=
’
for
\tau_{2}
.
Then

\displaystyle T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I;\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{l+1,\dotsc,m\}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I,J;\tau_{2};F(J))\ ,

(D.69)

\displaystyle T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{l+1,\dotsc,m\}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I,J;\tau_{2};F(J))\ ,

(D.70)

where
F(J)
is given by
Eq.

D.20
.

Proof 35
.

The proof is similar to that of
Lemma

D.2.5
.

First, let us prove
Eq.

D.69
.
By the definitions,
both sides of
Eq.

D.55

lie in the subspace of
A_{I}
spanned by the elements

e_{\sigma(1)}*\cdots*e_{\sigma(n)}
,
where
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
such that
\tau_{2}(e_{\smash{\sigma(1)}})\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\tau_{2}(e_{\smash{\sigma(n)}})
.
Note that the
e_{i}^{\vee}
cannot appear.
Therefore, it suffices to prove that for each of these monomials,
its coefficients on both sides are equal.

Let
I_{0}=\{l+1,\dotsc,m\}
.
We rewrite
Eq.

D.69
as

T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I,I_{0};\tau_{2};T^{\prime}(I_{0};\tau_{1}))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset I_{0}:\\[-4.52083pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\bigl(I,I_{0};\tau_{2};T^{\prime}(I_{0}\,,J;\tau_{2};F(J))\bigr)\ ,

(D.71)

which follows from
Eq.

D.58
.
This proves
Eq.

D.69
.

For
Eq.

D.70
,
using
Eq.

D.51
,
we see that the right-hand side of
Eq.

D.70
equals

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{l+1,\dotsc,m\}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\mspace{9mu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}I=I_{0}\sqcup I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{k}:\\[-1.50694pt]
I_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for }i=1,\dotsc,k,\\[-1.50694pt]
J\subset I_{j}\text{ for some }j>0\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\binom{-1/2}{k}\cdot\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{1};\tau_{2})*\cdots*\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{j}\,,J;\tau_{2};F(J))*{}

\displaystyle\mathllap{\cdots*\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{k};\tau_{2})*T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I_{0};\tau_{2})}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt{}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{l+1,\dotsc,m\}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\mspace{9mu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}I=I_{0}\sqcup I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{k}:\\[-1.50694pt]
I_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for }i=1,\dotsc,k,\\[-1.50694pt]
J\subset I_{0}\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\binom{-1/2}{k}\cdot\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{1};\tau_{2})*\cdots*\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{k};\tau_{2})*{}

\displaystyle\mathllap{T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I_{j}\,,J;\tau_{2};F(J))}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}I=I_{0}\sqcup I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{k}:\\[-1.50694pt]
I_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for }i=1,\dotsc,k,\\[-1.50694pt]
\mathclap{m\in I_{j}\text{ for some }j>0}\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\mspace{9mu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{l+1,\dotsc,m\}\cap I_{j}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\binom{-1/2}{k}\cdot\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{1};\tau_{2})*\cdots*\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{j}\,,J;\tau_{2};F(J))*{}\hskip-6.00006pt

\displaystyle\mathllap{\cdots*\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{k};\tau_{2})*T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I_{0};\tau_{2})}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt{}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}I=I_{0}\sqcup I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{k}:\\[-1.50694pt]
I_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for }i=1,\dotsc,k,\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in I_{0}\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\mspace{9mu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{l+1,\dotsc,m\}\cap I_{0}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\binom{-1/2}{k}\cdot\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{1};\tau_{2})*\cdots*\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{k};\tau_{2})*{}

\displaystyle\mathllap{T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I_{j}\,,J;\tau_{2};F(J))}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}I=I_{0}\sqcup I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{k}:\\[-1.50694pt]
I_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for }i=1,\dotsc,k\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\binom{-1/2}{k}\cdot\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{1};\tau_{1})*\cdots*\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{k};\tau_{1})*T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I_{0};\tau_{1})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau_{1})\ ,

(D.72)

where the second equal sign is by

Eq.

D.61
and
Eq.

D.69
.
This proves
Eq.

D.70
.

\Box

Lemma D.2.8
.

Let
1\leqslant m\leqslant n
.
Let
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
be two self-dual weak stability conditions on
I
,
satisfying

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\tau_{1}(e_{1})=\cdots=\tau_{1}(e_{m-1})<\tau_{1}(e_{m})<\tau_{1}(e_{m+1})<\cdots<\tau_{1}(e_{n})\ ,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\tau_{2}(e_{1})=\cdots=\tau_{2}(e_{m-1})=\tau_{2}(e_{m})<\tau_{2}(e_{m+1})<\cdots<\tau_{2}(e_{n})\ .

Then

\displaystyle T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I;\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{1,\dotsc,m\}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I,J;\tau_{2};\bar{F}(J))+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{1,\dotsc,m\}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I\setminus J;\tau_{2})*G(J)\ ,{}

(D.73)

\displaystyle T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{1,\dotsc,m\}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I,J;\tau_{2};\bar{F}(J))+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{1,\dotsc,m\}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I\setminus J;\tau_{2})*G(J)\ ,

(D.74)

where
\bar{F}(J)
and
G(J)
are given by

Eq.

D.21
and
Eq.

D.22
.

Proof 36
.

The proof is similar to that of
Lemma

D.2.5
.

First, let us prove
Eq.

D.73
.
By the definitions,
both sides of
Eq.

D.55

lie in the subspace of
A_{I}
spanned by the elements

e_{\sigma(1)}*\cdots*e_{\sigma(n)}
,
where
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
such that
\tau_{2}(e_{\smash{\sigma(1)}})\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\tau_{2}(e_{\smash{\sigma(n)}})
.
Therefore, it suffices to prove that for each of these monomials,
its coefficients on both sides are equal.

Let
I_{0}=\{1,\dotsc,m\}
.
We rewrite
Eq.

D.73
as

T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I,I_{0};\tau_{2};T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I_{0};\tau_{1}))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset I_{0}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\Bigl(T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\bigl(I,I_{0};\tau_{2};T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I_{0}\,,J;\tau_{2};\bar{F}(J))\bigr)\\[-17.22217pt]
{}+T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\bigl(I,I_{0};\tau_{2};T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I_{0}\setminus J;\tau_{2})\bigr)*G(J)\Bigr)\ .

(D.75)

Therefore, as before,
it suffices to prove
Eq.

D.73
in the case when
m=n
.
But this is precisely
Eq.

D.27
.
This proves
Eq.

D.73
.

For
Eq.

D.74
,
using
Eq.

D.51
,
as is similar to the proof of
Lemma

D.2.7
,
we see that the right-hand side of
Eq.

D.74
equals

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}I=I_{0}\sqcup I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{k}:\\[-1.50694pt]
I_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for }i=1,\dotsc,k,\\[-1.50694pt]
\mathclap{m\in I_{j}\text{ for some }j>0}\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\mspace{9mu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{1,\dotsc,m\}\cap I_{j}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\binom{-1/2}{k}\cdot\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{1};\tau_{2})*\cdots*\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{j}\,,J;\tau_{2};F(J))*{}\hskip-6.00006pt

\displaystyle\mathllap{\cdots*\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{k};\tau_{2})*T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I_{0};\tau_{2})}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt{}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}I=I_{0}\sqcup I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{k}:\\[-1.50694pt]
I_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for }i=1,\dotsc,k,\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in I_{0}\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\mspace{9mu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}J\subset\{1,\dotsc,m\}\cap I_{0}:\\[-1.50694pt]
m\in J\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\binom{-1/2}{k}\cdot\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{1};\tau_{2})*\cdots*\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{k};\tau_{2})*{}

\displaystyle\mathllap{\Bigl(T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I_{0}\,,J;\tau_{2};\bar{F}(J))+T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I_{0}\setminus J;\tau_{2})*G(J)\Bigr)}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}I=I_{0}\sqcup I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{k}:\\[-1.50694pt]
I_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for }i=1,\dotsc,k\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\binom{-1/2}{k}\cdot\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{1};\tau_{1})*\cdots*\bar{T}^{\prime}(I_{k};\tau_{1})*T^{\prime{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}(I_{0};\tau_{1})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau_{1})\ ,

(D.76)

where the first equal sign is by

Eq.

D.61
and
Eq.

D.73
.
This proves
