D.74
.

\Box

Now, we are ready to prove
Theorem

D.2.2
.

D.2.9. Proof of
Theorem

D.2.2
.

We only write down a proof of the more difficult part
Eq.

D.19
,
as the proof of
Eq.

D.17

is analogous and easier,
and
Eq.

D.18
follows from
Eq.

D.17

together with the fact that

U(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{m};\tau,0)=U(x_{m}^{\vee},\dotsc,x_{1}^{\vee};\tau,0)

for all
x\in P_{I}
.

Let
S
be the set of self-dual weak stability conditions on
I
.
For
\tau\in S
, let
T_{\tau}
be its codomain, which is a totally ordered set.
For
t,t^{\prime}\in T_{\tau}
, write

\operatorname{sgn}(t,t^{\prime})=\begin{cases}1,&t>t^{\prime}\ ,\\
0,&t=t^{\prime}\ ,\\
-1,&t<t^{\prime}\ .\end{cases}

Write
\operatorname{sgn}(t)=\operatorname{sgn}(t,0)
.

Define an equivalence relation
\sim
on
S

to be generated by the following relations:

(i)

\tau_{1}\sim\tau_{2}
if for any
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,n\}
,

\operatorname{sgn}\tau_{1}(e_{i})=\operatorname{sgn}\tau_{2}(e_{i})
,

\operatorname{sgn}\bigl(\tau_{1}(e_{i}),\tau_{1}(e_{j})\bigr)=\operatorname{sgn}\bigl(\tau_{2}(e_{i}),\tau_{2}(e_{j})\bigr)
,
and
\operatorname{sgn}\bigl(\tau_{1}(e_{i}),\tau_{1}(e_{j}^{\vee})\bigr)=\operatorname{sgn}\bigl(\tau_{2}(e_{i}),\tau_{2}(e_{j}^{\vee})\bigr)
.

(ii)

\tau_{1}\sim\tau_{2}
if there exists
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}

with
\tau_{1}(e_{i})=\pm\tau_{2}(e_{\sigma(i)})
for all
i
,
where the ‘
\pm
’ signs are arbitrary.

(iii)

\tau_{1}\sim\tau_{2}
if they satisfy the assumption of

Lemma

D.2.7
.

(iv)

\tau_{1}\sim\tau_{2}
if they satisfy the assumption of

Lemma

D.2.8
.

We claim that
\sim
is trivial,
that is, all elements of
S
are equivalent under
\sim
.

Indeed, every element
\tau\in S
is equivalent to
the trivial stability condition
0\in S
.
To see this, using
Item

(ii)
,
we may assume that

0\leqslant\tau(e_{1})\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\tau(e_{n})\ .

If all the inequality signs are equalities, then
\tau=0
and we are done.
If not, suppose that

0=\tau(e_{1})=\cdots=\tau(e_{l})<\tau(e_{l+1})=\cdots=\tau(e_{m})<\tau(e_{m+1})\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\tau(e_{n})\ ,

where
0\leqslant l<m\leqslant n
.
Using
Item

(iii)
,
we may increase the values of
\tau(e_{m})
by a small amount,
as long as it stays below
\tau(e_{m+1})
.
We then do the same thing to
e_{m-1}\,,\dotsc,e_{l+2}
,
so that we can now assume that

0=\tau(e_{1})=\cdots=\tau(e_{l})<\tau(e_{l+1})<\tau(e_{l+2})\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\tau(e_{n})\ ,

where
0\leqslant l<n
.
We can then use
Item

(iv)
to modify
\tau(e_{l+1})
,
so that now we have
\tau(e_{l+1})=0
.
Repeating this process,
we see that we eventually reach a point where
\tau=0
.

Therefore, what is left to prove is that
if
\tau_{1}\sim\tau_{2}
, and
\tau_{1}
satisfies
Eq.

D.19
,
then so does
\tau_{2}
.
To see this, we only need to check the cases

Item

(i)
–
Item

(iv)
individually.
By induction on
n
,
we can assume that this is already true for all smaller values of
n
,
as the case when
n=0
is trivial.

For
Item

(i)
,
we see that
U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};\tau_{1},0)=U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};\tau_{2},0)

for all
(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})\in P_{I}
,
so that
T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau_{1})=T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau_{2})
.
For
Item

(ii)
,
permutations does not affect
T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau)
either,
due to the permutation symmetry of
P_{I}
.
Switching the sign of
\tau(e_{i})
amounts to exchanging the roles of

e_{i}
and
e_{i}^{\vee}
, i.e., its effect on
T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau)

is swapping
e_{i}
with
e_{i}^{\vee}
in the expression.
However, since the subspace
L_{\smash{I}}^{+}\subset L_{I}

is fixed under this operation,
Eq.

D.19
is preserved.
For
Item

(iii)
,
we use
Eq.

D.70
,
whose right-hand side contains
T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau_{2})

as the term with
J=\{m\}
.
All other terms involve index sets of size
<n
,
and hence, after replacing
\smash{e_{J}}\mapsto F(J)
,
lie in
U(L_{\smash{I}}^{+})
, by our induction hypothesis.
For
Item

(iv)
,
similarly, we see from
Eq.

D.74

that the difference between
T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau_{1})
and
T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau_{2})

lies in
U(L_{\smash{I}}^{+})
.

\square

D.3   General wall-crossing

D.3.1.

Let notation be as in the previous subsection.
For
x=(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})\in P_{I}
, define

\displaystyle Q(x)

\displaystyle=\biggl\{y=(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m})\biggm|\begin{array}[]{l}m\geqslant 1,\ 0=a_{0}<\cdots<a_{m}=n,\\
y_{i}=x_{a_{i-1}+1}+\cdots+x_{a_{i}}\text{ for all }i\end{array}\biggr\}\ ,

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\biggl\{y=(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m})\biggm|\begin{array}[]{l}m\geqslant 0,\ 0=a_{0}<\cdots<a_{m}\leqslant n,\\
y_{i}=x_{a_{i-1}+1}+\cdots+x_{a_{i}}\text{ for all }i\end{array}\biggr\}\ ,

where each element
y_{i}
is regarded as an element of

K_{I}\simeq\bigoplus_{i}{}\bigl(\mathbb{Z}e_{i}\oplus\mathbb{Z}e_{i}^{\vee}\bigr)
.

Lemma D.3.2
.

For self-dual weak stability conditions
\tau,\hat{\tau},\tilde{\tau}
on
I
,
we have combinatorial identities

\displaystyle S(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,\tau)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,&n=1\ ,\\
0,&n>1\ ,\end{cases}

(D.81)

\displaystyle S(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in Q(x)}S(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m};\hat{\tau},\tilde{\tau})\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{m}S(x_{a_{i-1}+1}\,,\dotsc,x_{a_{i}};\tau,\hat{\tau})\ ,

(D.82)

\displaystyle S^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,\tau)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,&n=0\ ,\\
0,&n>0\ ,\end{cases}

(D.83)

\displaystyle S^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in Q^{\prime}(x)}S^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m};\hat{\tau},\tilde{\tau})\cdot{}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{m}S(x_{a_{i-1}+1}\,,\dotsc,x_{a_{i}};\tau,\hat{\tau})\biggr)\cdot S^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(x_{a_{m}+1}\,,\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,\hat{\tau})\ ,

(D.84)

\displaystyle U(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,\tau)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,&n=1\ ,\\
0,&n>1\ ,\end{cases}

(D.85)

\displaystyle U(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in Q(x)}U(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m};\hat{\tau},\tilde{\tau})\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{m}U(x_{a_{i-1}+1}\,,\dotsc,x_{a_{i}};\tau,\hat{\tau})\ ,

(D.86)

\displaystyle U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,\tau)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,&n=0\ ,\\
0,&n>0\ ,\end{cases}

(D.87)

\displaystyle U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in Q^{\prime}(x)}U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m};\hat{\tau},\tilde{\tau})\cdot{}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{m}U(x_{a_{i-1}+1}\,,\dotsc,x_{a_{i}};\tau,\hat{\tau})\biggr)\cdot U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(x_{a_{m}+1}\,,\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,\hat{\tau})\ ,

(D.88)

where
a_{i}
is as in
D.3.1
and
D.3.1
.

Proof 37
.

The identities
Eq.

D.81
,
Eq.

D.82
,

Eq.

D.85
, and
Eq.

D.86

were proved in
[
30
, Theorems 4.5 and 4.8]
,
using purely combinatorial methods.
The identities
Eq.

D.83
and
Eq.

D.87

follow from the definitions easily.

One could also prove the other two identities,

Eq.

D.84
and
Eq.

D.88
,
using combinatorics. However, we take a more intuitive approach
and deduce them from the results in
Section

5
.

Consider a self-dual quiver
(Q,\sigma,u,v)
,
as in
6.1.3
,
defined as follows.
The set of vertices of
Q
is

Q_{0}=I\sqcup I^{\vee}=\{1,1^{\vee},\dotsc,n,n^{\vee}\}
.
There is a unique arrow
i\to j
for any
i,j\in Q_{0}
,
making a total of
4n^{2}
arrows.
Define the involution
\sigma\colon Q\simeq Q^{\smash{\mathrm{op}}}

by exchanging the vertices
i
and
i^{\vee}
for all
i\in\{1,\dotsc,n\}
.
The action on the arrows is determined accordingly.
Let
u,v
assign the sign
+1
to all vertices and arrows.

Let
\mathcal{A}
denote the self-dual abelian category
\smash[b]{\textsf{{Mod}}}(\mathbb{K}Q)
.
As in
6.2.2
,
there is a self-dual moduli stack for
\mathcal{A}

to which we can apply the results in
Section

5
.
For convenience, for vertices
i^{\vee}\in Q_{0}
with
i\in I
,
we write
e_{i^{\vee}}=e_{i}^{\vee}\in C_{I}

and
e_{\smash{i^{\vee}}}^{\vee}=e_{i}\in C_{I}
.

Let
C^{\prime}_{\smash{I}}
be the set of all
\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A})

that is a non-zero sum of distinct elements of
C_{I}
.
For such
\alpha
, let
C_{\alpha}\subset C_{I}
be the set of terms appearing in
\alpha
.
Define an object
(E^{\alpha},e^{\alpha})\in\mathcal{A}
by

E^{\alpha}_{i}=\begin{cases}\mathbb{K},&i\in C_{\alpha}\ ,\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\ ,\end{cases}\qquad e^{\alpha}_{i\to j}=\begin{cases}1,&i,j\in C_{\alpha}\ ,\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\ .\end{cases}

One can see that
(E^{\alpha},e^{\alpha})
is a simple object,
and hence is semistable under any weak stability condition.

Let
\Sigma
be the set of all
\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}=(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{m})

with
m\geqslant 0
and
\alpha_{s}\in C^{\prime}_{\smash{I}}
for all
s
,
such that
\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{m}\in C^{\prime}_{\smash{I}}
.
For each
\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}\in\Sigma
,
define an object
(E^{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}},e^{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}})\in\mathcal{A}
by

\displaystyle(E^{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}})_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathbb{K},&i\in C_{\alpha_{s}}\text{ for some }s\ ,\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\ ,\end{cases}

\displaystyle(e^{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}})_{i\to j}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,&i\in C_{\alpha_{s}}\text{ and }j\in C_{\alpha_{t}}\text{ for some }s\geqslant t\ ,\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\ .\end{cases}

Then
(E^{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}},e^{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}})
has a unique Jordan–Hölder filtration
whose quotients are
(E^{\alpha_{1}},e^{\alpha_{1}})
,
\dotsc
,

(E^{\alpha_{m}},e^{\alpha_{m}})
, where the uniqueness follows from an elementary argument.

Define a partial order
\preccurlyeq
on
\Sigma
such that

\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}\preccurlyeq\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}^{\prime}
if and only if there exists

0=s_{0}<\cdots<s_{m^{\prime}}=m
such that

\alpha^{\prime}_{t}=\alpha_{\smash{s_{t-1}+1}}+\cdots+\alpha_{\smash{s_{t}}}
for all
t
,
where
m
and
m^{\prime}
are the lengths of
\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}
and
\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}^{\prime}
.

For a fixed weak stability condition
\tau
, and for
\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}\in\Sigma
, write

\delta_{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}}(\tau)=\delta_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)*\cdots*\delta_{\alpha_{m}}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})
.
Then
\delta_{\smash{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}^{\prime}}}(\tau)

being non-zero at
(E^{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}},e^{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}})

implies
\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}\preccurlyeq\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}^{\prime}
,
since any filtration is refined by the Jordan–Hölder filtration.
In particular,
\delta_{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}}(\tau)

is not in the linear span of
\delta_{\smash{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}^{\prime}}}(\tau)

with
\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}\not\preccurlyeq\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}^{\prime}
.
Since
\preccurlyeq
can be extended to a total order on the finite set
\Sigma
,
we conclude that the stack functions

\delta_{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})

for all
\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}\in\Sigma

are linearly independent.

As a result, the stack functions

\epsilon_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)*\cdots*\epsilon_{\alpha_{m}}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})

are also linearly independent,
essentially because upper triangular matrices with
1
’s on the diagonal are invertible.

At this point, as a side note, if we apply
Theorem

5.3.5

to express
\delta_{\alpha}(\tilde{\tau})
in terms of
\tau
invariants,
where
\alpha=e_{1}+\cdots+e_{n}
,
and compare the result with first converting

\tilde{\tau}
invariants to
\hat{\tau}
invariants,
and then converting to
\tau
invariants,
we have reproved
Eq.

D.82
.
Applying this to
\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)
reproves
Eq.

D.86
.

Now, let
\Sigma^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
be the set of
\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}=(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{m},\rho)

such that each
\alpha_{s}
is in
C^{\prime}_{\smash{I}}
,

\rho\in C^{\prime}_{\smash{I}}\cup\{0\}
,
\rho=\rho^{\vee}
,
and
\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{m}+\rho\in C^{\prime}_{\smash{I}}
.
For a fixed self-dual weak stability condition
\tau
, and for
\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}\in\Sigma^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
,
write
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}}(\tau)=\delta_{\smash{\alpha_{1}}}(\tau)\diamond\cdots\diamond\delta_{\smash{\alpha_{m}}}(\tau)\diamond\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash{\rho}}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
.
Similarly, we claim that the stack functions

\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}}(\tau)
for all
\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}\in\Sigma^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}

are linearly independent.

Indeed, for
\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}\in\Sigma^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
, define

\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\bar{\alpha}}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}=(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{m},(\rho{,})\ \alpha_{m}^{\vee},\dotsc,\alpha_{1}^{\vee})\in\Sigma
, where
\rho
appears only when it is non-zero.
The object

(E^{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\bar{\alpha}}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}},e^{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\bar{\alpha}}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}})
has a natural self-dual structure.
Using its unique Jordan–Hölder filtration,
we can show that
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}}(\tau)

is not in the linear span of
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}^{\prime}}}(\tau)

with
\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\bar{\alpha}}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}\not\preccurlyeq\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\bar{\alpha}}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}^{\prime}
.
Since the map
\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}\mapsto\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\bar{\alpha}}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}
is injective,
it follows that the
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}}(\tau)
are linearly independent.

Similarly, it follows that the stack functions

\epsilon_{\smash{\alpha_{1}}}(\tau)\diamond\cdots\diamond\epsilon_{\smash{\alpha_{m}}}(\tau)\diamond\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash{\rho}}

for
\smash[b]{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mspace{-1mu}\smash{\alpha}\mspace{-2mu}}\mspace{2mu}}\in\Sigma^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
are linearly independent.

Applying
Theorem

5.3.5

to express
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash{\theta}}(\tilde{\tau})
in terms of
\tau
invariants,
where
\theta=\bar{e}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{e}_{n}
,
and comparing the result with first converting

\tilde{\tau}
invariants to
\hat{\tau}
invariants,
and then converting to
\tau
invariants,
we have proved
Eq.

D.84
.
Applying this to
\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash{\theta}}(\tau)
proves
Eq.

D.88
.

\Box

D.3.3.

Now, define elements

\displaystyle V(I;\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}U(e_{\sigma(1)},\dotsc,e_{\sigma(n)};0,\tau)\cdot e_{\sigma(1)}*\cdots*e_{\sigma(n)}\ ,

(D.89)

\displaystyle V^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in P_{I}}U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};0,\tau)\cdot x_{1}*\cdots*x_{n}

(D.90)

in the algebra
A_{I}
.

Theorem D.3.4
.

We have

\displaystyle V(I;\tau)

\displaystyle\in L_{I}\ ,

(D.91)

\displaystyle V^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau)

\displaystyle\in U(L_{\smash{I}}^{+})\ .

(D.92)

Proof 38
.

The proof is essentially by formally inverting
the results in
Theorem

D.2.2
.

We use induction on
n
, and assume that the theorem is true
for all smaller values of
n
.
The cases when
n=1
in
Eq.

D.91

and when
n=0
in
Eq.

D.92

are trivial, since there is nothing to prove.

To prove
Eq.

D.91
,
we may assume that
n>1
.
By
Eq.

D.85
and
Eq.

D.86
,
for any
x\in P_{I}
, we have

\sum_{y\in Q(x)}U(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m};0,\tau)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{m}U(x_{a_{i-1}+1}\,,\dotsc,x_{a_{i}};\tau,0)=0\ .

(D.93)

Summing over all possibilities of

x=(e_{\sigma(1)},\dotsc,e_{\sigma(n)})

for
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, we obtain that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[50.6673pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-9.04166pt]
&m\geqslant 1,\ I=J_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup J_{m}\,\colon\\[-4.52083pt]
&J_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for all }i.\\[-4.52083pt]
&\textstyle\text{Write }y_{i}=\sum_{j\in J_{i}}e_{j}\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}U(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m};0,\tau)\cdot T(J_{1};\tau)*\cdots*T(J_{m};\tau)=0\ ,

(D.94)

where the
T(J_{i};\tau)
are as in
Eq.

D.14
.
By
Theorem

D.2.2
,

T(J_{i};\tau)\in L_{\smash{J_{i}}}
.
Therefore, by the induction hypothesis,
that is, by
Eq.

D.91
applied to
m
elements,
if
m<n
, then for a fixed choice of
J_{1},\dotsc,J_{m}
,
the sum of all the
m!
terms in
Eq.

D.94

involving a permutation of
J_{1},\dotsc,J_{m}

is in
L_{I}
.
Since
Eq.

D.94
equals zero,
the sum of the terms that were not involved above must lie in
L_{I}
as well.
These are precisely the terms with
m=n
.
This gives that

\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}U(e_{\sigma(1)},\dotsc,e_{\sigma(n)};0,\tau)\cdot e_{\sigma(1)}*\cdots*e_{\sigma(n)}\in L_{I}\ ,

(D.95)

which is a restatement of
Eq.

D.91
.

To prove
Eq.

D.92
,
we assume that
n>0
, and proceed as before.
By
Eq.

D.87
–
Eq.

D.88
,
for any
x\in P_{I}
, we have

\sum_{y\in Q^{\prime}(x)}U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m};0,\tau)\cdot\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{m}U(x_{a_{i-1}+1}\,,\dotsc,x_{a_{i}};\tau,0)\biggr)\cdot{}\\[-4.30554pt]
U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(x_{a_{m}+1}\,,\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,0)\ =\ 0\ .

(D.96)

Summing over all possibilities of

x\in P_{I}
, we obtain that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[50.6673pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-9.04166pt]
&m\geqslant 0,\ I=J_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup J_{m}\sqcup J^{\prime},\ x^{i}\in P_{\smash{J_{i}}}\colon\\[-4.52083pt]
&J_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for all }i.\\[-4.52083pt]
&\textstyle\text{Write }y_{i}=\sum_{j\in J_{i}}x_{j}\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m};0,\tau)\cdot T(x^{1};\tau)*\cdots*T(x^{m};\tau)*T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(J^{\prime};\tau)=0\ ,

(D.97)

where
T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(J^{\prime};\tau)

is as in
Eq.

D.16
,
and
T(x^{i};\tau)
is as in
Eq.

D.66
.
By
Theorem

D.2.2
,

T(x^{i};\tau)\in L_{\smash{J_{i}}}
,
and
T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(J_{i};\tau)\in U(L_{\smash{J^{\prime}}}^{+})
.
For
m<n
, fix a choice of
J_{1},\dotsc,J_{m},J^{\prime}
,
and a choice of the
x^{i}
.
Let
\Sigma
be the sum of all the
2^{m}m!
terms in
Eq.

D.97

involving a permutation of
J_{1},\dotsc,J_{m}
, and for each
i
,
either
x^{i}
or
(x^{i})^{\vee}
, where if
x^{i}=(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k})
,
then
(x^{i})^{\vee}=(x_{\smash{k}}^{\vee},\dotsc,x_{1}^{\vee})
.
Note that as in the proof of
Eq.

D.18
,
we have
T((x^{i})^{\vee};\tau)=T(x^{i};\tau)^{\vee}
,
where the latter
(-)^{\vee}
is the involution on
A_{I}
.
Now, we can apply the induction hypothesis,
or
Eq.

D.92
applied to
m
elements,
to see that
\Sigma
is a linear combination of products
of elements either in
L_{\smash{J^{\prime}}}^{+}
,
or of the form
T(x^{i};\tau)-T(x^{i};\tau)^{\vee}
,
which lie in
L_{\smash{J_{i}}}^{+}
.
Therefore,
\Sigma\in U(L_{\smash{I}}^{+})
.

It then follows that the sum of the terms in
Eq.

D.97

with
m=n
lies in
U(L_{\smash{I}}^{+})
as well,
which is, again, a restatement of
Eq.

D.92
.

\Box

D.3.5.

Next, for two self-dual weak stability conditions
\tau,\tilde{\tau}
on
I
,
define elements

\displaystyle W(I;\tau,\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}U(e_{\sigma(1)},\dotsc,e_{\sigma(n)};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot e_{\sigma(1)}*\cdots*e_{\sigma(n)}\ ,

(D.98)

\displaystyle W^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau,\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in P_{I}}U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot x_{1}*\cdots*x_{n}

(D.99)

in the algebra
A_{I}
.

Theorem D.3.6
.

We have

\displaystyle W(I;\tau,\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle\in L_{I}\ ,

(D.100)

\displaystyle W^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau,\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle\in U(L_{\smash{I}}^{+})\ .

(D.101)

Proof 39
.

Applying
Eq.

D.86
and
Eq.

D.88
with
\hat{\tau}=0
,
we may rewrite

\displaystyle W(I;\tau,\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[42.22275pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-6.02777pt]
&m\geqslant 1,\ I=J_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup J_{m}\,\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&J_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for all }i.\\[-1.50694pt]
&\textstyle\text{Write }y_{i}=\sum_{j\in J_{i}}e_{j}\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}U(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m};0,\tilde{\tau})\cdot T(J_{1};\tau)*\cdots*T(J_{m};\tau)\ ,

(D.102)

\displaystyle W^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau,\tilde{\tau})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[42.22275pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-6.02777pt]
&m\geqslant 0,\ I=J_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup J_{m}\sqcup J^{\prime},\ x^{i}\in P_{\smash{J_{i}}}\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&J_{i}\neq\varnothing\text{ for all }i.\\[-1.50694pt]
&\textstyle\text{Write }y_{i}=\sum_{j\in J_{i}}x_{j}\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}U^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m};0,\tilde{\tau})\cdot T(x^{1};\tau)*\cdots*T(x^{m};\tau)*T^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(J^{\prime};\tau)\ .

(D.103)

Reasoning as in the proof of
Theorem

D.3.4
,
we can deduce
Eq.

D.100
and
Eq.

D.101

from
Theorems

D.2.2
and
D.3.4
.
Indeed, we no longer need to use induction,
and instead of proving that some of the terms lie in
L_{I}
or
U(L_{\smash{I}}^{+})
,
the argument now shows that all the terms are in
L_{I}
or
U(L_{\smash{I}}^{+})
.

\Box

Now, the first part of
Theorem

5.4.6

already follows from
Eq.

D.101

in
Theorem

D.3.6
.
Finally, we prove the second part
Eq.

5.40
of
Theorem

5.4.6
.

Proof D.3.7
(Proof of
Theorem

5.4.6
).

Let
L_{\tau}\subset\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})
be the involutive Lie subalgebra
generated by
\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)
for all
\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A})
,
and let
M_{\tau}\subset\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
be the sub-
L_{\tau}^{+}
-module generated by

\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash{\rho}}(\tau)
for all
\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
.

We may rewrite
Eq.

5.33
as

\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tilde{\tau})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}n\geqslant 0\\[-1.50694pt]
\text{Write }I=\{1,\dotsc,n\}\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{2^{n}n!}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}\kappa\colon I\to C(\mathcal{A}),\ \rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
\textstyle\theta=\sum_{i\in I}\overline{\kappa(i)}+\rho\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}\\
\Biggl[\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}x\in P_{I}\\[-1.50694pt]
\text{Write }\alpha_{i}=\kappa(j)\text{ if }x_{i}=\smash{e_{j}}\,,\\[-1.50694pt]
\text{or write }\alpha_{i}=\kappa(j)^{\vee}\text{ if }x_{i}=\smash{e_{j}^{\vee}}\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}{}\hskip-10.00002ptU^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot{}\epsilon_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\diamond\cdots\diamond\epsilon_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\diamond\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tau)\Biggr]\ ,

(D.104)

since every term in
Eq.

5.33

appears
2^{n}n!
times in
Eq.

D.104
.

Now, every sum in the square brackets in
Eq.

D.104

lies in
M_{\tau}^{+}
.
This is because we can define an involutive algebra homomorphism

\varphi\colon A_{I}\to\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})
by sending
e_{i}
to

\epsilon_{\smash{\kappa(i)}}(\tau)
and

e_{i}^{\vee}
to
\epsilon_{\smash{\kappa(i)^{\vee}}}(\tau)
.
We have
\varphi(L_{I})\subset L_{\tau}
,
so
\varphi(L_{\smash{I}}^{+})\subset L_{\tau}^{+}
.
The sum in the square brackets is

\varphi(W^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(I;\tau,\tilde{\tau}))\diamond\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash{\rho}}(\tau)
,
so by
Theorem

D.3.6
,
it lies in
U(L_{\tau}^{+})\diamond\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\smash{\rho}}(\tau)\subset M_{\tau}
.

Moreover, the above also shows that every sum in the square brackets
can be written as a sum of terms of the form in
Eq.

5.40
,
with the
\tilde{U}({\cdots})
coefficients.
Each of these new terms appears
2^{n}n!
times in
Eq.

D.104
.
This proves
Eq.

5.40
.

D.4   Some combinatorial identities

D.4.1.

We prove some combinatorial identities that were used in the arguments above.
It is interesting that by working with wall-crossing structures,
we are able to write down several
combinatorial identities involving the Bernoulli numbers,
namely,
Lemmas

D.4.3
,
D.4.4
, and
D.4.5
,
and it is unclear whether there are deeper reasons why these identities are true.

Lemma D.4.2
.

For any integers
i,k,n
such that

1\leqslant i\leqslant n
and
1\leqslant k\leqslant n-1
, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox{\scriptsize$\begin{gathered}q\colon 0\leqslant q\leqslant n-i,\\[-4.52083pt]
0\leqslant k-q\leqslant i-1\end{gathered}$}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{q}\,\binom{k}{\,q\,}=(-1)^{i+k-1}\,\binom{k-1}{i-1}+(-1)^{n-i}\,\binom{k-1}{n-i}\ .

(D.105)

Proof 40
.

We have

l.h.s.

\displaystyle=\sum_{q=k-i+1}^{n-i}(-1)^{q}\,\biggl[\binom{k-1}{q-1}+\binom{k-1}{q}\biggr]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k-i+1}\,\binom{k-1}{k-i}+(-1)^{n-i}\,\binom{k-1}{n-i}

\displaystyle=\text{r.h.s.}

\Box

Lemma D.4.3
.

For any integers
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, we have

\sum_{p=0}^{i-1}{}\sum_{q=0}^{n-i}{}\frac{(-1)^{q}\,(n-1)!}{(n-p-q)!\,p!\,q!}\,B_{p+q}(x)=\binom{n-1}{i-1}\,x^{i-1}\,(1-x)^{n-i}\ ,

(D.106)

where
B_{k}(x)
denotes the
k
-th
Bernoulli polynomial.

Proof 41
.

Let
l(x)
and
r(x)
denote the left and right sides
of
Eq.

D.106
, respectively.
By
Eq.

D.105
, we have

\displaystyle l(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\,B_{0}(x)+\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{(n-1)!}{(n-k)!\,k!}\,\biggl[(-1)^{i+k-1}\,\binom{k-1}{i-1}+(-1)^{n-i}\,\binom{k-1}{n-i}\biggr]\,B_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}+\binom{n-1}{i-1}\cdot\biggl[\sum_{k=i}^{n-1}{}\frac{(-1)^{i+k-1}}{k}\,\binom{n-i}{n-k}\,B_{k}(x)+\sum_{k=n-i+1}^{n-1}{}\frac{(-1)^{n-i}}{k}\,\binom{i-1}{n-k}\,B_{k}(x)\biggr]\ .

Since
B_{k}(x+1)-B_{k}(x)=k\,x^{k-1}
, we have

l(x+1)-l(x)=\binom{n-1}{i-1}\cdot\biggl[\sum_{k=i}^{n-1}{}(-1)^{i}\,\binom{n-i}{n-k}\,(-x)^{k-1}+\sum_{k=n-i+1}^{n-1}{}(-1)^{n-i}\,\binom{i-1}{n-k}\,x^{k-1}\biggr]\ .

On the other hand,

\displaystyle r(x+1)-r(x)

\displaystyle=\binom{n-1}{i-1}\cdot\bigl[(x+1)^{i-1}\,(-x)^{n-i}-x^{i-1}\,(1-x)^{n-i}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\binom{n-1}{i-1}\cdot\biggl[\sum_{k=i}^{n-1}{}(-1)^{i}\,\binom{n-i}{n-k}\,(-x)^{k-1}+\sum_{k=n-i+1}^{n-1}{}(-1)^{n-i}\,\binom{i-1}{n-k}\,x^{k-1}\biggr]\ .

Therefore,
l(x+1)-l(x)=r(x+1)-r(x)
,
which means that

l(x)-r(x)=c

for some constant
c
.
To show that
c=0
, we use the fact that

\int_{0}^{1}B_{k}(x)\,dx=\begin{cases}1,&k=0\ ,\\
0,&k>0\ ,\end{cases}

so

\int_{0}^{1}l(x)\,dx=\frac{1}{n}\ .

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}r(x)\,dx

\displaystyle=\binom{n-1}{i-1}\cdot\mathrm{B}(i,n-i+1)

\displaystyle=\binom{n-1}{i-1}\cdot\frac{(i-1)!\,(n-i)!}{n!}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\ ,

where
\mathrm{B}
denotes the beta function.
This shows that
c=0
.

\Box

Lemma D.4.4
.

For any integers
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
,

\sum_{p=0}^{i-1}{}\sum_{q=0}^{n-i}{}\frac{(-1)^{q}\,2^{p+q-1}\,(n-1)!}{(n-p-q)!\,p!\,q!}\,B_{p+q}\Bigl(\frac{x}{2}\Bigr)\\
\hskip 10.00002pt{}+\sum_{k=n-i+1}^{n}{}\frac{(-1)^{n-i+1}\,2^{k-1}\,(n-1)!}{k\,(n-k)!\,(n-i)!\,(i+k-n-1)!}\,\Bigl[B_{k}\Bigl(\frac{x}{2}\Bigr)-B_{k}\Bigl(\frac{x+1}{2}\Bigr)\Bigr]\\
=\binom{n-1}{i-1}\,x^{i-1}\,(1-x)^{n-i}\ .

(D.107)

Proof 42
.

Let
l_{1}(x)
,
l_{2}(x)
and
r(x)
denote
the first and second terms on the left-hand side
of
Eq.

D.107
,
and the right-hand side, respectively.

By
Eq.

D.105
, we have

\displaystyle l_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n}\,B_{0}\Bigl(\frac{x}{2}\Bigr)+\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{2^{k-1}\,(n-1)!}{(n-k)!\,k!}\,\biggl[(-1)^{i+k-1}\,\binom{k-1}{i-1}+(-1)^{n-i}\,\binom{k-1}{n-i}\biggr]\,B_{k}\Bigl(\frac{x}{2}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n}+\binom{n-1}{i-1}\cdot\biggl[\sum_{k=i}^{n-1}{}\frac{(-1)^{i+k-1}\,2^{k-1}}{k}\,\binom{n-i}{n-k}\,B_{k}\Bigl(\frac{x}{2}\Bigr)+{}

\displaystyle\mathllap{\sum_{k=n-i+1}^{n-1}{}\frac{(-1)^{n-i}\,2^{k-1}}{k}\,\binom{i-1}{n-k}\,B_{k}\Bigl(\frac{x}{2}\Bigr)\biggr]\ .}

Proceeding as in the proof of
Lemma

D.4.3
,
we see that

l_{1}(x+2)-l_{1}(x)={}\\
\binom{n-1}{i-1}\cdot\biggl[\sum_{k=i}^{n-1}{}(-1)^{i}\,\binom{n-i}{n-k}\,(-x)^{k-1}+\sum_{k=n-i+1}^{n-1}{}(-1)^{n-i}\,\binom{i-1}{n-k}\,x^{k-1}\biggr]\ .

Similarly,

\displaystyle l_{2}(x+2)-l_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=n-i+1}^{n}{}\frac{(-1)^{n-i+1}\,(n-1)!}{(n-k)!\,(n-i)!\,(i+k-n-1)!}\,\bigl[x^{k-1}-(x+1)^{k-1}\bigr]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n-i+1}\,\binom{n-1}{i-1}\cdot\sum_{k=n-i+1}^{n}{}\binom{i-1}{n-k}\,\bigl[x^{k-1}-(x+1)^{k-1}\bigr]\ .

Setting
l(x)=l_{1}(x)+l_{2}(x)
, we see that

\displaystyle l(x+2)-l(x)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\binom{n-1}{i-1}\cdot\biggl[\sum_{k=i}^{n-1}{}(-1)^{i}\,\binom{n-i}{n-k}\,(-x)^{k-1}+\sum_{k=n-i+1}^{n-1}{}(-1)^{n-i}\,\binom{i-1}{n-k}\,(x+1)^{k-1}

\displaystyle\mathllap{{}+(-1)^{n-i+1}\,\bigl[x^{n-1}-(x+1)^{n-1}\bigr]\biggr]}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\binom{n-1}{i-1}\cdot\biggl[\sum_{k=i}^{n}{}(-1)^{i}\,\binom{n-i}{n-k}\,(-x)^{k-1}+\sum_{k=n-i+1}^{n}{}(-1)^{n-i}\,\binom{i-1}{n-k}\,(x+1)^{k-1}\biggr]\ .

On the other hand,

\displaystyle r(x+2)-r(x)

\displaystyle=\binom{n-1}{i-1}\cdot\bigl[\bigl((x+1)+1\bigr)^{i-1}\,\bigl(-(x+1)\bigr)^{n-i}-x^{i-1}\,(1-x)^{n-i}\bigr]

\displaystyle=l(x+2)-l(x)\ .

This means that

l(x)-r(x)=c

for some constant
c
.
To show that
c=0
, we use the facts that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2}B_{k}\Bigl(\frac{x}{2}\Bigr)\,dx

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2,&k=0\ ,\\
0,&k>0\ ,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2}B_{k}\Bigl(\frac{x+1}{2}\Bigr)\,dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{k-1}}\ ,

so

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2}l(x)\,dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}+\sum_{k=n-i+1}^{n}{}\frac{(-1)^{n-i}\,(n-1)!}{k\,(n-k)!\,(n-i)!\,(i+k-n-1)!}\ .

On the other hand, we have seen
in the proof of
Lemma

D.4.3
that

\int_{0}^{1}r(x)\,dx=\frac{1}{n}\ .

We then calculate

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{2}r(x)\,dx

\displaystyle=\binom{n-1}{i-1}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}(-x)^{n-i}(1+x)^{i-1}\,dx

\displaystyle=\binom{n-1}{i-1}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}{}\biggl[\sum_{k=n-i+1}^{n}{}(-1)^{n-i}\,\binom{i-1}{n-k}\,x^{k-1}\biggr]\,dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=n-i+1}^{n}{}\frac{(-1)^{n-i}\,(n-1)!}{k\,(n-k)!\,(n-i)!\,(i+k-n-1)!}\ .

This shows that
c=0
and we are done.

\Box

Lemma D.4.5
.

For any integers
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
,

\sum_{p=0}^{i-1}{}\sum_{q=0}^{n-i}{}\frac{(-1)^{p}\,2^{p+q-1}\,(n-1)!}{(n-p-q)!\,p!\,q!}\,B_{p+q}\Bigl(\frac{x}{2}\Bigr)\\
{}+\sum_{k=n-i+1}^{n}{}\frac{(-1)^{i+k-n-1}\,2^{k-1}\,(n-1)!}{k\,(n-k)!\,(n-i)!\,(i+k-n-1)!}\,\Bigl[B_{k}\Bigl(\frac{x}{2}\Bigr)-B_{k}\Bigl(\frac{x+1}{2}\Bigr)\Bigr]=0\ .

(D.108)

Proof 43
.

Let
l(x)
denote the left-hand side.
From a similar calculation as in the previous lemma, we have

\displaystyle l(x+2)-l(x)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\binom{n-1}{i-1}\cdot\biggl[\sum_{k=i}^{n}{}(-1)^{i-1}\,\binom{n-i}{n-k}\,x^{k-1}+\sum_{k=n-i+1}^{n}{}(-1)^{n-i+1}\,\binom{i-1}{n-k}\,(-x-1)^{k-1}\biggr].

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\binom{n-1}{i-1}\cdot\bigl[(-x)^{i-1}\,(1+x)^{n-i}-\bigl((-x-1)+1\bigr)^{i-1}\,(x+1)^{n-i}\bigr]

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=0\ .

Therefore,
l(x)
is constant.
Again, calculating as before,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2}l(x)\,dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}+\sum_{k=n-i+1}^{n}{}\frac{(-1)^{i+k-n}\,(n-1)!}{k\,(n-k)!\,(n-i)!\,(i+k-n-1)!}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}-\binom{n-1}{n-i}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}x^{n-i}\,(1-x)^{i-1}\,dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}-\frac{1}{n}=0\ ,

where the second integral was evaluated as in
the proof of
Lemma

D.4.3
.
This shows that
l(x)\equiv 0
.

\Box

Appendix E
Proof of the no-pole theorem

In this
Appendix
,
we prove one of the main results of this paper,

5.6.4
,
which implies that the numerical enumerative invariants

\prescript{\chi}{}{\mathrm{J}}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,
\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\textnormal{na\"{\i}}}}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,
and the Donaldson–Thomas invariants

\mathrm{DT}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
in
Sections

6.5
and
7.4
,
are well-defined.

E.1   Preparatory results

Lemma E.1.1
.

Let
A
be a finite-dimensional
\mathbb{K}
-algebra,
and
T\simeq(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}})^{n}\subset A^{\times}
a maximal torus.
Then the closure
S\subset A
of
T
in
A

is a
\mathbb{K}
-subalgebra isomorphic to
\mathbb{K}^{n}
, and
S^{\times}=T
.

Proof 44
.

Consider the embedding of
\mathbb{K}
-algebras

i\colon A\hookrightarrow\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{K}}(A)
,
which restricts to an embedding of algebraic groups

i\colon A^{\times}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{GL}(A)
.
Extend
i(T)
to a maximal torus
T^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{GL}(A)
,
and let
S^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{K}}(A)
be the closure of
T^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{K}}(A)
.
Since all maximal tori are conjugate in
\mathrm{GL}(A)
,
and conjugations extend to
\mathbb{K}
-linear automorphisms of
\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{K}}(A)
,
it follows that
S^{\prime}
is a
\mathbb{K}
-subalgebra of
\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{K}}(A)

isomorphic to
\mathbb{K}^{N}
, where
N=\operatorname{rank}T^{\prime}=\dim A
,
and
S^{\prime\times}=T^{\prime}
.
Since
S=i^{-1}(S^{\prime})
, we have the desired result.

\Box

E.1.2.

The following is an analogue of Joyce
[
27
, Proposition 5.8]

in the self-dual case,
and describes the local structure of the moduli stack of self-dual objects.

Lemma 0
.

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{M}
be as in
,
and let
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
be a quasi-compact locally closed substack.
Then there exists a finite decomposition

\mathcal{S}=\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{S}_{i}
into disjoint locally closed substacks

\mathcal{S}_{i}
of the form

\mathcal{S}_{i}\simeq[U_{i}/A_{i}^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}]\ ,

(E.1)

with
A_{i}
a finite-dimensional involutive
\mathbb{K}
-algebra,

A_{i}^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}
as in
4.1.2
,
and
U_{i}
an affine
\mathbb{K}
-scheme acted on by
A_{i}^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}
,
such that for each
u\in U_{i}(\mathbb{K})
mapping to
(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathbb{K})
,
there exists an involutive subalgebra
B_{u}\subset A_{i}

with
B_{u}^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}=\mathrm{Stab}_{A_{i}^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}}(u)
,
such that the induced map
\mathrm{Stab}_{A_{i}^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}}(u)\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\mathrm{Aut}(E,\phi)

extends to an isomorphism of involutive
\mathbb{K}
-algebras

B_{u}\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}\mathrm{End}(E)
.

Proof 45
.

As in the proof of
Kresch
[
37
, Proposition 3.5.9]
,
used in the proof of
[
27
, Proposition 5.8]
,
there is a decomposition
\mathcal{S}=\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{S}_{i}
,
such that each
\mathcal{S}_{i}
is a gerbe over a
connected, reduced
\mathbb{K}
-scheme
S_{i}
,
with a finite flat morphism
T_{i}\to S_{i}
of reduced
\mathbb{K}
-schemes,
such that
\mathcal{T}_{i}=\mathcal{S}_{i}\times_{S_{i}}T_{i}
as a gerbe over
T_{i}

is isomorphic to
\mathrm{B}G_{i}

for a flat group scheme
G_{i}\to T_{i}
.
Fibres of
G_{i}
over
\mathbb{K}
-points of
T_{i}

are automorphism groups of self-dual objects in
\mathcal{A}
.

Using the self-dual stack structure on
\accentset{\mspace{2mu}\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}{\mathcal{M}}
,
the argument above can be strengthened to give
an involutive algebra bundle
E_{i}\to T_{i}
,
whose fibres over
\mathbb{K}
-points of
T_{i}

give algebras of endomorphisms of objects in
\mathcal{A}
,
with the involution given by self-dual structures on these objects.

We claim that
E_{i}
has a self-dual faithful representation
in a self-dual vector bundle
V_{i}\to T_{i}
,
which can be chosen to be either orthogonal or symplectic,
such that the involution of
E_{i}

is compatible with the self-dual structure of
V_{i}
.
Indeed, one may take
V_{i}=E_{i}\oplus E_{i}^{\vee}
,
with the self-dual structure swapping
E_{i}
and
E_{i}^{\vee}
,
with
E_{i}
acting on
E_{i}
by left multiplication,
and on
E_{i}^{\vee}
by
(e,\eta)\mapsto(e^{\vee}\cdot(-))^{*}(\eta)
,
where
(-)^{*}
denotes the linear dual.

Now choose
V_{i}
symplectic as above,
and let
\mathcal{V}_{i}=V_{i}\times_{T_{i}}\mathcal{T}_{i}
,
as a symplectic vector bundle over
\mathcal{T}_{i}
.
Let
\mathcal{W}_{i}\to\mathcal{S}_{i}
be the symplectic vector bundle
which is the pushforward of
\mathcal{V}_{i}

along the finite flat morphism
\mathcal{T}_{i}\to\mathcal{S}_{i}
,
and let
U_{i}\to\mathcal{S}_{i}
be the associated
principal
\mathrm{Sp}(n_{i})
-bundle of
\mathcal{W}_{i}
,
where
n_{i}=\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{W}_{i}
.
Then
U_{i}
is an algebraic space acted on by
\mathrm{Sp}(n_{i})
,
and
\mathcal{S}_{i}\simeq[U_{i}/\mathrm{Sp}(n_{i})]
.
As in the proof of
[
37
, Lemma 3.5.1]
,
shrinking
U_{i}
if necessary,
we may assume that it is quasi-projective.
(This uses the fact that
\mathrm{Sp}(n_{i})
is connected,
which is why we chose
V_{i}
symplectic instead of orthogonal.)

We then choose
A_{i}=\mathrm{GL}(n_{i})
,
with an involution given by a symplectic structure on
\mathbb{K}^{n_{i}}
,
so that
A_{i}^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}=\mathrm{Sp}(n_{i})
.
Arguing as in the proof of
[
27
, Proposition 5.8]

verifies the compatibility conditions on stabilizer groups.

\Box

E.2   Virtual rank projections

In this section, we assume the conditions

,
,
, and

for a fixed self-dual weak stability condition
\tau
.
We study the virtual rank projections,
as in
Section

A.3
,
of the stack functions
\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)
and
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
.
The main results are
Theorems

E.2.3
and
E.2.4
.

Definition E.2.1
.

Define operations

\displaystyle\circledast

\displaystyle=\oplus_{!}

\displaystyle\colon\

\displaystyle\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})\otimes\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})\ ,

(E.2)

\displaystyle\mathbin{\hbox to6pt{\vbox to6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.9986pt\lower-2.9986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.87 0 C 3.87 2.14 2.14 3.87 0 3.87 C -2.14 3.87 -3.87 2.14 -3.87 0 C -3.87 -2.14 -2.14 -3.87 0 -3.87 C 2.14 -3.87 3.87 -2.14 3.87 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.38 0 L 0 2.38 L -2.38 0 L 0 -2.38 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}

\displaystyle=\oplus^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{!}

\displaystyle\colon\

\displaystyle\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})\otimes\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})\ ,

(E.3)

where
\oplus
and
\oplus^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
are maps defined in

2.11
and
3.23
.
This makes
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M})
into a commutative algebra,
and
\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})
into a module over this algebra.

Definition E.2.2
.

For classes
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
,
define elements

\sigma_{\alpha}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha}(\tau))
and

\sigma^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{ss}}}_{\theta}(\tau))
by

\displaystyle\sigma_{\alpha}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[47.83347pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-4.52083pt]

&\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}\,,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tau(\alpha_{n})\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}\cdot\delta_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\circledast\cdots\circledast\delta_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\ ,

(E.4)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[47.83347pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-4.52083pt]

&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tau(\alpha_{n})=0\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}\binom{-1/2}{n}\cdot\delta_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\mathbin{\hbox to6pt{\vbox to6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.9986pt\lower-2.9986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.87 0 C 3.87 2.14 2.14 3.87 0 3.87 C -2.14 3.87 -3.87 2.14 -3.87 0 C -3.87 -2.14 -2.14 -3.87 0 -3.87 C 2.14 -3.87 3.87 -2.14 3.87 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}

}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.38 0 L 0 2.38 L -2.38 0 L 0 -2.38 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\delta_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\mathbin{\hbox to6pt{\vbox to6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.9986pt\lower-2.9986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.87 0 C 3.87 2.14 2.14 3.87 0 3.87 C -2.14 3.87 -3.87 2.14 -3.87 0 C -3.87 -2.14 -2.14 -3.87 0 -3.87 C 2.14 -3.87 3.87 -2.14 3.87 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.38 0 L 0 2.38 L -2.38 0 L 0 -2.38 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tau)\ .

(E.5)

Compare with
Eqs.

5.2
and
5.7
.
The element
\sigma_{\alpha}(\tau)
was denoted by
\bar{\delta}_{\mathrm{si}}^{\alpha}(\tau)

in
[
28
, Definition 8.1]
.
More concisely,
Eqs.

E.4
to
E.5

can be written as

\displaystyle\sigma(\tau;t)

\displaystyle=\log_{\circledast}\delta(\tau;t)\ ,

(E.6)

\displaystyle\sigma^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\delta(\tau;0)^{\circledast(-1/2)}\mathbin{\hbox to6pt{\vbox to6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.9986pt\lower-2.9986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.87 0 C 3.87 2.14 2.14 3.87 0 3.87 C -2.14 3.87 -3.87 2.14 -3.87 0 C -3.87 -2.14 -2.14 -3.87 0 -3.87 C 2.14 -3.87 3.87 -2.14 3.87 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.38 0 L 0 2.38 L -2.38 0 L 0 -2.38 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\tau)\ ,

(E.7)

with notations analogous to those
in
Eqs.

5.4
and
5.9
.

Theorem E.2.3
.

For any
\alpha\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
,
we have

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathrm{vi}}_{1}(\delta_{\alpha}(\tau))

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\alpha}(\tau)\ ,

(E.8)

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathrm{vi}}_{0}(\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau))

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\ .

(E.9)

Proof 46
.

Equation
Eq.

E.8
was proved in
[
28
, Theorem 8.6]
.
Although the cited result is stated for abelian categories,
the argument works for
\mathbb{K}
-linear exact categories
\mathcal{A}

satisfying the condition in
Lemma

2.2.4
.

To prove
Eq.

E.9
,
we use the definition of
\Pi^{\mathrm{vi}}_{0}
from

[
29
, §5.2]
.

Choose a decomposition
\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{ss}}}_{\theta}(\tau)=\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{S}_{i}

into locally closed substacks

\mathcal{S}_{i}\simeq[U_{i}/A_{i}^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}]
as in
E.1.2
,
so that
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)=\sum_{i\in I}{}[\mathcal{S}_{i}]
.
Write
G_{i}=A_{i}^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}
,
and choose a maximal torus
T_{i}\subset G_{i}
.
Extending
T_{i}
to a maximal torus in
A_{i}^{\times}
,
and taking its closure in
A_{i}
,
then intersecting with its dual,
we obtain an involutive subalgebra
S_{i}\subset A_{i}

isomorphic to
\mathbb{K}^{I}
for a finite set
I
,
with involution given by an involution of
I
,
and such that
S_{i}^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}=T_{i}
,
as can be deduced from
Lemma

E.1.1
.

Consider the sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(U_{i},T_{i})

\displaystyle=\{\mathrm{Stab}_{T_{i}}(Z)\mid Z\subset U_{i}\text{ subscheme}\}\ ,

(E.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(G_{i},T_{i})

\displaystyle=\{T_{i}\cap\mathrm{C}(\mathrm{C}_{G_{i}}(S))\mid S\subset T_{i}\text{ closed subgroup}\}\ ,

(E.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(U_{i},G_{i},T_{i})

\displaystyle=\{P\cap Q\mid P\in\mathcal{P}(U_{i},T_{i}),\,Q\in\mathcal{Q}(G_{i},T_{i})\}

(E.12)

from
[
29
, Definitions 5.3, 5.5, and 5.8]
.
They are finite sets of closed subgroups of
T
,
closed under intersection.
The sets
Eqs.

E.10
,
E.11
, and
E.12

are contained in the set

\mathcal{T}_{i}=\{B^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}\mid B\subset S_{i}\text{ involutive subalgebra}\}\ ,

(E.13)

by the condition on stabilizer groups in
E.1.2
,
and the fact that
\smash{\mathrm{C}_{G_{i}}(S)}=\smash{\bigl(\mathrm{C}_{A_{i}}(S)}\cap\smash{\mathrm{C}_{A_{i}}(S)^{\vee}\bigr)^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}}

in the case of
Eq.

E.11
.
Using the fact that we can replace the sets

Eqs.

E.10
,
E.11
, and
E.12
by
larger sets of subgroups of
T_{i}

when defining the projections
\Pi^{\mathrm{vi}}_{k}
,
as in the proof of
[
28
, Theorem 8.6]
,
we now replace them by the set
\mathcal{T}_{i}
,
giving, by
[
29
, Definition 5.10]
,

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathrm{vi}}_{0}([\mathcal{S}_{i}])

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[33.7782pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &P,Q\in\mathcal{T}_{i}\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&M_{0}(P,Q)\neq 0\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}M_{0}(P,Q)\cdot[U_{i}^{P}/\mathrm{C}_{G_{i}}(Q)]\ ,

(E.14)

where
M_{0}(P,Q)
is a rational coefficient,

U_{i}^{P}
denotes the fixed locus of
P
in
U_{i}
,
and we write stacks themselves for the stack functions they represent.
The coefficient
M_{0}(P,Q)
is given by

\displaystyle M_{0}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|W_{Q}|}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[67.5564pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &P=P_{0}\supsetneq P_{1}\supsetneq\cdots\supsetneq P_{m}\text{ in }\mathcal{T}_{i}\mspace{2mu},\\[-1.50694pt]
&Q=Q_{0}\supsetneq Q_{1}\supsetneq\cdots\supsetneq Q_{n}\text{ in }\mathcal{T}_{i}\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\dim(P_{m}\cap Q_{n})=0\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}(-1)^{m+n}\ ,

(E.15)

where
W_{Q}=\mathrm{N}_{G_{i}}(T_{i}){\hbox{$\left/\vbox to8.5pt{}\right.$}}\bigl(\mathrm{C}_{G_{i}}(Q)\cap\mathrm{N}_{G_{i}}(T_{i})\bigr)
.
As in the proof of
[
28
, Theorem 8.6]
,
we have
M_{0}(P,Q)=0
unless
P=Q
,
so we may assume that
P=Q=B^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}

for an involutive subalgebra
B\subset S_{i}
,
isomorphic to
\mathbb{K}^{J}
for a finite set
J
,
with involution given by a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
action on
J
,
given by
(-)^{\vee}\colon J\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}J
.
Writing
s=|J^{\smash{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}}|
and
r=(|J|-s)/2
,
the sum in
Eq.

E.15
can be shown to be equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[67.5564pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &P=P_{0}\supsetneq P_{1}\supsetneq\cdots\supsetneq P_{m}\text{ in }\mathcal{T}_{i}\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\dim(P_{m})=0\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}(-1)^{m}=\begin{cases}(-1)^{r}\,(2r-1)!!&\text{if }s=0\text{ or }1\,,\\
0&\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

(E.16)

Indeed, a standard argument shows that
all terms in
Eq.

E.15
with
n>0
cancel out,
giving the left-hand side of
Eq.

E.16
.
The detailed proof of
Eq.

E.16
is postponed
to
Section

E.3
.

Let
\mathbb{K}^{r,s}
denote the involutive
\mathbb{K}
-algebra
\mathbb{K}^{J}
,
with
|J|=2r+s
and a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
J

fixing
s
elements, as above.
We can now rewrite
Eq.

E.14
as

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathrm{vi}}_{0}(\mathcal{S}_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[50.6673pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &r\geqslant 0,\,s\in\{0,1\};\\[-1.50694pt]
&q\colon\mathbb{K}^{r,s}\hookrightarrow S_{i}\text{ involutive subalgebra}.\\[-1.50694pt]
&\text{Write }Q=q(\mathbb{K}^{r,s})^{\smash{\mathrm{iso}}}\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}\frac{(-1)^{r}\,(2r-1)!!}{2^{r}r!}\cdot\frac{1}{|W_{Q}|}\cdot[U_{i}^{Q}/\mathrm{C}_{G_{i}}(Q)]\ ,

(E.17)

where the factor
2^{r}r!
is the number of maps
q

giving the same
Q
.
The Weyl group
W_{i}
of
G_{i}
acts on
S_{i}
by conjugation,
and the orbit of
q
is isomorphic to
W_{Q}
.

Each term in the sum
Eq.

E.17
, as a stack function,
parametrizes splittings of the form

(E,\phi)\simeq\bar{E}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\bar{E}_{r}\oplus(E_{0},\phi_{0})
,
with
E_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
non-zero for
i=1,\dotsc,r
,
and
E_{0}=0
if and only if
s=0
,
for an object
(E,\phi)\in\mathcal{A}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}

corresponding to a point
u\in U_{i}(\mathbb{K})
fixed by
Q
.
This uses the property in
Lemma

2.2.4

which was assumed in
.
Conversely, each such splitting corresponds to
a map
q\colon\mathbb{K}^{r,s}\hookrightarrow S_{i}
up to conjugation by
W_{i}
,
giving
|W_{Q}|
such maps.
Also, such
(E,\phi)
above is semistable if and only if

\bar{E}_{1},\dotsc,\bar{E}_{r}
are semistable of slope
0
,
and
(E_{0},\phi_{0})
is semistable.
Summing over
i
in
Eq.

E.17
gives

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathrm{vi}}_{0}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{ss}}}_{\theta}(\tau))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[33.7782pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-4.52083pt]
&\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{r}\in C(\mathcal{A}),\,\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{r}+\rho\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tau(\alpha_{r})=0\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}\frac{(-1)^{r}\,(2r-1)!!}{2^{r}r!}\cdot[\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\times\cdots\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash[b]{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\alpha_{r}}(\tau)\times\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd},\mspace{2mu}\mathrm{ss}}}_{\rho}(\tau)]\ .

(E.18)

Comparing this with
Eq.

E.5

proves
Eq.

E.9
.

\Box

E.2.4.

As a corollary, it is possible to write down
all virtual rank projections of

\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)
and
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
.

Theorem 0
.

Let
\alpha\in C(\mathcal{A})
and
\theta\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
, and let
n\geqslant 0
.
Then

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathrm{vi}}_{n}(\delta_{\alpha}(\tau))

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n!}\cdot{}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[50.6673pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-6.02777pt]
&\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}\,,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tau(\alpha_{n})\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\circledast\cdots\circledast\sigma_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\ ,

(E.19)

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathrm{vi}}_{n}(\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau))

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{n}n!}\cdot{}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[50.6673pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-6.02777pt]
&\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C(\mathcal{A}),\,\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{n}+\rho\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tau(\alpha_{n})=0\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}\sigma_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\mathbin{\hbox to6pt{\vbox to6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.9986pt\lower-2.9986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.87 0 C 3.87 2.14 2.14 3.87 0 3.87 C -2.14 3.87 -3.87 2.14 -3.87 0 C -3.87 -2.14 -2.14 -3.87 0 -3.87 C 2.14 -3.87 3.87 -2.14 3.87 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.38 0 L 0 2.38 L -2.38 0 L 0 -2.38 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.38 0 L 0 2.38 L -2.38 0 L 0 -2.38 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\sigma^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tau)\ .

(E.20)

Proof 47
.

Formally inverting
Eqs.

E.4
to
E.5
,
similarly to
Eqs.

5.3
and
5.8
,
we see that the sum of the right-hand sides of

Eqs.

E.19
to
E.20

over all
n\geqslant 0
is equal to

\delta_{\alpha}(\tau)
and
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,
respectively.
Also, the right-hand sides of

Eqs.

E.19
to
E.20

have virtual rank
n
,
which follows from
Theorem

E.2.3
and

[
29
, Proposition 5.14]
.
This proves the theorem.

\Box

E.3   A combinatorial identity

We prove the identity
Eq.

E.16
used in the proof of
Theorem

E.2.3
.

E.3.1.

Let
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\textnormal{-}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Fin}}}
be the category
consisting of finite sets acted on by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
,
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant maps between them.
For integers
r,s\geqslant 0
, let
(r,s)\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\textnormal{-}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Fin}}}

denote the set with
2r+s
elements,
with a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action fixing
s
elements.

Let
\left\{\begin{smallmatrix}r&s\\
r^{\prime}&s^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right\}

denote the number of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant surjections
(r,s)\twoheadrightarrow(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})

up to automorphisms of
(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})
.
A standard argument shows that

\displaystyle\left\{\,\begin{matrix}r&s\\
r^{\prime}&0\end{matrix}\,\right\}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{r}{\, r' \,}\,2^{r-r^{\prime}}&\text{if }s=0\ ,\\
0&\text{if }s>0\ ,\end{cases}

(E.21)

\displaystyle\left\{\,\begin{matrix}r&s\\
r^{\prime}&1\end{matrix}\,\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=\max\{0,1-s\}}^{r-r^{\prime}}{}\binom{r}{\,k\,}\,\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{r - k}{r'}\,2^{r-r^{\prime}-k}\ ,

(E.22)

where
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{r}{r'}
denotes the

Stirling number of the second kind
,
defined as the number of partitions of
\{1,\dotsc,r\}

into
r^{\prime}
non-empty subsets.

E.3.2.

We reformulate the identity
Eq.

E.16

as the following result.

Lemma 0
.

Let
J=(r,s)\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\textnormal{-}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Fin}}}
.
Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[67.5564pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &J=J_{0}\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow J_{m}\text{ in }\mathbb{Z}_{2}\textnormal{-}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Fin}}}\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&(J_{m})^{\smash[b]{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}}=J_{m}\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}(-1)^{m}=\begin{cases}(-1)^{r}\,(2r-1)!!&\text{if }s\leqslant 1\ ,\\
0&\text{otherwise}\ ,\end{cases}

(E.23)

where we sum over chains of non-bijective surjections

J=J_{0}\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow J_{m}
,
up to isomorphisms that are the identity on
J
.

Proof 48
.

We prove the lemma by induction on
r
, then on
s
.
If
r=0
, the result is standard,
since when
s>1
,
chains with
J_{m}=(0,1)
and
J_{m}\neq(0,1)

cancel out in pairs.

Let
A(r,s)
denote the left-hand side of
Eq.

E.23
.
We have the recursive formula

A(r,s)=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leqslant r^{\prime}\leqslant r\\
0\leqslant s^{\prime}\leqslant r+s\\
(r,s)\neq(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})\end{subarray}}{}\left\{\,\begin{matrix}r&s\\
r^{\prime}&s^{\prime}\end{matrix}\,\right\}\cdot A(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})\ ,

(E.24)

with notations as in
E.3.1
.
However, by the induction hypothesis,
only terms with
s^{\prime}\leqslant 1
contribute to the sum.
Using
Eqs.

E.21
to
E.22
, we obtain

\displaystyle A(r,0)=A(r,1)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{r^{\prime}=0}^{r-1}{}(-1)^{r^{\prime}}\,(2r^{\prime}-1)!!\cdot\sum_{k=0}^{r-r^{\prime}}{}\binom{r}{\,k\,}\,\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{r - k}{r'}\,2^{r-r^{\prime}-k}\ ,

(E.25)

where we changed the
k
in
Eq.

E.22
to
r-r^{\prime}-k
.
Therefore, to prove
Eq.

E.23
for
s=0
and
1
,
it is enough to show that

\sum_{r^{\prime}=0}^{r}{}(-1)^{r^{\prime}}\,(2r^{\prime}-1)!!\cdot\sum_{k=0}^{r-r^{\prime}}{}\binom{r}{\,k\,}\,\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{r - k}{r'}\,2^{r-r^{\prime}-k}=0\ .

(E.26)

We postpone the proof of this to
E.3.4
.

If
s>1
, then compared to the case when
s=1
,
the right-hand side of
Eq.

E.24

has an extra term
-A(r,1)
.
Since all other terms add up to
A(r,1)
,
we obtain
A(r,s)=0
,
which finishes the proof.

\Box

E.3.4. Proof of
Eq.

E.26
.

We translate the identity
Eq.

E.26

back to chains of surjections in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\textnormal{-}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Fin}}}
,
giving

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[47.83347pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &r=r_{0}>\cdots>r_{m}=0;\\[-1.50694pt]
&(r_{0},1)\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow(r_{m},1)\text{ in }\mathbb{Z}_{2}\textnormal{-}\smash[b]{\textsf{{Fin}}}\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}(-1)^{m}=(-1)^{r}\,(2r-1)!!\ ,

(E.27)

where we sum over chains of surjections
up to isomorphisms that are the identity on
(r_{0},1)
.
Note that we have reduced
Eq.

E.23

to the case when
s=1
,
and we are now restricted to contributions
from chains in which every term is of the form
(r^{\prime},1)
.

We now prove
Eq.

E.27
by induction on
r
.
Write the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
(r,1)
as an involution

(-)^{\vee}\colon(r,1)\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\sim$}}\mspace{3mu}}{\to}}(r,1)
.
Let
0\in(r,1)
be the unique element with
0^{\vee}=0
,
and fix an element
1\in(r,1)
with
1^{\vee}\neq 1
.

For a chain

(r_{\bullet},1)=\bigl((r_{0},1)\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow(r_{m},1)\bigr)
,
write
p_{k}\colon(r_{0},1)\to(r_{k},1)

and
p_{\smash{k}}^{\smash{k^{\prime}}}\colon(r_{k^{\prime}},1)\to(r_{k},1)

for the surjections, where
0\leqslant k^{\prime}\leqslant k\leqslant m
.
Define
K_{k}=(p_{k})^{-1}(p_{k}(1))\subset(r_{0},1)
,
giving chain of subsets

\{1\}=K_{0}\subset\cdots\subset K_{m}=(r_{0},1)
.

We say that an index
k\in\{0,\dotsc,m-1\}

is
special
for the chain
(r_{\bullet},1)

if
0\notin K_{k}\neq K_{k+1}
,
and
critical
if moreover
0\in K_{k+1}
.
Then every chain has a unique critical index.
A special index
k
is
good
if

p_{\smash{k+1}}^{\smash{k}}

does not factor as a composition of two non-bijective surjections
through an object of the form
(r^{\prime},1)
,
or
bad
otherwise.
We will show that only chains with all indices good
up to the critical one contribute to the sum,
and that other chains cancel out in pairs.

Fix a chain
(r_{\bullet},1)
of length
m
,
such that not all indices up to the critical index
k_{0}
are good.
Let
k\leqslant k_{0}
be the smallest special index
that is bad or has a preceding non-special index.
We construct a new chain
(r^{\prime}_{\bullet},1)
of length
m\pm 1
, as follows.
If
k
is good, then
k>0
and
k-1
is non-special,
and we set
(r^{\prime}_{\bullet},1)
to be the chain

(r_{0},1)\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow(r_{k-1},1)\twoheadrightarrow(r_{k+1},1)\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow(r_{m},1)\ ,

where
k-1
becomes bad.
On the other hand, if
k
is bad in
(r_{\bullet},1)
,
we set
(r^{\prime}_{\bullet},1)
to be the chain

(r_{0},1)\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow(r_{k},1)\twoheadrightarrow(r_{k+1}+1,1)\twoheadrightarrow(r_{k+1},1)\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow(r_{m},1)\ ,

where the map
(r_{k},1)\twoheadrightarrow(r_{k+1}+1,1)

sends
p_{k}(1)
and
p_{k}(1)^{\vee}
to the extra pair of elements,
and all other elements to
(r_{k+1},1)
via
p_{\smash{k+1}}^{\smash{k}}
;
the map
(r_{k+1}+1,1)\twoheadrightarrow(r_{k+1},1)

sends the extra pair of elements to

p_{k+1}(1)
and
p_{k+1}(1)^{\vee}
,
and all other elements to
(r_{k+1},1)
canonically.
The former map is non-bijective since
k
is bad.
In the new chain,
k
becomes non-special and
k+1
becomes good.

Note that applying the above construction twice to a chain
gives back the original chain.
This implies that contributions of these chains
to
Eq.

E.27
cancel out in pairs,
and we may consider only those chains
with all indices good up to the critical one.
We say that these chains are
good
.

We divide good chains into equivalence classes,
where two chains are equivalent if they are the same
before and including the critical index.
Fixing a critical index
k_{0}
,
the number of such equivalence classes with critical index
k_{0}

is equal to
(2r-2)(2r-4)\cdots(2r-2k_{0})
,
and the contribution of each class to
Eq.

E.27

is
(-1)^{r}\,(2r-2k_{0}-3)!!
,
by the induction hypothesis.
Therefore, the total contribution of good chains is

\sum_{k_{0}=0}^{r-1}{}(-1)^{r}\,(2r-2)(2r-4)\cdots(2r-2k_{0})\cdot(2r-2k_{0}-3)!!=(-1)^{r}\,(2r-1)!!\ ,

which completes the proof of
Eq.

E.27
.

\square

E.3.5.

For the reader’s convenience,
we also state the analogous result of

E.3.2

in the non-self-dual case,
which can be proved by a similar, but simpler argument.

Lemma 0
.

Let
Fin
be the category of finite sets,
and let
J\in\smash[b]{\textsf{{Fin}}}
be non-empty.
Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[67.5564pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &J=J_{0}\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow J_{m}=\{*\}\textnormal{ in }\smash[b]{\textsf{{Fin}}}\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}(-1)^{m}=(-1)^{|J|-1}\,(|J|-1)!\ ,

(E.28)

where we sum over chains of non-bijective surjections

J=J_{0}\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow J_{m}=\{*\}
,
up to isomorphisms that are the identity on
J
.
\square

E.4   Proof of the theorem

E.4.1.

A key idea in our proof is to apply
Theorems

E.2.3
and
E.2.4
,
not only to
\mathcal{A}
, but also to
the category
\mathcal{A}^{{\smash{(n)}}}
of filtrations of
\mathcal{A}
,
discussed in
Sections

3.3
and
3.6
.

For a self-dual weak stability condition
\tau
on
\mathcal{A}

satisfying
, and for a slope
t
,
let
\mathcal{A}(\tau;t)\subset\mathcal{A}
be the full subcategory of

\tau
-semistable objects
E\in\mathcal{A}
with either
E\simeq 0
or
\tau(E)=t
.
Then
\mathcal{A}(\tau;t)
is closed under direct summands,
so it satisfies
,
, and
,
and the trivial stability condition
0
on
\mathcal{A}(\tau;t)
satisfies
.
In particular, when
t=0
, the category
\mathcal{A}(\tau;0)

is self-dual, and also satisfies
.

Now, for an integer
n\geqslant 0
,
consider the category
\mathcal{A}(\tau;t)^{{\smash{(n)}}}

of filtrations in
\mathcal{A}(\tau;t)
, as in
Section

3.3
.
It again satisfies
,
, and
,
and the trivial stability condition
0

on
\mathcal{A}(\tau;t)^{{\smash{(n)}}}
satisfies
.
When
t=0
, it also satisfies

by
Section

3.6
.
We also denote this by
\mathcal{A}(\tau;t)^{{\smash{(I)}}}
,
where
I
is a totally ordered set with
n
elements.

For
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A}(\tau;t))
,
write
\delta^{{\smash{(n)}}}_{\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}})

for the stack function

\delta_{(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})}(0)

for the category
\mathcal{A}(\tau;t)^{{\smash{(n)}}}
,
the class
(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A}(\tau;t)^{{\smash{(n)}}})

in the sense of
2.3.2
,
and the trivial stability condition
0
.
Define
\sigma^{{\smash{(n)}}}_{\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{(n)}})

similarly, as the stack function
\sigma_{(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n})}(0)

for
\mathcal{A}(\tau;t)^{{\smash{(n)}}}
and the trivial stability condition
0
.

Similarly, for
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A}(\tau;0))

and
\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}(\tau;0))
,
define the stack functions

\delta^{{\smash{(2n+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n},\mspace{2mu}\rho}(\tau)
,

\sigma^{{\smash{(2n+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n},\mspace{2mu}\rho}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{{\smash{(2n+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}})

as the
\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
and
\sigma^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}
functions
for the self-dual category
\mathcal{A}(\tau;0)^{{\smash{(2n+1)}}}
,
the class
(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{n},\rho)\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A}(\tau;0)^{{\smash{(2n+1)}}})

in the sense of
3.3.8
,
and the trivial stability condition
0
.

Theorem E.4.2
.

The element

\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in\mathrm{SF}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}})

has pure virtual rank
0
.
In particular, we have

\int_{\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)\in{\smash{\widehat{\mathrm{M}}}\vphantom{M}}^{\circ}_{\mathbb{K}}\ .

(E.29)

Proof 49
.

For
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{k}\in C(\mathcal{A})

and
\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})
, we have

(\pi^{(2k+1),\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}})_{!}\,(\delta^{{\smash{(2k+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\alpha_{1},\mspace{2mu}\dotsc,\mspace{2mu}\alpha_{k},\mspace{2mu}\rho}(\tau))=\delta_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)\diamond\cdots\diamond\delta_{\alpha_{k}}(\tau)\diamond\delta^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\rho}(\tau)\ ,

(E.30)

where
\delta^{{\smash{(2k+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\alpha_{1},\mspace{2mu}\dotsc,\mspace{2mu}\alpha_{k},\mspace{2mu}\rho}(\tau)

is defined in
E.4.1
.
Recall from
Eq.

5.7

the definition of
\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)
,
which can now be written as

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[50.6673pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-4.52083pt]
&k\geqslant 0;\,\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{k}\in C(\mathcal{A}),\,\rho\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\theta=\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{k}+\rho\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tau(\alpha_{k})=0\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}\binom{-1/2}{k}\cdot(\pi^{(2k+1),\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}})_{!}\,(\delta^{{\smash{(2k+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\alpha_{1},\mspace{2mu}\dotsc,\mspace{2mu}\alpha_{k},\mspace{2mu}\rho}(\tau))\ .

(E.31)

To prove the theorem, it is enough to prove that

\Pi^{\mathrm{vi}}_{n}(\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau))=0

(E.32)

for all integers
n>0
.

By
[
29
, Proposition 5.14]
,
the virtual rank projection
\Pi^{\mathrm{vi}}_{n}
commutes with
the pushforward
(\pi^{(2k+1),\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}})_{!}
.
We therefore study the virtual rank projections of

\delta^{{\smash{(2k+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\alpha_{1},\mspace{2mu}\dotsc,\mspace{2mu}\alpha_{k},\mspace{2mu}\rho}(\tau)
.

Write
I=\{1,\dotsc,k,0,k^{\vee},\dotsc,1^{\vee}\}

and
J=\{1,\dotsc,n,0,n^{\vee},\dotsc,1^{\vee}\}
,
with total orders given by the written order,
and the obvious involutions.
Applying
E.2.4

to the category
\mathcal{A}(\tau;0)^{{\smash{(I)}}}
in
E.4.1
,
we obtain

\Pi^{\mathrm{vi}}_{n}(\delta^{{\smash{(2k+1)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\alpha_{1},\mspace{2mu}\dotsc,\mspace{2mu}\alpha_{k},\mspace{2mu}\rho}(\tau))=\frac{1}{2^{n}n!}\cdot{}\\[4.30554pt]
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[50.6673pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} \\[-6.02777pt]
&\alpha_{i,j}\in C^{\circ}(\mathcal{A})\text{ for }(i,j)\in(I\times J)\setminus(0,0);\,\rho_{0,0}\in C^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}(\mathcal{A})\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\tau(\alpha_{i,j})=0\text{ for all }(i,j)\text{ with }\alpha_{i,j}\neq 0,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\alpha_{\smash{i^{\vee},\mspace{2mu}j^{\vee}}}=\alpha_{i,j}^{\vee}\text{ for all }(i,j),\\[-1.50694pt]
&\textstyle\alpha_{i}=\sum_{j\in J}\alpha_{i,j}\text{ for all }i\in I,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\rho=\bar{\alpha}_{0,1}+\cdots+\bar{\alpha}_{0,n}+\rho_{0,0}\,,\\[-1.50694pt]
&\textstyle\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i,j}\neq 0\text{ for all }j\in J\setminus 0\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}\sigma^{{\smash{(I)}}}_{(\alpha_{i,1})_{i\in I}}(\tau)\mathbin{\hbox to6pt{\vbox to6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.9986pt\lower-2.9986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.87 0 C 3.87 2.14 2.14 3.87 0 3.87 C -2.14 3.87 -3.87 2.14 -3.87 0 C -3.87 -2.14 -2.14 -3.87 0 -3.87 C 2.14 -3.87 3.87 -2.14 3.87 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.38 0 L 0 2.38 L -2.38 0 L 0 -2.38 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\cdots\mathbin{\hbox to6pt{\vbox to6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.9986pt\lower-2.9986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.87 0 C 3.87 2.14 2.14 3.87 0 3.87 C -2.14 3.87 -3.87 2.14 -3.87 0 C -3.87 -2.14 -2.14 -3.87 0 -3.87 C 2.14 -3.87 3.87 -2.14 3.87 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.38 0 L 0 2.38 L -2.38 0 L 0 -2.38 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\sigma^{{\smash{(I)}}}_{(\alpha_{i,n})_{i\in I}}(\tau)\mathbin{\hbox to6pt{\vbox to6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.9986pt\lower-2.9986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.87 0 C 3.87 2.14 2.14 3.87 0 3.87 C -2.14 3.87 -3.87 2.14 -3.87 0 C -3.87 -2.14 -2.14 -3.87 0 -3.87 C 2.14 -3.87 3.87 -2.14 3.87 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.38 0 L 0 2.38 L -2.38 0 L 0 -2.38 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\sigma^{{\smash{(I)}},\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\alpha_{1,0},\,\dotsc,\mspace{2mu}\alpha_{k,0},\mspace{2mu}\rho_{0,0}}(\tau)\ .

(E.33)

We abbreviate each term in the sum
Eq.

E.33

as
\sigma^{(I),\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\underline{\alpha}}(\tau)
,
where
\underline{\alpha}=(\alpha_{i,j})_{i\in I,\,j\in J}

is a matrix with
\alpha_{0,0}=\rho_{0,0}
, and write

\sigma^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\underline{\alpha}}(\tau)=(\pi^{(I),\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}})_{!}\,\sigma^{(I),\mspace{2mu}{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}}_{\underline{\alpha}}(\tau)\ .

Let
A_{n,k}
be the set of matrices
\underline{\alpha}

that appear in
Eq.

E.33

for some choice of
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{k},\rho
.
Then
Eqs.

E.31
and
E.33
imply that

\Pi^{\mathrm{vi}}_{n}(\epsilon^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\theta}(\tau))=\frac{1}{2^{n}n!}\cdot{}\sum_{k\geqslant 0}\sum_{\underline{\alpha}\in A_{n,k}}{}\binom{-1/2}{k}\cdot\sigma^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\underline{\alpha}}(\tau)\ .

(E.34)

Note that the element
\sigma^{\smash{\mathrm{sd}}}_{\underline{\alpha}}(\tau)

only depends on the equivalent class of
\underline{\alpha}
,
where two matrices
\underline{\alpha}\in A_{n,k}
and
\underline{\alpha}^{\prime}\in A_{k^{\prime}}

are equivalent if for all
j\in J
,
the subsequence of
(\alpha_{i,j})_{i\in I}
with
\alpha_{i,j}\neq 0

is the same as
the subsequence of
(\alpha^{\prime}_{i,j})_{i\in I^{\prime}}
with
\alpha^{\prime}_{i,j}\neq 0
,
where
I^{\prime}=\{1,\dotsc,k^{\prime},0,k^{\prime\vee},\dotsc,1^{\vee}\}
.
It is then enough to prove that for a fixed
\underline{\alpha}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geqslant 0}{}\binom{-1/2}{k}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\makebox[21.11137pt][l]{\scriptsize$\begin{aligned} &\underline{\alpha}^{\prime}\in A_{n,k}\colon\\[-1.50694pt]
&\underline{\alpha}^{\prime}\sim\underline{\alpha}\end{aligned}$}\end{subarray}}{}1

\displaystyle=0\ .

(E.35)

To prove this, we first observe that
the number
\underline{\alpha}^{\prime}\in A_{n,k}
with
\underline{\alpha}^{\prime}\sim\underline{\alpha}

is equal to the number of subsets of
I\times J
,
invariant under the involution
(i,j)\mapsto(i^{\vee},j^{\vee})
,
such that the number of elements in each row
\{i\}\times J

is non-zero unless
i=0
,
and the number of elements in each column
I\times\{j\}

is equal to
a_{j}
, where
a_{j}
is the number of non-zero entries

\alpha_{i,j}
in
\underline{\alpha}
in that column.
Note that
a_{j}=a_{j^{\vee}}
for all
j\in J
.

Consider the generating series of this counting problem,
with a formal variable
x_{j}
assigned to each
a_{j}

for
j\in\{1,\dotsc,n,0\}
.
For convenience, we write
x_{j^{\vee}}=x_{j}
for
j\in J
.
Summing over
k\geqslant 0
, we obtain the generating series

\displaystyle F(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n},x_{0})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geqslant 0}{}\binom{-1/2}{k}\cdot\biggl(\ \sum_{\varnothing\neq J^{\prime}\subset J}\ \prod_{j\in J^{\prime}}x_{j}\biggr)^{k}\cdot\sum_{J^{\prime}\subset\{1,\dotsc,n,0\}}\prod_{j\in J^{\prime}}x_{j}

\displaystyle=\biggl(\ \prod_{j\in J}{}(1+x_{j})\biggr)^{-1/2}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{n}{}(1+x_{j})

\displaystyle=(1+x_{0})^{1/2}\ .

Therefore, the left-hand side of
Eq.

E.35

is zero unless
a_{j}=0
for all
j\neq 0
.
But this cannot happen, as we assumed that
n>0
.
This proves
Eq.

E.35
, and hence
Eq.

E.32
.
